- A reserved word used as a module qualifier, as in `go::x`, still gets "`go` is not imported" instead of E0007 (D-469).
- ~~The UI tests `naming_convention`, `reserved_word_go` and `reserved_words` pass only once E0038 is retired (D-451), and the negative case of D-141, a native-only module used by a package that also targets JS, needs the error code of C12.~~ Done: the three tests pass now that the front end no longer reports E0038 for their constructs (D-480), and `12-targets-and-ffi/module_target_both` covers the negative case of D-141 with E0067 (D-512).
- ~~The spec's error examples that have codes today, E0042, E0044 and E0047, get UI tests once their constructs are past E0038 (D-476).~~ Done: `07-modules-and-packages/module_not_imported` pins E0042, `05-expressions/shadowing/let_shadows_let` and `let_shadows_import` pin E0044, and `03-types/inference/mixed_list`, `dyn_not_inside` and `never_not_inside` pin E0047.

## 2026-10-10: M1 front end

Goal: record the user's decisions on M1's exit, and settle the spec gaps that the M1 front end found (name resolution, type checking, interfaces, exhaustiveness, the warnings of §13.8, the cross-target and constant checks and the tier-1 standard library), stating what the implementation does.

- **D-477 Later-tier examples move to their milestone**: Examples 005, 009, 013, 018, 019, 022 and 028 import tier-2 modules such as `std::fiber` and `std::channel`, and 031 imports `std::http`. They need not pass `cheby check` in M1, and the E0038 for those imports is expected until the milestone that ships the modules: M2 for the first seven and M3 for 031, as their `test.toml` files say. M1's exit criterion that every other example passes `cheby check` exempts them, as it exempts 030, and the done-when of task C9, "028 checks", becomes UI tests for bounds, `compare` and ordering. Why: the user decided it. Shipping fibers and channels in M1 would pull M2's runtime work forward, and the language constructs these examples use are covered by UI tests. Extends D-446.
- **D-478 Constants are checked but not evaluated before E6**: Until task E6 lands the JIT constant evaluator, `cheby check` checks a constant's declared type, its restrictions (E0068, D-511) and cycles of direct references between constants (E0069), and does not evaluate the initializer. `cheby run` reports E0038, "constants are not supported yet", at each use of a constant that `main` reaches. Panics and exceeded limits in initializers, cycles through function calls and the JS range of a constant's value are found from E6. Why: the user decided it. Every example with constants then checks in M1, without a second evaluator that E6 would replace. Refines D-451, D-459.
- **D-479 Std tests live in std files and are skipped in user builds**: The standard library's `test` blocks sit next to the code they test in `std/*.cheby`. A user build, that is `cheby check`, `cheby run` and the driver's MIR and CLIF dumps, neither checks them nor puts them in THIR, and the warnings of §13.8 ignore them. Testing std itself checks and runs them (G4). Why: the user decided it. Std has hundreds of tests, which would slow every user build and could only give diagnostics that the user cannot act on.
- **D-480 E0038 at M1 exit only for unshipped std modules**: At M1 exit the front end supports every language construct and reports E0038 for one thing only: an import of a std module that the stdlib spec or plan defines but `std/` does not ship. These are the modules of tiers 2 and 3 and the tier-1 modules `std::multimap`, `std::sorted_map`, `std::sorted_set`, `std::sorted_multimap`, `std::ordered_map`, `std::ordered_set` and `std::ordered_multimap`. The message is "the module `std::fiber` is not supported yet", and paths through such an import report nothing more. The other uses of D-451 end: no shipped std module is partial, so a missing name is E0042, a type that nothing determines is E0057, and a function without a body exists only on the targets of its `@external` attributes, with uses elsewhere reported as E0067 (D-512). No signature is gated any more, so the interim rule that skipped the body of an item with an unsupported signature has nothing left to apply to. MIR lowering still reports E0038 for each construct the back end cannot compile yet, as "<construct> are not supported yet" at the construct, such as "`case` expressions are not supported yet" (D-510), until the back-end phases land. `docs/diagnostics/E0038.md` says all this. Why: the user decided it. The examples of later tiers keep a precise error instead of a false E0039 (D-477), and running programs is the back end's work. Removing the code at M1 exit, as D-451 planned, was rejected because planned std modules and the partial back end still need it. Refines D-451.
- **D-481 Map, Set and Bytes are builtin items outside the prelude**: `Map<K, V>`, `Set<T>` and `Bytes` are compiler-known items of the builtin module of D-447, like the prelude types, but they are not in the prelude. Their home modules, `std::map`, `std::set` and `std::bytes`, do not declare them again: their module docs name the type, and their functions satisfy interfaces for it (§8.2). In its home module the type is in scope unqualified, as the spec's signatures write it. Every other module names it through an import of the home module: `map::Map`, `m::Map` after `import std::map as m`, or `Map` after `import std::map::{Map}`. Written unqualified elsewhere, it is E0042 with the help "`Map` is not in the prelude: add `import std::map` and write `map::Map`". Diagnostics print it through the home module's last segment whatever the import's alias, as in `map::Map<String, Int>`, bare inside the home module, and a full path is `std::map::Map`. Why: the runtime implements these types (D-274), so they need a compiler-known identity before any std module is checked, as the prelude types do, while D-112 keeps them out of the prelude. A declaration in the home module would make a second type. The printed form follows D-463. Extends D-447.
- **D-482 Std-only attributes `@std_clamped` and `@std_hooks`**: The attribute of D-303 is `@std_clamped(param, …)` on a function. It names one or more distinct parameters of the function, which the function clamps (D-298) and W0013 checks (D-518). The attribute of D-342 is `@std_hooks(equal_fn, hash_fn, debug_fn)` on a type. It names three functions of the type's module with the shapes `equal_fn(a: T, b: T) -> Bool`, `hash_fn(value: T) -> K` for any `K`, and `debug_fn(value: T) -> String`, where `T` is the type and no type parameter has a bound. The runtime hashes the `K` that `hash_fn` returns with the built-in structural hash. Only package `std` may write these attributes. Outside `std` they are unknown attributes, and a misplaced one or a violation of these rules is E0045 too. Why: D-303 and D-342 need these facts recorded on std items without naming the attributes. The runtime routine of D-252 calls the hooks without dictionaries, so they cannot have bounds, and a hash hook that returns a canonical value, such as a sorted list of entries, reuses the one structural hash. Extends D-461.
- **D-483 Std module layout**: The internal module that D-361 asks for is `std::json::tree`, which holds the raw tree and its parser. Both `std::json` and `std::json::decode` import it, and its items are package-visible, never `pub`. Imports between std modules are acyclic like any package's (D-145): `std::string` imports `std::bytes` and not the reverse, so `string::from_bytes` is written in `std::string`, and `bytes::from_string` is a kernel that needs only the builtin `String`. Where a parameter name of the stdlib spec would bind a name the module already binds, the std source renames the parameter: `list::max_with`, `min_with` and `sort_with` call their comparison `by`, because `std::list` has a function `compare`. A module with functions named after other modules imports those modules under other names, as `std::json::decode` imports `std::list as lists`. Kernels have no body that binds parameters, so they keep the spec's names, such as `count`. Why: the no-shadowing rule applies to std (D-051, D-062), and a parameter name is not part of a function's API, so `docs/stdlib/` keeps the spec's names. Package visibility keeps the raw tree out of every other package.
- **D-484 The kernel boundary of std**: Each numeric module, `std::int`, `std::float`, `std::f32` and the sized integer modules, declares its own kernels with one symbol per type, such as `cheby_i32_from_int`, instead of sharing generic kernels. The kernels of `std::list` are `length`, `get`, `push`, `prepend`, `append`, `set`, `slice`, `take`, `drop`, `split_at` and `unique`, and its other functions are written in Cheby. A kernel calls a Cheby closure only where the stdlib spec makes a function that takes one a kernel, which is `map::upsert` alone: it updates a unique map in place with one lookup (§9.3). Every other function that takes a closure, such as `bytes::fold`, is written in Cheby over kernels such as `bytes::get`. Every kernel is bound as `@external(native, "cheby_<module>_<function>")` and `@external(js, "./<module>.mjs", "<function>")`. The kernels of `std::io` return `Result<T, (Int, String)>`, a kind code and the operating system's message: `1` not found, `2` permission denied, `3` invalid UTF-8, and any other code another kind with the message. `std::io` builds its opaque `Error` from the pair. Why: kernels are monomorphic foreign functions, so one per type keeps every signature exact. A small kernel set keeps the semantics in Cheby (D-274), and each kernel that calls back into Cheby needs the runtime to re-enter compiled code, so only the one whose required cost needs it does. Fixed names let the native runtime and M4's JS modules follow without a table. A kernel cannot build a record of another module, and the pair keeps `Error`'s representation private to `std::io` (D-304, ADR-0051). Extends D-274.
- **D-485 json decode's error for a non-object**: When `decode::field` or `decode::optional_field` reads a value that is not an object, it records `expected: "Object"`, the kind found and the value's own path, and then calls `next`. `field` passes its decoder's placeholder (D-401), and `optional_field` passes `default`. Why: `json/decode.md` says only that `next` gets "the placeholder". For `optional_field`, `default` is what the caller chose for a member that is not there, and a value that is not an object has no members. Refines D-401.
- **D-486 Diagnostic code assignment and std staging during implementation**: The front-end phase adds E0057 type annotation needed, E0058 unknown field, E0059 invalid record, E0060 refutable pattern, E0061 non-exhaustive `case`, E0062 alternatives that bind different names, E0063 unsatisfied interface, E0064 `dyn` of an interface that is not object-safe, E0065 `dyn` conversion that needs a bound, E0066 invalid interface, E0067 target mismatch, E0068 invalid constant type, E0069 constant cycle, E0070 invalid alias, E0071 literal out of range, E0072 invalid operand, E0073 invalid test result and E0074 a less visible type in a signature. It adds the warnings W0002 unused binding, W0003 unused import, W0004 unused `priv` item, W0005 unused package-visible item, W0006 discarded `Result`, W0007 hidden prelude name, W0008 irrefutable `let assert`, W0009 `todo`, W0010 unreachable arm, W0011 `==` on functions or handles, W0012 deprecated item and W0013 negative count. Work done in parallel takes the next free number in `crates/cheby_diag/src/codes.rs` when it adds a check, and adds its file in `docs/diagnostics/` in the same change. A std module written before the checker can check it is listed in `STAGED_STD_MODULES`: it is embedded and parsed in tests but never loaded, and importing it stays E0038, until it moves to `STD_MODULES`. The list is empty at M1 exit. Why: D-419 assigns codes in order as checks arrive, and parallel work must not take one number twice. Staging let std be written alongside the checker without breaking user imports. Extends D-419.
- **D-487 Where prelude-hiding and opacity diagnostics point**: A declaration or import that hides a prelude type or constructor warns once where it is declared or imported, as "`Some` hides the prelude constructor `Some`" (W0007). A note says that the prelude item stays reachable through its home module, as `option::Some` after `import std::option`, and a help suggests another name. A use of an opaque type's constructor or fields from another package is E0043 at the written name, with the note "a `pub` type hides its constructors unless it is `pub exposed`", or "its constructors and fields" for a field access or a record update. Why: §7.6 and D-146 ask for the warning, and §4.3.4 for the error, without a shape. The use is what the user can change, since the declaration is in another package. Refines D-146.
- **D-488 Less visible types and aliases in signatures (E0074)**: A signature that mentions a type or interface less visible than its item is E0074 (§4.1.3, §7.4, D-148, D-149), once per item and offending type, at the first mention, with a label at the type's declaration. Bounds, parameter and return types, a constant's type, an alias's definition, embedded interfaces and interface functions count. So do a type's fields where they are visible (§4.3.4): the fields of a `pub exposed` type need `pub` types, and the fields of a `pub` or package-visible type must not mention `priv` types. An alias is transparent (D-081), so its own visibility does not matter, only that of the types it stands for: a `pub` function may use a `priv` alias of `Int`. Why: the spec states the rule without a code. Code that can see the item never needs the alias's name, because it can write the type. Checking the alias's own visibility was rejected, because it would reject signatures that every caller can use.
- **D-489 Satisfaction when a builtin type's home module is unreadable**: Interface satisfaction for a builtin type uses its home module's functions only when that module is among the checked module's dependencies. A std module that does not import the home module gets E0063 with the note "`Int` gets its functions from `std::int`, which this module does not import (D-447)" and the help "add `import std::int`". In user code the note would say that the home module "is not supported yet (D-451)", without a help, but every module of a local package depends on every shipped home module (D-447), and every home module ships. Why: the locality rule lets a module read only its dependencies' interfaces (D-246), and std modules depend only on what they import (D-447). Telling users to import a module they cannot use would lead them to E0038. Refines D-447.
- **D-490 Erroneous signatures and silent uses**: A function, alias or constant whose written types have an error gets an error signature, and its uses report nothing more. So does a function whose bound fails to lower: it is not published without the bound. The body of such a function is still checked for its own errors, with a type parameter whose bound failed taken as the error type, so uses that need the bound are silent. A constructor or interface function with an erroneous type keeps its shape, with the error type in place of that type, so its other uses are still checked. Type arguments written on an interface are E0023 once where they are written (D-462), and the interface is then used without them. Why: one diagnostic per root cause. Publishing a function without its failed bound would give it a weaker signature and cause false E0063 in its callers.
- **D-491 Declaration checks: `main` type parameters and bodiless types**: A top-level `main` with type parameters is E0050, labelled "`main` takes no type parameters" at the type parameters. A type declaration without a body is an external type whether or not it has an `@external`. Without one, its handles are forgotten when the last reference goes away, as §12.5.3 says, and App. A.2's note that such a type requires an `@external` is a spec bug. Why: `cheby run` cannot choose type arguments for `main` or pass dictionaries for its bounds (§4.2.1). An error for an external type without a drop function was rejected, because §12.5.3 defines that case and requiring the attribute would force a dummy drop function. Extends D-448.
- **D-492 `Never` in operators, satisfaction and exhaustiveness**: A `Never` operand of a binary operator converts to the other operand's type (D-235). An operator whose operand type stays `Never`, as in `x < x` after `let x = panic`, has no special case. `Never` is declared in no module and has no home module, so it satisfies only empty interfaces: overloadable operators, unary `-`, interpolation, interface calls and bounds on it are E0063, with the note "`Never` is declared in no module, so it satisfies only empty interfaces", and `%`, the bitwise operators and the shifts are E0072. Exhaustiveness treats `Never` as an opaque type, so a `case` over a `Never` subject needs a `_` or binding arm, and `Option<Never>` needs a `Some(_)` arm. Why: one rule covers every use of a `Never` value. An earlier built-in rule accepted `x < x` and `x % x` while `"{x}"` was E0063. Treating `Never` as empty would make every arm unreachable (W0010), which helps nobody. Refines D-161.
- **D-493 Types of test bodies**: A test body of type `Never` counts as `Nil`, and a body whose type is still undetermined at its end is unified with `Nil`. A body of type `Result` whose error type nothing determines is E0057. Any other type is E0073, "a test body must have type `Nil` or `Result<Nil, E>`, found `Bool`", with a help to write `assert` when the type is `Bool`. Why: D-140 allows only these types. A body that ends in `panic` or `todo` is a valid test that fails, and an undetermined error type is like any other type that nothing determines. Refines D-140.
- **D-494 Error recovery in bodies**: E0057 is not reported in a body that already has another error, since such a type usually follows from that error. A body with an error is left out of THIR, so exhaustiveness, reachability and irrefutability (E0060, E0061, W0008, W0010) are checked only in bodies without errors. They are not checked either for a `case` subject, `let`, `let assert` or parameter whose type contains the error type from a signature, such as after E0042 or E0051. A constructor, tuple or list pattern at the error type gives its sub-patterns the error type, so the names it binds cause nothing more. Why: one diagnostic per root cause. Matching over the error type ended in an internal compiler error that hid the real diagnostic.
- **D-495 Codes for checks the spec leaves uncoded**: `&&`, `||` and `!` check their operands against `Bool`, so another type is E0047 with a label on the operator, "`&&` takes `Bool` operands", and E0072 covers only `%`, the bitwise operators and the shifts. A `use` whose binder count differs from the parameter count of its callback's type is E0047, labelled "this `use` has 2 binders, but the callback takes 1 parameter", with a note giving the callback's type and a label on the called function, and the callback is then checked without an expected type. A constructor pattern of the wrong shape gets one diagnostic: E0048 when the count of fields differs, as with fields on a unit constructor or none on a positional one, and E0059 when the fields have the wrong form, named for positional or the reverse, or when a named-field constructor is written without its fields. Interpolation in a test name is E0012, "interpolation in a test name", from validation. An interface with type parameters, `interface I<T>`, is one E0013 from the parser, "an interface has no type parameters", with the note "interfaces are not generic (D-165)" and a help to remove them, and the rest of the declaration still parses. Why: §4.7, §5.4.5, §5.10, §6.4 and §8.1 state these rules without naming their diagnostics, and D-419 needs a code for each. Each reuses the code of the closest rule, and the parser's one error replaces a cascade of two. Extends D-462.
- **D-496 Range of float literals**: A float literal fits its type when it rounds to a finite value of that type. A `Float` literal must be finite as a 64-bit float. An `F32` literal must be smaller in magnitude than the largest finite `F32` plus half a unit in the last place, so `3.4028235e38` is the largest `F32` literal and the midpoint rounds to infinity. A literal that does not fit is E0071, "literal out of range for `F32`". Why: §3.3.4 says a literal must fit its type, and rounding is how a float literal gets its value. Refines D-084.
- **D-497 Order of inference at calls and `case` arms**: In a call of a generic function with an expected type, the callee's result type is unified with the expected type before the arguments are checked, so the expectation reaches the arguments: `Some(circle)` checked against `Option<dyn Shape>` converts its argument. A failed unification binds nothing, and the mismatch is reported on the result. A `dyn` expected type is not unified, and the result converts to it instead. The arms of a `case` are checked against the expected type when there is one. Otherwise the first arm whose type is not `Never` fixes the type, later arms are checked against it, and a `case` whose arms all diverge has type `Never`. Why: §3.12.5 needs the conversion of the argument, and conversions happen only where an expected type is known (D-235). D-234 orders the arguments but not the result. Refines D-234.
- **D-498 Desugaring of pipes and captures**: `x |> f(a, _)` is `f(a, x)` with `x` evaluated first. `x` is bound to a synthetic local before the call, unless the hole is the first argument of a function or constructor called directly, where nothing is evaluated before it. `x |> g`, with a path `g`, is `g(x)`. Any other right side, as in `x |> (make(c))`, evaluates and binds `x`, then evaluates the right side and calls it with `x`. In a capture `f(a, _)`, and wherever a pipe binds a value, a callee or argument that is a literal, a local, a top-level function, a constant or a constructor is used in place, and any other is bound once, in written order. Why: §5.15.1 evaluates the left side first, and D-155 evaluates the arguments of a capture once. Binding only where the order is observable keeps THIR small, and using a value without effects in place cannot be observed. Refines D-155, D-244.
- **D-499 THIR representation choices**: Inside a local function's body, closures nested there included, every reference to its own name is a self reference, never a capture, and MIR decides how a nested closure reaches it (D-110, D-453). During inference a `dyn I` type is always a closed type, because interfaces have no type parameters (D-165). A dictionary passed for a bound carries the descriptor of its type, and a type parameter's descriptor comes from the dictionary of its first bound (D-236). Why: MIR lowering then needs neither inference nor an interface search.
- **D-500 An interface function used as a value**: `I::f` used as a value, as in `list::map(xs, Show::show)`, is a closure that calls `I::f` through the dictionary chosen by the type of its `Self` argument, like an interface call (§8.5, D-173). A positional constructor used as a value is a closure too. Why: §8.5 allows interface calls but says nothing of values, and a value needs the same resolution.
- **D-501 Record update through aliases and its type arguments**: A record update may name an alias of the type, as in `A { ..base, x: 1 }`, and behaves as with the type it stands for. An alias of something without named fields, such as a tuple, is E0058, "`A` stands for `(Int, Int)`, which has no fields to update". An update has the type of its base: each listed value is checked against its field's type under the base's type arguments, so an update never changes a type argument. Why: an alias is interchangeable with its definition (§4.4, D-081), and an update copies a value of one instantiated type (§5.11.2). Refines D-156.
- **D-502 Descriptors for `dyn` conversions and dictionaries**: Every dictionary carries its type's descriptor (D-499), so a type that mentions a type parameter without a bound cannot satisfy an interface where a dictionary is built. A conversion of such a value to `dyn` is E0065, as §8.6 says, and so is passing such a type where a bound needs a dictionary, as in `list::compare` on a `List<Box<T>>` in a function whose `T` has no bound. An operator, interpolation or interface call applied to such a value directly needs no dictionary and is allowed. A `dyn J` value satisfies `J`, the interfaces `J` embeds and every empty interface, and keeps its own descriptor when it converts to `dyn` of an empty interface. Why: descriptors come from dictionaries (D-236, D-237), and a function with a bound may convert its argument to `dyn`. §8.6.1's example converts a `dyn` value to `dyn Any`, and wrapping it again would hide the value's type. Extends D-237.
- **D-503 Which bound provides an operator on a type parameter**: On a type parameter, an operator is provided by the first of its bounds, in written order and with what each embeds, that has a function with the operator's name and signature, whether or not it is an interface of `std::ops`. So `T: Num`, where `interface Num: ops::Add + ops::Sub`, allows `+`. Why: operators resolve by function name and signature (§8.7, D-169), and requiring `std::ops`' own interface would reject a bound that embeds it. Refines D-169.
- **D-504 How a satisfying function is matched**: A function counts toward an interface for a type when matching its `Self` parameter one way against the type determines every type parameter of the function, and every parameter and the result then equal those the interface requires. A type parameter that this leaves undetermined makes the function not count, with its own E0063 note, "for `Box<Int>`, nothing determines the type parameter `U` of `show`, so it does not count toward `Show` (§8.2)", and the help "remove the type parameter `U` from `show`". A function `show(b: Box<Int>) -> String` without type parameters makes only `Box<Int>` satisfy `Show`. Why: D-064's matching needs a precise rule, and an undetermined type parameter would need a type and dictionaries from nowhere. Refines D-064.
- **D-505 An interface's own function with the name of an embedded one**: A function of an interface with the name of a function that an embedded interface requires is E0066, "`f` is already required by the embedded interface `J`", even when the signatures are identical. Two embedded interfaces may require functions of one name only with identical signatures, which then count once, and otherwise the interface is E0066, "`I` inherits two different functions named `f`". Why: §8.1 requires distinct names, inherited ones included, and §8.3 relaxes this only between embedded interfaces. An own copy of an embedded function adds nothing. Refines D-117.
- **D-506 Bindings in alternatives and duplicate bindings**: An alternative that lacks names bound by another alternative is one E0062 that names all of them, "this alternative does not bind `x` and `y`", with a label where each is bound. A name bound twice on one path through a pattern is only E0044, "`x` is bound twice in this pattern". Why: §6.2 and §6.7 give the rules without a shape, and one mistake should give one diagnostic. The E0047 that the checker used to add for the alternatives was rejected as a duplicate.
- **D-507 Text of non-exhaustiveness witnesses**: The missing values of E0061 are written as patterns a user would write: `None`, `Some(Some(False))`, `Circle { .. }`, `(True, _)`, `[_, _, ..]`, and `_` for an infinite type such as `Int`. Several subjects are a comma-separated row, as in `True, False`. Constructors of the prelude and of the current module are bare, and others are `<last module segment>::Name`, as in `shape::Circle { .. }`. The label lists at most three, as in "`Tuesday`, `Wednesday`, `Thursday` and 1 more not covered", and ends in "and more" without a number when the search stopped at its limit of witnesses (D-508). Why: §6.9 asks for the missing values without fixing their text, a user can paste a pattern as a new arm, and the qualification follows D-463.
- **D-508 The exhaustiveness budget**: Each `case` is checked in two passes, reachability of the arms and then the missing values, and each pass may take at most 2,000,000 steps, one per recursive call plus one per matrix row looked at. The second pass collects at most 64 missing values. A `case` with an unguarded arm of wildcards is exhaustive without any search. When reachability runs out, the remaining arms count as reachable and get no W0010. When the missing-value search runs out, E0061 reports `_` as missing, with the note "the patterns are too complex to check in full". Rows are grouped by the constructor or literal they start with, so a large table of literal or variant arms checks in about linear time. Why: the worst case is exponential, and D-264 needs results that do not depend on timing. One budget shared by both passes was rejected, because it gave a false E0061 on a table of 2,000 literal arms that ends in `_`. Refines D-022.
- **D-509 Irrefutability follows §6.1's syntactic list**: E0060 and W0008 use §6.1's rules: `_`, bindings, a constructor of a type with exactly one variant whose sub-patterns are irrefutable, a tuple of irrefutable patterns, `[..]` and `[..rest]`, alternatives of which one is irrefutable alone, and `p as x` with `p` irrefutable. Alternatives that cover every value only together do not count, so `let True | False = b` is E0060, labelled "its alternatives match every value only together", and `let assert True | False = b` gets no W0008. Why: this is not a gap, but early builds used a semantic rule that differs from §6.1. Refines D-159, D-242.
- **D-510 What the back end compiles and reports**: `cheby run` and the driver's MIR and CLIF dumps, which goldens use, lower only the items reachable from the `main` being run, across modules, with the constructor layouts of every module that reachable code uses. Unreachable bodies are never lowered and never report E0038. Every reachable item is lowered before anything is compiled, so one run reports every E0038 of the back end, sorted by position among themselves and after the front end's diagnostics, which can only be warnings at that point. On native, a function with an `@external(native, …)` and a body lowers to the wrapper of D-455, and reachability does not walk the body, which is for the other targets (D-207). Why: a linker drops unused code too, so a partial back end can run programs whose unused functions it cannot compile yet, and std modules that every program depends on (D-447) cost nothing when unused. The dumps show what a run compiles. Lowering both the wrapper and the body caused an internal compiler error on example 010.
- **D-511 Constant cycles, type containment and the JS literal check**: E0069 covers cycles of direct references between the constants of one module. Imports are acyclic, so no cycle crosses modules, and a cycle through a function call is found when constants are evaluated (E6, D-478). For E0068, a constant's type contains what its tuples, builtin collections and the fields of every constructor of its named types contain, transitively and with type arguments in place, so `Option<Db>` and a record with a function field are rejected like `Db` and `fn() -> Int`. A type argument that no field uses is not looked at, and channels and fibers join the check with the tier-2 library. The JS safe-integer check of `Int` literals (E0071, D-037) covers code that runs on JS, and not constant initializers, which run at compile time. From E6 the evaluated value is checked instead (§4.5). Why: a constant's value must not hold functions or handles (D-139), and a type argument that no field uses holds no value. Refines D-139.
- **D-512 Granularity and scope of the cross-target check (E0067)**: E0067 is reported once for each pair of a using item and a used top-level item, at the first use, with the signature's uses before the body's, as "`name` exists only on `native`, but `main` also runs on `js`". A constructor counts as its type, and an interface function as its interface. A constant's declared type is checked against the constant's targets, and its initializer is not, because the initializer runs at compile time on the host (D-090). Why: one root cause gives one diagnostic, and a use of a type and of its constructors is one root cause. Refines D-056.
- **D-513 An item that exists on no target**: An item whose `@target(t)` contradicts its module's `@!target(m)` is E0045, "`f` exists on no target". So is a function without a body whose `@target(t)`, or whose module's `@!target(t)`, leaves a target `t` for which it has no `@external`. Why: such an item could never be compiled, which is never what was meant. Refines D-461.
- **D-514 Attributes on variants, record fields and interface functions**: The parser reports a run of attributes before a variant, a record field or an interface function as one E0045, "attributes are not allowed on a variant", with the note "only items take attributes (D-461)" and a help to remove it. It keeps the attributes in an error node and parses the member after them. `tree-sitter-cheby` rejects the same input. Why: D-461 forbids these attributes and names E0045, and the parser used to report a false E0015 "unclosed `{`" with follow-up errors. Refines D-461.
- **D-515 Imports are used only through written names**: A module import is used when a written path starts with the name it binds, and an item import when a name it binds is written, in either namespace. Implicit references, such as the `show` an interpolation calls or the function behind an operator, never use an import, so an `import std::int` that serves only `"{n}"` is unused (W0003). Why: satisfaction never needs an import (D-447, §7.6), and §8.2 counts satisfying functions wherever the type is visible. Counting an import as used when satisfaction picked a function from its module was rejected, because it hid real W0003s. Refines D-080.
- **D-516 What counts as an unused item, and when**: A type is used when its name or any of its constructors is used, in an expression or a pattern, and an interface when its name or any of its functions is. Constructors and interface functions are never reported on their own, and a type uses the functions its `@std_hooks` names. An item's uses of itself, as in recursion or a recursive type, and a local function's calls in its own body do not make it used, and an item used only by another unused item is not reported, since there is no transitive dead-code analysis. §2.3's exemption for names that start with `_` covers bindings only, so such items are reported. W0003, W0004 and W0005 are reported only when every body of the module, or of the package for W0005, type-checked, while W0002 needs only name resolution. The whole-package pass of W0005 runs once per `cheby check` after every local module is checked, and never in `cheby run` or the dumps. Why: D-152 speaks of items, and implicit uses come only from THIR, which a body with an error lacks, so checking those bodies would give false warnings. Running W0005 at all in `cheby run` would cost the edit-run path a whole-package pass. Refines D-152, D-266.
- **D-517 Which uses of deprecated items warn**: Every written path or name that resolves to a deprecated item of another module warns with the item's message (W0012), and so does a constructor of a deprecated type or a function of a deprecated interface. Uses in the item's own module do not warn, nor do implicit uses such as a deprecated `show` called by an interpolation. An item import of a deprecated item does not warn, but each use of the name it binds does. Why: the warning belongs on code the user wrote and can change, once per use. Refines D-200.
- **D-518 Scope of W0011 and W0013**: W0011 looks through tuples, builtin containers and the fields of every constructor of a named type, with type arguments in place, for a function or external type. It stops at type parameters of the enclosing item, at `dyn` values and at types with `@std_hooks`, which compare through their hook, and it guards against recursive types. W0013 warns about a negative integer literal written for a parameter in `@std_clamped`, also when a capture such as `list::take(_, -1)` moved the literal into a synthetic local. Constants and other expressions are not looked through (D-303). Why: only statically known contents can be judged, and the rest panics at run time if it holds a function (D-070, D-233). Desugaring must not hide a literal that the user wrote. Refines D-070, D-303.
- **D-519 Chains count toward the nesting limit**: The counter of D-441 also counts each finished operator of a binary or pipe chain, each call or field access of a postfix chain, and each `use` statement until the end of its block, one level each for the rest of the chain or block. A step's own right operand or arguments are parsed before it counts. Past 256 levels the chain is E0016. Why: later passes recurse along the left spine of a chain and into a `use`'s callback, so sums of 4,000 terms, pipes of 3,000 steps, field chains of 20,000 steps and 1,000 `use` lines overflowed the stack with no diagnostic. D-441 exists so that no input overflows. Making every pass iterative was rejected as a much larger change. Refines D-441.

Open:

- ~~The `GAP(<slug>)` markers in `crates/`, `std/`, `tests/` and `fuzz/` name the proposals that D-477 to D-519 settle, and should cite the decisions instead.~~ Done: every marker now cites its decision, and no `GAP(` is left in the code.
- ~~Phase-A wording: the help of E0013 for a path with two module qualifiers shows the fixed example `ops::Add` instead of the path written; `type Empty {}` says "a record type needs at least one field" while §4.3.1 speaks of variants; and a subjectless `case` with `_` in the middle reports both E0035 errors.~~ → D-637
- ~~The help of E0074 for a `pub` function that mentions a `priv` type suggests making the function package-visible, which still breaks D-149. It should suggest `priv`, or making the type package-visible.~~ → D-636
- ~~The real standard library's `test` blocks are checked by no build and no test until G4, since only unit tests with stand-in std modules check std tests (D-479).~~ Done: `cargo xtask test-std` checks and runs them (D-542, D-599).
- ~~Two tests of the stdlib spec are not in `std/`, because each needs helper items that user builds would report as unused: `ops.md`'s "a bound names an operator interface" and `json.md`'s "list encodes each element with its type's to_json". The `"{done}"` assertion of `nil.md` is missing too, because it needs an import that only a skipped test uses.~~ → D-630, D-631
- `cheby run` stops with E0038 on every example except 001, from MIR lowering or from tier-2 and tier-3 imports, until the back-end phases land. The native kernels (`cheby_*`) and the JS `.mjs` kernels do not exist yet, and the conformance suite passes 2 of its 57 pending entries (001 and 020).
- Every check loads every shipped home module (D-447), and the driver's integration and unit tests take about 69 s and 35 s. Caching std interfaces, or the precompiled std of M3 (D-256), would help.
- The resolver, the checker, MIR lowering and the warnings still recurse along chains. Only the parser's limit (D-519) bounds them, and a deep argument at the head of a long postfix chain reaches about 512 levels. Making these passes iterative would remove the dependence.
- The `check` fuzz target, which runs the whole front end, ran for 3 minutes only, and CI does not run it, because `cargo-fuzz` needs a nightly toolchain.
- `STAGED_STD_MODULES` is empty but kept with its tests (D-486). It can go once no later tier stages modules.
- ~~`Collector::member_attrs` and `Placement::Member` in `cheby_resolve` are unreachable, because the parser now reports member attributes itself (D-514).~~ Done: both are removed, and the parser and the UI test `04-declarations/attributes/member_attributes` cover the rule.
- D-480 leaves `std::multimap`, `std::sorted_*` and `std::ordered_*` unshipped at M1 exit, and M1's exit criteria now exempt them. No decision says which milestone ships them.

## 2026-10-10: M1 back-end plan

Goal: before the M1 back end is built (MIR lowering, code generation and the JIT, the runtime, constant evaluation, the tools and the standard library), settle the spec gaps its plan answers, and record the user's decision on Windows in M1.

- **D-520 Runtime helpers for generated code**: Besides kernels, generated code calls these runtime functions through the C ABI: `cheby_rt_str_with_capacity` `u64 (ctx*, i64 cap)`, `cheby_rt_str_append` `u64 (ctx*, u64 acc, u64 piece)`, `cheby_rt_str_eq` `bool (u64, u64)`, `cheby_rt_list_push` `u64 (ctx*, u64 list, u64 elem)`, `cheby_rt_list_append` `u64 (ctx*, u64 a, u64 b)`, `cheby_rt_list_length` `i64 (u64)`, `cheby_rt_list_get` `u64 (u64, i64)`, `cheby_rt_list_slice` `u64 (ctx*, u64, i64 start, i64 end)`, `cheby_rt_equal` `bool (ctx*, u64, u64)`, `cheby_rt_debug` `u64 (ctx*, u64)`, `cheby_rt_effect_check` `void (ctx*, u64 symbol_string)`, `cheby_rt_box_i64` `u64 (ctx*, i64)` and `cheby_rt_box_u64` `u64 (ctx*, u64)` for values outside the tagged range, `cheby_rt_box_f64` `u64 (ctx*, f64)`, `cheby_rt_box_f32` `u64 (ctx*, f32)`, `cheby_rt_fmod_f64` `f64 (f64, f64)`, `cheby_rt_fmod_f32` `f32 (f32, f32)`, `cheby_rt_const_missing` `u64 (ctx*, u64 slot_address)` and the stub `cheby_rt_unresolved` (D-532). The runtime also provides the static empty list `cheby_rt_static_empty_list` and the builtin descriptors `cheby_rt_desc_<name>` (D-523), and keeps `cheby_rt_alloc`, `cheby_rt_dup`, `cheby_rt_drop`, `cheby_rt_panic`, `cheby_rt_stack_slow` and its static objects. A helper takes `ctx` first when it allocates, may panic or may call back into Cheby code, and kernels never take it (D-453). `cheby_rt_str_append` consumes its accumulator and borrows the piece, `cheby_rt_list_push` consumes the list and the element, and both update the first argument in place when it is unique. `cheby_rt_list_append` consumes its first list and borrows the second, the other helpers borrow their arguments, and every `u64` result is owned. Tagging, untagging, unboxing and the fast paths of boxing are inline in generated code. Why: interpolation, list literals and spreads, list patterns, structural `==` and debug text (D-252), boxing outside the tagged range and float `%`, for which Cranelift has no instruction, all need runtime code, and D-455 already reserves the `cheby_rt_` prefix for the runtime's internals. Extends D-455.
- **D-521 Uniform words of every number type**: In the uniform representation, `Int` and `I64` are tagged as `(v << 1) | 1` when `v` fits in 63 bits and are a boxed `Int` (layout 3) otherwise. `I8`, `I16`, `I32`, `U8`, `U16` and `U32` are always tagged with their value, sign-extended for the signed types. `U64` is tagged when `v` is at most 2⁶² − 1 and is a boxed `U64` (layout 8) otherwise. `Float` is always a boxed `Float` (layout 4), `F32` a boxed `F32` (layout 5), and `Bool` the static `True` or `False` object. In monomorphic code, `Int` and `I64` are 64-bit integers, the sized types have their own width, `Float` and `F32` are 64-bit and 32-bit floats, and `Bool` is one byte, 0 or 1. The encoding is canonical: a value has exactly one uniform shape, so word equality decides `==` for tagged values, and equality and hashing never meet a tagged and a boxed form of one number. Why: D-278 fixes only `Int`, a `U64` above 2⁶² − 1 does not fit a signed 63-bit tag, and a second shape for one value would make every comparison and hash check both. Refines D-278, D-454.
- **D-522 Reading a constructor's tag**: The constructors of one type are registered consecutively in declaration order, so a value's tag is its header's layout id minus the layout id held in the `cb$layout$` word of the type's first constructor. For `Option`, `Result` and `Order` the tag is the layout id minus 22, 24 or 19. Nullary constructors are static objects with their constructor's layout id, so the same subtraction works on them. A type with one constructor never reads a tag, and `Bool` is branched on unboxed. Why: generated code must not embed user layout ids (D-454), and one load and one subtraction keep `case` cheap without a table lookup. Refines D-454.
- **D-523 Static objects and their symbols**: Dictionaries, type descriptors and function values that need nothing from their caller are static objects emitted by code generation, with count 0 (D-454), and are never written. Their symbols add the kind `dict` to D-456: `cb$dict$<module path>$<name>` for a dictionary, `cb$desc$<module path>$<Type>` for a type's nominal descriptor, `cb$desc$<module path of I>$dyn$<I>` for the type `dyn I`, and `cb$fnval$<module path>$<item>` for a function value, a closure whose code is the function's shim `cb$shim$<module path>$<item>`. A static's name is unique in its module and built from paths and names, never from ids. Builtin types have runtime statics `cheby_rt_desc_<name>`, where `<name>` is `int`, `float`, `f32`, `i8`, `i16`, `i32`, `i64`, `u8`, `u16`, `u32`, `u64`, `bool`, `nil`, `string`, `bytes`, `list`, `map`, `set`, `option`, `result`, `order`, `tuple` or `fn`. A tuple descriptor's arity comes from its arguments, and a function descriptor's arguments are the parameter types followed by the result. A static is defined once per process: a later JIT unit that names the same symbol imports it. Why: D-456 lists `desc` and `fnval` but not dictionaries, and a static defined twice would be two objects with one name, so code compiled in another batch, such as a REPL entry, would see a different object. Refines D-456.
- **D-524 Layouts of dictionaries, descriptors and `dyn` values**: Of the layout ids that D-454 reserves, 9 is a dictionary, 10 a `dyn` value, 11 a descriptor and 12 a nominal descriptor, and 13 to 15 stay reserved. Collection nodes use 26 to 31 for lists and 32 to 39 for maps and sets, and 40 to 63 stay reserved. A dictionary holds the header, its type's descriptor, one closure for each function of the interface, then the dictionaries of the directly embedded interfaces in written order. The functions come in the interface's order: those of its embedded interfaces first, in embedding order and depth first, then its own in declaration order, with a name that occurs twice kept at its first place (§8.3). A `dyn` value holds the header, the dictionary of its interface for the underlying type, and the underlying value as a uniform word. A descriptor holds the header, a nominal descriptor and the descriptors of its type arguments. A nominal descriptor is one static object per type that holds the header, the type's printed name and its module path, both as static `String`s. A dictionary or descriptor that does not depend on a dictionary passed for a bound is a static object (D-523), and one that does is built at run time. Why: §8.5.1, D-236, D-453 and D-499 say what dictionaries and descriptors contain but not their shape, and closed ones then cost nothing at run time. Refines D-236, D-454, D-499.
- **D-525 Kernels may update unique arguments in place**: The Cheby wrapper of an `@external(native, …)` function (D-455) owns its arguments and drops them after the call returns, and the native function borrows them (D-187). A native function that sees an argument with count exactly one may update it in place, `dup` it and return it, and the wrapper's drop then leaves it with count one. The wrapper never reads an argument after the call. Why: D-187 passes arguments borrowed, while D-274 and D-316 require amortized O(1) updates at count one, and this keeps both without a second calling convention for kernels. Refines D-187.
- **D-526 Kernels that call Cheby closures**: A kernel that calls a closure, such as `map::upsert` or a caller of `@std_hooks` (D-484), does so through the runtime, which keeps a thread-local record of the running entry: its `ctx` and its trampolines. The JIT passes the trampolines with each entry, one for each arity from 0 to 4, as `cb$tramp$<n>` with the C signature `u64 (ctx*, u64 closure, u64 a0, …, u64 a<n-1>)`, one uniform word per argument. The closure is borrowed, and the arguments and the result are owned. A call with more than four arguments is a fatal runtime error, and a panic inside the closure parks the entry's thread like any other panic (D-457). Generated code still passes `ctx` explicitly, and foreign functions still take none, so ADR-0055 is unchanged. Why: D-453 keeps `ctx` out of foreign calls, so a kernel has no context of its own to call Cheby code with, and no tier-1 kernel calls a closure with more than four arguments. Extends D-453, D-484.
- **D-527 Float operators**: On `Float` and `F32`, `%` is `fmod`: the exact remainder of truncating division, with the dividend's sign (D-154). Unary `-` flips the sign, so it turns `0.0` into `-0.0` and a NaN into a NaN. `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=` follow the total order of `compare` (§3.14, D-135): NaNs are equal to each other and greater than every other float, and `-0.0` and `0.0` are equal, so `1.0 < x` is `True` for a NaN `x`. `==` and `!=` follow §3.3.2. Arithmetic follows IEEE 754 and never panics. Why: §3.14 defines the comparison operators through `compare`, so generated code must not use IEEE comparisons, which are false whenever an operand is NaN. Refines D-118, D-135.
- **D-528 Messages of failed assertions**: A failed `assert e` has the message `assertion failed: <source text of e>`. When `e` is a comparison (`==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>` or `>=`), two more lines follow, `  left: <debug text>` and ` right: <debug text>`, with the operands' debug text. With `as m`, the first line is `assertion failed: <m>`, and the operand lines still follow. A failed `let assert` has the message `` `let assert` pattern did not match ``, or `` `let assert` pattern did not match: <m> `` with `as m`, followed by the line `  value: <debug text>` of the value that did not match. The panic report writes the message after its `panicked at` line (D-449). Why: §11.4 and §11.5 say what the message contains but not its text, and run-fail goldens need one. The right-aligned `left` and `right` labels follow Rust's `assert_eq`. Refines D-096, D-159, D-160, D-449.
- **D-529 How an `Err` from `main` or a test is printed**: When `main` returns `Err(e)`, the program writes the text of `e`, by `Show` when its type satisfies it and by debug printing otherwise (D-182), then a newline, to standard error, with no prefix, and exits with status 1. A test whose body returns `Err(e)` fails, and its failure block in `cheby test`'s output (D-541) shows `e` the same way. Why: the program chose the error's text, so a prefix such as Rust's `Error: ` would add words the program cannot change, and one rule for `main` and tests keeps their output alike. Refines D-140, D-182.
- **D-530 Equality, hashing and debug text of `dyn` values**: Two `dyn` values are equal when their descriptors are structurally equal, meaning the same nominal type with equal argument descriptors, and their values are equal. Hashing a `dyn` value combines the hash of its descriptor with the hash of its value. Debug printing writes only the value inside (D-233). Why: D-236 fixes the meaning, and comparing descriptors by structure rather than by address keeps it true for descriptors built at run time (D-524). Refines D-236.
- **D-531 Debug text of strings**: Debug printing writes a `String` in double quotes with exactly the escapes that a string literal (§2.5) needs to read back as the same text: `\"`, `\\`, `\n`, `\t`, `\r`, `\{` for `{`, and `\u{…}` for every other control character. Every other character is written as itself. `Bytes`, `Map` and `Set` print as their stdlib files say (D-388, D-344, D-382). Why: §3.13 does not say which escapes debug text uses, and text that reads back as a literal can be pasted into a test. Refines D-134.
- **D-532 Unresolved native symbols**: A build that runs code, such as `cheby run`, reports E0053 for every native symbol it cannot resolve, one diagnostic each, sorted by the position of the `@external` that names it. `cheby check` compiles code only to evaluate constants (D-459), and binds each unresolved native symbol to the runtime stub `cheby_rt_unresolved`, which takes no `ctx`, like every foreign function. A constant that calls the stub gets E0077 (D-533), and otherwise nothing is reported, so `cheby check` never reports E0053 (D-455). Why: one run should show every missing symbol, not one per attempt. Which symbols exist depends on the process (D-455), while a constant that really calls a missing symbol cannot be evaluated. Refines D-455, D-459.
- **D-533 Diagnostic codes of constant evaluation**: Constant evaluation adds E0075 constant panicked, E0076 constant evaluation limit exceeded, for time or memory (D-195, D-536), E0077 forbidden effect in a constant, for an external function or effect that evaluation may not use (D-537), including a native symbol missing under `cheby check` (D-532), and E0078 constant out of the JS range (D-540). Each is reported at the constant's name. For E0075 the panic report's message and location are a note, and E0076 and E0077 likewise say in a note what was exceeded or reached. Constant evaluation never prints the panic report itself (D-449). A cycle through a function call found during evaluation is E0069, labelled "`a` needs its own value while it is computed". Why: D-419 assigns codes in order as checks arrive, and a failing constant is a compile error about that constant (§4.5, §13.9). Extends D-486, D-511.
- **D-534 Which constants each command evaluates**: `cheby run` evaluates the constants that `main` reaches, `cheby test` those that the selected tests reach, and `cheby check` every constant of the local package. Each lowers only what those initializers reach (D-510). Std constants are evaluated when reached. `cheby check` evaluates nothing when the front end reported an error, so later-tier examples still report only E0038 (D-477). Why: D-459 has `cheby check` report every failing constant of the package, as a build would, while D-510 lowers only what is reachable, and evaluating constants despite front-end errors would report failures caused by those errors. Refines D-459, D-510.
- **D-535 Order of constant evaluation**: A build compiles its code, its constant initializers and its entries into one JIT. Each constant is evaluated on an entry of its own (D-457), in the dependency order of the initializers, with ties broken by module order and then by name, and its value is stored in the constant's `cb$const$` slot (D-456). A read of an empty slot during evaluation runs that constant's initializer on the current entry and marks the constant in progress, and a read of a constant in progress is a cycle, E0069 (D-533). A constant forced this way is evaluated under its own hash seed (D-538), and the reader's seed is restored afterwards, so a constant's value never depends on which constant reached it first. Outside evaluation, an empty slot is a fatal runtime error. Why: a cycle through a function call can only be found while running (D-511), and forcing on demand evaluates each constant exactly once, whatever order the static analysis finds. Refines D-090, D-511.
- **D-536 Constant evaluation limits and `[const-eval]`**: The time limit is wall-clock time per evaluation of a constant at the top level, including the constants it forces, 10 s by default. It stops a loop at its next function entry or tail call, so a single long kernel call cannot be interrupted. The memory limit is the peak number of heap bytes live in the build while the constant is evaluated, including what kernels allocate, 1 GiB by default. Exceeding either is E0076. `cheby.toml` sets them in `[const-eval]` as `time-limit = "10s"`, an integer followed by `ms` or `s`, and `memory-limit = "1GiB"`, an integer followed by `KiB`, `MiB` or `GiB`. Any other key or value there is E0036. Why: D-195 fixes the defaults but not what is measured, and D-444 left the spelling open. Measuring live bytes rather than allocated ones lets a constant that builds and drops large temporaries finish. Refines D-195, D-444.
- **D-537 Numeric kernels in constants**: Besides the kernels of `std::list`, `std::map`, `std::set`, `std::string` and `std::bytes` (D-360), constant evaluation may run the kernels of the numeric modules `std::int`, `std::float`, `std::f32` and the sized integer modules, so `const label: String = "{n}"` works. Reaching any other `@external` during evaluation is E0077. Why: D-484 moved numeric functions such as `int::show` into kernels after D-360 was written, and they are effect-free and give the same result on every target (D-396). Refines D-360.
- **D-538 Hash seeds**: Outside constant evaluation, `Map` and `Set` hash with SipHash-1-3 keyed by one seed per process, drawn once from the operating system's randomness (D-093). A constant is evaluated under its own seed (D-269): SipHash-1-3 with a zero key over the package name, the module path and the constant's name, followed by, for each module that the initializer reaches, sorted by module path and std modules included, the module path and a SipHash of its source files' bytes. Every non-empty map or set records the seed it was built with. `from_list` and an insertion into an empty one take the current seed, and every other operation uses the collection's own seed, so a constant may read and extend another constant's map. An empty collection has no seed. Equality of two maps or sets looks each entry of one up in the other with the other's seed, and hashing one as a value combines its entries' hashes independently of their order, so equal collections hash equally whatever their seeds. Hashing a constructor value uses its type's module path and name and its tag, never a layout id. A constant therefore gets the same value under `cheby check`, `cheby run`, `cheby test`, the REPL and any `--jobs`. M3's build cache may replace the source hash with the interface hash. Why: D-269 says what the seed depends on but not how it is computed before a cache exists, and layout ids depend on registration order, which differs between commands and thread counts (D-264). Refines D-093, D-269.
- **D-539 Results of constant evaluation**: When every constant of a build is evaluated and the build will run code (`cheby run`, `cheby test` or the REPL), every `Map` and `Set` in the results is rebuilt with the per-process seed (D-196). The rebuild is one memoized pass over all constants together, inner values first, so a collection that two constants share is rebuilt once and both point at the new one, and a collection nested in another, such as a `Set` used as a `Map` key, is rebuilt before its parent. `cheby check` skips the rebuild. Then every result becomes static in place: its object graph gets count 0 (D-454) and leaves the leak check (D-458). Results hold no closures or handles (E0068). A serialized image of the results comes with M3's cache. Why: D-196 asks for an iteration order randomized per run, a rebuild per constant would duplicate shared collections, and static results need no reference counting and cannot leak. Refines D-196, D-454.
- **D-540 Which constants the JS range check covers**: E0078 is reported for a constant that exists on `js`, meaning that its target set intersected with the package's targets contains `js`, and whose evaluated value holds an `Int` outside ±(2⁵³ − 1) anywhere in it. `int::min_value` and `int::max_value` (`@target(native)`, D-358) and user constants with `@target(native)` are never reported. Why: the value is computed once on the host (D-090), but a constant that JS code reads must be representable there (D-037), and a native-only constant never reaches JS. Refines D-037, D-511.
- **D-541 `cheby test`**: `cheby test [FILTER] [--module PATH]` runs the selected tests of the local package. `FILTER` selects the tests whose full name, `<module path> "<test name>"`, contains it, and `--module` selects the tests of that module and its submodules. In M1 tests run one after another, by module path and then in source order. Standard output gets one line per test, `test <module path> "<name>" ... ok` or `... FAILED`, then for each failure a block that starts with `---- <module path> "<name>" ----` and holds the panic report or the `Err` text (D-529), then a summary such as `test result: ok. 3 passed; 0 failed; 1 filtered out`, with `FAILED` in place of `ok` when a test failed. The program's own output is not captured and appears as it is written. The exit status is 0 when every selected test passes, also when none is selected, and 1 when one fails. A compile error stops the command with status 1 before any test runs. In a toolchain with debug assertions, the live objects are counted before and after each passing test, and a difference fails that test with D-458's message. Why: §13.6 asks for filtering by name and by module without fixing a surface, and this layout follows `cargo test`, which users know. Running tests in order keeps uncaptured output readable, and §13.6 allows any order. Refines D-055, D-458.
- **D-542 Testing the standard library**: `cargo xtask test-std [FILTER]`, which CI runs, checks every shipped std module with its tests, then builds and runs the tests one std module at a time. If a module's tests do not build together, each test is built alone to find the ones that do. A module or test that does not build is reported as `module <path> did not build: <codes>` or `test <module path> "<name>" did not build: <codes>`, is counted, and does not stop the other modules. The report ends with counts per module and in total, and the command exits with status 1 if any test failed or did not build. `cheby` itself gets no flag for it. Why: D-479 keeps std tests out of user builds, and one broken std module must not hide the results of the others. Refines D-479.
- **D-543 UI-test input, arguments, command and flags**: A file `<name>.stdin` next to a UI test, whether a file or a package directory (D-471), is fed to `cheby run` as standard input, and without one standard input is null. A file `<name>.args` holds one argument per line, passed after `--`. The directive `// @command: test` makes a `run-pass` or `run-fail` test run `cheby test` instead of `cheby run`, and its goldens hold that output. The directive `// @flags: …` adds command-line flags after the subcommand, such as `// @flags: --deny-warnings` or `// @flags: --target js`. `tests/ui/README.md` documents every directive. Why: D-289's directives cannot express a run that reads input or arguments, a test command or a flag. Input and arguments are data, like goldens and the examples' optional input and arguments (D-275), so they sit beside the test, while what to run stays in the header. Refines D-289, D-471.
- **D-544 Strict runs of pending tests**: The UI and conformance harnesses read three environment variables. `CHEBY_MILESTONE=<Mn>` overrides `tests/milestone` for one run, so with `M2` every M1 test is required, which is how M1's exit is checked before `tests/milestone` is bumped. `CHEBY_PENDING=require` turns a failing pending test into a failure, except for tests whose name matches a line of the file that `CHEBY_PENDING_ALLOW` names, one glob per line with `#` comments. At the end the harness lists the pending tests that passed and the failures it allowed. Without the variables the behavior is D-288's, and CI sets none of them. The harnesses also start every `cheby` with `CHEBY_RT_POISON=1`, which makes a toolchain with debug assertions poison freed objects, keep them in a bounded quarantine and stop with a fatal "use after free" error when one is used again. Why: D-288 only reports pending tests, so an example that passed once could break silently during the milestone, and the exit must be checked as the next milestone will see it. Reference-count mistakes otherwise show up far from their cause. Refines D-288.
- **D-545 Windows in M1**: M1 is judged on Linux and macOS. On Windows, CI builds the workspace and runs the unit tests, and the harnesses skip the UI run tests, the conformance runs and `::test` trials and the REPL suite until M6, which brings Windows support. Why: the user decided it. The JIT's memory path has never run on Windows, native symbol lookup has no Windows fallback, and example 010 calls `arc4random_uniform`, which Windows lacks, so making Windows pass is M6's platform work. Making Windows pass in M1, and dropping Windows from CI until M6, were rejected. Refines D-277.
- **D-546 REPL prompt, output and transcripts**: The prompt is `> `, and `. ` while a bracket or a block is open. On a terminal the REPL has line editing, and there is no history file in M1. When standard input is not a terminal, there is no line editing, and each input line is echoed after its prompt, so a session's output reads as a transcript. An expression's value prints as `<debug text> : <type>`, with types written as in diagnostics (D-463), and a `Nil` value prints nothing. A value whose type a redefinition replaced prints its type followed by ` (stale)`. Declarations and imports print nothing on success. Diagnostics and panic reports show a location in an entry as `<repl:N>:line:col`, with entries counted from 1. The commands are `:type expr`, which prints `<expr text> : <type>`, `:module path`, `:reload`, `:help` and `:quit`. There is no leak check (D-458). REPL tests are transcripts `tests/repl/<name>.repl`: lines that start with `> ` or `. ` are input, and every other line is expected output, standard output and standard error merged in order (D-548). A directory `tests/repl/<name>/` with a `cheby.toml` runs its session inside that package. Why: §13.5 lists what the REPL accepts but not what it shows, and transcripts need one fixed form. Refines D-043, D-098.
- **D-547 REPL entries and redefinition**: A prompt-level `let` or `let assert` is evaluated once, and its bindings are session values that later entries read. A later `let` may rebind the name (D-193). Besides functions, foreign functions and types (§13.5.2), an interface may be redefined like a type: the old interface and its implementations stay with the old values, and the new one is a different interface. A constant may be redefined like a function without parameters: with the same type, readers see the new value, and with another type, its dependents are checked again. An alias is replaced for later entries, and definitions already checked keep what they were checked against. Every generated symbol of a redefined item carries the item's generation, `$r<gen>` appended when the generation is above 0, so redefined types, constants and function values never collide with the old ones, and `:reload` gives every item of a changed module a new generation. Why: §13.5.2 does not cover constants, interfaces or aliases, and D-456 gives generations only to function slots, while a redefined type or `:reload` emits its other symbols again. Refines D-043, D-193, D-456.
- **D-548 Standard streams in the REPL**: The REPL reads its input lines through the same buffered standard-input reader as `io::read_line`, so an entry that reads standard input consumes the next lines of a piped session in order, and those lines are not echoed as input. On a terminal, the line editor reads the prompt lines and an entry reads the terminal directly. The REPL flushes standard output before it writes a diagnostic or a panic report to standard error. The transcript harness gives both streams one pipe, so the merged order is the order of writes. Why: two readers with their own buffers would each take lines meant for the other, and the flush keeps earlier output ahead of a report, as D-449 does for panics. Refines D-346, D-474.
- **D-549 `--deny-warnings`**: `cheby check`, `cheby run`, `cheby test` and `cheby repl` accept `--deny-warnings`. Each warning is then rendered as an error with its own code, as in `error[W0002]: unused binding …`, and the first one has the note "warnings are denied by `--deny-warnings`". The command then fails with status 1, and `run` and `test` run nothing. Why: D-080 promises the flag for CI without saying how a denied warning looks, and keeping the warning's code lets a reader look it up with `cheby explain`. Refines D-080.
- **D-550 `cheby check --target`**: `cheby check --target native` or `--target js` checks the code as it runs on that target only, so the cross-target check of §12.6 reports E0067 only for that target. A target outside the manifest's `targets` is a tool error, "the package does not target `js`". Why: D-282 speaks of selected targets without a flag to select them, and a package for both targets may be checked for one at a time. Refines D-282.
- **D-551 `--jobs` and the determinism check**: `-j N` or `--jobs N` sets the number of worker threads for the build graph and code generation, by default the available parallelism. Output never depends on it (D-264). `cargo xtask check-determinism` builds every example and the benchmark projects with `--jobs 1` and with the default, from two different absolute paths, and compares their interface encodings, MIR dumps, CLIF dumps and evaluated constant values byte for byte. Why: D-263 builds in parallel and D-264 asks CI to check determinism, and the thread count and absolute paths are the inputs most likely to leak into output. Refines D-263, D-264.
- **D-552 One Unicode version in the native runtime**: On native, case mapping uses the Rust standard library's Unicode tables and grapheme segmentation uses the `unicode-segmentation` crate. A test asserts that both follow the same Unicode version, and the version is recorded in `std::string`'s documentation. Why: D-310 fixes one Unicode version for every std table, and these two sources are updated separately. Refines D-310.
- **D-553 Implementing correctly rounded math**: On native, `float::pow`, `exp`, `ln`, `sin`, `cos`, `tan` and `atan2` (D-396) come from a pure-Rust port of CORE-MATH, such as the `pxfm` crate. It is accepted only after each function is checked against reference values computed with at least 200 bits of precision and rounded to binary64, with a rounding-boundary check that raises the precision, or drops the input with a note, when the reference lies too close to a midpoint. The inputs are at least 10,000 random values per function over its whole domain, the special values (zeros, infinities, NaN, subnormals and huge arguments of `sin`, `cos` and `tan`) and published hard cases. If any function fails, none of the seven is registered, and the fallback is an open question of this session. Why: CORE-MATH's functions are proven correctly rounded, which ADR-0054 requires, a pure-Rust port keeps the plain `cargo build` without a C compiler, and the port must be shown to keep that property. Refines D-396.
- **D-554 E0038 leaves MIR lowering**: The rule of §12.5 that the type parameters of a foreign function must not have bounds, also for a function that has a body and an `@external` for some targets (D-207), is checked by the front end: such a bound is E0045, at the bound. At M1 exit, MIR lowering reports no E0038, and a construct it still cannot lower is an internal compiler error (D-464). E0038 then remains only for imports of unshipped std modules (D-480), and `docs/diagnostics/E0038.md` says so. Why: the bound rule had no diagnostic, so MIR reported it as not supported, which D-448 rules out for a real error, and D-480 kept MIR's E0038 only until the back-end phases land. Refines D-461, D-480.
