- None.

## 2026-10-10: M1 back end, wave 2

Goal: log the gaps that wave 2 of the M1 back end (scalars, operators, panics, patterns, closures, records, strings and I/O) settled in code, stating what the implementation does.

- **D-569 Float literals are rounded once for each type**: The front end rounds a float literal's decimal text directly to each float type: to the nearest `Float`, and separately to the nearest `F32`. It never narrows the `Float` value to get the `F32` one. THIR's `Lit::Float` holds both values as `FloatLit { wide, narrow }`, and every later phase, constant evaluation and the REPL included, reads `narrow` for an `F32` and `wide` for a `Float`. An `F32` literal is E0071 exactly when its once-rounded value is infinite. So `3.4028235677973366e38`, just below the overflow midpoint 2^128 - 2^103, is `F32::MAX`, and `1.00000005960464477539062500001` is `1.0000001`, not `1.0`. A negative literal negates both rounded values, which is exact. Why: D-496 gives a literal the value of rounding it to its type, and D-313 makes `f32::parse` round straight to the nearest `F32`, so a literal and the parse of its text must agree. Rounding through `Float` first rounds twice. It can produce an exact tie that then rounds to even, one ulp off, and it rejected the valid literal above with E0071. Keeping the source text in THIR was rejected, because every later phase would have to parse it. Refines D-496.
- **D-570 Locations of operator and assertion panics**: A failing built-in operator reports the start of the whole operator expression: the start of the left operand for a binary operator, and the `-` of a negation. This covers integer overflow, division by zero, a shift amount out of range, and a structural `==` or `!=` that reaches a function value (D-556). In `a + b * c`, an overflow of `*` reports the start of `b`. A failed `assert e`, with or without `as m`, reports the start of its condition `e`, not the `assert` keyword. A failed `let assert` reports the start of its pattern. Why: D-449 requires a location but not which column, and THIR keeps no span for an operator token or for an `assert` or `let assert` statement. Each chosen start is on the statement's line and points at what failed, and rustc also reports an overflow at the start of the expression. Refines D-449, D-528, D-556.
- **D-571 A guard runs at most once per arm**: When an arm's pattern has alternatives and the arm has a guard, the alternatives are tried from left to right, and the guard runs once, with the names bound by the first alternative that matches. When the guard is `False`, the whole arm is skipped: its later alternatives are not tried, and matching goes on with the next arm. So `case (0, 9) { (0, n) | (n, _) when n < 5 => … _ => … }` takes `_`, although `(n, _)` would bind `n` to 0. Why: §5.9.1 evaluates the guard "with the pattern's bindings", one set of bindings, §6.7 tries alternatives from left to right, and §5.15.1 runs guards in arm order. A guard can have effects, such as printing, so running it more than once would be observable. Rust's rule, which runs the guard again for each later alternative that matches, was rejected because it contradicts §5.9.1's single set of bindings. The UI test `tests/ui/05-expressions/case/guard_alternatives.cheby` pins the rule. Refines D-052.
- **D-572 Symbols of closures inside tests**: Closure `n` of a test, a local function included, is `cb$clo$test$<module path>$<ordinal>$<n>`, where the ordinal is the test's position in its module, as in the test's own symbol `cb$test$<module path>$<ordinal>`. Closures of functions and constants keep `cb$clo$<module path>$<item>$<n>`. Why: a test's name is free text, so naming its closures by it collided. `test "spin"` and `fn spin` in one module both gave `cb$clo$app$spin$0`. An item name never starts with a digit, so the segment before the closure number tells the two forms apart, even in a package named `test`. Refines D-456.
- **D-573 Error pairs of the `std::io` kernels**: A `std::io` kernel reports not found as kind 1, permission denied (`EACCES` and `EPERM`) as kind 2, invalid UTF-8 read from a file or from standard input as kind 3, and every other failure as kind 0. The message is the operating system's text without the ` (os error N)` suffix that Rust appends. A failure without an operating-system code, such as a path that contains U+0000, carries Rust's own text, and invalid UTF-8 carries `invalid UTF-8`. The kernels pass a message for every kind, and `std::io` drops it for kinds 1 to 3 (D-345). Why: D-484 leaves the code of other kinds open, and raw operating-system codes would collide with 1 to 3, since `EPERM` is 1 and `ENOENT` is 2. The suffix is Rust's formatting, not part of the operating system's message. Refines D-484.
- **D-574 Flushing after a reported `io::write_stdout` failure**: After `io::write_stdout` returns `Err`, the bytes it could not write may stay unwritten. A later failing flush of standard output then does not end the program, whether it is the flush at exit, before a panic report or before standard input is read. So a program that handles the `Err` and returns keeps its exit status. The excuse ends when `io::print` or `io::println` adds text to standard output's buffer, or when a later `io::write_stdout` succeeds. From then on a failing flush ends the program with status 1 again (D-306). Why: D-365 makes `write_stdout`'s result cover everything written so far, so its `Err` has already reported the buffered bytes, and failing the program again for them would punish a program that handled the error. Text added later without a `Result` was never reported, so D-306 still applies to it. Excusing every failure for the rest of the process was rejected for that reason. Refines D-306, D-365.
- **D-575 Lines the REPL reads**: `cheby_runtime::io::read_input_line() -> Option<String>` reads the REPL's next input line through the buffered reader of `io::read_line`. It flushes standard output first, and a failed flush ends the program with status 1 (D-306). It removes a final `\n` or `\r\n` (D-305), replaces invalid UTF-8 with U+FFFD, and returns `None` at the end of input and when standard input cannot be read. Why: D-548 fixes the shared reader but not what the REPL does with invalid UTF-8 or a read error. The REPL has no `Result` to report them through, so it replaces bad bytes as `env::args` does (D-311) and treats an unreadable input as its end. Refines D-548.
- **D-576 Reading the input files of UI tests**: `<name>.stdin` is fed byte for byte, with line endings unchanged, so a test can send `\r\n` and invalid UTF-8. An empty `.stdin` gives a standard input that ends at once, and without the file standard input is null. `<name>.args` is read as text, with invalid UTF-8 replaced and `\r\n` turned into `\n`, and holds one argument per line. An empty line is an empty argument, and a final line ending adds none. An input file beside a test whose mode is not `run-pass` or `run-fail` is a harness error. Why: D-543 says what the files hold but not how they are read. Raw standard input is needed to test D-305 and D-311, and rejecting input beside a test that never runs catches a misplaced file. Refines D-543.
- **D-577 String kernels that update a unique argument**: When its text argument has count one and no other argument is the same object, a `std::string` kernel updates it in place. `add` and `pad_end` append to it when its capacity suffices. `trim`, `trim_start`, `trim_end`, `remove_prefix`, `remove_suffix` and `slice` move the kept bytes to its start and keep its capacity. When `add` or `pad_end` cannot write in place, the copy gets a capacity of max(needed, 2 × old capacity), so repeated `acc + piece` on a unique accumulator costs amortized O(1) per byte. A result equal to the whole argument is the argument itself with one more reference: nothing trimmed or removed, a `slice` of everything, an `add` of `""`, a `pad_start` or `pad_end` already wide enough, `repeat` with count 1, and the one part of a `split` whose non-empty separator does not occur. Likewise, a `join` of a one-element list is that element itself. Every other kernel builds a new string. Why: D-525 allows in-place updates without naming the kernels, and D-300 requires the costs of `string.md`. The alias rule is D-563's for lists. Refines D-525.
- **D-578 Who sets the program's name and arguments**: The runtime keeps one process-wide program state, the arguments and the name that `env::args` and `env::program_name` return. `run_main` sets it from its `RunConfig`, and `cheby run` passes the package name. Any other host that runs entries as the program calls the public `cheby_runtime::entry::set_program(args, name)` before they run: `cheby test` with the package name (D-369), and the REPL. An empty name means not set, and `env::program_name` then returns the process's first argument with invalid UTF-8 replaced, D-369's fallback. Why: only the runtime holds the state that the kernels read, and D-369 requires the package name under `cheby test` as well as `cheby run`. Refines D-369.

Open:

- ~~What `env::program_name()` and `env::args()` return in a REPL session, inside a package and outside one, since D-369 names only `cheby run` and `cheby test`. W4-B settles it before the REPL calls `set_program` (D-578).~~ → D-612

## 2026-10-10: M1 back end, wave 3

Goal: log the gaps that wave 3 of the M1 back end (dictionaries, equality and hashing, maps and sets, constant evaluation and `cheby test`) settled in code, stating what the implementation does.

- **D-579 Static object symbols end the module path with `$$`**: The symbol of a static dictionary, descriptor or dictionary closure is `cb$<kind>$<module path>$$<name>`. The module path is joined with `$`, and the name is kept exactly as MIR built it, `::` paths included, as in `cb$dict$app$app$$app::m::I$app::t::T` and `cb$desc$geo$$Point`. A module path joined with `$` never holds `$$`, so the first `$$` always ends it. The static function value of a top-level function keeps D-523's `cb$fnval$<module path>$<item>`. When a JIT meets a static symbol again, it compares the new object's layout, strings and fields with the first one's, and different contents are an internal compiler error instead of a silent reuse. Why: D-523's form, which also joined the paths inside the name with `$`, gave module `app::app` with `app::m::I$app::t::T` and module `app` with `app::app::m::I$app::t::T` the same symbol. The JIT then reused the first dictionary, so an interface call ran another interface's function. Refines D-523, D-561.
- **D-580 Names and captures of static dictionaries**: Static names are built from types printed with full module paths, such as `List<app::shapes::Circle>`, `(Int, String)` and `fn(Int) -> Bool`, where builtin types and interfaces print bare, as `Int` and `Show`. A dictionary is named `<interface path>$<type>`, as in `std::ops::Compare$(Int, String)`, with kind `dict`. The closure of function `n` of a static dictionary is named `<dictionary name>$<n>`, with kind `fnval`, where `n` is the function's position in the dictionary (D-524). The descriptor of an applied type is named `<type>`, with kind `desc`. The module path in these symbols is the unit's own. The entries unit has no module, so there it is the module of the entry item whose error type's `show` needs the dictionary. A unit defines each name once. A satisfying function that needs dictionaries (D-064) becomes a static closure whose layout is `Closure(k)` for its `k` captures: its value shim's code, then the static dictionaries it needs. A tuple dictionary's function (D-167) becomes a static closure of the tuple function's code and the static element dictionaries. Why: D-523 requires names built from paths and names, never ids, but does not say which. With fully qualified types the name determines the contents, so equal symbols in two units or batches denote equal objects, which D-561's define-once rule needs. A static may point only at statics, and D-523's static function values had no captures, so conditional conformance and tuple dictionaries needed static closures with static captures. Refines D-523, D-524.
- **D-581 Shape of descriptors and the builtin nominal descriptors**: A type without type arguments is described by its nominal descriptor itself (layout 12): `cheby_rt_desc_<name>` for a builtin type and `cb$desc$<module path>$$<Type>` for a user type. Any other type is a descriptor (layout 11) of its nominal descriptor and one or more argument descriptors. A tuple uses `cheby_rt_desc_tuple` with its elements, and a function type uses `cheby_rt_desc_fn` with its parameters, then its result, so `fn() -> Int` has one argument. A closed descriptor is a static object (D-580), and one that mentions a type parameter is built at run time. A type parameter's descriptor is field 0 of its first bound's dictionary (D-499). The runtime also reads a descriptor with no arguments as the bare type. The printed name of a builtin nominal descriptor is the type's name, `Int`, `Float`, `F32`, `I8` to `U64`, `Bool`, `Nil`, `String`, `Bytes`, `List`, `Map`, `Set`, `Option`, `Result` or `Order`, or `Tuple` and `Fn`, and its module path is empty. When the generic debug walk reaches a descriptor, it writes `Name<A, B>` with the printed name and no module path, `(A, B)` for a tuple and `fn(A, B) -> C` for a function type. Why: D-524 fixes the layouts but neither when a bare nominal descriptor stands for a type nor the builtin names. One canonical form keeps equality and debug text in agreement, and the empty module path matches the type keys of D-559. Refines D-524.
- **D-582 Equality and hashing of descriptors**: Two descriptors are equal when their nominal descriptors are the same object, they have the same number of arguments, and their arguments are pairwise equal. Each type has exactly one nominal descriptor per JIT (D-561), so a type redefined in the REPL, or a type of another major version of a package, is a different type even under the same name. Hashing writes the nominal descriptor's module path and printed name, then the argument count and the arguments, and never an address. Why: identity of nominal descriptors is identity of nominal types, as §8.6.1 requires, while comparing names and module paths would merge the types it keeps apart. Hashing names keeps hashes independent of where objects live (D-264), and two different types with one name merely collide. Refines D-530.
- **D-583 Location of built-in tuple code**: The code of the built-in tuple `Show::show` (D-167, D-560) has no source, so its function-entry location is the text `<builtin>`, and a stack overflow inside it reports `panicked at <builtin>`. The tuple code of `Compare::compare` uses the location of `Compare::compare` in `std::ops`. Why: every function of the Cheby ABI needs an entry-check location (D-449), and the builtin `Show` has no span. Refines D-449.
- **D-584 `@std_hooks` in `==`, hashing and debug text**: Two values of one type with hooks, meaning equal type keys (D-559), are compared by the type's `equal` hook alone, whatever their constructors. Hashing such a value writes the constructor mark and the type key, then hashes structurally, in the same walk, the value that the `hash` hook returns, and never the tag. Its debug text is the `debug` hook's `String` as it is, without quotes, wherever the value appears. Why: D-342 exists so that different internal shapes of one value compare, hash and print by contents, so the tag must not count, and values that the `equal` hook equates must hash equally. Refines D-482.
- **D-585 Every hook of a compiled module is compiled**: When a module gets a code unit, the `@std_hooks` functions of every type it declares, and what they call, are reachable (D-510), whether or not reachable code mentions the type's constructors. Their value shims and `cb$fnval$` statics are compiled, and the JIT registers the three function values in the layout table for every constructor of the type. Why: a module's unit registers the layouts of all its types, and values of a hooked type can reach `==` and debug printing through std code that never names their constructors. Keying hooks to constructor mentions left registered layouts without hook code, an internal compiler error in every program that imports `std::io`. Refines D-510.
- **D-586 Walks over maps and sets nested in each other**: Equality, hashing and debug text of a `Map` or `Set` run on the explicit worklist of the walk that reached it. Hashing one as a value writes a mark byte, 16 for a map and 17 for a set, and its entry count, then hashes its entries as an unordered group. Each key, value and element is hashed in a fresh SipHash-1-3 state keyed with the walk's seed. A set adds up its elements' hashes. A map adds up, for each entry, SipHash-1-3 keyed with the walk's seed and the fixed second key `0x6d61_7065_6e74_7279` over the key's hash, then the value's hash. The wrapping sum is written after the group. Two collections are equal when they are of one kind and size and, for each key of the first, hashed under the second's seed, the only entry of the second that the key can equal is found from that hash, and the key pair and the value pair go on the caller's equality worklist. Only keys in a node of keys whose full 64-bit hashes are equal are compared in a nested walk, which a random seed makes vanishingly rare. Debug text keeps the items of each sorted group as segments of one text arena, with nested groups by reference, sorts the items by comparing their segments (D-587), and writes every character once. So values that nest maps and sets through keys or values compare, hash and print at any depth without deepening the Rust stack, and printing costs time linear in the text plus the sorting. Why: plan §0 requires heap worklists for deep data. A nested walk per entry aborted the process on values a few hundred thousand levels deep, in programs and in constant evaluation under `cheby check`, and copying each sorted group into its parent made printing a 100,000-deep tree take 13.6 s. Refines D-538.
- **D-587 Order of map and set entries in debug text**: Map entries and set elements print sorted by their whole debug text, compared as UTF-8 bytes, which is Unicode scalar value order, so `10` comes before `9`. A map entry's text is that of the tuple `(key, value)`, and a nested collection counts with its own sorted text. Entries with equal text keep their iteration order, which cannot show. Why: D-344 and D-382 say "sorted by the whole debug text" without naming the comparison. Byte order is the order of `String`'s `compare` (D-136). Refines D-344, D-382.
- **D-588 Inserting a key equal to a stored one keeps the stored key**: `map::insert` and `map::upsert` on a key that is `==` to a stored key replace the value and keep the stored key. So `map::from_list` keeps the first of repeated keys with the last value (D-329), and `set::insert` of a present element returns the set itself. The difference shows only for keys that are `==` but print differently, such as `0.0` and `-0.0`. Why: `map.md` says only "replacing any value already there", and the choice shows in debug text. Keeping the stored key leaves the trie's keys alone, matches `set::insert`, which leaves a set unchanged when the element is present, and matches Clojure and JavaScript's `Map`.
- **D-589 `list::unique` without repeats**: When no element repeats, `list::unique` returns its argument itself with one more reference. It hashes the elements like `Set` elements with the running entry's seed, and an element that holds a function value or a handle panics with `EqualityOnFunction` at `<unknown location>` (D-233, D-556). Why: a result equal to the whole argument is the argument, as for the string kernels of D-577. Extends D-577.
- **D-590 Values of external types**: A value of an external type is a 16-byte runtime object: the header, then the foreign handle as a raw word at offset 8. Each external type has a layout of its own at a user layout id, registered by `cheby_runtime::layout::register_external(type_name, type_key)`, of the runtime kind `EXTERNAL_HANDLE`. Its debug text is `<Name>` with the unqualified type name (D-233), and `==` and hashing stop on it with `EqualityOnFunction`, as on a function value (D-189). Calling the type's drop function when the last reference goes away (D-101) is not implemented, since M1 creates no external values and the native host library comes with M3 (OQ-P9). Why: no decision fixes how a foreign handle is represented. One object per handle gives reference counting (D-100) and the placeholder text a layout to read. A kind of the runtime has the behavior a new `LayoutKind` variant would have.
- **D-591 Functions with only a JS implementation on native**: A function whose only implementation is `@external(js, …)` stays in the native program. Its native body calls the effect check with the function's path, then panics with "`<path>` exists only on the `js` target" at the function's location. A constant whose initializer reaches it therefore gets E0077, whose note names that path (D-592). Checked native code never reaches the panic, because the cross-target check keeps it from calling the function. Why: a constant's initializer is not target-checked and runs on the host (§12.6, D-512), so a `@target(js)` constant, or any constant of a package with `targets = ["js"]`, may call such a function. Before this, `cheby check` and `cheby test` stopped with an internal compiler error. Refines D-533, D-537.
- **D-592 Where a failed constant is reported, and the note of E0077**: When a constant's evaluation panics, the diagnostic goes to the innermost constant in progress, whose initializer panicked, which may be a constant that the top-level one forced. A cycle is E0069 at the constant that a read found in progress. Every constant then in progress is marked failed, and a later initializer that reads a failed constant stops without a diagnostic of its own, so each failure is reported once. E0077's note names what the effect check received: "it reached the external function `<symbol>`; only the kernels of `std::list`, `std::map`, `std::set`, `std::string`, `std::bytes` and the numeric modules run at compile time", where `<symbol>` is the native symbol, such as `cheby_io_println`, or the path of a JS-only function (D-591). For the stub of a native symbol missing under `cheby check` (D-532), the note is "it called a native function that nothing in this build defines". Why: the error then points at the initializer that actually panicked, as rustc does for constants. Reporting every reader again would bury the cause: a cycle `a` → `b` → `a` would give two E0069s, and `next = base + 1` an E0075 for every user of `base`. The runtime knows only the string it was given (D-520), and naming the Cheby path of a native external would change that contract. Refines D-533, D-535.
- **D-593 Modules in a constant's hash seed**: The modules that D-538 hashes into a constant's seed are the constant's own module and the modules of every function and constant that its initializer reaches, transitively and through the initializers of the constants it reads, dictionary functions included, together with the modules of every constructor that any of them mentions. The builtin module is left out, std modules count, and a module without a source file hashes as 0. The package name in the seed is the first segment of the constant's module path, `std` for a std constant. Why: a constant's value depends on the code it runs, including the initializers of the constants it reads, and on the definitions of the types it builds, whose field order and debug text it may observe. D-538 says only "each module that the initializer reaches". Refines D-538.
- **D-594 Ties in the order of constant evaluation**: D-535's module order is the build order of modules, dependencies first. Ties in the dependency order of constants go by that position, then by the constant's name. When only constants in cycles remain, the smallest by the same key comes next, and evaluating it reports the cycle (E0069). Why: build order is the module order every other back-end step uses. The tie-break never changes a value, since forcing makes values independent of order (D-535). It only fixes which constant is evaluated first, and so where a cycle is reported (D-592). Refines D-535.
- **D-595 What a test entry returns, and its panic report**: A test's entry thunk prints nothing and returns one uniform word: the static `Nil` when the test passed, and otherwise the text of its `Err` as an owned `String`, made as for `main` (D-529), by `show`, through the type's static dictionary when that `show` needs dictionaries (D-580), and else by debug text. A `main` thunk keeps its status of 0 or 1. A panic in a test flushes standard output, and the runtime hands the report to `cheby test` without printing it to standard error, as it does for constant evaluation. `cheby test` prints it in the test's failure block. Why: D-541 puts the `Err` text and the panic report in the failure block on standard output, so the host needs them, and printing the report to standard error too would show it twice. MIR cannot return a raw 0 where a uniform word is expected, so a passed test returns `Nil`. Refines D-529, D-541.
- **D-596 Stack of a test**: Each test runs on an entry stack as large as that of `main`, 64 MiB (`DEFAULT_ROOT_STACK_SIZE`), instead of the 8 MiB of other entries (D-457). Why: a test calls the same code as `main`, so deep non-tail recursion that works under `cheby run` must not overflow in a test. D-125 sizes only the root fiber. Refines D-457.
- **D-597 Which tests `cheby test` selects**: `cheby test` and the std self-test select a test only when it exists on `native`: its targets, which include its module's `@!target`, contain `native`, and for a local test the package's `targets` contain `native` too. A test that exists only on `js` is neither run nor counted as filtered out. After the package checks without errors, `--module PATH` must be a well-formed module path that is a module of the package or the prefix of one. Otherwise the command stops with status 1 before any test is compiled, with the tool error "`x` is not a module path" or "there is no module `x` in this package", the texts of `cheby run` (D-464). Why: an item marked for one target exists only on that target (§12.6), and the native test command builds for native, where a JS-only test that called a JS external crashed the build. Running no test and exiting 0 on a mistyped `--module` would hide the typo. Refines D-541.
- **D-598 Layout of the test reports**: `cheby test` writes a test's line when the test finishes, so the test's own output comes before its line. The failure blocks follow all the lines, each preceded by an empty line. The summary is preceded by an empty line when a test line was printed, and with no test selected it is the only output. The std self-test (D-542) prints its report after every test has run: a line per test, or `module <path> did not build: <codes>` for a module whose tests did not build at all and `test <module path> "<name>" did not build: <codes>` for one test, then the failure blocks, an empty line, `counts per module:` with one line `  <module path>: P passed; F failed; D did not build` per module, a line `  total: P passed; F failed; D did not build; N filtered out`, an empty line and the summary. Its summary adds `D did not build; ` before the filtered count when D is not 0. `<codes>` are the distinct error codes, sorted and joined by `, `, or `internal compiler error: <message>`, and a module that the front end did not check without an error of its own says `not checked`. Why: D-541 and D-542 fix the pieces but not when a line appears relative to uncaptured output, nor the separators. A line printed before its test ran would split the test's own output. Refines D-541, D-542.
- **D-599 How the std self-test reaches std**: The std self-test checks the standard library through an in-memory package `std_self_test`, version `0.0.0`, whose root module imports every shipped std module. The front end runs in a mode that only the self-test uses, which checks the `test` blocks of std modules and keeps them. The selected tests of each std module build as one batch, and when the batch does not build, each test builds alone (D-542). A module with front-end errors in its own file does not build. While std tests run, `env::program_name()` returns `std_self_test` (D-578). Why: D-479 and D-542 need every std module checked with its tests in one front-end run, and std modules are loaded only by import. Refines D-542.
- **D-600 Words of `// @flags:`**: The value of a UI test's `@flags` directive is split at whitespace into command-line words, which go after the subcommand and before `--` and the program's arguments. The directive may appear more than once, and its words are appended in order. There is no quoting, so a word cannot contain whitespace. A `.args` file beside a test with `@command: test` is a harness error, "`cheby test` takes no program arguments". Why: D-543 introduces the directive without saying how its value is split. Whitespace splitting covers every flag of M1, such as `--deny-warnings`, `--target js`, `--module p` and a filter, and `cheby test` accepts nothing after `--`. Refines D-543.
- **D-601 The compiler runs on a 256 MiB stack**: The `cheby` command runs its subcommand on a thread named `cheby` with a 256 MiB stack, of which only address space is reserved up front. If the thread cannot start, the command prints "error: cannot start the compiler's thread: …" and exits with status 1, and if the thread panics, the exit status is 101. Why: parts of the compiler recurse over the source's structure, notably the decision trees of list patterns at about six frames per tested element, and on the 8 MiB main thread a valid 24 KB program aborted. This is a stopgap until the decision-tree builder uses an explicit stack (W5-C, W6-A). Other hosts of the compiler, such as the driver's tests, keep their default stacks.

Open:

- ~~The symbol of the descriptor of `dyn I`, documented in MIR as `cb$desc$<module path of I>$$dyn$<I>` (D-579), for an interface with an empty module path, the builtin `Show`, which would give `cb$desc$$$dyn$Show`. No `dyn` descriptor is built yet. W4-A settles it.~~ → D-603
- ~~How a REPL redefinition gives the statics it changes new symbols, for example through the generations of D-547, since a JIT now rejects a static symbol defined again with different contents (D-579). W4-B settles it.~~ → D-611

## 2026-10-11: M1 back end, wave 4

Goal: log the gaps that wave 4 of the M1 back end (`dyn` values, the REPL session, constant evaluation limits, flags, correctly rounded math and the parallel front end) settled in code, stating what the implementation does, and the user's two decisions on the REPL.

- **D-602 `dyn` forwarders are keyed by the receiver's interface**: The forwarder through which a `dyn J` value satisfies a bound is keyed by the interface function `f` and by `J`, the interface of the `dyn` receiver: MIR's `FnKey::DynForward { iface_fn, dyn_iface }`. Its symbol is `cb$dynfwd$<module path of f>$<I>$<f>$$<module path of J>$<J>`, where `I` is `f`'s interface and module paths are joined with `$`, as in `cb$dynfwd$geo$Shape$area$$geo$Named`. It depends only on its key, so it is defined once per JIT (D-561). It has the uniform signature: the closure `self`, the `dyn J` receiver, then the other arguments. It reads the dictionary and the value inside the receiver, loads `f`'s closure from its field in the `J` dictionary, and tail-calls it with the inner value in place of the receiver. Its entry-check location is `f`'s location, or `<builtin>` for the builtin `Show::show` (D-583). The static dictionary through which `dyn J` satisfies `I`, `J` itself or an interface `J` embeds, has kind `dict` and the name `<I path>$dyn <J path>` (D-580). It holds the descriptor of `dyn J`, then for each function of `I` a static `Closure(0)` named `<dictionary name>$<n>` that holds the function's forwarder, then the forwarding dictionaries of `I`'s embedded interfaces for the same `dyn J`. Why: a function's field in a dictionary depends on the interface, because D-524 puts embedded interfaces' functions first. With `J: A + B`, `B`'s first function is field 1 + |A| of a `J` dictionary but field 1 of a `B` dictionary. Keyed by `f` alone, two different codes had one symbol, and the UI test `08-interfaces/run/dyn_bounded_calls` failed. Refines D-560.
- **D-603 Descriptor of `dyn` of a builtin interface**: The nominal descriptor of `dyn I` (layout 12) is one static per JIT, of kind `desc`, whose module is `I`'s module path and whose name is `dyn$<I>`, giving `cb$desc$<module path of I>$$dyn$<I>`. It holds the printed name `dyn <I>` and `I`'s module path. For the builtin `Show`, whose module path is empty, the symbol is `cb$desc$$$dyn$Show`, and the first `$$` still ends the empty module path (D-579). The MIR validator accepts an empty module path for these descriptors only. Why: D-523 assumed that every interface has a module path, as the open item of the wave-3 session noted. Keeping the general form avoids a special case, and the kind and the `dyn$` prefix rule out collisions. With one object per JIT, `dyn I` is one nominal type under D-582. Refines D-523, D-579.
- **D-604 A `dyn` value inside a `dyn` value**: For `==`, hashing and debug text, a `dyn` value whose value is itself a `dyn` value counts as that inner value, with the inner value's descriptor. The rule repeats until the value is not a `dyn` value. Such a value arises only when generic code converts a value of a type parameter that is a `dyn` type to `dyn`, as in `fn boxed<T: Any>(x: T) -> dyn Any` called with a `dyn Shape`. That call and a direct conversion of the `dyn Shape` to `dyn Any` then compare and hash alike. Why: D-502 says that a `dyn` value keeps its descriptor when it converts to `dyn` of an empty interface, and a direct conversion does. Through a bound, the dictionary describes `dyn J` and would hide the value's type. Descriptors are observable only through `==` and hashing, so reading through the nesting there gives the direct conversion's result everywhere. A run-time check at every generic conversion was the alternative. Refines D-502, D-530.
- **D-605 What a REPL entry is**: The REPL reads one entry at a time. An entry goes on to the next line while a bracket, a block, a string, a raw string or an interpolation is open, or while its last non-blank line starts with `@` or `///`, which an item must follow. Text inside `//` comments and strings does not count, except the code of an interpolation. A closing bracket that matches nothing ends the entry, and the checker reports it. At the end of input, an unfinished entry is still checked, so its error is reported. Text of only blanks and comments is not an entry and takes no number. An entry is declarations and imports when its first line that is neither blank nor a `//` comment starts with `@`, `///` or `//!`, or with one of the words `fn` not followed by `(`, `pub`, `priv`, `type`, `interface`, `const`, `import` or `test`. It is then checked as a module. Any other entry is statements, checked as the body of a block. When its last statement is an expression whose type is not `Never`, its value is shown as `<debug text> : <type>`, and nothing for `Nil` (D-546). The names bound by its top-level `let` and `let assert` statements become session values (D-547, D-608). A shown `Result` is not a discarded result (W0006), and a session value is not an unused binding (W0002). An entry with an error binds nothing, and its W0002 warnings are dropped as noise. Why: §13.5.1 lists what the REPL accepts but not how input splits into entries, nor how a statements entry splits into a value and bindings. Refines D-546, D-547.
- **D-606 REPL entries are modules of the local package**: Each entry is checked as the module `<repl:N>` of the local package, so the package's package-visible items are usable at the prompt after an import, without `:module`. Outside a package, the session's package is `repl`, version `0.0.0`, with no modules. A `priv` item of an entry is visible only inside that entry, because it never becomes a session name. Value types and diagnostics write session types and items without the `<repl:N>::` qualifier, as in `Point`, while locations keep `<repl:N>:line:col` (D-546). When the session starts, it loads every shipped std module through a module `<package>::<repl>` that imports them all and that no entry can import, so every std import of an entry resolves. Entries are checked for the `native` target only, meaning the package's targets intersected with `native`, so a native-only item of the package and an `@external(native, …)` entered at the prompt are usable. Why: §13.5 does not say which package REPL input belongs to, what `priv` means at the prompt, or how session types print, and D-463's own-module rule would print `<repl:3>::Point`. The session runs on the native JIT, and checking entries for `js` too rejected native-only items that work there. Refines D-098, D-546.
- **D-607 When a REPL redefinition takes over a slot**: A redefined function takes over the old function's slot only when their type signatures are equal: the same generics, parameter types, clamped parameters and result, with parameter names ignored. A redefined constant takes over the old constant's slot `cb$const$…` only when its type is the same. Any other redefinition gets a new slot whose symbol ends in `$r<gen>`, which later code uses, while code compiled earlier keeps the old slot. A constructor never takes over anything, and session values never share a slot (D-608). When an entry whose code was compiled is rejected, because evaluating its constants failed or its body panicked, the slots it repointed are pointed back at their old code. Why: D-456 repoints a slot by ABI signature, but `fn f(x: String)` and `fn f(x: List<Int>)` share an ABI, and repointing between them would run code on values of the wrong type, which §13.5.2 forbids. A rejected entry must change nothing in the session. Refines D-456, D-547.
- **D-608 Prompt-level `let` rebinding shadows**: Each prompt-level `let` or `let assert` creates a new session value with its own slot and generation, whatever its type. Later entries read the new binding. Functions, closures and constants checked or evaluated before the rebinding keep reading the binding they were checked against. So after `let x = 1`, `let f = fn() -> Int { x }`, `fn read_x() -> Int { x }` and `let x = 2`, both `f()` and `read_x()` return 1. A `let` rebinding never checks other definitions again, whatever the new type. Functions and constants stay late-bound, as §13.5.2 and D-547 say. Why: the user decided it. Values then never change observably, which matches D-193, an exception to the shadowing ban, D-547, under which a `let` is evaluated once, and D-017, no mutation. The late binding first implemented stored a same-type rebinding into the old slot, so an existing closure returned a new result, and a rebinding with another type gave no note (review SEM4-2). Refines D-193, D-547.
- **D-609 Which REPL definitions are checked again**: When a function or constant is redefined with another signature or type, every current function and constant whose signature or body names it is checked again from its own text. That includes the definitions that named an earlier definition of the name, also through a slot that a same-signature predecessor shared, and the definitions that relied on a function for interface satisfaction (D-610). They are checked in the order they entered the session. Each one that checks is compiled again as a redefinition of itself, with a new generation, and takes over its own slot. Each one that fails is reported and keeps its old code, and each of its errors gets the note "`f` was checked again because a definition it uses changed its signature; it keeps its old definition". Its warnings are not shown again. A redefinition with the same signature or type checks nothing again. Neither does a `let` rebinding (D-608), nor a redefined type, interface or alias: definitions already checked keep the old one, whose values are stale (D-043, D-547). Why: D-043 and §13.5.2 require re-checking dependents and reporting breakage but do not say which definitions count or how the report reads. Refines D-043, D-547.
- **D-610 Interface satisfaction across REPL entries**: For interface satisfaction (§8.2), the prompt entries of a session count as one module. A type entered at the prompt satisfies an interface through the session's current functions, whichever entries declared them. So after `type Cat { Cat(Int) }` and, in a later entry, `fn show(c: Cat) -> String { … }`, `Cat` satisfies `Show` in every entry after the one with `show`. Definitions checked before keep what they were checked against. §8.2's visibility rule applies unchanged, and a `priv` function counts only in its own entry, because it never becomes a session name (D-606). A redefinition of `show` with the same signature replaces the implementation through its slot (D-607), so code checked earlier calls the new one. A redefinition with another signature replaces the function for later entries, and the definitions that relied on the old one are checked again (D-609). A session name means one function, so the session satisfies an interface for one type at a time, as a module does (D-045). Entering `fn show(d: Dog) -> String` after `Cat`'s `show` is such a redefinition: later entries see `Dog` satisfy `Show`, and `Cat` no longer. A redefined type is a new type (D-043). A function that names the old type satisfies the interface for the old type only, and the new type satisfies it once a function that names the new type is entered. For types of the package and std, §8.2's rule is unchanged, and functions entered at the prompt never count for them. Why: the user decided it. An entry is submitted at a type's closing `}`, and two items on one line are E0013, so with every entry a module of its own, no type entered at the prompt could satisfy a non-empty interface (review R4-7). A paste mode that reads several items as one entry was the other option, and satisfaction would still depend on how the input was split. Refines D-061, D-547.
- **D-611 Symbols of REPL session items and statics**: Symbols of session items use the module path `<repl>` and the item's session name, `f`, `T` or `I::f` written `I$f`, with `$r<gen>` appended when the generation is above 0, as in `cb$fn$<repl>$f$r1` and `cb$layout$<repl>$Circle$r2`. A slot is identified by the path `<repl>::f` and its ABI signature (D-607). An item's generation is the smallest number above the generation of every item compiled before it that has the same top-level name or the same text, or 0 when there is none. So constructors `Red` of two different session types get `cb$layout$<repl>$Red` and `cb$layout$<repl>$Red$r1`. An item counts as compiled once its code is compiled, even when its entry is then rejected, so no symbol is defined twice. In the names of static objects, every `<repl:N>::X` of a session item `X` becomes `<repl>::X` with its generation, in the statics of any module. The nominal descriptor of a session type `X` is `cb$desc$<repl>$$X$r<gen>`, and that of `dyn I` for a session interface `I` is `cb$desc$<repl>$$dyn$I$r<gen>`. The dictionaries, dictionary closures and structural descriptors that entries build, such as the descriptor of `(Int, String)`, are statics of the module `<repl>`, so an identical static of two entries is one symbol, which later entries import (D-561, D-580). A nominal descriptor of an entry item that is not a session item keeps its entry's module path. A definition checked again (D-609) is compiled as the module `<repl:K:n>`, where `K` is its entry and `n` counts the re-checks of the session, and its items keep their session names. Why: D-547 gives every symbol of a redefined item its generation, but MIR names statics from module paths and types, not from items. Constructors were counted under their type's name, so `type A { Red }` and `type B { Red }` both gave `cb$layout$<repl>$Red`. Dictionaries moved to `<repl>` while structural descriptors kept their entry module, so one dictionary name had different contents in two entries, an internal compiler error under D-579's check. Extends D-547. Refines D-579.
- **D-612 Program name and arguments in the REPL**: When a REPL session starts, it calls `set_program` (D-578) with no arguments and the package's name, or `repl` outside a package. So `env::program_name()` returns that name, and `env::args()` is empty. Why: this answers D-578's open item the way D-369 answers it for `cheby run` and `cheby test`, and it keeps transcripts deterministic. Refines D-369, D-578.
- **D-613 Stack of a REPL entry**: The body of each REPL entry runs on a 64 MiB entry stack (`DEFAULT_ROOT_STACK_SIZE`), as `main` and tests do (D-596). Constants entered at the prompt are evaluated on the 8 MiB stack of other entries. Why: §13.5.4 runs each entry as if it were the body of `main`, while D-457's 8 MiB default is for other entries. Refines D-457.
- **D-614 Standard-input lines in REPL transcripts**: In a transcript `tests/repl/<name>.repl`, a line that starts with `< ` is a line of standard input that an entry reads, for example with `io::read_line`. It is fed right after the input line before it and is not echoed, so it is not part of the expected output. Blessing keeps such lines after the same input lines. Why: D-546's format had no way to give an entry standard input that the REPL does not echo (D-548). Refines D-546.
- **D-615 Parser cross-check of REPL inputs**: `cargo xtask cross-check` parses each input of a REPL transcript, a `> ` line with its `. ` lines, in two forms: as it is, as the items of a module, and inside `fn repl_entry() { … }`, as statements, which covers expressions. Of the commands, only the expression of `:type` is parsed, and the others are skipped. For each input, the two parsers must agree on whether at least one form parses. An input that both reject is a syntax error on purpose, which the transcript shows as a diagnostic, and not a mismatch. The `.cheby` files of a transcript's package directory must parse. Why: `testing.md` §5 asks to parse inputs as expressions, statements or items, but neither parser has an entry point for fragments. Wrapping gives both parsers the same text, and only a disagreement shows that a parser is wrong (D-272). Refines D-272, D-546.
- **D-616 Values of `[const-eval]`**: A limit in `[const-eval]` is a string of decimal digits followed by a unit: `ms` or `s` for `time-limit`, and `KiB`, `MiB` or `GiB` for `memory-limit`. Leading zeros are allowed, as in `"0010s"`. A limit of zero is E0036, "`memory-limit` must be greater than zero", and does not mean "no limit". A limit whose milliseconds or bytes overflow 64 bits is E0036, "`time-limit` `…` is too large". A missing number or an unknown unit is E0036, "`10m` is not a valid `time-limit`", with the help "write an integer followed by `ms` or `s`, such as `"10s"`". A value that is not a string, a `[const-eval]` that is not a table, and any other key are E0036 too, the last with the help "the keys are `time-limit` and `memory-limit`". Why: D-536 gives the spelling but not whether zero is valid. A zero limit would fail every constant, so it is surely a mistake, and no spelling means "unlimited". Refines D-536.
- **D-617 The memory limit counts what one evaluation keeps live**: The memory limit of a top-level constant evaluation (D-536) counts the heap bytes that this evaluation keeps live at once: the live bytes of the build's accounting domain (D-564) minus those live when the evaluation started. That includes the constants it forces and what kernels allocate. Constants evaluated earlier, the objects of an abandoned evaluation and REPL session values do not count. Every allocation, those of kernels included, is checked against the limit before memory is requested, so the peak never passes it, and a single request over the limit is E0076, never an allocation failure. E0076's note reads "the limit counts the heap bytes that the evaluation keeps live at once". Why: D-536's "live in the build" made a constant's verdict depend on how many constants came before it. In the REPL, once one constant hit the limit, or after a large `let`, every later constant failed, and a huge single request aborted the process instead of giving E0076 (review MEM-1, MEM-2). D-536's own reason, that dropped temporaries do not count, and its per-constant message already describe a measure per evaluation. Refines D-536.
- **D-618 Where E0076 points**: E0076 is reported at the constant whose evaluation started at the top level, also when the limit ran out while it was computing a constant that it forced. A note then names that constant: "it was computing the constant `x`, which it reads". The message names the limit and its value as `[const-eval]` spells it, as in "evaluating the constant `table` took longer than the time limit of 10s" and "evaluating the constant `table` used more than the memory limit of 1GiB". A time is written in whole seconds when it is one, else in milliseconds. A memory size is written in the largest of `GiB`, `MiB` and `KiB` that divides it, else in bytes. The help says to compute less at compile time or raise the limit in `[const-eval]`. Constants that read the stopped one are not reported again, as with D-592. Why: D-536 applies the limits to the top-level evaluation with the constants it forces, so the top-level constant is where the user raises the limit or cuts the work, and the note keeps the inner constant visible. D-592's innermost-constant rule is for initializers that panic. Refines D-536.
- **D-619 The JS range check of types that grow as they unfold**: The E0078 check (D-540) walks a graph built from the constant's type, with one node per type, where a recursive type reuses its node. The graph has at most 65,536 nodes. Only a type that grows as it unfolds, through polymorphic recursion, can reach that bound, and each further type is then treated as holding no `Int`, so an out-of-range `Int` inside it is not reported. Why: such a type has no finite unfolding, so a walk led by the type must stop somewhere. Following the value instead would need its types rebuilt at every node, and such types do not occur in practice. Refines D-540.
- **D-620 Determinism dumps and what `check-determinism` compares**: When `CHEBY_DUMP_DIR` names a directory, every build writes there, overwriting earlier files. It writes `iface/<module path>.iface` with the segments joined by `.`, the canonical encoding of each checked module's interface. It writes `mir/<module path>.mir` for each code unit, `mir/<module path>.tests.mir` for each tests unit and `mir/entries.mir` for the entry thunks, as `cheby_mir::dump` prints them, with the module path written with `::`. It writes `clif.txt`, the CLIF of every compiled function joined with newlines in compile order, unless the caller captures the CLIF itself. It writes `consts.txt`, one line `<module path>::<name> = <debug text>` per evaluated constant, sorted by module path and name, and empty when none was evaluated. `cargo xtask check-determinism` first generates each benchmark shape into two directories and compares the trees. Then it copies every example and a generated project of each shape, 20,000 lines by default and at least 40,000 for `large`, under two absolute paths, and builds each copy with `--jobs 1` and with the default. Each build is `cheby check`, which evaluates the constants, and `xtask bench-build`, which compiles every function, both with `CHEBY_DUMP_DIR` set. The two dump trees must be identical byte for byte, and so must each command's exit status and standard error, and the standard output of `cheby check`. The standard output of `bench-build` holds its timings and is not compared. Why: D-551 names the artifacts but not their files or the builds that produce them. `check` evaluates the constants, and building every function gives the MIR and CLIF of all code. Comparing command output also catches absolute paths and schedule-dependent text in diagnostics. Refines D-551.
- **D-621 Denied phase-A warnings do not stop the front end**: With `--deny-warnings`, a phase-A warning, such as one about the manifest, is rendered as an error (D-549), but it does not stop the front end the way a phase-A error does (D-452). Name resolution and type checking still run and report their diagnostics. The command then fails with status 1, and nothing after the front end runs: `cheby check` evaluates no constants, and `run` and `test` compile and run nothing. Why: D-549 does not say whether a denied warning counts as an error for D-452's stop rule. Reporting every warning in one run is more useful in CI, and the later phases never see a broken tree, since warnings do not come from one. Refines D-452, D-549.
- **D-622 `-j` is global and sizes the worker pool**: `-j N` and `--jobs N` are global options, accepted before or after the subcommand. `check`, `run`, `test` and `repl` start rayon's global worker pool before they build, with N threads or by default the available parallelism. Each worker is named `cheby-worker-<i>` and has a 256 MiB stack, of which only address space is reserved, as for the command's own thread (D-601). The other commands ignore the option. The argument parser rejects 0 as a usage error. Sizing the pool a second time with another number of threads is the tool error "cannot start N worker threads: …". Why: D-551 names the option but not where it goes, and scripts may pass it to every command. The parallel front end runs the recursive checker on the workers, and Rust's default 2 MiB worker stack would overflow on input that the sequential build accepts. Refines D-551, D-601.
- **D-623 Precision cap and tables of the correctly rounded functions**: Ziv's loop in the accurate phase of the correctly rounded functions (D-568) tries 256, 512, 1024 and 2048 bits, each with 64 guard bits that it does not trust. If the rounding is still undecided at 2048 bits, the 2048-bit value is rounded to nearest as it is. Exact results never reach the loop: each function settles them first, including the powers `x^y` that are dyadic rationals, with integer powers computed exactly up to 8192 bits. The fast phase reads its constants and tables from the generated file `crates/cheby_runtime/src/num/math/tables.rs`, where each entry is the double-double nearest to the value computed at 256 bits with the runtime's own big floats. A test generates the file in memory and fails when it differs, and the ignored test `num::math::tables_gen` rewrites it. Why: a loop without a cap has no proven end, and no bound for `pow`'s worst cases is proven, while no input of D-553's verification, a million random inputs per function included, needed more than 256 bits. Computing the tables at the first call cost about 17 ms per process. Refines D-568.

Open:

- ~~REPL work for W5-A: the commands `:type`, `:module`, `:reload` and `:help`, which now report that they are not available yet, the ` (stale)` marker, E0054 over the session's live bindings (D-475), `--color` in the REPL, and what entries of a package whose targets lack `native` may use (D-606).~~ → D-624, D-625, D-626, D-628. Done: W5-A (wave 5), and `--color` reaches the REPL since gate 5.
- ~~D-608 (shadowing `let` rebinding) and D-610 (session-wide interface satisfaction) are implemented in W5-A, with the parts of D-607 and D-609 that follow from them: session values never sharing a slot, no re-check after a `let` rebinding, and the re-check of definitions that relied on a function for satisfaction. The code still stores a same-type `let` rebinding into the old slot and checks readers of a `let` again when its type changes, and `tests/repl/let_rebinding.repl` encodes that.~~ Done: W5-A (wave 5).
- ~~Review R4-8: one run of example 027 in about 800 printed nothing and exited with status 0. It was not reproduced in 200 more runs. Gate 5 loops the examples, also on Linux.~~ Done: gate 5, not reproduced in 1,100 runs on macOS and 1,100 on Linux.
- ~~Releasing an abandoned entry's stack after a panic (R4-5) is unix-only and was verified on macOS only. Gate 5's Linux run must confirm it.~~ Done: gate 5, the stack release test passes on Linux.
- E0078 does not say where in the constant's value the out-of-range `Int` is.

## 2026-10-11: M1 back end, wave 5

Goal: log the gaps that wave 5 of the M1 back end (the REPL commands and `:reload`, the standard library's own tests, parallel code generation, explicit stacks for patterns and runtime performance) settled in code, stating what the implementation does.

- **D-624 The REPL commands `:type`, `:module`, `:help` and `:quit`**: `:type expr` checks `expr` as the next entry `<repl:N>`, so it uses up an entry number and its diagnostics have a location of their own. It runs nothing and keeps nothing, so `:type 1 / 0` prints `1 / 0 : Int` and does not panic. It shows only errors, and on success it prints `<expr> : <type>`, the type of the final expression as diagnostics write types, `Never` included. Empty text or a declaration is the tool error "`:type` needs an expression, as in `:type 1 + 2`", and statements whose last one is not an expression, such as `:type let y = 1`, are "`:type` needs an expression, and the last statement here is not one". `:module path` enters a module of the package or of std, written as in an import, such as `bakery::oven`, `bakery` or `std::list`, and `:module` alone leaves it. Later entries are still modules of the local package (D-606), but they see the entered module's items, `priv` ones included, and its imports. These names rank as if the session had defined them when the module was entered, so a name the session defines later wins. Only checking changes: entries are checked against interfaces in which the entered module's `priv` items are package-visible. For a std module, every package-visible item of std becomes visible and every std type `exposed`, so the package boundary is that module's package. Code is still generated from the real interfaces. A definition entered inside a module is checked again inside it (D-609). The prompt stays `> `. A path that names no module is the tool error "there is no module `path`". `:help` prints the fixed text `cheby_repl::HELP`, which lists the commands, and `:quit` ends the session as the end of input does. An unknown command is the tool error "there is no command `:name`; `:help` lists the commands", and an argument given to `:quit`, `:help` or `:reload` is "`:name` takes no argument" (D-464). A command that succeeds prints nothing, except `:type`, `:help` and the report of `:reload` (D-625). Why: §13.5.3 and D-098 name the commands but not their output, numbering, scoping or errors. Checking `:type` as an entry gives its diagnostics a place of their own. Opening interfaces only for checking gives "as if written at the end of that module" without changing generated code or D-606's rule that entries are modules of the local package. Refines D-098, D-546.
- **D-625 What `:reload` does**: `:reload` runs package discovery again and compares each module file's text with the loaded version. Each module whose text changed, each new module, and each module of the package that imports one of them, transitively, is checked again as a new version, in import order, all or nothing. A module that imports one that failed is not checked, since its errors would only repeat. A new version's internal module path marks its last segment with the reload's generation, as in `store::prices<r1>`, which no import can name, so its symbols never collide with the old version's (D-547). The generation counts the `:reload` commands that found a change, and diagnostics show the original path. The reload is rejected when a module has a syntax or type error, when the reloaded modules import each other in a cycle (E0040), when discovery reports an error, or when there is any warning under `--deny-warnings` (D-549). A rejected reload reports its diagnostics and changes nothing, not even how values print: its versions leave the build and the table of displayed paths. When it succeeds, the REPL prints "reloaded `path`" for each new version in check order. Imports then reach the new versions, including the session's earlier imports and the module entered with `:module`. The old versions' types are stale (D-626), and E0054 is checked again over the current modules (D-475). Every current session definition that refers to any earlier version of a reloaded module is checked again from its text, in the order the definitions entered the session. This includes a definition that an earlier `:reload` broke, which still runs code compiled against an older version. Those that check are compiled again. Each error of the others gets the note "`f` was checked again because `:reload` changed a module it uses; it keeps its old definition", so a definition that stays broken is reported again at every later `:reload` of the module it uses, as D-609 reports a function's users at each redefinition. Discovery diagnostics, such as E0037 for a bad file name, are shown even when no module text changed, and an error among them refuses the reload. When no text changed and nothing refused it, `:reload` prints "no module changed". Outside a package it is the tool error "`:reload` reloads the modules of the package, and this session has no package", and a module file that cannot be read is a tool error that changes nothing. Two limits hold in M1: a module whose file was deleted stays loaded, and `cheby.toml` is not read again. `:reload` would stop detached fibers (D-194), but M1 has none. Why: §13.5.3, D-098 and D-547 say only that `:reload` recompiles changed modules with new generations. Session values and compiled code keep referring to the old items, so the old versions must stay. A marked path is D-547's generation for a whole module and needs no new kind of symbol. All or nothing matches D-607's rule that a rejected entry changes nothing, checking the users of every earlier version matches D-609 for a function redefined twice, and showing discovery diagnostics matches `cheby check`. Refines D-098, D-547. Extends D-609.
- **D-626 How stale types print**: A type is stale when a later entry redefined it, or redefined the interface of a `dyn` type, or when it belongs to a module of the package that `:reload` replaced (D-625). Wherever a stale type is written, in a value's type, in `:type` output and in diagnostics, ` (stale)` follows it, after its type arguments: `Shape (stale)`, `prices::Item<Int> (stale)`, `dyn Named (stale)`. A type that holds a stale type is not stale itself, as in `Holder<Shape (stale)>` and `List<prices::Item (stale)>`. Mixing an old type with its new version is an ordinary type error, such as "expected `List<prices::Item>`, found `List<prices::Item (stale)>`". Why: §13.5.5 says that a value whose type was replaced prints its type followed by ` (stale)`, but not how nested types or diagnostics show it. Old and new types share a name, so without the marker an error would read "expected `Shape`, found `Shape`". Refines D-463, D-547.
- **D-627 Session functions satisfy interfaces through forwarders**: D-610 is implemented with forwarders. When an entry defines a non-`priv` session function whose parameters mention a session type, stale types included, each such type's entry module gets a forwarder: a function with the same name, signature, visibility and targets that calls the session function through its slot, so §8.2's lookup in the type's declaring module finds it. Its symbol uses the session name `T::f` with a generation that no earlier item of that text has (D-611), as in `cb$fn$<repl>$Cat$show`. A redefinition with the same signature keeps the forwarder, since it took over the slot (D-607). Any other definition of the name in the value namespace, a `let` rebinding included, retires every forwarder of the name, so the type's module no longer finds it. A `priv` definition retires nothing, since it never becomes a session name (D-606). Types of the package and std never get forwarders. A definition that used a forwarder counts as using its function, so it is checked again when that function's name gets another signature (D-609). Why: §8.2 looks for a type's functions in the module that declares it, and each entry is a module of its own (D-606). Forwarders give that module the session's current functions without changing the checker or code generation, and calling through the slot keeps D-607's late binding for code checked earlier. Refines D-610, D-611.
- **D-628 REPL entries are checked for `native` whatever the package's targets**: Entries are checked for `native` only, also in a package whose targets in `cheby.toml` lack `native`. In a js-only package, the package's items that exist only on `js`, such as a function with only an `@external(js, …)`, are E0067 at the prompt ("`alert` exists only on `js`, but the entry runs on `native`"), while its portable items, std and the session's own definitions are usable (`tests/repl/js_targets.repl`). Why: D-606's intersection of the package's targets with `native` is empty for a js-only package, which would leave nothing to check against, and a `js`-only `@external` would then be compiled for the JIT. The session runs on the native JIT, and a package's targets narrow where its code is checked, not whether its items exist (D-190), so portable items run natively, as they do in tests. Refines D-606.
- **D-629 What REPL diagnostics call the entry and the session**: A statements entry is checked as the body of a wrapper test, and diagnostics call it "the entry", not `the test "<repl>"`, as in "`alert` exists only on `js`, but the entry runs on `native`". An entry module named on its own, "module `<repl:9>`" or "`<repl:9>`", is written "the session", as in "the session has no function `show(Cat) -> String`". Locations keep `<repl:N>:line:col` (D-546). Why: users never write the wrapper test or a module `<repl:9>`, and D-610 makes the prompt entries one module for satisfaction, so naming one entry would mislead. Refines D-546, D-606.
- **D-630 Std tests that need a module importing theirs**: A test of the stdlib spec for module `std::M` that needs a module `N` which imports `M` lives in `N`. It goes under the comment "Tests of `std::M` that need this module." and keeps the spec's name and body, with module prefixes adjusted. When two moved tests would share a name in `N`, each gets its module's name in front, as "int add is plus as a function" and "float add is plus as a function" in `std::list`. A test that needs `N` only to show or compare a builtin type goes to a module that already depends on that type's home module: `nil.md`'s "Nil shows as Nil", whose `"{done}"` shows a `Result`, lives in `std::result`. The driver test `stdlib_spec_tests_are_in_std` checks that every spec test of a shipped module is somewhere in `std/`, under its own or its prefixed name. Why: imports between std modules must be acyclic (D-145), satisfaction needs the home module as a dependency (D-489), and D-542 runs every spec test in the std self-test. Earlier waves already put the bool, float, int, order and string tests in `std::list` this way without a log entry. Refines D-479, D-542.
- **D-631 Helper items of std tests need a written use**: A helper item of a std test, such as a `priv` type, function or import, must be named by a written path in a test. User builds skip std tests (D-479) but still resolve their written paths, which count as uses (D-515, D-516), while a use through satisfaction does not count. So a helper used only through satisfaction is also named once: `std::json`'s test "list encodes each element with its type's to_json" also asserts `list(points) == array(points, to_json)`. Without the written use, `cheby check` of any user package that imports the module would report W0004 or W0003 in std, which the user cannot act on. Why: exempting the helpers of std tests from warnings would need a new rule in the warnings pass, and the extra assertion only checks what `json.md` already states. Refines D-479.
- **D-632 Parallel MIR lowering and code generation in a fixed order**: A build lowers, counts and checks the MIR of each needed module in parallel on rayon's global pool (D-622), and takes the resulting units in build order. Code generation then translates and compiles the functions of each batch in parallel on the same pool, collects the results in declaration order and defines the functions in that order. When several fail, the error reported is that of the first module or function in that order. CLIF dumps, the JIT image and every diagnostic are therefore the same for any number of threads, which `cargo xtask check-determinism` confirms on its 37 projects. Why: D-263 asks for parallel work per function, and D-264 for output independent of scheduling. Lowering reads only a module's THIR and the interfaces (D-246), and translation reads only its batch's MIR and declarations, so neither needs coordination. Checking and code generation went from about 1.1x to between 2.2x and 3.7x at 8 threads. Refines D-263, D-264.
- **D-633 No compiler pass recurses per element of a list pattern**: The usefulness search behind E0060, E0061 and W0010 keeps its pending calls on an explicit heap stack, in the order a recursion would make them, so D-508's step budget and the witnesses are unchanged. MIR's decision-tree compiler, its lowering to blocks and the freeing of trees use explicit stacks too, with occurrence interning and block order unchanged, so every MIR and CLIF dump is byte-identical. A list pattern of thousands of elements therefore needs no deep Rust stack: 3,000 elements check on rayon's default 2 MiB workers and lower on a 1 MiB stack. Hosts that never size the pool, such as the driver's tests, the in-process benchmark and a future LSP, handle such patterns on default stacks. The 256 MiB stacks of the command thread (D-601) and of the workers (D-622) stay, as a margin for the passes that still recurse over the source's structure, such as along chains (D-519). Why: D-601 called its stack a stopgap until the decision-tree builder uses an explicit stack. The checker recursed once per list element and overflowed default worker stacks at about 1,000 to 2,000 elements. A differential test against the old recursive search on 3,000 generated matrices shows that the result is unchanged. Refines D-601, D-622.
- **D-634 Owned list slices**: The runtime helpers for generated code gain `cheby_rt_list_slice_owned` `u64 (ctx*, u64 list, i64 start, i64 end)`. It consumes `list` and returns an owned slice, clamped as by `cheby_rt_list_slice` (D-298). It returns the list itself for the whole range, and changes the root in place when the list's count is one. MIR's RC pass (`RuntimeFn::ListSliceOwned`, printed `list_slice_owned`) emits it instead of `list_slice` followed by a drop of the list when the slice is the list's last use, as when `[x, ..rest]` matches a list that dies at the match. A recursive walk over `[x, ..rest]` then allocates nothing per step. What a program observes is unchanged. Why: allocating and freeing a slice root at each step dominated the runtime of 004's minimax tests, and with W5-D's other runtime fixes, 004's `cheby test` went from 9.4 s to 2.4 s. It applies D-525's in-place rule to D-053's O(1) slices. Extends D-520.
- **D-635 `CHEBY_RT_STATS` runtime counters**: Each accounting domain (D-564) counts allocations and the path each in-place operation took: list pushes and other root updates in place or copied, string appends in place, grown or copied, `String` and `Bytes` kernels in place, grown or copied, and map and set updates in place or copied. Counting is always on. With `CHEBY_RT_STATS=1`, read once per process, `cheby run` writes to stderr after `main` the line "runtime stats: main <time>, <n> entries <time>, peak <bytes> bytes", then one line per counter. `CHEBY_RT_POISON=0` turns off the poisoning that the runtime's own unit tests use by default, so they can run on the block cache. Both are developer tools, not program behavior, and their output is not a stable interface. Why: M1's performance done-when needs evidence that the examples hit the in-place paths (D-525) and what share of a run the runtime takes. No program can observe the counters. Extends D-564.
- **D-636 The help of E0074**: E0074's help offers two fixes, each enough on its own: make the mentioned type as visible as the item needs, or make the item as visible as the mentioned type. A `pub` function `reveal` that mentions a `priv` type `Secret` gets "make `Secret` `pub`, or make `reveal` `priv`", and one that mentions a package-visible `Shared` gets "make `Shared` `pub`, or make `reveal` package-visible". For the fields of a `pub exposed` type `Envelope`, the help is "make `Secret` `pub`, or make `Envelope` `priv`" when the field type is `priv`, because without `exposed` its fields still must not mention `priv` types (D-198), and "make `Shared` `pub`, or remove `exposed` from `Envelope`" when it is package-visible. For the fields of a type that is not `exposed`, it is "make `Secret` package-visible, or make `Envelope` `priv`". Why: the old help suggested making a `pub` function package-visible, which still breaks D-149 when the type is `priv`. Refines D-488.
- **D-637 Wording of three syntax-layer errors**: E0013 for a path with more than one module qualifier helps with the path as written. The segments before the first capitalized one are its modules, or all but the last when none is capitalized, and the help reads "import the inner module and qualify with its last name: `import std::io` and `io::println`". With fewer than two module segments it falls back to the fixed example `ops::Add`. `type Empty {}` is E0013 "a type declaration needs at least one variant", with the help "for a type without fields, declare a unit variant, such as `type Empty { Empty }`". In a subjectless `case`, a `_` arm before the last is reported once, as E0035 "`_` must be the last arm of a subjectless `case`" with the help "the arms after `_` can never be taken; remove them or move `_` last", and the missing final `_` is then not reported as well. Why: the old help showed `ops::Add` whatever was written, the old message spoke of fields while §4.3.1 speaks of variants, and one mistake should give one error. Refines D-437, D-439.

## 2026-10-11: M1 back end, wave 6

Goal: log the gaps that wave 6 of the M1 back end (the retirement of E0038 in MIR lowering, the `case` subject rules shared with tree-sitter-cheby and the REPL transcripts' share of decision coverage) settled in code, stating what the implementation does.

- **D-638 The decisions header of a REPL transcript**: A transcript `tests/repl/<name>.repl` may start with a header: every leading line that starts with `// @`, up to the first line that does not. Each header line is a directive `// @key: value`, as in a UI test's header (D-289). The only key is `decisions`, and its value lists the decision-log entries the session pins, as `D-NNN` with exactly three digits, separated by commas. A line without `:`, an unknown key or a malformed number is an error that fails the transcript in the harness and fails `cargo xtask coverage`. The header is not part of the session. The REPL harness (`tests/repl_harness/main.rs`) neither feeds it to `cheby repl` nor expects it as output, and `CHEBY_BLESS=1` writes it back unchanged before the new output. `cargo xtask coverage` counts its decisions as covered, as it counts a UI test's, and prints "D-number coverage: <n>/<m> decisions cited in the spec have a UI test or a REPL transcript". `cargo xtask cross-check` reads only the input lines of a transcript, so it ignores the header. The parser is shared as `cheby_test_support::repl`: `split(text) -> (&str, &str)` returns the header and the session, and `Header::parse` and `Header::of` return `Header { decisions: BTreeSet<u32> }` or a message. 23 of the 24 transcripts have a header. Why: some decisions, such as the REPL commands and the session's behavior (D-098, D-612, D-624), can be pinned only by a transcript, and coverage read only UI-test headers, so every REPL decision the spec cites stayed uncovered. A session's output always starts with the echo `> …` of its first input line, so a leading `// @` line cannot be output. Reusing the UI header keeps one convention. A separate metadata file per transcript was rejected as a second file to keep in sync. Gate 6 counted 122 of 436 cited decisions uncovered, against 135 of 428 at gate 5. Extends D-292, D-546.
- **D-639 No `case` subject may start with `{`, and subjects take no trailing comma**: Every subject of a `case` follows A.5's `subject_expr`, not only the first: none may start with `{`, and an `UPPER` path followed by `{` ends it, also in an operand, a call or a field access outside brackets (§5.9.6). A `{` right after `case` still starts a subjectless `case`, and a block later inside a subject, as in `case a + { 1 } { … }`, stays valid. After a `,` between subjects, a `{` is a block subject or the arms, and `case_expr` in `cheby_syntax` tells them apart by brace matching. It finds the `{`'s matching `}`, counting braces and ignoring the grammar, and looks at the first token after it that is not trivia, where a line break counts as a token (`Parser::brace_group_continues_line`). When that token is anything but a line break, the end of input, `}`, `)` or `]`, the `{` is a block subject: E0013 "a `case` subject cannot start with `{`" at the `{`, with the help "put the block in parentheses, such as `({ … })`". The block is still parsed as the subject, so `case n, { 1 } { … }` reports that one error. Otherwise, and when no `}` closes it, the `{` opens the arms: E0013 "expected a `case` subject after `,`" at the comma, with the help "remove the trailing comma". The arms are parsed as the body, so `case n, m, {` reports that one error. A record or a block as the first subject, as in `case Point { x: 0, y: 1 } { … }` and `case { 1 } { … }`, still gets generic E0013 errors. tree-sitter-cheby 1d891ef, pinned in `tests/tree-sitter-cheby.rev`, rejects a record literal or a block as an unparenthesized subject, first or later, so the parse-fail UI tests `block_subject_unparenthesized`, `record_subject_unparenthesized`, `later_subject_block` and `trailing_subject_comma` in `tests/ui/05-expressions/case/` pass the cross-check. Why: Appendix A, which wins over the chapters, already restricts every subject, but the compiler checked only the first, and §5.9.6's prose named only a `{` right after `case`. A.5's `subjects` has no trailing comma, and D-107 allows trailing commas only in arguments, list items, fields and variants. Since `cheby fmt` adds trailing commas elsewhere, the typo is likely, and it used to get the parenthesize help plus four follow-on errors. Matching the brace is needed because a `{` after a `,` could start either form. Refines D-052, D-107.

- ~~The Windows CI jobs still run the JIT and runtime unit tests, the driver tests that compile and run examples, and the check-pass UI tests that evaluate constants through the JIT. None has run on Windows, so D-545 is unverified for them. W7-A or the user decides whether to gate them with `cfg(not(windows))`.~~ → D-641.
- Optional: a dedicated "parenthesize the record" help for the two first-subject shapes of §5.9.6, which get generic E0013 errors (D-639).
