## 2026-10-16: M2 checkpoint 4b

Goal: record the user's answers at M2's checkpoint 4b: the decisions its packages proposed for reuse, fusion and the reuse report, the naive-counting variant, the tier-2 collections and their readings, and OQ-P13's factor.

- **D-838 Reuse tokens**: A reuse token is an ordinary counted local made by `t = drop_reuse x of L`. On a unique `x` it releases and zeroes `x`'s value fields, so the token is a childless block of count one; otherwise it gives up one reference and the token is 0. A shared or static object never reuses. `y = alloc_reuse t, L'(fields)` writes `L'` over a nonzero token, or allocates as `alloc` does, with its unwind edge, when the token is 0. Any other path, pads included, releases the token with a plain `drop`. The reuse size is exact and computed once in MIR; code generation fails with an internal error if its own size differs, and in poison mode the runtime checks both objects' sizes. The validator checks that the forms appear only after counting, that a token is used only by `alloc_reuse` or `drop`, that the two sizes are equal, that only constructors with fields and tuples take part, and that the source is neither borrowed nor a tuple being filled. Naive counting has no reuse. Why: a zeroed, childless token needs no new pad shape and no special case in the ownership simulation; one size function and the checked path guard against freeing with a wrong layout. Refines D-716, D-698, D-808 (naive counting is the MIR before borrowing and reuse, not only before borrowing).
- **D-839 What reuse pairs**: A source is a `drop x`, in a normal block, of a counted local that is not borrowed, not a constant's value and not a tuple being filled, whose constructor `L` a field read of `x` proves, in the same block or in a dominating one. A read proves `L` only when it pins the constructor: `L` is a tuple; or `L` is its type's only constructor, read from the type's interface (D-246); or the read is in a block dominated by an arm of a `switch` on `x`'s tag that exactly one tag, `L`'s, enters and that has no other predecessor. A field that every constructor holds at the same place is read once without a tag test (D-071), and constructors that hold a field alike share one arm of the tag's switch; neither read proves a layout. A sink is an allocation of a constructor with fields or a tuple of the source's size. In the source's block each sink takes the nearest unpaired source of its size before it. A source still unpaired at the end of its block holds its token into the blocks that only its block enters; on each path the first allocation of its size takes it, and a path that reaches none drops the token where it leaves those blocks, in a new block when that splits an edge into a join. The `drop` itself never moves. Why: counting drops a matched value in the block that reads its fields, before the inner `case`, while the rebuilds sit in the arms: with pairing inside one block, `std::sorted_map`'s inserts made 568,432 allocations and 0 hits; with the tokens held, 42,528 allocations and 525,904 hits. Holding the token is Perceus's own form; moving the drop into the arms would move a drop later (D-716, D-851). The proof must pin the constructor: with D-071's shared read as proof, a 24-byte object was rebuilt as a 40-byte one, a heap overflow that only poisoning caught. Refines D-716. D-071 is unchanged; its shared read proves no layout.
- **D-840 Large tuples and reuse**: A tuple filled field by field (D-810) never takes a token, and a tuple being filled is never a reuse source. Why: D-810 says reuse must know the form; its fields are written after the allocation, and it is not finished while they are computed. Refines D-810, D-716.
- **D-841 Parameters that reuse needs stay owned**: A parameter whose `drop` would pair with an allocation for reuse, within its block or across blocks (D-839), counts as consumed, so it is not borrowed. Borrowing inference and the reuse pass share one predicate. Why: a borrowed parameter is never dropped, so it can never be reused; D-806's list of consuming uses would otherwise borrow a record update's base and lose the update in place. Masks, and with them ABI signatures and slot keys, change accordingly. Refines D-806, and D-715 through it.
- **D-842 Reuse counters**: `CHEBY_RT_STATS` prints `reuse hits` and `reuse misses`, counted on demand. A miss is counted by its own allocation entry, which costs what an allocation costs. A hit makes no call: generated code loads the counting flag and calls out of line only while counting is on or not yet settled. Why: D-768 counts only on demand, and one load and one predictable branch per hit keeps the cost off the RC benchmarks. Checkpoint 4b's quiet window could not separate that cost (no build without the load exists); D-760 holds with it, with a margin of 3.3 points. Refines D-768, D-635.
- **D-843 The RC column counts fused forms once**: `bench-compile`'s RC column counts `dup`, `drop`, `drop_reuse` and drops specialized by constructor outside cleanup pads, one operation each whatever its keep list holds; `alloc_reuse` is an allocation. Until checkpoint 4 the column also counted pad drops, against D-759's text (4961 against 2642 on the 10k `small` shape), so its numbers since D-759 include them. Why: the column tracks what Perceus changes on normal paths, and counting a keep list's release once is what shows fusion (2142 from 2642 on `small`). Refines D-759.
- **D-844 The differential oracle covers reuse**: `rc_differential` requires at least 25 of its 40 programs to have a `drop_reuse`, keeps the borrowing floor of 30, runs Perceus a third time with `CHEBY_RT_POISON=0`, compares the three runs, and asserts `reuse hits` above 0 in a 5-seed stats run. The fuzz target shares the third run. Every generated program declares a sum type whose variants share a `String` field at the same place and differ in size, and some of its rebuilds read that field and build another variant from it. Why: the floors keep the oracle from being vacuous, only runs with poison off execute the inline reuse paths, and the shared-field type is the shape of fix review TOK-1, which the generator never made before. A program below a floor extends the generator, never lowers the floor. Refines D-809, and D-718 and D-781 through it. Relies on D-716.
- **D-845 The sorted modules' hooks and helpers carry no bound**: D-385's `Compare` bound binds the sorted modules' public API. Their hooks (`equal`, `hash`, `debug`) and `sorted_map`'s package-visible helpers `entries`, `fold_entries` and `count_entries` carry no bound and never call `compare`. Why: D-482 forbids bounds on hooks, and the hooks of `sorted_set` and `sorted_multimap`, and the ordered family's walks, need the entries without a `Compare` value. Refines D-385. Applies D-482 and D-342 unchanged.
- **D-846 A multimap insert costs O(log n + log m)**: `insert` in `sorted_multimap` and `ordered_multimap` costs O(log n + log m), m the number of values of the key, as `multimap::insert` states: one descent of the key structure and one push onto the key's list. Why: both store a `List<V>` per key (D-338); O(log n) alone would need another representation of the values, which the pages do not ask for. Refines D-300, D-338.
- **D-847 `multimap`'s debug text sorts keys by their text**: `multimap`'s debug text is `multimap::from_list([...])` with one pair per value: the keys sorted by their debug text compared as UTF-8 bytes, each key's pairs in insertion order, and keys with the same debug text ordered by their pairs' text. Why: D-344 and D-587 require text that is the same on every run and rebuilds the value; a hashed map has no order to print, and keys need not be `Compare`. Refines D-344, D-382, D-587 (for `multimap`). D-299 applies unchanged.
- **D-848 Ordered walks look values up**: `ordered_map`'s `to_list`, `values`, `fold`, `each` and `map_values` walk the insertion order and look each key's value up in the hashed index: n lookups, each O(log n) and effectively constant, so a walk is O(n) in the sense of `Map`'s costs. Why: the representation `{ index: Map<K, (Int, V)>, order: SortedMap<Int, K>, next: Int }` keeps a present key's place (D-340) with one lookup per update; storing the values in `order` too would double every update's work. Refines D-300 (how a required O(n) walk over a hashed index is read), D-331 (the representation it leaves "hashed like Map"). D-336 unchanged.
- **D-849 A copied text is sized by its length**: When a `std::string` or `Bytes` kernel cannot write in place, the new text's capacity is max(needed, 2 × capacity) when the argument was unique and full, as D-577 says, and max(needed, 2 × length) when the argument is copied because it is shared or static, or under D-563's alias rule. Why: a text that stays shared, such as a record's string field updated through a helper, is copied at every append; under D-577's rule its capacity doubled at every step while its length grew by one byte, and the program ran out of memory after about 47 appends. Amortized appends need the doubling only on the unique path. Programs cannot observe capacity. Refines D-577.
- **D-850 Fusion, specialized drops and constant propagation**: Under Perceus only, a pass `const_prop` runs before counting and a pass `fuse` after reuse and before the unwind pass. Sinking: the `dup` that counting puts after a projection `p = field b.i` moves to the first statement that consumes `p` or releases `b` or a base up `p`'s chain; across a branch it moves into each successor when each has one predecessor, else it stops before the terminator. No drop moves. Cancelling: a `dup x` followed by `drop x`, with only releases of other locals and `dup`s that do not need `x` between them, cancel. Keep lists: the `dup`s of projections of `b` right before a `drop_reuse b` become its keep list; before a `drop b` they make a drop specialized by constructor, `drop b of L keep [...]`, only when a read pins `b`'s constructor (D-839), only with a non-empty list, never in a pad and never for a token, a constant's value or a tuple being filled. On a unique `b` the kept fields stay and the others are released, and a specialized drop frees the block; on a shared `b` each kept field is `dup`'d and the ordinary release runs. Constants: a local defined as a static constant becomes the constant operand before counting, so it is never counted. Naive counting turns both passes off. Why: D-716 asks for fusion and specialized drops; sinking makes a `dup` meet the release it cancels or folds into, a keep list saves a `dup` and a decrement on a unique base, and a static constant's count never changes. Reuse decisions are the same with fusion on and off on every example; bench-compile's `small` shape has 4.8% less code and 19% fewer RC operations. Known cost: sinking also moves `dup`s that end in neither a cancel nor a keep list; in a function with about a thousand or more projections of one live base they pile up at the base's release and register allocation slows, to about twice as long at 4000 projections (7.29 s against 3.75 s without fusion; 1.47x at 2000). Bounding the sinking is an Open item. Refines D-716, D-262 (pads are built after the final counts), D-808 (naive counting also excludes fusion and constant propagation), D-205 (a projection whose `dup` is sunk). Extends D-838 (a keep list on `drop_reuse`); uses D-839's pinning rule. Relies on D-454 and D-539.
- **D-851 Drops stay at last use**: In debug builds, under the full ownership check, the validator checks that between a counted local's last use and each statement that releases it, nothing runs but `dup`, `drop`, `drop_reuse`, specialized drops and block entry. A `dup` or a release is not a use. A reuse token's own `drop` and cleanup pads are exempt. Why: D-716 requires that fusion and specialization never move a drop of a value that may hold a handle later (ADR-0024, D-205); checking every counted value needs no type facts, and naive placement already satisfies it. A token holds no reference, and D-839 holds it across calls. The user chose it. Refines D-716 (stricter: every counted value, not only those that may hold a handle), D-205, D-774 (one more debug-only check with the full simulation), D-839 (a token's late drop is exempt).
- **D-852 The reuse report's lines**: `cheby run --reuse-report` and `cheby test --reuse-report` write to standard error, after the warnings and before anything runs, lines of the form `reuse: <file>:<line>:<col>: <text>`, sorted by file, line, column and text. The candidates are the allocations of a constructor with fields or a tuple in the package's own modules, never std or a dependency, located at the expression that builds them. Five reasons: it reuses a named value when that is unique; it does not, because the value is used again later or released elsewhere, or is borrowed from the caller, or is a constant; or it allocates because no value of the same size dies before it. A kernel line (`` `set::insert` gets `held` at its last use: in place when it is unique at run time ``) is printed for a call of one of the 25 std kernels that update their first argument in place, when that argument is a local at its last use, not borrowed, not passed twice, not a constant on every path, and held at the call by no other live value of the function. The holders come from a may-hold analysis to a fixed point over the function's control flow: aliases, projection bases, list elements and views, values built from the argument, call results whose type may contain the argument's type (read through the interfaces), and block parameters that every jump passes a holder. A kernel line is a claim about one function: a caller's or a callee's holder is not seen. A kernel call in tail position gets no line, and the lines of a pipe chain share its start column. Under `CHEBY_RC=naive` the report is the one line `reuse: reuse is off (CHEBY_RC=naive)`. A test build may report differently from a run build (D-779, D-813). Code generation never reads the report's sites, so MIR and CLIF are the same with and without the flag. Why: the user's answer; sorted lines are testable (D-717). A literal "no `dup` before the call" rule dropped lines the runtime honours (013: 62 updates in place, 0 copied); a last-use rule printed lines for values another value holds (027: 0 in place, 17 copied); the fix review's five holder shapes needed one analysis, not five patches. Refines D-717, and D-170 through it. Applies D-525, D-779, D-813, D-205, D-053. Consistent with D-246; D-048 unchanged.
- **D-853 The naive-counting variant**: Every run and `::test` trial of an example also runs as `<name>@rc=naive` with `CHEBY_RC=naive`, the base trial's goldens and milestone, at the default thread count. Every other trial removes `CHEBY_RC` from the child's environment. The UI harness removes `CHEBY_RC` for a test whose flags include `--reuse-report`, and every other UI test inherits it, so `CHEBY_RC=naive cargo test -p cheby_cli --test ui` checks the suite under naive counting. A CI job `rc-naive` runs that command and `CHEBY_RC=naive cargo xtask test-std`. `cargo xtask bless` ignores the variants. Why: D-718 asks for a conformance variant and a CI job; one variant keeps the trial count bounded, and removing `CHEBY_RC` elsewhere keeps the other trials' counting fixed. Refines D-718, D-747 (and D-722 through it: a new variant name and a new step in the child's environment). Applies D-746.
- **D-854 The collections' naive reading**: D-664's first reading of the seven collections is the final checkpoint-4b tree's `collections` benchmark group under `CHEBY_RC=naive` (no borrowing, reuse or fusion, D-808), not M1's binary, which cannot run the new modules. It is committed as `bench/runtime/baseline-collections.json`, the reference for M6's second reading (D-336). OQ-P13 is settled at checkpoint 4b's review, not at M2's exit. `cargo xtask bench-run` gains `--rust` and `--rust-bin`, which run four Rust variants (`rc`, `btree`, `hash-btree`, `hash-vec`) and fail when a checksum or size differs. Why: naive counting of the same binary is the closest stand-in for M1's counting. On checkpoint 4b's quiet machine Perceus is faster than naive on all seven, by 1.03x (multimap) to 1.63x (sorted_set). Refines D-664 (when and against what the naive reading is taken, and when OQ-P13 is settled), D-718 (this baseline is not recorded before Perceus and unwinding), D-336.
- **D-855 OQ-P13's factor**: A standard-library collection written in Cheby stays in Cheby while, under Perceus on native, it takes at most F times the time of a persistent Rust tree with the same algorithm (`rc` in `bench/baselines`), measured by `cargo xtask bench-run --group collections --rust` at each program's default size; a slower one moves to a runtime kernel (D-336). The same factor decides M6's review of the kernels (D-274): a kernel whose Cheby version is within F of it moves to Cheby. F is 1.5, the user's answer. Why: on checkpoint 4b's quiet machine every collection is faster than its persistent Rust variant under Perceus (0.48 to 0.96, geometric mean 0.67), so none moves; under naive counting the sorted family reads 1.21 to 1.56. The mutable Rust variants are 4.69 to 10.46 times faster, a gap a persistent collection keeps (D-039), so they measure persistence, not the compiler. For the ordered family and multimap the Rust variant replaces the runtime's hashed index with the same tree, so their factors compare different structures. Settles OQ-P13. Refines D-336 (the agreed factor and what it is read against), D-274 (the cost its review accepts), D-664.

## 2026-10-17: M2 H1 checkpoint

Goal: record the user's answers to OQ-10-11 and OQ-13-8 and to H1's plan before H1's code: how a deadlock is detected and reported, the sanitizer evidence before the push, the slot cache's keep rule, and the bound on fusion's sinking.

- **D-856 Where a deadlock report finds a fiber's location**: For each blocked fiber, the deadlock report gives the location of the innermost call on the fiber's stack whose function is not defined in package `std`, found by mapping that frame's return address to the call's source location through a table the JIT records per call site. A call's location is the start of the call expression, where the reuse report locates it (D-852): the callee's path for `f(x)`, the start of the chain for a pipe, and the `use` for a `use` statement. A function defined in `std` counts as std also where a user unit instantiates it. The search passes through std frames and Cheby-ABI shims, such as the value shim of a function used as a value; C-ABI code (entry thunks, the runtime's closure trampolines, `@blocking` shims) and Rust code end it, and the line then says `<unknown location>`. A tail call replaces its caller's frame (D-015), so a wait reached through tail calls is reported at the innermost call still on the stack (D-861). Panics raised by std operations keep `<unknown location>` (D-732 is unchanged). Why: the user's answer to OQ-10-11; a table recorded per call site costs no instruction at the call and no argument in any kernel, while caller locations passed to every waiting operation would cost both; the reuse report already computes each call's site. Settles OQ-10-11. Refines D-724.
- **D-857 Deadlocks are judged per accounting domain**: Every host judges a deadlock per accounting domain (D-691). `cheby run` judges `main`'s domain, which holds every fiber of the program, so its rule is §10.4's; `cheby test` judges each test's domain (D-833); the REPL judges the session's domain, whose root is the running entry and whose fibers include the detached fibers of earlier entries. A domain is deadlocked when its root is registered and has not finished, every fiber of the domain is parked and none waits on the blocking pool, no timer armed by a fiber of the domain is pending, no blocking-pool job of the domain is pending, and no IO registration of the domain exists. In the REPL, the fibers of a stuck group, a group whose teardown at `:reload` skipped members blocked in a system call or on the blocking pool and which the session keeps until its end (D-827), are left out: they are not surveyed or listed, they do not count as running, and their pending blocking-pool jobs do not count, so an entry that deadlocks later is reported while they stay blocked. A timer is pending from its arming until its firing has woken or cancelled what it serves; a pool job from its submission until its completion has woken its fiber; a span past the clock's range arms nothing. Only the deadlocked test fails; tests still running or not yet started go on. Why: the user's answer to OQ-13-8. Domains are closed under wakes: tests share no data (D-004), constants reach no fiber operation (E0077), and hosts wake fibers only in teardowns. A pending count that fell before its wake would let a firing timer look like a deadlock. Stuck groups are left out because a call that never returns would otherwise keep detection off for the rest of the session (the user's answer to H1's plan, UQ-12); the cost is that a stuck fiber whose call does return later still unwinds, and can wake a later entry's fiber through a value a session value shares, such as a channel's last `Sender`, after that entry was reported. Settles OQ-13-8. Refines D-724, D-691, D-723 ([ADR-0062](../adr/0062-parallel-tests-with-ordered-output.md)), D-833, D-682, D-712.
- **D-858 The deadlock report's text**: The report starts with `fatal error: all fibers are blocked`, followed by one line per blocked fiber of the domain, `  blocked in <operation> at <location>`. The operation is `channel::send`, `channel::receive`, `selector::select`, `fiber::join`, `fiber::scope` for the end of a scope, or `fiber::timeout` for the end of a timed scope. The location is `<path>:<line>:<column>`, as in panic reports (D-449), or `<unknown location>`. Lines are sorted by path compared as bytes, then by line and column as numbers, then by operation; `<unknown location>` lines come last; equal lines are all kept, and there is no cap. Why: the order depends only on the set of waits, never on fiber ids, workers or timing (D-264, D-722); the names are those a program calls (D-174). Refines D-724, D-449.
- **D-859 What each host does on a deadlock**: `cheby run` fixes status 103 first, flushes standard output, writes the report to standard error and halts with nothing unwinding (D-708); a write failure after that changes nothing (D-785, D-346), and no `CHEBY_RT_STATS` report and no leak check follow, as after a termination. Every root (`main`, a test, a REPL entry) has a parentless base scope of its own, the root scope of §13.5.4 and §13.6, as D-798 gives detached fibers. In `cheby test` the runtime cancels the deadlocked test's base scope: the test's fibers unwind, and the test fails with the report as its failure block and no leak check, as every failing test (D-541); its detached fibers are torn down as at every test's end. In the REPL only the entry's base scope is cancelled: the session's detached fibers keep waiting and are listed in the report, except those of stuck groups, which detection leaves out (D-857), the entry is reverted as a panicked one, the report goes to standard error after standard output is flushed (D-548), and the session continues. A cancellation that reaches a root's catch point is legal only for a deadlocked root. Why: D-724 asks `cheby test` and the REPL to cancel and unwind the stuck fibers, and a root without a scope gives a cancellation no target; the REPL's detached fibers belong to the session until `:reload` or its end (D-712). Refines D-724, D-708, D-709, D-785, D-346, D-723 ([ADR-0062](../adr/0062-parallel-tests-with-ordered-output.md)), D-541, D-712, D-548. Extends D-798.
- **D-860 How the runtime detects a deadlock**: The runtime's monitor thread judges a domain by surveying its fibers twice, from its root's base scope and from its detached groups, except the REPL's stuck groups (D-857), which a teardown that skips members marks. The domain is deadlocked when both passes find the same fibers, each parked outside the blocking pool with the same resume count, and the domain's pending timer and pool counts read zero after each pass. D-857's condition on IO registrations holds trivially while the poller registers no IO for fibers (M2, D-680); when it does, a per-domain count of registrations joins the pending counts. A survey is skipped while a pending count is not zero or the root is not parked. The report's lines are built only after that confirmation, each under its fiber's scope lock, and the report is given up if a fiber's state or resume count changed. Each resume stores the fiber's count with a relaxed store; the pending counts change only when a timer is armed, fired or cancelled, when a pool job is submitted or completed, and when a teardown marks a group stuck, which removes its members' pending pool jobs from the count once. The monitor surveys at once when the last worker parks, and otherwise on its ticks for each candidate domain none of whose fibers runs on a worker, backing off from 10 ms to 160 ms after a failed survey, so a deadlocked test among running ones is found within about 200 ms. Runtime unit tests whose host wakes fibers turn detection off with a test-only setting; every shipped host has it on. Why: one relaxed store per resume on a line the resume already writes, against an atomic read-modify-write on a line the whole domain shares at every park and wake, whose cost the user weighed against counters before (D-768); an exact per-domain counter stays the fallback if the survey's cost or latency fails its reading. Refines D-724.
- **D-861 Tail-called waits keep their caller's frame**: A tail call from a function outside `std` to `channel::send`, `channel::receive` or `fiber::join` must be compiled as a call followed by a return, so the caller's frame stays on the stack and gives a deadlock report its location. `channel::send` and `channel::receive` are `@external`, which §12.8 already leaves outside the guarantee; for them the rule says that the user's call to their Cheby-ABI wrapper is §12.8's ordinary call, not only the wrapper's own foreign call. For `fiber::join` it is an exception to §5.15.3's guarantee (D-015), the only one besides calls to `@external` functions. These three never call a function value, so the change adds at most one frame per waiting fiber, and no loop can grow the stack through them. `selector::select` keeps its tail call, since it calls the chosen arm's function in tail position and select-driven loops rely on that. Why: one-liners such as `fn() { channel::receive(rx) }` passed to `fiber::spawn`, 20 of them in the tree, would otherwise report `<unknown location>`. Refines D-015 ([ADR-0006](../adr/0006-guaranteed-general-tail-calls.md)).
- **D-862 Sanitizer evidence before the push**: Until the `runtime` branch is pushed, the evidence for D-721's criterion is a local run of the nightly workflow's job script, on its pinned nightly, for ThreadSanitizer and AddressSanitizer in a `linux/arm64` container and for the Miri subset on macOS arm64. An emulated x86-64 AddressSanitizer run is informative; x86-64 ThreadSanitizer, which Docker's emulation cannot start, is first checked by the scheduled run after the push. The M2 exit report names the platform and says that JIT code is not instrumented. Why: agents never push, and D-781 set the same rule for the fuzz runs. Refines D-721, D-781.
- **D-863 Sanitizer reports outside Cheby's code**: A sanitizer report whose racing or faulting accesses (the top frames of each access) lie in a dependency (crossbeam, the precompiled parts of Rust's std, loom's generator) gets one line in a suppression file next to the workflow, with its reason; a report in Cheby's own crates is fixed, never suppressed. A report caused by behavior a logged decision makes intentional, such as objects held by fibers that a halt freezes (D-708, D-859), is answered by telling the sanitizer about it (for example registering the stack slots' regions with LeakSanitizer) or by a per-step option the workflow's header names, never by a suppression. The `loom` models are skipped under the sanitizers and Miri, since loom switches stacks on its own. An example that fails under ThreadSanitizer only because its own timeouts expire under the slowdown runs under AddressSanitizer only, and the workflow's header says so. Why: a suppression in Cheby's code would hide the bugs the jobs exist to find; examples never change to make a timing test pass (D-725). Refines D-721, D-725, D-708.
- **D-864 The stack-slot cache stays unless it is slower**: H1's quiet window times spawn_join at 1, 2 and 4 threads on the final tree with D-825's cache and with a build whose cache holds no slot, in 5 interleaved rounds with a layout control. The cache is removed only if, at some thread count, it is slower than the build without it by more than the layout control's largest difference in at least 4 of the 5 rounds; otherwise D-825 stands. Why: D-825's condition was "not slower", and a cache of 4 slots per worker costs little memory. Refines D-825.
- **D-865 Fusion sinks at most 8 dups to a point**: Sinking (D-850) moves at most 8 `dup`s to one point that neither cancel nor fold there. A `dup` cancels at a `drop` of its projection, and folds when the point releases its direct base and it is the last arrival of its layout and field. A move that would exceed the cap at any of its points is not made, and its `dup` stays after the projection's definition. Why: a pile of `dup`s at one release ran with every projection live, and register allocation took 1.47x as long at 2000 projections and 1.94x at 4000 (ROB7-2); the longest run of `dup`s in the examples, std, the RC programs and `bench-compile`'s shapes is 4, so the cap changes none of their code. Sinking only to a cancel or a fold was rejected: it moves `dup`s back in ordinary functions and gives up part of fusion's smaller pads and code. Refines D-850.
- **D-866 The inline reuse paths keep their compile cost**: In a function with thousands of reuse sites, the inline reuse forms of D-838 add about 30% to compile time, in proportion to their code (33% more CLIF blocks in a match of 8000 arms). M2 keeps them: moving the miss path out of line costs run time where `record_updates` keeps 3.3 points of D-760's margin, and dropping the hit counter breaks D-842. The lever is splitting long functions (the open item beside D-780). Why: projects pay 0.5% to 0.8% of build throughput for checkpoint 4b's Perceus packages (P2 to P4, against checkpoint 4a's tree), these paths included (the item struck beside D-776). Refines D-838, D-776.

- ~~spawn_join's verdict under D-864 and ROB7-2's quiet reading under D-865 strike the checkpoint-4b items when H1's window has run.~~ Done: both struck with the window's numbers.

## 2026-10-18: M2 H1 checkpoint report

Goal: record the user's answers to H1's checkpoint report: the decisions H1's packages proposed for the deadlock report's locations, fusion's tests, the runtime's tests on Windows and under the sanitizers, the sanitizer and Miri jobs, and the serial group, and the readings of H1's quiet window.

- **D-867 The call-site map's lifetime**: One process-wide map from code addresses to call locations sits behind a leaf lock. It holds sorted, non-overlapping ranges, one 16-byte record per call site (return offset, path, line, column), and each path interned once per process. A JIT takes a fresh owner id, registers a batch's functions only after the whole batch is finalized (a failed batch registers nothing), and unregisters right before its code memory is freed. A REPL redefinition keeps the old ranges, so a fiber still in old code is located. Why: D-856 needs a table per call site that the detector reads only after a confirmation; registering by owner follows the code's own lifetime (D-708 keeps frozen code alive, D-712's session keeps old code), and a leaf lock keeps the lock order. Refines D-708, D-712. Applies D-856.
- **D-868 The control block's wait-frame word**: The control block's generated-code view gains a word at offset 16: the frame pointer of the std function that calls a `@std_context` kernel or `scope_close`, stored right before that call and nowhere else. The runtime's part of the block moves from offset 16 to 24. A waiting kernel copies the word into its fiber's wait site before it parks. Why: the walk must start at a frame record that generated code published, and D-856 adds no argument to any kernel; one store before a waiting call names that frame. Refines D-685, D-692, D-693. Applies D-856.
- **D-869 CLIF dumps leave out the call-site tags**: The CLIF text dump strips the calls' location tags, so the dumps change only by the wait-frame stores and D-861's call-then-return. Why: the tags are metadata for pairing return offsets, not code. Refines D-551.
- **D-870 Locations when std's own tests run**: When std's own tests run (`cargo xtask test-std`), the calls in std's `test` blocks and their closures are located like user code, so a deadlocking std test names the line of its wait. Std's other functions stay std, and their frames are passed through. A user build never lowers std's tests, so this costs nothing there. Why: under D-856 alone every std test's report would say `<unknown location>`; locating every std function would record a table for each of them in every build. Refines D-856, D-837.
- **D-871 The generator's projection runs**: The program generator draws, last of all its choices, a projection-run shape in 60% of programs: a function that reads 9 to 14 projections of one boxed field type and consumes them after the record's last use, in 40% of cases in one arm of a `case` only. `rc_differential` requires at least 10 of its 40 programs to hold one, beside D-844's floors. Why: only such runs reach D-865's cap, and the differential test and the fuzz run (D-781) must reach it; drawing the shape last keeps every existing seed's other choices. Refines D-809, D-844. Supports D-781.
- **D-872 The runtime tests' waits scale with `CHEBY_HARNESS_SCALE`**: Every bound a runtime unit test puts on how long something may take goes through a test helper that multiplies it by `CHEBY_HARNESS_SCALE` (1 to 100, as the harnesses read it; unset, empty or invalid is 1 here, while the harnesses reject an invalid value themselves): a wait after which an outcome counts as missing, a deadline after which a test gives up, and an asserted upper bound on an elapsed time. The hang bound is 60 s scaled. Waits that assert that nothing arrives, and far deadlines a test arms but never waits for, keep their literals. A test scans the crate for unscaled forms. The sanitizer jobs set `CHEBY_RT_TEST_EMULATED=1` and `CHEBY_HARNESS_SCALE=10`. Why: D-748's scale covered only the harnesses, so the runtime's own tests timed out under the sanitizers' slowdown (D-721) and under emulation, while D-725 keeps time assertions as lower bounds. Refines D-725, D-722, D-721. Extends D-748.
- **D-873 The private-pool tests are ignored on Windows**: The runtime's tests that run the blocking pool alone carry the Windows ignore, so D-714's rule reads as testing.md words it: tests that switch stacks or run a scheduler, a poller or the blocking pool. Why: the pool's Windows path has never run, and the rest of its tests and models are already gated. Refines D-714, D-641.
- **D-874 Tests Windows does not build**: D-641's list of tests Windows does not build gains `cheby_cli`'s `blocking_io`, `closed_streams`, `repl_runtime` and `test_parallel` test files and `cheby_driver`'s runner and stuck-test modules and its FIFO test; `cheby_jit`'s `start_root_tests` is built and its test ignored. `ci.yml`'s header states the list and D-714's extension to the runtime. Why: these tests need Unix pipes, a shell or FIFOs. Refines D-641, D-714.
- **D-875 The sanitizer cfg**: A sanitizer build passes `-Zsanitizer=<s>` together with a custom `--cfg cheby_sanitize="<s>"` (`address` or `thread`), declared in the workspace's `check-cfg`; not a cargo feature and not the unstable `cfg(sanitize)`. Without the cfg every hook is an inline no-op. Why: a feature is unified across the workspace and could reach a plain build, and `cfg(sanitize)` exists only on nightly, while the hooks must lint on stable. Refines D-721.
- **D-876 The fiber switches told to the sanitizers**: The scheduler's resume and park switches tell AddressSanitizer (`__sanitizer_start_switch_fiber` and `__sanitizer_finish_switch_fiber`, with a fake stack per fiber freed when the fiber retires, not at its last switch) and ThreadSanitizer (fiber create, switch with flags 0, destroy). Why: without the calls both sanitizers misread every switch between D-676's stacks; the worker still reads an ending fiber's last action from its fake stack; a fiber frozen by D-708 is never destroyed. Refines D-676, D-721. Applies D-708.
- **D-877 Reused stack slots are unpoisoned**: Under the address cfg, a slot is unpoisoned below its top before a new fiber's first frame is written. Why: a reused slot (D-677) may still hold the previous fiber's redzones. Refines D-677, D-721.
- **D-878 The stack position under AddressSanitizer**: Under the address cfg, the overflow check reads the stack pointer with inline assembly (aarch64 and x86-64, not Windows) instead of a local's address. Why: `detect_stack_use_after_return` moves locals to a fake stack, which would make every check fail. Refines D-721.
- **D-879 The shared count's acquire under ThreadSanitizer**: Under the thread cfg, the acquire after a shared count's last decrement is an Acquire load of the count, not an Acquire fence; the scheduler's SeqCst fences stay, and no run reported them. Why: ThreadSanitizer does not model standalone fences, so the fence form reports a false race on every freed shared object. Refines D-721.
- **D-880 Cached blocks are poisoned under AddressSanitizer**: Under the address cfg, the allocator's cached blocks are poisoned while cached and unpoisoned when handed out again; the quarantine is not poisoned. Why: cached blocks never return to the system allocator (D-844), so a use after free through one would go unreported. Refines D-721, D-844.
- **D-881 Where LeakSanitizer runs, and what is told to it**: On Linux, LeakSanitizer is on for the `jit` and `ui-deadlock` steps. It is off for `runtime` and `runtime-stress`, whose unit tests leak on purpose, and for `conformance`, `examples-poison-off` and `repl`, whose programs keep objects for good on purpose: D-539 makes a constant's objects static, held only by the constant's slot in the JIT's data memory, which LeakSanitizer does not scan, and the REPL never frees its session values (D-547; the REPL has no leak check, D-458). The workflow's header names these causes, and a test checks it. macOS passes no leak setting. The runtime's own leak check stays on everywhere. Under the address cfg, fiber stack slots are root regions, so objects held by fibers a halt freezes (D-708, D-859) are not reported, and the compiler's interners mark each entry they leak on purpose (D-268). Telling LeakSanitizer about the constants and the session values is left open. Why: D-863 asks that intended behavior be told to the sanitizer or covered by a per-step option whose reason the header names, never suppressed; without the interners' marks every `cheby` process gave reports at exit, and with LeakSanitizer on, any program that reads a std constant gives one. Refines D-721, D-831, D-708, D-863, D-268, D-539, D-547, D-458. Applies D-859.
- **D-882 One sanitizer driver**: `cargo xtask sanitize <address|thread|miri>` is the one driver for the nightly workflow and for local runs. Its address and thread steps are `runtime`, `runtime-stress`, `jit`, `ui-deadlock`, `conformance`, `examples-poison-off` (address only) and `repl`; each builds with the workflow's dated nightly, `-Zbuild-std`, the sanitizer flag and cfg, `CHEBY_RT_TEST_EMULATED=1` and `CHEBY_HARNESS_SCALE=10`, then runs. Its Miri steps are one per group. A sanitizer report fails a step even when the command exits 0, and each step prints one summary line. Why: the workflow cannot run before the push (D-862), so the local evidence must run the same commands. Refines D-721, D-781. Applies D-862, D-725, D-748.
- **D-883 CI lints the sanitizer cfg on stable**: A `ci.yml` step runs clippy on the runtime with each `cheby_sanitize` value, on the pinned stable toolchain, on Linux x86-64 and macOS arm64. Why: otherwise the annotations compile only in the nightly workflow and could rot between its runs. Refines D-277, D-721.
- **D-884 The Miri subset**: Miri runs the runtime's pure modules in 14 groups (hamt, num, alloc, list, generic, text, bytes, locate, rc, layout, string, abi, values, timer) with permissive provenance, isolation disabled, leaks ignored and `PROPTEST_CASES=4`. Tests too large for Miri are skipped with their reasons or take a smaller size under `cfg(miri)`; the out-of-memory tests are skipped, and D-552's Unicode version test is ignored under the sanitizer and Miri cfgs, whose nightly std follows another Unicode version than the stable pin. Why: fibers, the scheduler and JIT code cannot run under Miri, so the subset is the pure modules (D-721); Miri's leak check cannot follow objects held as integer words and would report the immortal objects of D-454 and D-753, which the runtime's own accounting checks instead. Refines D-721, D-552, D-863.
- **D-885 Static descriptors expose their strings' provenance**: The static descriptors and symbol tables expose the provenance of the string pointers they hand out as words. Why: Cheby words are integers, and under permissive provenance a pointer read back from a word must have been exposed; exposing costs nothing at run time. Refines D-721.
- **D-886 The shape of `test.toml`'s `timing` key**: `timing` is an optional boolean, false by default. Another type is a harness error ("unknown or ill-typed key `timing`"), and so is `timing = true` with `skip-run = true` ("`timing` has no effect on a `skip-run` example"); the error names the file, and the harness exits 2. 009, 013 and 019 set it. Why: D-725 names the key but not its type; a check-only example (D-445) waits for nothing, so `timing` there is a mistake. Refines D-725, D-445.
- **D-887 The serial group is a second libtest run**: The conformance harness runs the parallel batch, then, only when some example sets `timing`, a second `libtest_mimic` run on one thread for the serial group. Filters, `--exact`, `--skip`, `--ignored` and `--list` apply to each batch. One combined line follows: `conformance harness: <p> passed; <f> failed; <i> ignored; <x> filtered out (serial after the parallel batch: <n>)`, and reports quote it. Pending trials are judged once. D-725 orders trials (`cheby` processes); the tests inside one `cheby test` still run in parallel (D-723). Why: `libtest_mimic` has no per-trial thread control, and the gates quote one line. Refines D-725.
- **D-888 Every variant of a timing example runs serially**: Every run and `::test` trial of a `timing = true` example, at every variant (the base trial, `@threads=N`, `@rc=naive`, and the stress run's 4-thread trials), is in the serial group: 24 trials, or 30 under `CHEBY_THREADS=4`, listed last. Why: the base trial waits as long as the variants, and leaving it in the parallel batch would keep the flake D-725 removes. Refines D-725.

## 2026-10-18: M2 exit

Goal: log M2's exit: the compile-speed reading against D-247 that D-644 asks to report, the user's approval of the exit, the move of `tests/milestone` to M3, and the Open items M2 leaves.

- **D-889 Throughput reading at M2 exit**: D-247's throughput budget stays as written, and M2 misses it. On the Apple M3 of D-640, on a quiet machine, `cargo xtask bench-compile` with a release build of `33dbaf8`, whose compiler code equals the exit tree's, measured 90.2k to 91.1k lines per second per core for checking with code generation on `bench/gen` projects of 100k and 1M lines, 90.4k and 90.7k as the geometric mean of each size's shapes, 9.6% and 9.3% under 100k, and 81k to 98k on projects of 10k lines. M1 read 104k to 107k (D-644), and `c300fdb`, the tree before unwinding, reads 104.7k and 105.4k in the same window. The cause is unwinding: the same binary with `CHEBY_UNWIND_EDGES=off` reads 101.3k at both sizes, so the unwind edges cost 10.7% and 10.5% of throughput and add 18.3% to the code, as D-733, D-735 and D-776 accepted; H1's checkpoint costs 0.5% and 0.6%. On 8 threads builds scale 3.5x to 3.6x on projects of 100k lines or more, except 3.2x on the 100k-line `large` project. Editing one function of the 1M-line `wide` project and running it took 0.496 s by the median of 5 rounds, from 0.490 s to 0.512 s with 2 rounds over 500 ms, so D-247's latency budget is met only at the edge; it stays with OQ-P11. The budgets stay reported, not enforced, until M6, and M3's exit reads both again on CI's Linux runner. Why: the user chose to report the miss and keep D-247 as written. Revising the budget to the measured rate was rejected, as D-640's revision was superseded once M1 met the budget, and a recovery package with C1's remaining levers before the exit was rejected, because the cost is the return flag's, which the user chose with its measured cost (D-733). Refines D-644.

## 2026-10-18: Global allocator

Goal: replace the system allocator with mimalloc in the `cheby` binary and in AOT executables, from a benchmark of both.

- **D-890 mimalloc is the global allocator**: The `cheby` binary uses mimalloc as Rust's global allocator: the `mimalloc` crate at 0.1.52 with its default, mimalloc v3, set by `cheby_cli`. So every runtime object, the runtime's Rust-side buffers and the compiler's own allocations go through it. The runtime's per-fiber block cache stays in front of it. From M3, every AOT executable links mimalloc as its global allocator too. Builds with `--cfg cheby_sanitize` keep the system allocator, because the sanitizers intercept only that one (D-721, D-875). "The system allocator" of D-642, D-765 and D-771 now means the global allocator, mimalloc outside sanitizer builds: the allocator, as opposed to the memory budget, refuses the request. `libmimalloc-sys` compiles mimalloc from C, as C++17 under MSVC. So every CI job that builds `cheby_cli` first runs `.github/actions/c-compiler`, which checks the image's compiler and installs nothing. Why: on the Apple M3 of D-640, under a load of 3 to 5 with each benchmark's runs interleaved across binaries, mimalloc ran `bench/runtime` 4.7% faster by the geometric mean of wall time (`map_updates` 18%, `spawn_join` 18%, `sieve` 13%) and 6.8% by CPU time. The compiler's front end ran 7% to 17% faster, its 8-thread code generation 15% to 25% faster, and the edit-to-run of the 1M-line `wide` project went from 0.48 s to 0.41 s. The cost is 20% more peak RSS by geometric mean, 3 to 15 MB on the benchmarks. The runtime gains less than the compiler because its block cache already serves most frees and allocations. mimalloc v2 was rejected: it avoids v3's 2% to 7% slowdown on the three `sorted_` collections, but at the same geometric mean it uses 32% more RSS, against v3's 20%. Keeping the system allocator was rejected because D-889 leaves compile speed 9.3% under D-247. AOT executables use mimalloc so that a program runs on the same allocator under `cheby run` and when built. The user chose v3 and its use in AOT builds. Refines D-642, D-765, D-771, D-721.

Open:

- ~~How an AOT executable gets mimalloc: a `#[global_allocator]` in the runtime's staticlib, or a separate object at link time. M3's object-output work decides it; the JIT's rlib must not get a second global allocator.~~ → D-928
- D-890's numbers come from a loaded machine. M3's exit reading of D-247 on CI's Linux runner includes mimalloc, and the next quiet RC reading compares against the system allocator's `string_building` modes too.
- Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.

## 2026-10-18: Windows CI

Goal: fix the runtime tests that failed on CI's Windows and macOS x86-64 runners: make the span that never elapses the same on every platform, and drop macOS on x86-64.

- **D-891 The longest span that elapses**: The runtime's clock of D-652 represents spans up to 2⁶³ − 1 nanoseconds, about 292 years, on every platform and target. A function that waits for a longer span, such as `fiber::timeout` or `selector::after`, treats it as one that never elapses, so the runtime arms no timer for it. A span up to the cap waits for its full length. Why: Rust's `Instant` holds `i64::MAX` seconds from now on Windows but not on Unix, so a cap set by `Instant` made `fiber::timeout(duration::seconds(i64::MAX))` without a scheduler fatal on Windows only (D-692), and the timer tests failed there. A fixed cap gives every platform the same results. The cap is Go's longest `time.Duration`. Rejected: keeping `Instant`'s range and fixing the tests per platform, which leaves the difference visible to programs. Refines D-652.

- **D-892 macOS on x86-64 is not supported**: The native targets are macOS on aarch64, and Linux and Windows on x86-64 and aarch64. CI drops its macOS x86-64 job, and M6's platform matrix has five native platforms. CI's macOS test job runs at `CHEBY_HARNESS_SCALE=5`, and the runtime's preemption tests scale their bounds, as D-872 already required. Why: the user decided to drop the platform after CI's macOS x86-64 runner failed a preemption test in two runs, each time a different one, with latencies near 100 ms where D-688 allows 50 ms. A probe on CI's runners then logged every monitor tick: the monitor never backed off while a fiber ran, and a plain 10 ms wait with no runtime took a median of 40 ms on the Intel runner and 36 ms on the Apple silicon one, against 10.08 ms on Linux. So the latencies come from the hosted macOS VMs' timers, on both architectures, and the macOS arm64 job had passed by chance. The scale keeps that job from flaking, and Linux still checks D-688's 50 ms unscaled. Rejected: keeping the Intel job at the same scale, which the user declined. Refines D-074, D-277, D-872.

- ~~`sched::tests::a_looping_fiber_is_preempted_within_fifty_milliseconds` failed on CI's macOS x86-64 runner, with 91 to 130 ms against D-687's 50 ms.~~ → D-892.
- ~~The cause of the macOS x86-64 latencies is not explained. In both runs they were near 100 ms, which is the monitor's idle interval, so the monitor may stay backed off while a fiber runs. A review of `kick` and the backoff found no race, and a local run with every core oversubscribed did not reproduce it. A probe that logs the monitor's ticks on that runner decides it.~~ Done: the probe found no monitor bug; the hosted macOS VMs stretch 10 ms waits on both architectures (D-892).
- Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.

## 2026-10-19: Slices and last-use release

Goal: fix the deadlock where a `Sender` walked out of a list with `[first, ..rest]` never closed its channel, because `rest` kept the cut element alive.

- **D-893 Owned slices release the elements only they hold**: An owned list slice, which `[first, ..rest]` makes when the list dies at the match (D-634), releases each element it cuts whose slot only this list holds: where every node on the slot's path has count one, the runtime overwrites the slot with an immediate and drops the element, in place and without allocating. A cut of more than four elements from a tree with count one restructures the tree to hold just the slice. An element under a node that another list shares is not released by the cut, and lives until the last list holding that node dies, which may be the slice itself. A borrowed slice, such as `list::slice`, `list::take`, `list::drop`, `list::split_at` or a spread of a list that is used again, stays an O(1) view that shares the whole tree. So D-205's release at last use holds for an element walked out of a list that the walk alone holds, and a cut element that other lists shared is held at most as long as the slice. A pattern step on such a list now costs O(log n), effectively constant, instead of O(1). Why: a slice only moved its view, so `rest` kept every element it cut alive, and a program that walked a `Sender` out of a list deadlocked. Releasing every cut element would mean copying the shared nodes along a slice's edges, and so did a first version: against `main` on the Apple M3 of D-640, with three to five runs each, it measured 2.0× to 6.3× on walks of shared or borrowed lists, 1.56× on a `map`, `filter` and `fold` chain over a million elements, and 1.44× to 2.25× on the cheby-compare suite's `spectral_norm`, `word_freq` and `json_roundtrip`. This rule measured 1.27× on that chain, whose walks now descend the tree twice per step, 1.10× on `spectral_norm`, 1.06× on `word_freq`, and 0.92× to 1.05× elsewhere, within the noise of runs that short. D-634's remark that a program observes nothing of owned slices no longer holds: whether a slice is owned now decides when a handle it cuts is released. Refines D-205, D-053 and D-634.
- **D-894 std's slicing kernels cut a list in place at its last use**: `list::slice`, `list::take` and `list::drop` on a list whose only reference is the call's own, as when the call is the list's last use, cut the list in place as an owned slice does (D-893): the root is reused and the cut elements that only this list holds are released at the call. `list::split_at` on such a list reuses the root for its back half; it cuts nothing, since each element is in one half. On a list that is used afterwards they stay O(1) views. This is the count-one rule that `push` and `set` already follow (D-522), so lowering does not change. Why: after D-893, `list::drop` on a list that died at the call still kept a cut `Sender` alive while the slice lived. Against `main`, on the Apple M3 of D-640, a `list::drop` loop takes 0.46× and a `list::split_at` loop 0.57×, and seven runs each put the RC group's geometric mean at −7.0% to −9.0% and the collections group's at −1.0% to +0.2%. Refines D-893 and D-484.

Open:

- ~~Recover the second descent per pattern step: a fused helper that moves the head out of a unique list in the same descent (it would extend D-520), and a forward scan from the radix guess in `Node::locate` instead of a binary search. Both are measured against the 1.27× of D-893.~~ Done for the scan: `Node::locate` now checks up to four children forward from the radix position before it falls back to the binary search. Against `main`, seven runs each on the Apple M3 of D-640, `rc/list_pipeline` takes 0.739 s (−13.3%), the RC group's geometric mean is −5.3% and the collections group's −2.3%; on the external suite, list walks take 0.48× to 0.68× and `json_roundtrip` 0.72×. No benchmark is slower beyond the noise of its runs.
- ~~A fused helper that moves the head out of a unique list in the same descent as the clear (it would extend D-520). The scan already recovers D-893's cost, so it is only worth what it gains beyond that.~~ → D-909.
- ~~Read `cargo xtask bench-run`'s `rc/list_pipeline` before and after D-893; its numbers above come from an external suite.~~ Done: on the Apple M3 of D-640, seven runs each, D-893 as first committed (d768f61) against `main` (20a7a73) took `rc/list_pipeline` from 0.852 s to 1.195 s (+40.3%), the RC group's geometric mean +8.1% and the collections group's +5.5%, led by `multimap` (+17.0%), `sorted_multimap` (+12.9%) and `ordered_multimap` (+11.4%). The external suite had understated the cost.
- ~~Whether `list::drop`, `list::take` and `list::slice` on a list that dies at the call should get owned variants, as patterns did in D-634, so that they release cut elements too.~~ → D-894.
- Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.

## 2026-10-19: Run-time regression gate

Goal: make CI fail a change that slows list walks or the RC group by much, after an inlining change made `[x, ..rest]` walks a third slower without any test noticing.

- **D-895 CI gates the RC group's run time against the base**: A CI job, `bench-gate`, builds a release `cheby` for the change and for its base, which is a pull request's merge base or the previous `main` of a push, on one runner. It runs `cargo xtask bench-run --group rc` with seven runs of each binary, interleaved run by run (`--against`), and fails when the geometric mean of the RC group's ratios, or `rc/list_pipeline`'s ratio alone, is more than 10% above the base's (`--fail-above 10 --gate rc/list_pipeline`). Every other benchmark and group stays reported, never enforced. Why: while D-894 was written, a second caller of `update` made the compiler stop inlining it, which made `[x, ..rest]` walks a third slower, and no test or check noticed; only a manual reading did. Timing both binaries in turn on one runner cancels most of a shared runner's drift, which an absolute threshold could not. 10% sits above the run-to-run spread seen on the Apple M3 of D-640 (about 3% on the group, 6% on one benchmark). Refines D-718.
- **D-896 A decision accepts a slowdown past the gate**: A non-merge commit of a change may carry the git trailer `Accepted-Slowdown: D-NNN`, naming the decision-log entry that records the slowdown as a trade-off. The decision must be one the change adds: live in the change's log and absent from the base's. `bench-gate` reads the trailers with git's trailer parser and passes each value as written to `bench-run --accepted-by`, with `--accepted-new-since` and the base's log, so a malformed value or an older decision fails the job. When every value is valid, what D-895's gate would reject is printed in the step summary as a note, and the job passes. A size or checksum that differs from a baseline's still fails. A squash merge keeps the trailer only if its message does. Why: D-895 would have blocked D-893's first version, a correctness fix that cost 40% on `rc/list_pipeline`, with no way through. A trailer that cites a decision keeps the acceptance in git history, is reviewed with the code, and works for pull requests and pushes alike; a pull-request label was rejected because it lives outside git and does nothing on a push. Requiring a new decision keeps an old one, such as D-001, from switching the gate off, and merge commits are skipped because their message, a pull request's title, is not reviewed as code. Refines D-895.
- **D-897 What the gate leaves ungated, and runs on `main`**: With `--against`, a benchmark that the base binary cannot run, or for which it prints another size or checksum than the change's binary, gets no baseline: its row is reported with a note and is left out of the geometric mean and of `--gate`, and the rest of the group is still gated. The base is built from its own worktree, with its own toolchain and without `-D warnings`. The workflow cancels an older run only on a pull request, never on `main`, so every merge is tested and gated. Why: both binaries run the change's own benchmark sources, so a change that uses a new std function in an RC benchmark failed the gate with no way through, and a quick second merge to `main` cancelled the first merge's run, which then was never gated. Refines D-895.

Open:

- The gate's noise on GitHub's hosted runners is not measured yet. If it fails changes that do not touch the runtime, raise the runs or the threshold rather than drop it.
- ~~A pull request that changes an RC benchmark's program can make the base print another checksum, which fails the gate. Such a change then needs its baseline reading in the pull request instead.~~ → D-897.
- Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.

## 2026-10-19: The `check` fuzz target under LeakSanitizer

Goal: make the nightly `fuzz (check)` job pass, after its first scheduled run failed on the initial corpus with LeakSanitizer reports of the interners' entries.

- **D-898 How the fuzz targets build and run**: The nightly fuzz step sets `RUSTFLAGS='--cfg cheby_sanitize="address"'`, which cargo-fuzz appends to its own `-Zsanitizer=address`, and passes `-rss_limit_mb=6144` to every target. `check` also runs with `ASAN_OPTIONS=detect_leaks=0`, which cargo-fuzz merges with its own options, and the workflow's header says why. The commands in `fuzz/Cargo.toml` and `fuzz/fuzz_targets/check.rs` say the same. Why: `cargo fuzz` builds under AddressSanitizer, but without the cfg the runtime's fiber switches, which constant evaluation reaches, are not told to the sanitizer, and the interners' marks (D-881) are no-ops, so libFuzzer's leak check reported every name and type a check's dropped `Globals` leaves behind. With the cfg, a run over the seed corpus still reported a constant's result that D-539 makes static, held only by the JIT's data memory, which LeakSanitizer does not scan. That is the cause for which D-881 turns LeakSanitizer off for `conformance`, so `check` takes the same per-step option that D-863 allows. It goes through `ASAN_OPTIONS`, because libFuzzer's `-detect_leaks=0` only stops the check after each input and leaves the one at exit, which reported 174 MB of frozen constants after a 600-second run. Telling LeakSanitizer about frozen constants, which would keep it on, stays open as in D-881. Every input builds new interners and new constants and keeps them, about 40 KB, so RSS grows with the input count: 306 MB to 801 MB in 120 seconds at 31 inputs per second on Linux arm64, past libFuzzer's default 2048 MB within a 900-second run. The hosted runners have 16 GB. Sharing one `Globals` across inputs was rejected as a driver API change made only for the fuzzer. Refines D-781, applies D-863 and D-881.

Open:

- Telling LeakSanitizer about frozen constants (D-539), for example with `__lsan_ignore_object` in `alloc::freeze` under the address cfg, would let `check` and the sanitizer jobs' `conformance` step run with it on (D-881).
- Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.

## 2026-10-19: Faster std::json

Goal: make `std::json` parse, decode and write faster, in Cheby first, without changing what any of it returns or prints.

- **D-899 A JSON group in the runtime benchmarks, reported and ungated**: `bench/runtime/json/` holds two programs that `cargo xtask bench-run --group json` times. `roundtrip` writes 20,000 records as JSON text (4.7 MB), reads them with `json::parse` and record decoders, changes the active ones and writes them with `json::to_string`. `value` writes 9,000 items, reads them with `json::parse_value`, writes the value with `json::to_pretty_string` and reads that back to an equal value. Each program writes its own input, with escaped strings, `\u` escapes with surrogate pairs, floats with fractions and exponents, nested arrays and `null`, and in `value` integers past 2^53, and prints the FNV-1a hash of the text it writes last. CI's `bench-gate` still runs only the RC group (D-895), so the JSON group is reported, never enforced, and has no baseline file. Why: the only JSON timing so far came from the external cheby-compare suite's `json_roundtrip`, which this repository cannot run and whose 15 MB input has no escapes, floats or pretty output. With `std::json` as it was when the group was added, on the Apple M3 of D-640 with a release `cheby`, `roundtrip` takes 1.37 s, of which writing the input is 0.08 s, and `value` takes 1.58 s, inside the 0.5 to 2 seconds that `bench/runtime/README.md` asks of a default. Gating the group was rejected for now: this session's rewrites change its times on purpose, and a gate on a second group would need its own threshold decision under D-895. Refines D-718.
- **D-900 The JSON parser scans bytes and finds a position only on error**: `std::json::tree` reads the UTF-8 bytes of the text, taken once with `bytes::from_string`, by offset. Every loop is a tail call: the innermost open array or object is an argument, and the outer ones stay in the heap list of D-326. The parser tracks no line or column. On a syntax error it scans the bytes again up to the error's offset, counting line feeds for the line and, after the last one, the bytes that do not continue a UTF-8 sequence for the column, which are the code points of D-324. A string without escapes is one slice of the bytes, found four bytes at a time with `bytes::read_u32_le`. An integer of at most 15 digits is summed from its digits; longer integers, fractions and exponents still go through `int::parse` or `float::parse` on their text, so no `Int` beyond 2⁵³ is built on JS (D-037, D-394). The parser is generic over a package-visible `Builder`, so `json::parse_value` builds a `json::Value` directly instead of building the raw tree and converting it with `rebuild`, which `decode_value` still uses. Why: the old parser turned the text into a `List<Int>` of code points and carried the line and column through a record at every code point. On cheby-compare's 15.4 MB `json_roundtrip` input, json-bench.json (Apple M3, release `cheby`), parsing alone goes from 2.4 s to 0.6 s and `parse_value` from 3.1 s to 0.6 s (the third of three runs in one process); cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip` goes from 4.08 s to 2.24 s with its hash and from 3.83 s to 2.10 s without (medians of 3), and peak RSS from 338 MB to about 206 MB. In D-899's group, `roundtrip` goes from 1.34 s to 0.81 s and `value` from 1.65 s to 0.70 s, in this step's interleaved runs rather than the readings recorded in D-899. The four-byte window saves 0.09 s per parse over reading one byte at a time. Keeping the old parser to rerun on failure was rejected: the rescan gives the same position with much less code. Results and positions are unchanged: the std tests of this session, 17,176 texts made from prefixes and one-code-point changes of seed documents, compared with the previous parser, and json-bench.json all give the same output. Refines D-326, D-361, D-483.
- **D-901 JSON decoders build error paths only on error, once, in `run`**: A decoder in `std::json::decode` records its errors as a tree relative to the value it runs on. `field`, `optional_field`, `list` and `pairs` put their key or index above the errors of a member only when it has errors, so a decode that succeeds builds no path and formats no index, and a step is stored once for all the errors below it. `run` walks the tree once, with the parts still to visit in a list on the heap (D-326), and builds each error's whole path with `list::push`, as the decoders did before on the way down. It runs `list::unique` only on a non-empty error list. `field` searches the object for its member and returns what `next` gives, as a tail call, without an `Option` in between. The primitive decoders are a variant of their own of the opaque `Decoder`, `Leaf`, that `field` and `list` look into: they check a value and take it without building a result and its errors. Errors, their order and their paths are unchanged (D-322, D-401, D-404, D-485). Two things that do not belong to the API change with the representation: debug output shows `decode::int()` as `Leaf("Int", 0, <fn>, <fn>)` rather than `Decoder(<fn>)`, and `==` between a primitive decoder and another kind of decoder, which W0011 warns about, gives `False` instead of the panic of D-233, since it reaches no function value. Why: building the path on the way down cost every successful decode a `list::push` per field and an `int::show` per element. On cheby-compare's json-bench.json (15.4 MB, 50,000 records; Apple M3, release `cheby`), medians of five runs interleaved with the writer step (9e44727), parsing alone stays at 0.62 s; a decode into records goes from 0.91 s to 0.79 s, about 41% less decode time once the parse is taken out; a decoder that fails at every record's `id` and every `history` element's `day` goes from 0.81 s to 0.79 s, and one that fails at every leaf from 1.15 s to 1.10 s. Without `Leaf`, a successful decode takes about 25% more decode time (0.22 s against 0.17 s per decode, over four decodes in one run). A first version copied the errors below each level with that level's step added, which costs the sum of the squares of the errors' depths: a recursive decoder that fails at every level of 8,000 nested objects took 34 s against 0.42 s on `main`; with the tree it takes 0.68 s, as `main` does, and 4.1 s at 20,000 levels, where `main` takes 4.2 s. cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip` goes from 1.50 s to 1.37 s with its hash and from 1.29 s to 1.15 s without (medians of 3), against 4.16 s and 3.84 s on `main`; D-899's `roundtrip` goes from 0.577 s to 0.530 s (−8.2%), and `value`, which decodes nothing, is unchanged at 0.504 s. The std tests of this session, a program that prints the results and errors of five decoders over 20 documents, the errors of three decoders over the first 2,000 records of json-bench.json, and the errors of recursive decoders over objects nested up to 20,000 deep and over 10,000 failing strings in arrays nested up to 200 deep, compared by their number, their path lengths and a hash of their text, give the same output as `main`.
- **D-902 A static `Some` for every byte**: The runtime holds a static `Some(n)`, count 0 (D-454), for each `n` from -128 to 255, one table for `U8` and `I8`, since a `U8` and an `I8` of the same value have the same uniform word (D-521). `bytes::get` and `bytes::read_i8` return these objects and the static `None`, so they allocate nothing and cannot raise: they run without a guard, are on the runtime's `NEVER_UNWINDS` list, and join the never-raise kernel table, so their calls get no unwind edge. Like every static object, these are never written, freed or reused: a count of 0 is never 1, so a `drop_reuse` of one gives no token and the `alloc_reuse` that follows allocates (D-838, D-839). They are runtime objects with no symbol in generated code. A constant may hold one, since constant evaluation may run `std::bytes` (D-360), and making constant results static in place skips it, as it skips every static object (D-539). The other kernels that return an `Option`, `read_u16_be` and the wider reads, still allocate their `Some`. Why: `bytes::get` is the parser's step over the text (D-900) and `bytes::fold`'s, and allocating its `Some` was most of its cost. On the Apple M3 of D-640 with a release `cheby`, against the decoder step (e9fc1aa), a loop of `bytes::get` over cheby-compare's 15.4 MB json-bench.json goes from about 17 ns to about 5 ns per byte, and `bytes::fold` from about 18 ns to about 8 ns. cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip` goes from 1.36 s to 0.97 s with its hash, which folds over the output with `bytes::fold`, and from 1.09 s to 0.90 s without (medians of five interleaved runs). D-899's `roundtrip` goes from 0.514 s to 0.418 s (−18.7%) and `value` from 0.507 s to 0.342 s (−32.6%), and the RC group stays within noise against the same base (geometric mean +0.1%, `rc/list_pipeline` −0.3%, seven interleaved runs). The never-raise table entries add nothing measurable on their own (about 1% on the hashed run, within noise); they are listed because the kernels qualify, as D-767 keeps the table. A static `Some` for every small integer that any kernel returns was left out: it adds a range check to every `Some` a kernel builds, and no other kernel returns one per byte of a loop. Refines D-454. Extends D-767.
- **D-903 A package-visible kernel quotes JSON strings**: `std::string` declares `add_json_quoted(out, text)`, package-visible, bound as `@external(native, "cheby_string_add_json_quoted")` and `@external(js, "./string.mjs", "add_json_quoted")` as D-484 binds every kernel. It returns `out` followed by `"`, then `text` escaped as D-326 and D-398 say, then `"`: `"` and `\` get a backslash, the bytes 0x08, 0x0C, 0x0A, 0x0D and 0x09 become `\b`, `\f`, `\n`, `\r` and `\t`, every other byte below 0x20 becomes `\u00` and two lower-case hex digits, and every other byte is copied, DEL, the C1 controls and U+2028 and U+2029 included. Each byte of a multi-byte UTF-8 sequence is 0x80 or more, so working byte by byte is exact. Like `string::add`, it extends a unique `out` in place when it has room and otherwise grows it as D-577 and D-849 say, and it never writes in place when `out` and `text` are the same object, by the alias rule of D-563 and D-577. Text with no byte to escape is written in one step; otherwise the kernel measures the escaped length first, then writes the plain runs and the escapes. It allocates, so it runs under a guard and stays off the never-raise table (D-767). `json::to_string` and `json::to_pretty_string` call it for every string and key; the Cheby `needs_escape`, `quote`, `escape` and `hex` of `std::json` are gone, and so is its import of `std::bytes`. `std::json` still uses only modules that constant evaluation may run (D-360, D-537). The JS kernel, which M4 writes, loops over the UTF-16 code units with the same table, since every code unit of a non-ASCII code point is 0x80 or more. It is not in the `--reuse-report` table of in-place kernels (D-852): the report covers calls in the user's package, which cannot name a package-visible std function. Why: the Cheby writer read every byte of every string with `bytes::get` to find one to escape, added a plain string with three `+`, and turned an escaped one into a `List<Int>` of code points and back. On the Apple M3 of D-640 with a release `cheby`, against D-902 (0b6b506), cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip` (15.4 MB, 1 rep, medians of five interleaved runs) goes from 1.02 s to 0.96 s with its FNV hash (−5.9%) and from 0.91 s to 0.85 s without (−6.6%), with the same checksum (1358032951 with the hash) and peak RSS unchanged at about 180 to 210 MB; `main` takes 4.07 s and 3.94 s (medians of 3). D-899's `roundtrip` goes from 0.409 s to 0.384 s (−5.9%) and its allocations from 8.94 million to 8.62 million, and `value` from 0.352 s to 0.332 s (−5.5%) and from 5.70 million to 5.46 million (seven interleaved runs, geometric mean −5.7%), with the same checksums. The RC group stays within noise against the same base (geometric mean −0.5%, `rc/list_pipeline` +0.5%, seven interleaved runs). Making the kernel `pub` was rejected: JSON's escaping is no general text operation, and a package-visible kernel keeps `docs/stdlib/string.md` unchanged. Refines D-274. Extends D-484.
- **D-904 A package-visible kernel finds the plain runs of JSON strings**: `std::bytes` declares `json_plain_end(data, from)`, package-visible, bound as `@external(native, "cheby_bytes_json_plain_end")` and `@external(js, "./bytes.mjs", "json_plain_end")` as D-484 binds every kernel. It returns the offset of the first byte at or after `from` that is `"` (0x22), `\` (0x5C) or below 0x20, or `bytes::length(data)` when there is none. `from` is clamped to `0..=length` as `bytes::slice` clamps its bounds (D-298, D-334), so a negative `from` scans from 0 and one past the end gives the length. Every byte of a multi-byte UTF-8 sequence is 0x80 or more, so none of them stops the scan. It is O(k) in the bytes it scans, allocates nothing and cannot raise: it runs without a guard, is on the runtime's `NEVER_UNWINDS` list as a kernel that reads its arguments and allocates nothing, and joins the never-raise kernel table (D-735, D-767), so its calls get no unwind edge. The parser of D-900 calls it through `plain_end` for the plain run of every string and key; the Cheby loop that read four bytes at a time with `bytes::read_u32_le`, and its byte-at-a-time tail, are gone. The JS kernel, which M4 writes, is an indexed loop over the `Uint8Array`. Why: `read_u32_le` allocates a `Some(U32)` for every four bytes, which D-902's table of static `Some` objects does not cover, and the loop paid a call and four comparisons per window on top. On the Apple M3 of D-640 with a release `cheby`, against D-903 (c25a387), cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip` (15.4 MB, 1 rep) goes from 0.93 s to 0.90 s with its FNV hash (−3.2%, medians of twelve interleaved runs) and from 0.85 s to 0.79 s without (−8.2%, medians of ten), with the same checksum (1358032951 with the hash) and peak RSS unchanged at about 180 to 210 MB; with 3 reps and no hash it goes from 2.47 s to 2.30 s (−6.9%, −0.057 s per rep, medians of five). A program that parses json-bench.json three times with `json::parse` goes from 1.28 s to 1.11 s, about 0.057 s less per parse (medians of five). D-899's `roundtrip` goes from 0.392 s to 0.377 s (−3.6%) and its allocations from 8.62 million to 7.93 million, and `value` from 0.327 s to 0.315 s (−3.7%) and from 5.46 million to 4.90 million (seven interleaved runs), with the same checksums. The RC group stays within noise against the same base (geometric mean +0.0%, `rc/list_pipeline` +0.1%, seven interleaved runs). Returning a static `Some(U32)` from `read_u32_le` was rejected for the reasons D-902 gives for wider integers, and a `pub` kernel because the stop bytes are JSON's, not a general byte operation; a package-visible kernel keeps `docs/stdlib/bytes.md` unchanged. Refines D-274, D-900. Extends D-484, D-767.
- **D-905 A package-visible kernel reads JSON texts from the bytes**: `std::string` declares `from_byte_range(data, start, end)`, package-visible, bound as `@external(native, "cheby_string_from_byte_range")` and `@external(js, "./string.mjs", "from_byte_range")` as D-484 binds every kernel, with `@std_clamped(start, end)` as `bytes::slice` has. It returns the bytes of `data` from `start` up to but not including `end` as a new `String`. Both bounds are clamped to `0..=length` as `bytes::slice` clamps them (D-298, D-334), and `end` at or below `start` gives `""`. Each invalid UTF-8 sequence becomes U+FFFD, by the maximal-subpart practice that Rust's `String::from_utf8_lossy` and the `TextDecoder` of JS both follow; valid bytes are copied after one UTF-8 check. A leading U+FEFF is kept, as `string::from_bytes` keeps it (D-366). The result never shares `data`, even for the whole range. It allocates, so it runs under a guard and stays off the never-raise table (D-767). The parser of D-900 reads the text of every string and key without escapes, of every plain run before an escape, and of every number with it; its `text_of`, which did `let assert Ok(text) = string::from_bytes(bytes::slice(data, start, end))`, is gone. The bytes are the UTF-8 of a `String` and every cut falls next to an ASCII byte, so no range the parser reads holds invalid UTF-8 and results are unchanged. `std::json` still uses only modules that constant evaluation may run (D-360). The JS kernel, which M4 writes, is `decoder.decode(data.subarray(s, e))` with one shared `new TextDecoder("utf-8", { ignoreBOM: true })`, `fatal` left false. The default decoder would drop a leading U+FEFF, so without `ignoreBOM` the two back ends would not give the same text for a string or key that starts with one. Why: `text_of` paid, per string or number text, a `Bytes` allocation, a copy, a UTF-8 check, an `Ok` allocation and two frees, about 1.15 million times per parse of cheby-compare's json-bench.json. The kernel is lossy rather than returning a `Result` because the `Ok` is part of what it removes, and the parser has no use for an error it can never get; a `Result` kernel would keep one allocation and a `let assert` per text. On the Apple M3 of D-640 with a release `cheby`, against D-904 (389d832), cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip` (15.4 MB, 1 rep) goes from 0.898 s to 0.828 s with its FNV hash (−7.7%) and from 0.751 s to 0.697 s without (−7.1%, medians of twelve interleaved runs in alternating order), with the same checksum (1358032951 with the hash), about 5 to 6% fewer instructions, and peak RSS unchanged at about 180 to 210 MB; with 3 reps and no hash it goes from 2.22 s to 2.00 s (−9.9%, about −0.073 s per rep, medians of eight). A program that parses json-bench.json three times with `json::parse` goes from 1.05 s to 0.92 s, about 0.043 s less per parse (medians of seven).
