Against `main`, medians of 3 interleaved runs: 4.07 s to 0.84 s with the hash, 3.91 s to 0.73 s without, peak RSS about 338 MB to about 180 to 210 MB. D-899's `roundtrip` cuts its allocations from 7.93 million to 7.23 million and `value` from 4.90 million to 4.23 million, with the same checksums. Their times vary more between runs of the group: over four runs, two of seven and two of fifteen interleaved runs each, `roundtrip` reads −2.0% to −7.7% (0.375 s to 0.346 s in the first) and `value` −4.5% to −5.9% (0.316 s to 0.299 s), geometric means −3.8% to −6.5%. The RC group stays within noise against the same base in two runs with bench-gate's command (geometric mean +0.7% and −0.5%, `rc/list_pipeline` −0.2% and −0.5%); `rc/record_updates`, which calls no changed code, reads +4.8% and +4.9%, with the same 16.0 billion instructions on both binaries and about 4% more cycles, a code-layout effect of the rebuilt runtime. Taking the range with `bytes::slice` and a lossy `from_bytes` was rejected, since the copy into a `Bytes` is half of the cost, and a `pub` kernel because a public API would need `docs/stdlib/string.md` to settle the lossy decode for every user; a package-visible kernel keeps it unchanged. Refines D-274, D-900. Extends D-484.
- **D-906 `std::json` keeps its open arrays and objects in a chain of cells that each step rewrites in place**: The parser of `std::json::tree` keeps the array or object it is reading, and the ones around it, innermost first, in one private type `Stack<T>` with the cases `Top`, `InArray(items, below)` and `InObject(members, key, below)`, instead of a `Frame` argument and a `List<Frame>` of the outer ones. The writer of `json::to_string` and `json::to_pretty_string` keeps what is left of each open array and object in a private `Open` with the cases `Done`, `Items(items, depth, below)` and `Members(members, depth, below)`, instead of a `List<Frame>` matched with `[..rest, frame]`. Opening an array or object allocates one cell and closing it frees one. A step takes the innermost cell apart and builds its new version in the same arm or block, so Perceus writes a unique cell over in place (D-838, D-839). The parser does this once for each value inside an array or object, and once more for each member, whose key the object's cell holds from the `:` until the value is read. The writer does it once for each element or member after the first. The stack of containers itself runs no list kernel and makes no owned slice (D-634, D-893); only the elements are pushed onto their array's or object's list. Reuse pairs only when the rebuild is in a block that only the block taking the cell apart enters (D-839), so a `let x = case ..` placed between the two turns each step back into an allocation and a free without changing any result. Comments at both places say so, and `json_containers_reuse_their_cells` in `crates/cheby_cli/tests/run.rs` parses and writes a document with `CHEBY_RT_STATS=1` and fails when the reuse hits fall below one per step or any reuse misses. Smaller changes go with it. A number is read from its integer part with its sign as an argument, not through a tuple. The rest of `true`, `false` and `null` is compared with an `Int` that packs its bytes, not by walking a `List<U8>`, which sliced once per byte. The parser calls `bytes::json_plain_end` without a wrapper and tests digits inline. The parser and the `list` decoder add an element with a spread, `[..items, value]`, which lowers to the push helper without the call to `list::push`. Nesting is still limited only by memory, and every loop is still a tail call (D-094, D-326). Results, error offsets and positions are unchanged (D-324, D-901). Why: the writer cut its list of frames with `[..rest, frame]` and pushed a new frame for every element, and the parser allocated a new frame for every element and freed the old one, and pushed onto and cut its list of frames for every array and object. On the Apple M3 of D-640 with a release `cheby`, against D-905 (955eaab), cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip` (15.4 MB, 1 rep) goes from 17.38 to 15.35 billion instructions retired with its FNV hash (−11.6%) and from 14.49 to 12.45 billion without (−14.1%), and its wall time from 0.82 s to 0.74 s and from 0.73 s to 0.62 s (medians of seven interleaved runs), with the same checksum (1358032951 with the hash) and peak RSS at about 180 to 200 MB. Its allocations go from 16.42 million to 14.36 million, its reuse hits from 1.70 million to 3.09 million, and its reuse misses from 625,184 to 0. D-899's `roundtrip` goes from 0.353 s to 0.321 s (−8.9%), from 7.37 to 6.64 billion instructions (−9.8%) and from 7.23 million to 6.45 million allocations; `value` goes from 0.301 s to 0.253 s (−16.1%), from 6.44 to 5.56 billion instructions (−13.6%) and from 4.23 million to 3.22 million allocations (seven interleaved runs for the times, five for the instructions), with the same checksums. The RC group, which does not import `std::json`, stays within noise: its instructions change by less than 0.1% on every benchmark, and its wall times by a geometric mean of +0.8% (`rc/list_pipeline` +0.4%, seven interleaved runs). The design study measured each part on the same input: the writer's chain −4.3% of the instructions, the parser's chain −2.9%, writing the cell in place −0.8%, the number tuple and the word list −2.7%, the inline digit test −1.2%, the direct kernel call −0.3% and the spread −0.65%. It rejected three variants that measured worse: folding the separator into `close_value` through helpers with a joined binding (+270 million instructions), finding a decoder's field by index with `list::get`, whose `Option` costs more than a borrowed slice (+380 million), and a `case pretty` join in the writer (+100 million, with 650,000 fewer reuse hits). Refines D-900 (the outer containers are no longer a heap list), D-904 (the parser calls the kernel directly), D-326 and D-094 (the heap stack is kept, as a chain of cells).
- **D-907 Freeing reads a free-shape table, and a fiber allocates and charges through its control block**: Four runtime changes, none of which a program can observe. (1) A dense table holds one word for each of the first 16,384 layout ids: an object's allocation size, where its value fields start and how many there are, and whether a reuse may keep its block (D-716). The word is derived from the layout when the layout is registered, the builtins' when the layout table is first read, and it never changes. A runtime-defined kind declares its shape in a new `CustomKind::shape` field: list roots a fixed size and one field, list leaves and branches a meta word at offset 8 that counts their fields, with a fixed size or one that follows the capacity. A kind with a finalizer, or a handle kind, may not declare a shape, and registering one is an error. `free`, `drop`'s free, the worklist, `release_fields` and `free_unique` read the word and push the block straight to its size class. They take the layout table and the kind's `size` and `children` functions only for a word of 0, which a higher id and a kind with no declared shape have (today map and set nodes and the handles), and for a tuple, closure, dictionary or descriptor in the escaped form. A constructor with more than 254 value fields is still freed from its word. Debug builds check every answer of the word on every free against the layout table and against the kind's own functions, down to the very words that `children` visits, and against the debug count of runtime kinds (D-711). (2) `cheby_rt_alloc`, still inside its guard (D-697), serves a fiber that has no memory budget inline: a block of the size's class from the block cache that the control block records (D-739), or on a miss from the system allocator. A kernel's allocation finds the running fiber with the existing `current_fiber` accessor and then takes the same path, so it no longer calls the accessor that finds the fiber and the cache together. The allocations given the fiber's control block, a reuse miss (`cheby_rt_alloc_miss` and `alloc_reuse`'s) and a new handle, take it directly. Frees by a kernel find the cache the same way. No thread-local is added (D-686). (3) The control block caches the address of the shard it charges, with a bit for an exclusive one, and rewrites it at every switch-in together with the shard index (D-691, D-773). Charges through a control block skip the shard arithmetic and the bounds check. Debug builds assert, on every such charge, that the cached slot is that of the current shard index and that a thread charging an exclusive shard holds it. The counters, the peak sampling and the `CHEBY_RT_STATS` output are unchanged (D-635, D-768): on cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip`, allocations stay at 14,358,846, the reuse hits at 3,088,212 and the peak at 140,284,400 bytes. (4) When the system refuses a block on the inline path, the general path asks once more before it raises. A refusal that does not repeat therefore neither raises `out of memory` nor marks the domain; before, the first refusal did both. A refusal on the second request raises and marks the domain as D-642 and D-771 say. Unit tests pin both cases through a test hook that refuses one request on the inline path, and the notes of §10.10 and §13.6 now say that the leak check is skipped when a refused request ended in `out of memory`. Why: an instruction-level profile of the yardstick found no thread-local or remote-free cost, but generic work on every object: finding a shape cost three dependent loads through the layout table, a match on the kind with checked arithmetic and, for list nodes, two indirect calls, and the allocation took a closure through the accessor that finds the fiber and the cache. On the Apple M3 of D-640 with a release `cheby`, against D-906 (5dbe0ff), with nine interleaved runs, cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip` (15.4 MB, 1 rep) goes from 15.35 to 13.48 billion instructions retired with its FNV hash (−12.2%) and from 12.45 to 10.57 billion without (−15.1%). Its median wall time goes from 0.72 s to 0.62 s and from 0.62 s to 0.55 s, with the same checksum (1358032951 with the hash) and peak RSS at about 180 to 200 MB. Instructions fall on every benchmark of the four groups (medians of three interleaved runs): `json/roundtrip` −12.4%, `json/value` −7.5%, `rc/list_pipeline` −2.8%, `rc/map_updates` −4.7%, `rc/record_updates` −3.4%, `rc/string_building` −12.0%, `rc/tree_inserts` −3.0%, the collections group −2.2% to −13.5% (`multimap` −13.5%, `ordered_multimap` −9.7%), and the fiber group −5.4% to −9.9%. In `bench-run`'s wall times (seven runs), the RC group's geometric mean is −3.5% and −3.6% in two runs, with `rc/list_pipeline` at −2.9% and −3.1%; collections are −3.2%, fiber −2.7% and json −10.6%. `rc/record_updates` reads +2.6% and +4.8%, with 3.4% fewer instructions and about 4% more cycles. That is code alignment: a build that differs only in where it writes the list kinds' words, which moves `list::ops`, runs `rc/record_updates` in about 2.48 billion cycles against 2.70 billion for this build and 2.60 billion for D-906. In the slow builds the hot closure of `list::ops::slice_owned` starts at 20 modulo 32 bytes, and in the fast ones at 28. Deriving the list kinds' words from `CustomKind::shape` was kept over writing them by hand next to the registration, because hand-written words and the kind's functions could drift apart and free a block with the wrong size. The design study also measured a JIT-inlined allocation path, estimated at 2 to 3% more on the yardstick, and left it, with the shapes of map and set nodes, for later work; moving the system call of a miss out of line cost 0.12 billion instructions and was rejected. Refines D-279 (freeing reads one dense word per layout instead of a table lookup), D-454 and D-524 (the list kinds declare their shapes), D-739 and D-686 (kernels find the fiber, then read its record), D-772, D-773 and D-691 (charges through the control block's cached slot), D-697 (the inline path runs inside the guard) and D-771 (a refusal that a second request does not repeat is not marked).
- **D-908 A pattern reads its elements and fields before its spreads**: Where an arm's names are bound, lowering reads the positions they bind level by level from the subject down, and within a level it reads every field, tuple element and list element before any spread. Before, it read them in the order of the names. A spread is then the last read of its list, so a list that dies at the match is cut in place (D-634) and the elements it cuts that only it holds are released at the match (D-893). This now holds for every pattern with a bound element after its spread, such as `[..rest, x]`, `[..rest, _, x]`, `[..rest, a, b]` and `[first, ..mid, last]`: the spread used to be read before that element, and so shared the whole tree, which kept alive every element before it too. A matched element's fields are read before a list among them is cut. In `[..below, Frame([first, ..more], depth)]`, the frame is released, keeping its list (D-850), before that list is cut, so the list is cut in place when the frame was unique. Programs can observe the change: an element that the cut releases, or that a name of the pattern binds, no longer lives as long as the spread. A `Sender` in the `_` of `[..rest, _, x]` now closes its channel at the match. One bound by `last` in `[..rest, last]`, by `first` in `[first, ..mid, last]`, or by `first` in that `Frame` pattern, closes it at that name's last use. Before, each stayed open until `rest`, `mid` or `more` died. `tests/ui/09-memory-model/pattern_reads_before_spreads.cheby` pins the four cases. This is what D-893 and §9.2 already required of a list that dies at the match. A parameter matched with `[..rest, x]` is now consumed rather than borrowed, because its last use is the spread (D-806). Why: inside one pattern the order of the reads has no meaning in the source, since the match is the subject's last use (D-205), and reading a spread first kept elements alive that D-893 releases. On its own, against D-907 (8633fcd) on the Apple M3 of D-640 with medians of five interleaved runs, it takes cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip` from 13.49G to 13.39G instructions (−0.8%) with its hash and −1.0% without, and `json/roundtrip` −0.7%. The RC, collections and fiber groups are unchanged. Refines D-205, D-634, D-806 and D-893.
- **D-909 A list pattern step reads its element and its spread in one call**: The runtime helpers for generated code gain `cheby_rt_list_uncons` and `cheby_rt_list_uncons_owned`, both `u64 (ctx*, u64 list, i64 at, i64 start, i64 end, u64 *head)`. Each writes element `at` to `*head` and returns the spread `start..end`, clamped and owned as `cheby_rt_list_get` and `cheby_rt_list_slice` or `cheby_rt_list_slice_owned` give them. `*head` is written only when the call returns normally. The first borrows the list. The second consumes it, even when it raises (D-698), and first checks a constant evaluation's memory budget as `cheby_rt_list_slice_owned` does (D-738). When the root is unique and the spread is the list without `at`, at either end, one descent moves the element out of its slot where every node on the path is unique, leaving the tagged `Int` 0, or dups it where a node is shared, and the root becomes the spread in place. Any other shape is the get and the owned slice. Pattern lowering reads an element next to its spread, `x` in `[x, ..rest]` or `[..rest, x]`, together with it as MIR's `list_uncons(list, at, start, end)`, followed at once by `list_uncons_head()`, which takes the element and runs no code. The RC pass makes it `list_uncons_owned` where the list dies, as D-634 does for slices, and puts what it releases after the step after the head. The MIR validator checks that each `list_uncons` and `list_uncons_owned` defines its result and is followed at once by its head in the same block, that each head follows one, and that the owned form appears only after counting. Code generation passes the address of one 8-byte stack slot per function and loads the head from it, instead of returning it in a second register, so every target's C ABI passes it the same way. Borrowing inference counts a `list_uncons` at its list's last use as consuming, as it counts `list_slice` (D-806). An element and a spread read in different blocks, as when the element's own pattern is tested first, keep the get and the slice. The reuse report reads the pair as the get and the slice it replaces (D-852). What a program observes is unchanged: the element is moved out where D-893 released it with a drop. Why: a step descended the tree twice, once for the get and once to clear the slot, with a dup and a drop of the element. Pattern lowering emits the pair because it knows which element and spread a step reads. A pass that fused a get and a slice after lowering was rejected: it scanned each block quadratically and would have had to re-derive what lowering already knows. Against D-908 alone, on the Apple M3 of D-640, `rc/list_pipeline` goes from 14.91G to 11.99G instructions (−19.6%), `rc/record_updates` −17.3%, `collections/multimap` −2.4%, `json/roundtrip` −1.1% and the yardstick −1.4% with its hash and −1.8% without. Extends D-520 and D-634. Refines D-806 and D-893.
- **D-910 Generated code reads a list's length inline**: MIR's `list_length` compiles to a load of the root's length word at byte offset 24, `cheby_runtime::abi::list::ROOT_LEN_OFFSET`, instead of a call of `cheby_rt_list_length`. D-563 fixes that a root holds its length raw in its fourth word, and the static empty list holds 0 there, so the load needs no test. The offset becomes part of the ABI between generated code and the runtime, which M3's AOT objects and build cache will depend on: a change to D-563's root layout then means rebuilding generated code. The runtime checks at compile time that its root and its static empty list keep the length there. `cheby_rt_list_length` stays for other callers. Why: every list pattern reads the length. Against D-909's build, on the Apple M3 of D-640, it takes `rc/list_pipeline` −3.6% in instructions, `rc/record_updates` −4.2%, `collections/multimap` −2.1%, `json/roundtrip` −1.1% and the yardstick −1.2% with its hash and −1.6% without. D-908 to D-910 together take the yardstick from 13.49G to 13.04G instructions (−3.4%) with its hash and from 10.59G to 10.14G (−4.3%) without, with the same output and peak RSS. For D-908 to D-910 together, in `cargo xtask bench-run --against` D-907 (8633fcd), five interleaved runs each, `rc/list_pipeline` goes from 0.699 s to 0.500 s (−28.5%), `rc/record_updates` from 0.688 s to 0.487 s (−29.2%), the RC group's geometric mean is −13.8% and the collections group's −2.5%, and no benchmark is slower beyond the noise of its runs. Refines D-520 and D-563.
- **D-911 Field reads move up to the consumption of a sibling projection**: At the end of lowering, a pass looks in each block for a statement that consumes a counted field projection `p = field b.i` at `p`'s last use: an owned use other than a called closure, or the list of a `list_slice` or `list_uncons`, which counting makes owned there (D-634, D-909). When every later use of `b` in the block is a field read and `b` is dead at the block's end, those reads move, in their order, to just before the earliest such statement after `b`'s last use of any other kind. Counting then releases `b` there, so `p` is unique when `b` was. In `State { items: list::push(s.items, n), count: s.count + 1 }` and in `State { ..s, items: list::push(s.items, n) }`, the push now writes in place and, under Perceus, `s`'s block is reused. Only a base whose type is handle-free by D-715's test moves. Type variables, `fn` types, `dyn`, external types and types the module cannot look into count as possibly holding a handle. A local with no type, such as a closure's environment or a dictionary, does not move. A moved base releases the fields that nothing reads before the source's last use of the base, and only a handle could observe that (D-205). The pass belongs to lowering, so it also runs under `CHEBY_RC=naive`. Naive mode still turns off only borrowing, constant propagation, reuse and fusion (D-808, D-850): the pass changes no count, only where reads sit, so both modes count the same statements in the same order, and naive counting pushes in place in these shapes too. Borrowing inference sees the moved reads, so a parameter whose projection is now consumed after the parameter's last use is owned (D-806), as the yardstick's `encode_address` parameter is. Drops still sit at the last use in MIR, which is now the last moved read, so the checks of D-716 and D-851 hold as they are. A moved read's result lives across every statement between its new and its old place, so a base moves only while its reads cross at most 128 statements that may unwind in all, counting each read's crossings, the consumer included; past that, the pass tries a later consumer of the same base. The pass is one backward scan per block over liveness computed once per function. `base_used_again` and `kernel_field_of_live_record` in `tests/ui/09-memory-model/reuse_report/` now pass their record on whole, since reading it field by field no longer keeps it alive. `record_update_before_reads` and `kernel_field_before_later_reads` pin the new report lines and the handle rule, and the `cheby_cli` run test `record_field_pushes_write_in_place` checks that neither mode copies. Why: lowering read `s.count` after the push, so `s` still held `items` across it, and every push copied the list. A field read raises nothing (D-767) and reads an immutable object, so moving it earlier in its block changes no result. Running the pass only under Perceus, as `const_prop` runs (D-850), was rejected: it would have to run before borrowing inference in every pipeline, and naive counting would then count MIR in another order than Perceus. Moving the reads of every base was rejected because a `Sender` in a field that nothing reads would close its channel before the base's last use in the source. On the Apple M3 of D-640 with a release `cheby`, against D-910 (271a73c), medians of five runs of 300,000 pushes with compilation included: the field form goes from 1.18G to 0.39G instructions (−67%) and the record update from 1.19G to 0.39G (−67%), with allocations from 1,466,485 to 56,563 and list push copies from 299,999 to 0, and under naive counting both go −63%. The yardstick and the benchmark groups have no such site on their hot paths: the yardstick stays at 13.04G instructions with its hash (−0.08%) and 10.14G without (−0.07%), as D-910 left it, and the RC, collections and JSON groups stay within 0.1% in instructions, so the RC group is unchanged rather than faster. In wall time, `bench-run --against` 271a73c gives geometric means of −0.4% for the RC group, +0.4% for collections, +0.7% for JSON and +0.3% for fiber, all within the noise of their runs; the RC group's ranged from −1.1% to +1.6% over four runs, because `rc/string_building`'s wall time is bimodal on this machine for both binaries while its instructions and allocations do not change. `bench-compile --quick` gives the same code size and RC operations. The yardstick's own module has 8% more MIR lines, because a moved read lives across the statement it moved before and so lengthens cleanup-pad chains, and 0.3% more CLIF. The budget bounds that cost. Without it, a function that pushes onto one field of a 40-field record and then reads every other field after 60 calls that may unwind compiles and runs in 0.31G instructions instead of 0.23G, and one with 800 reads after 1,200 calls in 12.2G instead of 3.0G, nearly all of it in register allocation: the extra work grows with the reads times the calls. With the budget, both stay within 3% of 271a73c. The moved reads also cost a little at run time where the base is shared, since nothing is then updated in place: a shared 7-field record pushed onto and read after four calls, 2,000,000 times, takes 0.9% more instructions under Perceus and 1.3% more under naive counting. Refines D-205, D-716, D-806, D-808, D-850 and D-851.
- **D-912 `cheby run` keeps Cranelift's optimizer off, and a type with one field keeps its own object**: Two rejections from this session's design study. (1) Run mode, the debug profile, keeps Cranelift's `opt_level` at `none` and its default backtracking register allocator, as D-259 says. Cranelift optimization stays with M6's release profile (D-251, D-267). (2) A type with one constructor and one field keeps its own object, with its layout id, instead of being represented as its field. Why: (1) on the Apple M3 of D-640, against 955eaab, `opt_level=speed` changed the yardstick's instructions by +0.2% with its hash and without, and the JSON, RC and collections groups' by −0.2% to +0.7%, with cycles within the noise of the runs. Code size at 100k lines changed by −0.1%: MIR already reaches Cranelift as tight CLIF, and run time goes to the runtime and to call overhead that the optimizer cannot remove. Code generation at 100k lines took 13% to 22% longer, checking with code generation fell from 81k-91k to 71k-75k lines per second per core against D-247's 100k, which M2 already misses (D-889), and startup on an empty input took 11% more instructions. The `single_pass` register allocator compiled about 35% faster but made code 34% larger and the groups 25% to 47% slower in cycles, the yardstick 33%. (2) Representing such a type as its field would break D-454's rule that every uniform value describes itself for the layout-driven equality, hashing and debug printing of D-252. `{x:?}` must show the constructor and its field (D-134), and an erased value inside a `List` or a type variable would print as its bare field, unless debug printing were driven by type descriptors. The gain is small too: `Decoder` has two cases since D-901, so std's only such types are `random::Generator` and `ordered_set::OrderedSet`, an estimated 0.3% of `collections/ordered_set`. Replacing objects that do not escape by their fields inside MIR keeps the representation and is the compatible form; it is left open with tuple returns. Confirms D-259.

Open:

- ~~Rewrite the parser over bytes~~ → D-900.
- ~~Rewrite the writer over one string as its own measured step that keeps the std tests added in this session passing.~~ Done: `json::to_string` and `json::to_pretty_string` add each piece to the end of one string that nothing else refers to, so D-577 extends it in place, and keep the rest of each open array and object in a heap list of frames, as D-326 asks; a string with no byte to escape is added whole. On the Apple M3 of D-640 with a release `cheby`, medians of five interleaved runs against the parser step (14e5c8b), cheby-compare's `json_roundtrip` goes from 2.28 s to 1.51 s with its hash and from 2.11 s to 1.25 s without, with peak RSS unchanged at about 190 to 210 MB, and D-899's `roundtrip` goes from 0.81 s to 0.57 s and `value` from 0.66 s to 0.50 s. The output hashes are unchanged.
- ~~Rewrite the decoders with paths built only on error, as its own measured step that keeps the std tests added in this session passing.~~ → D-901.
- ~~After the rewrites, a static `Some(U8)` table for `bytes::get`~~ → D-902.
- ~~Narrow package-visible kernels in `std::bytes` or `std::string` if profiles still show them, each with its own decision.~~ Done: D-903, D-904 and D-905.
- On JS, the parser of D-900 pays one UTF-8 decode per string or number text, through `string::from_byte_range` (D-905), and one encode of the text through `bytes::from_string`, where native pays a copy. This cannot be measured before M4, so `m4-js.md` asks M4 to measure `std::json` on Node and to give the byte steps JS kernels that work on the string if the conversions dominate. The writer no longer converts (D-903).
- Map and set nodes declare no free shape yet, so they are still freed through their kinds' functions (D-907). They would need a counted shape at other offsets. A JIT-inlined allocation path, with the block cache's free lists and the charge visible to generated code, was estimated at 2 to 3% more on the yardstick.
- An element read in a test block before its spread, as the `Frame` of `[..below, Frame([first, ..more], depth)]` when the inner list's length is tested, keeps a `list_get` and an owned slice (D-909). Reading the spread in the test block too would fuse it, but the list may still be used on the paths that fail.
- Calls of std kernels without their wrapper: a direct call of a std function bound to a plain native kernel would compile to the foreign call at the call site, with each counted argument moved into an owned temporary so that the kernel sees the counts the wrapper gives it (D-525). `@std_context`, `@blocking`, intrinsic and effect-checked externals would keep the wrapper. It needs a change to D-455, which keeps externality out of the module interface, since std's interfaces would then carry the binding. On top of the prototypes of D-906 to D-911, on the Apple M3 of D-640, the design study measured the yardstick at −8.1% in instructions with its hash and −4.9% without, the JSON group at −6% to −9%, and the RC and collections groups at 0% to −2%. Much of the hash gain is the yardstick's own FNV loop through `bytes::get` and `int::from_u8`, not `std::json`. The reuse report (D-717, D-852) would have to learn these calls, and a UI test must pin a kernel's in-place update of a value used afterwards: the prototype once miscompiled `027_lru_cache` before it moved arguments into temporaries.
- ~~Unboxed scalar results of top-level functions, which D-453 lets release builds add and D-734 already signals: deferred to M3's cache design. Whether a function can return unboxed depends on its body's tail calls, through a fixed point over the whole build that keeps D-015, so the result's representation becomes an ABI fact in slot keys (D-456) that M3's cache and callers in other packages must store, or that stays inside a package as D-787's borrowing does. On top of the same four prototypes it measured 0% on the yardstick with its hash and −0.25% without, 0% on the JSON group, −1.4% to −3.6% on the collections group, −2.2% on `rc/tree_inserts` and +0.4% on `rc/map_updates` from D-734's flag load, all in instructions, and −64% to −84% on Float micro-benchmarks, which no benchmark group exercises yet.~~ → D-976
- Returning tuples without allocating them: skipped for now. Replacing a tuple that does not escape by its fields within one function gave −1.1% in instructions on the yardstick against 955eaab, but only −0.05% with its hash and −0.08% without once D-906 removed `read_number`'s tuple, nothing on the RC, collections and fiber groups, and it changed five reuse-report goldens. Most hot tuples are returned through closures, such as a decoder's, which neither form reaches. The design of unpacked-result entry points for direct calls, which D-453 allows, is kept for a workload with hot direct tuple returns.
- Code alignment of the Rust runtime: wall times move by a few percent when unrelated edits shift hot runtime code within the CPU's 32- or 64-byte fetch blocks. `rc/record_updates` read +2.6% to +4.8% after D-905 and D-907 with the same or fewer instructions, because `list::ops::slice_owned`'s closure moved from byte 28 to byte 20 of a 32-byte block. A workspace-wide flag such as `-C llvm-args=-align-loops=64` in `.cargo/config.toml` would pin that placement, at some binary size and with no average gain. It needs its own decision. Take it up if these swings cause false bench-gate failures (D-895) or hide changes of 2–3%.
- Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.

## 2026-10-21: M3 design

Goal: log M3's design from the user's answers to the 25 questions of the M3 plan: the code model and code records, executables and linking, OQ-P9 and foreign code, interface artifacts and the cache, constants, precompiled std, tier 3, the harness, the M5 lane and the plan itself.

The user accepted every draft that only fills a gap in bulk, and every draft that refines a logged decision or changes the spec item by item. Every answer is the recommended option except the panic backtrace (D-939).

- **D-913 Non-PIC code with import words**: Code for the JIT, the cache and executables is compiled with `is_pic=false`. Every generated symbol is colocated, across modules too, so references between generated names are PC-relative. Runtime functions, runtime statics and foreign symbols are reached through module-local import words `cb$imp$<symbol>`: 8-byte data objects with an absolute data relocation, which the code loads and then calls or uses. Cranelift libcalls never appear, and a codegen test asserts that no record has a libcall relocation. Unwind info stays off and frame pointers stay on. A link experiment confirmed the model before any code relies on it: on macOS arm64 with ld64 and on Linux x86-64 and arm64 with GNU ld, two objects that call each other through slots and call a runtime function through an import word link into a PIE with no text relocation and run, and the same bytes load in cranelift-jit through `define_function_bytes`. A platform where import words fail falls back to PIC objects, with our own loader for `cheby run`. Why: cranelift-jit refuses PIC, since it asserts `!is_pic` and panics on GOT and PLT relocations, so PIC objects would need our own loader for `cheby run`. Under `is_pic=true`, Cranelift 0.135.5 also loads every name through the GOT, colocated ones included. The link experiment found that GNU ld on x86-64 and ld64 relax those loads, but GNU ld 2.40 on arm64 keeps one extra load per slot, layout word and string read. Import words leave only PC-relative relocations in text, which PIE and arm64 Mach-O accept, and a runtime call costs one load of its word, as many instructions as today's absolute address. Rejected: PIC objects with runtime and foreign symbols through the GOT (the cost above, and a loader on the critical path); separate code for `cheby run` and executables (against ADR-0047, and twice the compile work); non-PIC objects linked with `-no-pie` (loses ASLR, and arm64 Mach-O needs PIE). Extends D-475. → [ADR-0064](../adr/0064-code-records-and-import-words.md)
- **D-914 One code record per module is the cached artifact**: The cache holds one code record per module, build kind and settings. It holds what `define_function_bytes` and `define_data` take: the machine code, data and symbolic relocations, with a symbol table that gives each symbol's kind and linkage. It also holds the module record (D-919), call sites, source positions and reuse-report lines. A recording implementation of `cranelift_module::Module` produces it, so declaring and defining stay as they are, and declaration order fixes its bytes (D-264). Every host loads records into cranelift-jit over the JIT's arena. Global symbols of the load set are declared by name and each record's local symbols anonymously, so copies in two records never clash. `cheby_link` replays records into `cranelift_object::ObjectModule` for executables (D-915). Fresh code takes the same path: a changed module is compiled into a record, then loaded. Our own loader replaces cranelift-jit only if loading misses OQ-P11's budget (D-974). Why: one artifact serves the JIT and AOT (ADR-0047) without a second code generation. cranelift-jit already takes precompiled bytes and relocations, which the JIT path relies on today, so D-254's "needs a loader of our own" no longer holds. A record does not depend on the object format, so it needs no ELF, Mach-O or COFF parser, and COFF in M6 needs a writer, not a second cache. Rejected: caching native objects, with a loader for each format; our own loader now, before a measurement asks for it. Refines D-254. → [ADR-0064](../adr/0064-code-records-and-import-words.md)
- **D-915 Objects are replayed from records**: For each link, `cheby_link` replays each module's code record into one relocatable object through `cranelift_object::ObjectModule`, at the workspace's Cranelift version. It adds one generated program object, which holds the program image, the C `main`, the trampolines and the stubs of D-943. Objects are never cached. §13.12.4 describes records and objects in an informative note. Why: a record that names nothing specific to a program can be cached and shared (D-248, D-255, ADR-0047), the lowering is already generic over `Module`, and replay is serialization, not a second code generation. Rejected: one object per program (no reuse between builds); a per-program object for every shared symbol (breaks caching per module); CFI for Cheby frames (nothing reads it, D-733).
- **D-916 Calls between Cheby functions go through slots in executables too**: Calls between Cheby functions go through slots (`CallMode::Indirect`) in objects, as in the JIT. Direct calls are reconsidered with the release profile in M6. §13.12.4 notes it. Why: one record then serves executables, the cache and the JIT (ADR-0047). Rejected: direct calls in executables, which need a second code generation per module and double the cache's and the compiler's work.
- **D-917 Linkage of symbols, and masks in slot names**: Definitions that other objects use are global with hidden visibility: package-visible and `pub` functions and their slots, layout words, `cb$ctor$` statics, constant slots and module records. Everything else, import words included, is local, and uses in other objects are imports. Runtime and foreign symbols are named only by import words' data relocations. `main` is the only exported symbol of the program object. D-456 gains three symbol kinds: `mod` for module records, `image` for the program image and `imp` for import words. A slot whose borrow mask is not all-owned carries the mask in its name, `cb$slot$<path>$b<mask in hex>`, and later body-derived ABI facts join that suffix. D-456's REPL suffix `$r<generation>` stays. Why: hidden symbols link across objects without entering the executable's dynamic symbol table or clashing with shared libraries. A caller compiled against a stale mask then fails to resolve, an internal error, instead of calling with the wrong convention. Rejected: `Export` linkage (dynamic and interposable); mangling names into C identifiers now (D-456's names work in ELF and Mach-O); relying on cache keys alone for masks (one key bug would be a silent miscall). Refines D-456.
- **D-918 Code and statics fixed by their key are copied into each object**: In objects, code and read-only statics that their key determines are local copies in every object that uses them: value shims, tuple functions, `dyn` forwarders, `@blocking` shims, dictionaries, descriptors and static function values. Writable or patched objects (slots, layout words, `cb$ctor$` statics and constant slots) are defined only by their own module. The "once per JIT" rules of D-561, D-579 and D-818 stay rules of one JIT, where the copies are declared anonymously (D-914). Debug toolchains run D-579's check, that one symbol has one content, over the copies. Why: copies of objects without identity are harmless, and each object stays self-contained. Rejected: weak symbols (`cranelift-object` has no weak hidden linkage, and our own loader would have to merge them); an owner module per shared symbol (a builtin tuple function has no owner in the program). Refines D-561, D-579, D-818.
- **D-919 Module records, registered by one routine**: Each code record holds, and each object replayed from it defines, a read-only module record `cb$mod$<path>`. It lists the module's layouts in tag order, external types and drop functions, hooks, free shapes, the layout words and `cb$ctor$` statics to patch, the call-site table (D-856) and the constant slots, versioned by `abi::IMAGE_VERSION`. One runtime routine registers module records in build order. An executable's start calls it with the program image, and every JIT host calls it with the records it loaded. Why: data tables are deterministic and cheap, and both hosts then register the same facts the same way. Rejected: a generated initializer function per module (code that runs before the scheduler); call sites from DWARF (line tables are a separate concern, D-938).
- **D-920 Layout ids are assigned at start**: An executable's start registers user layouts and external types in build order, then patches the layout words and the `cb$ctor$` headers, which are therefore writable data. Why: generated code never embeds a layout id (D-454), so objects stay independent of the program. Rejected: ids fixed at link time and baked into data, a step over the whole program that would change cached records.
- **D-921 One fixed CPU feature set per target**: The JIT and objects use one ISA, built from a fixed feature set per target: x86-64-v2 (SSE4.2 and POPCNT) on x86-64, and the base architecture each OS guarantees on aarch64 (Apple M1's on macOS, Armv8.0 on Linux). The JIT stops reading the host's features. The set is in the compiler's source, so the build ID covers it. Why: caches on shared home directories and executables on other machines must run the same code, and Cranelift's scalar code gains little from newer extensions. `bench/runtime` measures the difference once. Rejected: the host's features in the cache key, which would need another compile for every executable.
- **D-922 The load set**: A command loads, in one batch before its entry runs, the records of the modules that hold reached items or the constructors they mention, in build order, std included. Why: registration needs every layout before code runs (D-454), and one batch keeps one owner of call sites (D-867). Rejected: mapping a module on its first call (the layout words of modules not loaded yet), which stays a lever if loading misses OQ-P11.
- **D-923 Changed modules are compiled eagerly and whole**: A cache miss compiles the whole module before `main` starts, its functions in parallel as today. D-254's lazy compilation, each function on its first call, is not built in M3. It is built only if CP3's reading of OQ-P11 shows code generation of changed modules missing the budget. Then a changed module's slots start at stubs, and a first call parks the fiber and compiles that function on a compiler thread with a 256 MiB stack, behind a per-function once-lock. The slot is repointed with a release store (D-712), the call sites are registered (D-867), and lazily compiled functions are never cached. §13.12.4's "compiles changed modules lazily" becomes eager compilation of whole modules. Why: D-779's masks, D-534's constants and D-516's warnings already need a changed module whole, and its code costs milliseconds: about 30 ms for a 20,000-line module on 8 workers. Compiling on a fiber's stack under M:N scheduling is the riskiest mechanism M3 could add, and its records could not be cached whole. Rejected: D-254's lazy compilation in M3 as written; lazy MIR lowering (breaks D-779); dropping lazy compilation from the design. Refines D-254.
- **D-924 The first slice, and `cheby build` before the cache**: The first vertical slice builds 001 as two in-memory code records, its module and `std::io`. It replays them into two objects, adds the program object, the start function and the runtime archive, links on macOS arm64 and Linux x86-64, and checks the exit statuses and that two builds are identical byte for byte. Until the cache stores code records, `cheby build` lowers what `main` reaches (D-510) and compiles from scratch through in-memory records. Then it builds from the cache's records. Programs that reach constants, and packages with `[native]`, get "not available yet" tool errors until their work lands. Why: the slice tests the object split, the start and the link before constants, line tables and foreign code, each of which depends on another workstream, and it already exercises the replay that the cache will feed. The slice must not wait for the cache. Rejected: one object per program first, which postpones the riskiest part, the linkage rules.
- **D-925 An executable's start**: The program object's C `main` calls `cheby_rt_main(argc, argv, &cb$image)` in the runtime archive. It reopens closed descriptors 0 to 2 on `/dev/null`, ignores `SIGPIPE`, installs the image, builds the run configuration and calls `run_main`. It prints a runtime error as `error: …` with status 1, and returns the status. It reads `argv` from `main`'s parameters. §10.10 says that executables ignore `SIGPIPE` and treat closed standard streams as D-474 says. Why: it reproduces exactly the parts of Rust's startup that Cheby relies on, and `run_main` already holds the exit policy. A write to a closed pipe or socket must fail with `EPIPE`, as under `cheby run`, not kill the process. Rejected: `main` inside the archive (an implicit contract on `cb$image`, and a clash with C code that defines `main`); a Rust `main` through `lang_start` (unstable). Refines D-474.
- **D-926 Exit statuses of executables**: An executable exits with `cheby run`'s statuses: 0, 1, 101, 102 with a debug toolchain's archive, 103, and a termination's status. Its `main` returns the status, so libc's `exit` runs after the runtime has stopped. Each toolchain build has one runtime archive, built with the toolchain's profile. A debug toolchain's executables keep the leak check, poisoning and guard pages and tear the runtime down at exit. A release toolchain's executables freeze it, as `cheby run` does. §10.10's leak-check note names executables. Why: the exit criterion compares executables with `cheby run` from the same toolchain, and foreign libraries get their usual exit behavior. Rejected: `_exit` (skips foreign stdio buffers and `atexit`); calling `exit` from the runtime (D-709); two archives chosen by `cheby build`'s profile (D-458 ties the leak check to the toolchain build). Extends D-797.
- **D-927 Runtime settings in executables**: `[runtime]` is baked into the program image at build time. Executables honor `CHEBY_THREADS` (D-658) and `CHEBY_RT_STATS`. The poisoning, guard-page and stress variables work only with debug-assertion archives. `env::program_name()` is the executable's first argument, as D-369 says. §13.3's `[runtime]` bullet says that the table is read at build time for executables. Why: the same program behaves the same under `cheby run` and as an executable, and the harness drives thread counts through the environment. Rejected: reading `cheby.toml` at run time; ignoring `CHEBY_THREADS`; baking the package name in, which a renamed binary would contradict. Extends D-658, D-660. Confirms D-369.
- **D-928 The runtime archive crate `cheby_rt_exe`**: A new workspace crate, `cheby_rt_exe`, is built only as a staticlib. It holds mimalloc as its `#[global_allocator]` outside sanitizer builds, re-exports `cheby_runtime`, and holds nothing else. `cheby_rt_main`, the image installer and the image format live in `cheby_runtime`, so the runtime ID (D-930) covers them, and `cheby_runtime` becomes an rlib only. The workspace then has 21 crates. Executables take D-890's mimalloc from this crate, not from `cheby_cli`. Why: a staticlib fixes its allocator at its own link, and `cheby_cli` already declares one, so the executables' allocator needs a crate root of its own. Rejected: `#[global_allocator]` in `cheby_runtime` (clashes with `cheby_cli`'s and with the runtime's counting-allocator test); a cargo feature (unified across the workspace, the same clash); mimalloc's `malloc` override with the system allocator (process-wide, foreign code included, unlike D-890); embedding the archive in `cheby` through a nested cargo build now. Refines D-284, D-890.
- **D-929 Where `cheby build` finds the runtime archive**: In M3, `cheby build` links `libcheby_rt_exe.a` from the directory of the running `cheby` binary, which is `target/<profile>/` in cargo builds. A hidden `CHEBY_RT_ARCHIVE` overrides the path. A missing archive is a tool error that names `cargo build -p cheby_rt_exe`. No test skips when the archive is missing: every CI job that runs `cheby build` builds it first with `cargo build -p cheby_rt_exe --locked`, and the xtask commands that run `cheby build` build it too. Why: no setup in development, no recursive cargo build, and no evidence for the exit criterion silently dropped from CI. Rejected: embedding through `cheby_cli`'s `build.rs` now (a nested cargo build in another target directory builds the runtime twice and is fragile); building the archive when the user links (needs cargo); skipping `@aot` trials and `@command: build` tests without the archive (hides a missing CI step).
- **D-930 The runtime ID**: `cheby_runtime`'s build script hashes the runtime's sources, `Cargo.lock`, the toolchain file, the target, the features and the `cfg` values it reads into a runtime ID, `abi::RUNTIME_ID`. The archive defines the symbol `cheby_rt_id$<hex>`. Before linking, `cheby build` reads the archive's symbol index, also for an archive that `CHEBY_RT_ARCHIVE` names, and gives a tool error naming the archive and `cargo build -p cheby_rt_exe` when its own ID's symbol is missing. The program object imports the symbol, so a mismatched archive cannot link. The build ID (D-957) includes the runtime ID. Why: after `cargo build -p cheby_cli`, the archive next to the binary can be stale, and its layout constants (D-910) and kernel signatures would then differ from generated code, a silent miscompile that `abi::IMAGE_VERSION` does not catch. The runtime ID changes only when what the archive holds changes. Rejected: the full build ID in the archive, through a crate both depend on (any compiler edit would make the archive stale, and it needs a 22nd crate); checking only when the program starts (the error would come after a successful build); hashing the archive at each build (it is megabytes large, and the expected hash would come from the same build).
- **D-931 The runtime archive and D-031**: The archive next to the `cheby` binary is a development arrangement until M6. M6 embeds the archive in `cheby` and writes it to the cache on first use, keyed by the runtime ID, as `cheby.h` is written (D-945). D-031's single binary and §13's opening sentence stay as they are. Why: it changes no logged decision and no spec text. In M6 the embedding is a step of the release packaging, which builds the archive before `cheby`, so ordinary cargo builds never need a nested build. The cost is a larger binary, by the size of the stripped archive, measured in M6. Rejected: refining D-031 and §13 to "one command, with support files installed beside the binary". Confirms D-031.
- **D-932 Kernels the linker finds by name**: The seven `cheby_float_*` math kernels and `cheby_rt_stats_counting` get exported names. A test checks that every name the runtime's `symbols()` returns is a global symbol of the built archive. Why: the linker resolves by name, where the JIT resolves by address. Rejected: linker aliases, which differ per platform and hide the problem.
- **D-933 How `cheby build` links**: `cheby build` links with `cc` from `PATH`, ignoring `CC`, `LD` and `CFLAGS`. The flags and the input order are fixed per platform. The per-target list of native libraries is a constant that an xtask step checks against `rustc --print native-static-libs`, and the macOS minimum version is the archive's. The linker writes into a fresh private directory inside the output directory, under the output's final basename, and the file is then renamed into place. A missing `cc` and a failed link are tool errors, and linker messages are never parsed. §13.1 notes that `cc` comes from `PATH`. Why: D-088's system linker with the smallest surface, and no input from the environment against D-264. ld64 derives the ad-hoc signature's identifier and `LC_UUID` from the output's name, so a varying temporary name would change the bytes. Rejected: honoring `CC` now; hard-coded library lists without a check (they drift with Rust); parsing linker messages.
- **D-934 Executables are deterministic**: An executable is bit-identical for the same inputs, toolchain, C compiler and linker. The link has a fixed input order, a content-derived build ID on Linux, no debug map on macOS, the final basename as the linker's output name and no build paths in the output. The output's basename is an input. Executables are not cached. `cargo xtask check-determinism` builds each runnable example twice, from different absolute paths and cache directories, and compares the executables. §13.12.6 names executables, and for them the C compiler, the linker and the output's basename are inputs too. Why: the M3 exit criterion and D-264. The link experiment gave identical bytes on all three platforms for one basename, and ld64 outputs that differ in `LC_UUID` and the signature for another basename. Rejected: leaving executables outside D-264; fixing ld64's signature identifier with a flag (it has no documented one; revisited with the bundled linker in M6). Refines D-264.
- **D-935 Platforms for executables in M3**: M3 builds executables on macOS arm64, Linux x86-64 and Linux arm64. On Windows, `cheby build --target native` is a "not available yet (M6)" tool error, and D-641 extends to tests that build or run executables: they carry `#[cfg_attr(windows, ignore = …)]`, as JIT tests do. Everything still builds and passes clippy for both Windows targets. Why: Windows has no fibers until M6 (D-714), and `link.exe`, CodeView and the C compiler's flags are M6's platform work. Rejected: linking on Windows with `link.exe` in CI without running (MSVC flags and archive work three milestones early); macOS arm64 and Linux x86-64 only. Extends D-641.
- **D-936 Builds for the host only in M3**: M3 builds executables for the host platform only, with no target triples. Builds for other platforms come with the bundled linker in M6, which is when D-256's "every supported native platform" applies. §13.1 says so in an informative note. Why: a system linker cannot cross-link. Rejected: target triples now. Refines D-256.
- **D-937 The shape of `cheby build`**: `cheby build [module_path] [--target native|js] [-o PATH]` builds one `main`, chosen by `cheby run`'s rules (D-137, D-464). It writes `build/native/<package>`, or `build/native/<module path with :: replaced by ->` when a module path is given, atomically. The default target is `native` when the manifest allows it. Warnings go to standard error, and success prints nothing. `-j` and `--deny-warnings` apply. It uses the debug profile only, and only the executable goes to `build/`. D-282's interim error ends for `--target native` on the platforms of D-935, and stays for `--target js` until M4. §13.1's row and paragraph and §13.2's `build/` line say so. Why: it mirrors `cheby run` and §13.2's `build/` directory, and `-o` lets the harness and scripts build without writing into a package. Rejected: one executable per `main` (slow and surprising); requiring `--target`; a hidden environment variable for the output directory. Refines D-282.
- **D-938 DWARF line tables**: MIR records a position per statement, code generation calls `set_srcloc`, and every code record keeps the positions. When `cheby_link` replays a record, it writes `.debug_line` and a minimal `.debug_info` through `gimli`, with diagnostic paths (D-421) and no `DW_AT_comp_dir`, so every object carries line tables. Linux executables keep them. macOS executables are linked with `-S` and carry no debug map until a `dsymutil` step or the bundled linker in M6. The cost is measured with `bench-compile` on 100k- and 1M-line projects with positions on and off, and the user decides at CP2 whether records for `cheby run` keep positions; if not, they keep only call sites. §12.1 gains a note on macOS, and §13.12.5 follows. Why: D-259 asks for line-table DWARF, and debuggers and `perf` on Linux use it. On macOS the debug map would point at the link's object files by absolute path and modification time, against D-264. Rejected: no line tables in M3 (against D-259); line tables only for `cheby build` (two record variants); a debug map with relative object paths (it dangles once the link's objects are removed); `dsymutil` on every build (another tool, with unverified determinism).
- **D-939 An opt-in panic backtrace**: M3 adds a panic backtrace that a program run turns on with an environment variable, drafted as `CHEBY_BACKTRACE`. It is built from the frame walk and the call-site map that deadlock reports use (D-856, D-867). It needs no DWARF, changes no golden, and works the same under `cheby run`, `cheby test`, the REPL and in executables. D-259's line tables serve debuggers and profilers, and the backtrace takes its positions from the call-site map. §12.1's sentence on line tables and plan 3.1 say so, and §11.6 gains a paragraph on backtraces. The variable and its values, the output's format, where the walk starts, how frames are named, where the backtrace prints, and its tests are D-1038 to D-1043. Plan package N7 splits into N7a, line tables in wave 5, and N7b, the backtrace in wave 7 after complete module records, off every critical path. Why: the user chose a debugging aid for every host now, at no cost to programs that do not ask for it. Rejected: no backtrace in M3 (the plan's recommendation); a backtrace by default (changes §11.6, D-449 and every panic golden); parsing DWARF when a panic happens (slow and platform-specific, and the call-site map already holds the positions). Refines D-259.
- **D-940 Native code in packages**: A package supplies native code with an optional `[native]` table in `cheby.toml`, with two keys. `sources` lists C files by package-relative path; each must end in `.c`, exist, and stay inside the package after `..` and symlinks are resolved. `libraries` lists system libraries by the name the linker takes after `-l`, each a non-empty string of letters, digits, `_`, `-`, `.` and `+`. No other key is allowed. A violation is E0036, like every manifest error (D-444), and so is `[native]` in a package whose `targets` leave out `native`. Every command validates the table, but only commands that run or link code compile C. There are no build scripts, no prebuilt archives and no other languages in v1. Dependencies follow the same rules from M5, each package's C compiled in that package. §13.3 lists the table, §7.2 says that C files may sit under `src/`, and a new §12.5.4, "Native code in packages", describes it. Why: the smallest surface that runs 020 and §12.5's SQLite example, keeps native code cacheable and deterministic, and works for executables and the JIT alike. The manifest freezes at 1.0, so the smallest surface is the safest. Rejected: build scripts (arbitrary code at build time, against D-057's spirit and D-264); prebuilt static archives by path (platform binaries in git, and archives built without `-fPIC` cannot go into the shared library that `cheby run` needs); system libraries only (020 needs its own C); a `--native-lib` flag or an environment variable outside the manifest (the package would not build by itself). Settles OQ-P9. → [ADR-0063](../adr/0063-native-code-in-packages.md)
- **D-941 Native code under `cheby run`, `cheby test` and the REPL**: Commands that run code in the `cheby` process build one shared library per package with a `[native]` table, from its C objects, a small forwarder object and its `libraries` (`cc -shared -Wl,-z,defs` on Linux, `cc -dynamiclib` on macOS). A package that names only `libraries` gets a library with the forwarder alone. They load it with `dlopen(path, RTLD_NOW | RTLD_LOCAL)` and fill the forwarder's `cheby_ffi_table` with the process's runtime functions, so C code's calls of `cheby_ffi_*` jump through the table. D-455's resolver looks in the runtime's symbols, then in the package libraries in build order, then in the process. A library is never unloaded. A REPL `:reload` that changes a C source loads a new library from a new path, and old code keeps the old one (D-712). `cheby check` compiles no C (D-532). §12.5's first rule on native symbols says that "a linked library" includes a package's native code under `cheby run`, and §13.5 describes reloading. Why: D-455's "the process" stays one rule, and nothing has to be exported from the `cheby` binary, which stable Rust cannot do for executables. Linking the `libraries` into the shared library lets the system's loader find them, without the toolchain guessing file names. Rejected: exporting the runtime's symbols from `cheby` (`-Zexport-executable-symbols` is unstable, and the linker flags differ per platform); `dlopen` of system libraries by guessed file names (`libsqlite3.so` exists only with development packages); building an executable for `cheby run` when native code is present (loses the REPL and tests). Extends D-455. → [ADR-0063](../adr/0063-native-code-in-packages.md)
- **D-942 Compiling a package's C sources**: Each source is compiled once, with the package root as the working directory, by `cc -c -O2 -fPIC -g0 -ffile-prefix-map=<package root>=. -ffile-prefix-map=<header dir>=cheby -I<header dir>`, with `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH=0`. The compiler's output is shown only on failure, which is a tool error that names the source. C objects are cache entries keyed by the source's bytes, the compiler's identity (its resolved path and `cc --version`), the flags in their fixed form and `cheby.h`'s hash. As in ccache's direct mode, the entry records every file the compile read, and a hit needs each of them to have the same hash. The key leaves out the `-I` and `-ffile-prefix-map` paths. The recorded list stores package files relative to the package root, the embedded header as `cheby.h`, and only other files, such as system headers, as absolute paths. The same object serves executables and the shared library of D-941. §12.5.4 shows the compile line in an informative note. Why: one compile for both paths, no stale headers, no paths in objects, and no package or cache path in keys or records, so D-264's comparison of records from two paths and two caches holds and checkouts share entries. Rejected: keying on the source alone (a changed header would not recompile); preprocessing on every lookup (slow); a fixed C standard flag (`-std=c11` hides POSIX functions such as `strdup`).
- **D-943 E0053 for reached symbols, wherever code runs**: One rule decides E0053 for `cheby run`, `cheby test` and `cheby build`, computed before anything runs or links, never from linker messages. E0053 covers the symbols that the externals of reached functions and of reached external types' drop functions name, the same set as today (D-510). A missing symbol that only unreached functions of a whole module (D-966) name binds to `cheby_rt_unresolved`. The JIT and the loader bind it directly. In an executable, the program object defines a hidden stub of that name that jumps to `cheby_rt_unresolved`, so the linker resolves the import word to the stub, and replaying a record stays a pure function of the record. For `cheby build` the resolver sees what the link sees: the runtime's exported names, the package's shared library (its C code and its `libraries`), then the archive's system libraries, opened by the file names recorded next to the native library list. `cheby run` keeps the process as its last step. A link that still fails is a tool error. §12.5's "even if the function is never called" becomes "every symbol that an `@external` of a reached function names", and `E0053.md` describes the build's resolver and that `cheby run` can also find symbols of the toolchain's own libraries. A UI test runs `cheby run` and `cheby build` on one package with an unreached missing symbol and pins one golden. Why: whole modules put unreached externals in every record, which the linker would otherwise reject while `cheby run` stays silent. Diagnostics must not depend on linker messages (D-532). Every program that runs today keeps running, and D-510's reason, that a linker drops unused code too, still holds. Rejected: E0053 for every external of every module in the load set (matches §12.5's text, but a program fails on an unused binding in a module it imports); a stub only in the JIT (run and build would disagree); pointing the import words at `cheby_rt_unresolved` when `cheby_link` replays a record (replay would depend on the link, against the record-hash test and `check-determinism`); parsing linker errors; the compiler's own process as the build resolver's last step (it passes E0053, then the link fails). Refines D-455, D-532.
- **D-944 Names the runtime exports are reserved**: E0055 extends to every symbol the runtime exports, for `@external(native, …)` outside std. A package's C object that defines a name the runtime exports is a tool error before linking or loading. §12.5's third rule and `E0055.md` say so. A toolchain upgrade that adds a kernel can then break a package that used its name, with an error that names the clash. Why: the JIT binds such a name to the runtime, while the linker reports a duplicate or picks one by archive order. Rejected: reserving the whole `cheby_` prefix (D-455 keeps names such as `cheby_sqlite_open` free); leaving it to the linker. Refines D-455.
- **D-945 The `cheby.h` header**: `cheby.h` is embedded in `cheby` and written to the cache's include directory for C compiles. It declares the opaque `typedef struct cheby_value cheby_value;` and functions with the prefix `cheby_ffi_`: `cheby_ffi_dup` and `cheby_ffi_drop` for counts; `cheby_ffi_string_new`, which replaces invalid UTF-8 (D-311), and `cheby_ffi_string_bytes`; `cheby_ffi_bytes_new` and `cheby_ffi_bytes_data`; `cheby_ffi_int_new`, `cheby_ffi_int_get`, `cheby_ffi_float_new`, `cheby_ffi_float_get`, `cheby_ffi_bool_new`, `cheby_ffi_bool_get` and `cheby_ffi_nil` for scalars in generic positions; `cheby_ffi_some`, `cheby_ffi_none` and `cheby_ffi_option_get`; `cheby_ffi_ok`, `cheby_ffi_err`, `cheby_ffi_result_is_ok` and `cheby_ffi_result_get`; `cheby_ffi_list_new`, `cheby_ffi_list_length` and `cheby_ffi_list_get`; and `cheby_ffi_write_stdout` and `cheby_ffi_write_stderr` (D-951). Arguments are borrowed and results owned, as D-187 says. A function that takes a payload takes ownership of it, and a NULL argument means nothing. Passing a value of another type is undefined behavior, which debug runtimes check and stop with a fatal error. There is nothing for user ADTs, tuples, maps, `dyn` values or descriptors. The header only grows. §12.5.1 lists the functions. Why: enough for 020 and §12.5's examples, with a prefix that cannot meet the std kernels' `cheby_<module>_` names. Rejected: user ADTs and tuples (exposes layouts, against D-187); no prefix (`cheby_string_…` would collide with kernels). Refines D-187.
- **D-946 How `cheby.h` finds its fiber, and failed allocations**: A `cheby_ffi_*` function finds the running fiber through the runtime's thread-local accessor (D-686), since foreign functions get no `ctx` (ADR-0055). An allocation that fails sets the unwind flag with D-642's `out of memory` panic and returns NULL. Every foreign function's wrapper loads the flag after the call, releases a non-NULL owned result and unwinds. Inside a drop function, where no wrapper checks the flag, a failed allocation is a fatal runtime error, `out of memory in a drop function`, as §9.5 and D-171 make a panic there fatal. §12.5.1, §11.8.2 and §9.5 say so. Why: D-642 requires a panic, not an abort, but a flag set inside a drop function would surface later in unrelated code. Rejected: aborting on every failed allocation (against D-642); a pending panic inside drop functions; passing `ctx` to foreign functions (changes §12.5.1's C signatures). Applies D-171 to D-642's case.
- **D-947 NULL results of foreign functions**: A foreign function whose result is an external type returns a raw handle, which the wrapper wraps in a D-590 object even when it is NULL, and the drop function is never called with NULL. A NULL result of any other counted type panics at the call, located at the `@external` function's declaration, with the message ``foreign function `<symbol>` returned null``. §12.5.1, §11.6 and §11.8 list the message. Why: under return-flag unwinding a NULL word reads as an unwinding with no record (ADR-0061, R3-ROB-2). Rejected: NULL as `None` (the types would lie); leaving it undefined. Extends D-590.
- **D-948 External values only as direct results**: In v1, foreign code returns an external value only as the function's direct result. A native foreign result type outside std that holds an external type below its top level, such as `Result<Db, String>` or `Option<Db>`, is a compile error at the `@external(native, …)`. It gets a new error code, the next free one (E0079 at this writing), with its explanation in `docs/diagnostics/` and a UI test. Parameters may hold external types at any depth, as opaque values. A type parameter instantiated with an external type is not checked. Std is exempt, because its kernels are Rust and can build any value. §12.5.1 states the rule, and §12.5's SQLite example becomes a Cheby wrapper over a foreign `open_raw(path) -> Db` and an error accessor. Why: C cannot name an external type's layout id, which is assigned at start (D-454, D-590), and every way to name the type from C needs new rules. Without the error, §12.5.1 would accept a signature that no C code can implement. An error can be lifted later, for example by a `cheby_ffi_external_new` that names the type, without breaking anything. Rejected: `cheby_ffi_external_new(type, handle)` now (needs a rule for how C names a type); a C constructor generated per external type (needs a C name per type, which D-461's attribute form cannot give); a warning, or a note that the case is undefined behavior (the program would still compile into undefined behavior).
- **D-949 Drop functions of user external types**: An external type's native drop function is `void f(void *handle)`, checked by E0054 and E0053. `register_external` stores it per layout (D-590), and the external handle kind's finalizer calls it once, inline on the releasing fiber, never with NULL, in cleanup pads too, on the JIT and in executables. Inside it, `cheby_ffi_drop` queues onto the free worklist that is running instead of nesting a free, the two output functions are allowed, and a failed `cheby_ffi_*` allocation is a fatal runtime error (D-946). It should return quickly but may block its worker, as any call without `@blocking` can, and it cannot be `@blocking`. Teardown at exit does not call it. D-703's stricter contract stays the contract of std's finalizers. §9.5 and §12.5.3 state the user's contract. Why: D-703 reserved the finalizer slot for this, data per layout is the smallest change, and running inline keeps §9.5's order, which 020's golden pins. Rejected: one runtime kind per external type (kinds are few, and they would differ by one pointer); running drop functions on the blocking pool (moves the closing point, against D-171 and D-205); forbidding drop functions that block (C cannot be checked). Refines D-590, D-703.
- **D-950 `@blocking` externals from packages**: A package's `@blocking` externals run on the blocking pool through `cb$blk$` shims, as std's do (D-683, D-818). During the job, the pool thread acts for its parked fiber through the thread-local host slot, so `cheby_ffi_*` calls build values charged to the fiber's domain, and a failed allocation is raised on the fiber when it resumes. A cancelled call's owned result is released by the fiber when it resumes, and an external result is wrapped first, so its drop function runs on the fiber. A given-up job's result leaks (D-815). After a give-up or a halt the job is abandoned. Each `cheby_ffi_*` call on a pool thread enters the job under a short per-job lock, the give-up or halt marks the job under that lock, and later calls return NULL (allocations), do nothing (`cheby_ffi_drop`) or return -1 (output). Teardown never frees a domain that an abandoned job names, so borrowed arguments stay readable until the process exits. §12.7's note becomes normative, and §10.9 and §10.10 say so. Why: it answers D-818's two open questions, how foreign code on a pool thread builds Cheby values and how an owned result of a cancelled call is released, and §12.5's own example is `@blocking`. D-816's rule, that the Rust result is dropped before any Cheby value is made, cannot bind C code that keeps running after a give-up. Rejected: forbidding `cheby.h` in `@blocking` calls (a blocking call could not return a string); releasing results on the pool thread (a drop function would run off its fiber, against D-171); invalidating the host slot without a lock (races with teardown). Refines D-797, D-710, D-818.
- **D-951 Output from foreign code**: `cheby_ffi_write_stdout` and `cheby_ffi_write_stderr` write once through the runtime's stream funnel under the stream lock (D-684), in order with `io::println` and inside `cheby test`'s capture of each test. They return 0, or -1 on failure instead of ending the program, as `io::write_stdout` does (D-574). C stdio output has no ordering guarantee. §12.5.1 and §10.10's stream rules say so. Why: 020's golden depends on the order, and a drop function must never trigger D-306's termination. Rejected: flushing C stdio around every foreign call (a cost on every call, and still outside the test capture). Refines D-306, D-684.
- **D-952 Example 020's C code**: Example 020 gets `src/store_ffi.c`, which includes `cheby.h` and implements the five functions that `store.cheby` binds, and its manifest gets `[native] sources = ["src/store_ffi.c"]`. `ffi_handle_store_close` writes its `closing <name>` line with one `cheby_ffi_write_stdout` and releases the name with `cheby_ffi_drop`. `store.cheby`'s docs point to the file, `expected.stdout` is the output in `main.cheby`'s module comment, and `test.toml` drops `skip-run`, so its run, thread variants, `::test` and `@aot` trials are M3 trials (D-472). The standard library adds nothing for 020, and the harness needs no fixture of its own. `testing.md` §1 and the conformance harness's note on fixtures change. Why: examples show the real mechanism, and the C file sits next to the module that binds it, as JS helpers do (D-208). Rejected: a fixture library only for the harness, `LD_PRELOAD` or `DYLD_INSERT_LIBRARIES` (a link channel outside the manifest, only for tests, that does not reach executables).
- **D-953 The interface artifact**: A module's interface artifact is a blob with a magic, the build ID, the interface hash, a section H that holds D-460's canonical encoding byte for byte, and an unhashed section S of item spans. Doc comments join it in M5. D-460's encoding becomes decodable. If its first version cannot be decoded, a second version replaces it, and only then does the interface hash change. The artifact has no compatibility promise across builds. Why: D-249 and D-460 already fix what the hash covers, and when the hashed bytes are the artifact, a reader can check them. Spans must not reach dependents' keys. Rejected: serializing the compiler's interface structure with derived serde (internal ids and interning numbers, against D-264); a second encoding just for the cache; doc comments in the hash (a doc edit would recheck dependents). Refines D-249, D-460.
- **D-954 Warm builds check against decoded interfaces**: On the warm path, dependents are checked and lowered against decoded artifacts only. Debug toolchains round-trip every interface they build, and `check-determinism` compares cold and warm builds. Why: early cutoff is then sound by construction, because a fact that a dependent reads but the encoding misses shows up as a difference. Rejected: trusting the encoding's coverage by review.
- **D-955 Hidden reads are recorded in every phase**: The compiler's view of other modules records every package module whose interface a module reads beyond its imports, in every phase: resolution, checking, lowering and borrow inference. A module's front identity is found in two steps. A front key covers its source and its imports' interface hashes. Under it, a hint names the front end's hidden reads, and the front record sits under the key extended with their interface hashes. Lowering's hidden reads take the same two steps under the front identity, one set for the code unit and one for the tests unit, and the identities they give key the facts, masks, code and tests records. Reads of std are not recorded, since the build ID fixes std. A cache-hit test edits an interface that only a reader's lowering reads and checks that the reader's code misses. §13.12.3 says that hidden reads are recorded and checked. Why: D-260 records hidden dependencies, a verifying trace keeps hits after a branch switch, and lowering reads through its own view, later and often in another command, so its reads cannot join the front identity. Rejected: recording only the front end's reads (an interface edit that only lowering sees would leave stale code under an unchanged key); proving lowering's reads a subset of the front end's (kept only as a debug assertion); the transitive closure of imports (an edit deep in a chain rechecks everything above it), which stays the fallback. Extends D-260.
- **D-956 The cache is a store of blobs and records**: The cache is a content-addressed store of immutable blobs, and of small records that map a key to blobs, with keys hashed by SipHash-1-3 128 over a canonical encoding with a kind tag first. It holds front records, interface artifacts, facts, masks, code and tests records, constant results, C objects, read-set hints and package indexes, under `v1/blob/`, `v1/rec/`, `v1/hint/` and `v1/tmp/`. Why: Go's split of actions and outputs gives early cutoff without a query system (D-249, ADR-0044) and needs no locks. Rejected: one entry per module that holds everything (a body edit would rewrite the interface artifact's entry and defeat sharing); a database file (locks across processes, D-268).
- **D-957 The build ID**: The "compiler version" of D-255 is a 16-byte build ID that a build script in `cheby_cli` computes. It hashes the compiler's and std's sources, `Cargo.lock`, the toolchain file, the cargo profile, the target, the `cfg` flags that change behavior and `abi::RUNTIME_ID` (D-930). It is in every key and every file header of the cache. Why: a version string stays the same across development builds, and hashing the binary at each start costs tens of milliseconds. `cheby_runtime::abi` is among the sources, so D-910's layout constants change the key, and including the runtime ID makes the cache and the archive agree on the runtime. Rejected: the version string; a hash of the executable; a crate that `cheby_cli` and the archive both depend on, with the full build ID in the archive (D-930).
- **D-958 The settings in cache keys**: The settings that change code are the build kind (`run`, `test`, `plain`, `std` or `std-self-test`), `CHEBY_RC`, unwind edges, checked count operations under poisoning, the stack check and the target. `[const-eval]` limits key only constant results. `--jobs`, `--deny-warnings`, `--reuse-report`, color, `CHEBY_DUMP_DIR` and `[runtime]` key nothing. Why: D-255's "flags" must be an exact list, or the cache returns code built for other settings (D-565, D-779, D-813). Rejected: hashing the whole environment (spurious misses).
- **D-959 Where the cache lives**: `CHEBY_CACHE`, an absolute path, names the cache; a relative one is a tool error. Otherwise it is the OS cache directory: `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/cheby` or `~/.cache/cheby` on Linux, `~/Library/Caches/cheby` on macOS, `%LocalAppData%\cheby` on Windows. It is created owner-only, and a root that others can write, or that another user owns, is a tool error. A cache that cannot be created or written makes the command use a fresh cache in the system's temporary directory, created owner-only and deleted at exit, with one note on standard error that names `CHEBY_CACHE` and says the cache is temporary.
