If that fails too, it is a tool error. Every harness process sets `CHEBY_CACHE` to one fresh temporary directory shared by all its runs. §13.12.4 describes the location. Why: D-255's one cache per machine and Go's locations. A temporary cache keeps every environment where `cheby run`, `cheby check` and the REPL work today working, such as a read-only home, some containers and sandboxed CI, and the note says why it is slow. A writable cache takes the normal path, so the latency path is unchanged. An unsafe root stays an error, because a fallback would hide a misconfiguration or an attack, and `cheby run` executes code from the cache. Rejected: a tool error whenever the cache cannot be written; a cache per project (D-255); running without any cache (a second code path for the rule that output never depends on the cache, and it hides the latency budget); a silent fallback.
- **D-960 Writers rename and nothing locks**: Writers write into `tmp/` and rename into place. Nothing locks or calls `fsync`. Every file starts with a magic, the build ID and its length, which a reader checks; debug toolchains also check a blob's hash against its name. A bad file is a miss and is rewritten. Hints and indexes are last-writer-wins. A stress test runs eight `cheby check` processes on one cold cache. Why: deterministic artifacts make concurrent writers write the same bytes (D-264, D-268). Rejected: file locks (stale locks, network file systems).
- **D-961 Trimming the cache**: A command touches an entry it uses when the entry is older than an hour. At most once a day, `cheby check`, `cheby build` and `cheby test` delete entries unused for five days, keeping blobs that a recently used index names. `cheby run` and the REPL never trim. There is no size limit, and `cheby clean` waits for a decision of its own. Why: Go's policy needs no shared index, and the latency path stays free of trimming. Rejected: an LRU with a size cap (a shared index and contention).
- **D-962 The package index**: Each command writes, per package root and command family, an index of every module's keys and records, inline, keyed by a hash of the root's absolute path. A warm command reads it first and opens files of single modules only for what it cannot verify from it. The index is a hint, not an artifact, and never reaches output. Why: one open instead of several per module, which at 4,000 modules saves hundreds of milliseconds. Rejected: per-module files only; an index in the project's `build/` (D-282 lets `cheby check` write only the cache).
- **D-963 Every command hashes every source**: Every command hashes every package module's bytes, in parallel, with the SipHash-1-3 128 that D-538 uses, so each hash is computed once. There is no shortcut through modification times in M3; a stat cache in the index is the lever if the OQ-P11 reading needs it. Std's hashes are computed when `cheby` is built. Why: content hashes cannot be fooled by clocks or coarse timestamps, and 30 MB hash in tens of milliseconds on the worker pool. Rejected: modification times as the source of truth; a faster hash such as BLAKE3 before a measurement asks for it.
- **D-964 What is not cached**: Nothing is cached for a module whose front end reports an error, for a constant that hits the time limit, for the entries unit, for REPL entries and reloads, or for lazily compiled functions if they ever exist. Executables are not cached (D-934). Why: each depends on something outside its key: other modules' names in suggestions, the machine's speed, the program or the session. Rejected: caching errors with the package's module list in the key.
- **D-965 Outputs replayed from the cache**: Diagnostics are cached structured and rendered at replay, so color, D-421's paths and `--deny-warnings` apply as for fresh ones. A span into another module is cached as that module's path and an item, and resolved against its current spans at replay; a debug assertion forbids any other span into another module. W0005 runs over cached use summaries. E0054 runs over cached externals in every build. Reuse-report lines are cached with the code record, and package modules always record their sites. `CHEBY_DUMP_DIR` bypasses reads. Why: output must not depend on the cache's state (D-268, D-516, D-549, D-852), and spans into other modules are unhashed, so a moved item would leave a stale byte range under an unchanged key. Rejected: printing only recompiled modules' warnings and reports; hashing spans into the interface (moving an item would recheck every dependent).
- **D-966 Whole modules are compiled**: A module that holds any reached item is lowered and compiled whole: every function, constant initializer and layout. Reach selects modules and constants. Which externals of a whole module get E0053 is D-943's rule. The driver's MIR and CLIF dumps for goldens keep D-510's view of reached items, and `CHEBY_DUMP_DIR` dumps what the build compiled. Why: a record that depends on the program cannot be shared between programs, with `cheby build` or with the next run (ADR-0047), and D-510's reason, a partial back end, is gone (D-554). Rejected: caching per reached set (keys would depend on the program). Refines D-510.
- **D-967 Reach walks summaries**: The front record holds a summary of each item's references by path and name: for each function, constant, hook and test, the functions, constants, dictionary functions and constructors it mentions. Reach (D-510, D-534, D-593) walks summaries instead of THIR, and gives the load set, the constants each command evaluates and each constant's module set. Why: unchanged modules are then never parsed. Rejected: caching THIR (large, and only reach would read it).
- **D-968 The scope of borrowing inference**: The local package's masks are inferred over the code units of the load set's modules, and in test builds over the tests units of the modules with selected tests. Every other package, std now and dependencies from M5, is inferred over all its modules, whatever the program imports. The entries unit contributes no facts. §13.12.2 names the scope. Why: masks of other packages then never depend on the program, so std has one code per build ID, and calls across packages are owned anyway (D-812). Keys handle the local package's variation. Rejected: the whole local package as the scope (a cold run would lower every module); masks that depend on every package at once (std could not be precompiled). Refines D-779.
- **D-969 Borrowing facts, masks records and code keys**: Each module caches the borrowing facts its lowered MIR contributes and the list of package-visible functions of other modules it calls, keyed by its code identity. Its masks are cached per kind under the digest of the build's facts about its own functions. Its code key holds its own masks and its callees' masks. If the facts turn out not to be a union of per-unit parts, the facts record stores each unit's inputs instead. Why: D-787 and D-812 make callers depend on masks, and this makes that dependency exact, so an edit that changes no mask recompiles only its module. Rejected: masks in the interface artifact (facts from bodies in the interface, against ADR-0044); no borrowing across modules in cached builds (run and build code would differ).
- **D-970 A module's tests unit is whole**: A module's tests unit holds all its tests, and a filter selects only which entries run. Why: a module's tests unit then does not depend on which of its own tests are selected. Masks still vary with the set of modules that have a selected test, since their tests units join the inference scope (D-779), and the masks key's facts digest covers that. Rejected: one unit per selection (a cache entry per filter).
- **D-971 The REPL runs std's normal code**: The REPL compiles package modules in the `plain` kind and runs std's normal code, so D-808's "std included" narrows to package modules. Every command, `cheby check` included, evaluates constants on `plain` code (D-978), so `cheby check` never infers masks. Why: a REPL entry calls only std's `pub` functions, which are never borrowed (D-787), and cannot redefine a std item, so std's masks are safe there. Rejected: a borrow-free std for the REPL (a second std compile for no gain). Refines D-808.
- **D-972 Determinism with a cache**: The cache's blobs and records are artifacts under D-264. `cargo xtask check-determinism` builds every example and benchmark shape into two fresh caches from two absolute paths and compares `blob/` and `rec/` byte for byte; `hint/` holds indexes keyed by paths and is left out. C objects are included without exception, since their keys hold no path and their records hold normalized header paths (D-942); this holds for one C compiler on one machine. Each example is then built again warm into one of the caches, and its exit status, outputs and constant values must equal the cold build's. The cold build runs with `CHEBY_DUMP_DIR`, so today's dump comparison stays. §13.12.6 names the cache's contents. Why: D-264 covers artifacts, and D-268's "bit-identical to a fresh process" covers warm builds. Rejected: comparing dumps only; leaving C objects' records out of the comparison. Refines D-264.
- **D-973 Cache-hit tests, and the exit criterion's wording**: A hidden `CHEBY_CACHE_LOG=<file>` lists every lookup as a hit or a miss, sorted, at the end of the command. Driver tests cover a body edit that changes no mask, a mask-changing edit, an edit to a body a constant reaches, an interface edit, a second run with no edit, an interface edit that only a reader's lowering reads, a constant near the memory limit across `cheby check` and `cheby run` in both orders, and a cached note into a module whose item moved. M3's exit criterion reads: "Editing a function body recompiles only its module, and the modules of its package that call a function whose borrow mask the edit changed (D-812), checked by a cache-hit test." Why: tests based on timings are flaky, and the criterion as written cannot hold once masks change (D-812), as §13.12.3 already says.
- **D-974 How OQ-P11 is read**: OQ-P11's latency is read on GitHub's `ubuntu-24.04` x86-64 runner, the reference machine of D-1035, with D-640's Apple M3 reported beside it. It uses a release `cheby`, a fresh `CHEBY_CACHE` per shape and `bench/gen` projects of 1M lines with seed 1, in two shapes: `wide`, whose `main` reaches about ten modules, and `deep`, whose `main` reaches all 4,000 modules. One cold run is timed and reported, not budgeted. Then come five rounds of an edit to one function body in a module `main` imports, followed by `cheby run`, and five rounds of an edit to `main`'s own body; each reading is the median of its five whole-command times. A hidden `CHEBY_PHASES=<file>` gives each phase's time. The budget is D-247's 500 ms on both shapes. At CP3 the breakdown decides three fallbacks: lazy compilation (D-923) if code generation dominates, our own loader (D-914) if declaring symbols dominates on `deep`, and a stat cache (D-963) if hashing dominates. OQ-P11 settles at M3's exit with the readings; if they miss, the user revises the budget from the numbers, as D-644 did. Why: `wide` alone barely exercises loading, the part M3 adds, and the exit criterion names a machine that no decision defined. Rejected: only D-644's one `wide` reading; enforcing the budget before M6 (D-644). Refines D-644.
- **D-975 D-247's throughput keeps its reading**: D-247's throughput is still read in memory, with `cargo xtask bench-build`, without the cache. `bench-compile` adds two reported columns, a cold `cheby check` and a cold `cheby build`, both writing to an empty cache. M3's exit reads D-247 from the existing column. Why: readings stay comparable with D-644 and D-889, and the cost of writing artifacts becomes visible. Rejected: redefining the reading to include the cache.
- **D-976 No deferred performance item lands in M3**: None of the three performance items D-912 left with the cache design is built in M3. Direct calls of std kernels are the first performance step after M3's exit, with a decision that refines D-455; the cache makes them free to invalidate, since std is keyed by the build ID. Unboxed scalar results wait for M6's release profile or a workload heavy in `Float`, as one more field beside the masks in code keys and slot names (D-917). Tuple returns stay skipped (D-912). The rule that std's facts from bodies may cross the package boundary is written, with an ADR, by whichever of the first two lands first. Why: M3's exit criteria are large already, and the design leaves each item one field or one decision away. Rejected: direct kernel calls in M3, on its critical path or as an optional package after its exit criteria.
- **D-977 Each constant's result is a cache entry of its own**: Each constant's result is its own cache entry, keyed by the build ID, the evaluation kind and settings, the package's `[const-eval]` limits and the exact bytes that D-538 and D-593 hash into its seed. The seed keeps hashing the reached modules' sources; D-538's option of interface hashes is not taken, and §13.9's note about it goes. Module code reads constants only through `cb$const$` slots, never as folded values (D-257). A failure is cached with its diagnostics, except a time-limit failure. §13.9's "Results are cached" names the key. Why: a body edit then re-evaluates only the constants that reach it and recompiles no other module, as the exit criterion, D-257 and §13.9 ask. The key needs the bodies anyway, and switching the seed to interface hashes would only change every seed, while a body edit could still change the value. Rejected: values as data in their module's record (any reached body edit recompiles it); evaluating constants at program start (D-090); interface hashes in the seed. Refines D-538.
- **D-978 Constants are evaluated on `plain` code**: Every command evaluates constants on `plain` code, the package's modules without borrowing and std's one code, in a JIT module of its own that is dropped once the images are written. The kind and the settings are in the constant's key. D-617's memory peak is measured on that code, and `E0076.md` says so. A cache test evaluates a constant whose peak is just under the limit with `cheby check` cold and `cheby run` warm, and in the reverse order, and compares the verdicts. Why: borrowing changes when values are dropped and whether reuse in place happens, so the peak, and with it E0076, depends on the kind. Kinds per command would make the cached verdict depend on which command filled the cache first (D-268) and let `cheby check` disagree with `cheby build` (D-459). `plain` is the only kind whose code does not depend on the program. The cost is that a constant miss in `cheby run` or `cheby test` also compiles the modules it reaches in `plain`. Rejected: each command's own kind with the kind and a masks digest in the key (check and build still disagree, and no result is shared across commands); a borrowed kind for constants (its masks would depend on the roots). Refines D-459, D-617.
- **D-979 Constant images, installed before `main`**: A constant's result is stored as an image that describes values, not memory: objects in post-order from the root, children in field order, with references to its own objects, to runtime statics by symbol and to objects of other constants' images. Strings and bytes are stored inline, user constructors by type key (D-559) and constructor index, lists as their elements, and maps and sets as their entries in the evaluation's order. An image holds no layout id, address or seed. Every run installs constants from images before `main`, fresh results included, in D-535's order: static objects with count 0, type keys resolved to registered layout ids, lists built, maps and sets built with the process seed (D-196), and slots written. Executables call the same routine. D-539's freezing in place remains only inside evaluation. §9's note on constants describes installation from images. Why: images stay independent of the runtime's memory layouts and of the process, sharing survives across constants as D-539 asks, and cold and warm runs take one path. Rejected: memory-layout images with relocations (they tie images to the runtime's layouts, and maps and sets need a rebuild anyway); generated code that builds the values; freezing fresh results in place beside images for cached ones (two paths to test). Refines D-539.
- **D-980 Std is compiled into the cache on first use**: In M3, std is compiled into the global cache on first use, keyed by the build ID, through the same code path as any package, as one inference unit over every std module. A first use lowers all of std for its facts, then generates code only for the std modules the load set holds; this costs about 0.15 s once per machine and toolchain. Std's sources stay embedded, because diagnostics, D-538's seeds and later tools need the text. A warm build reads std's artifacts and records instead of checking std, and D-450's rule, that a build checks only the std modules its imports reach, applies only on a miss. `cargo xtask test-std` uses the `std-self-test` kind, so std's tests never change the code user programs run. A pre-filled std cache and std code for other platforms are M6 packaging, which is when D-256's "ships" applies. §13.12.4 says that std is compiled into the cache. Why: one mechanism for std and packages, and no bootstrapping step in the cargo build. Rejected: compiling std in `cheby_cli`'s build script and embedding it (builds the compiler twice in every cargo build); a directory of precompiled std next to the binary (the same bootstrapping, and against D-031 as D-931 reads it). Refines D-256, D-450.
- **D-981 Tier 3's IO error kinds**: File and socket functions return `io::Error`. Tier 3 adds to `std::io` one predicate, one fixed `show` text and one kernel code for each kind its functions cause, continuing D-573's numbering: 4 `is_already_exists` (`already exists`), 5 `is_a_directory` (`is a directory`), 6 `is_not_a_directory` (`not a directory`), 7 `is_directory_not_empty` (`directory not empty`), 8 `is_connection_refused` (`connection refused`), 9 `is_connection_reset` (`connection reset`, also for `ECONNABORTED` and a socket's `EPIPE`), 10 `is_address_in_use` (`address in use`), 11 `is_host_not_found` (`host not found`, any failure of name lookup), 12 `is_closed` (`closed`, a `File` used after `close` or after its `with_*` call) and 13 `is_invalid_input` (`invalid input`: a path with U+0000, a port outside 0 to 65535, or a handle in the wrong mode). Kinds 1 to 3 stay as D-573 has them, and every other failure stays kind 0 with the operating system's message. Equality and hashing follow D-368. `std::file` and `std::net` build their errors with `std::io`'s package-visible `lift`, so `Error`'s representation stays inside std (D-484). Kind 13 also applies to `std::io`'s whole-file functions when a path contains U+0000, which D-573 gave kind 0 with Rust's text. `docs/stdlib/io.md` lists the ten predicates. Why: one IO error type, a predicate for each kind a function can cause, as D-367 foresaw, and fixed texts so goldens are the same on every platform (D-345). Resolvers' codes differ between platforms and their texts are not stable, so every failure of name lookup is one kind. Rejected: `file::Error` and `net::Error` (§5.10's `Result<Nil, io::Error>` and D-367 point away from them, and a program that reads a file and a socket would match two error types); raw operating-system codes (they collide with codes 1 to 3); a predicate for every kind Rust has (D-367). Refines D-573. Extends D-367.
- **D-982 Tier 3's handles**: `file::File`, `net::Listener` and `net::Connection` are external types of their modules without an `@external`, whose drop functions the runtime supplies, as D-647's are. They are runtime kinds 45, 46 and 47, each with the handle flag and a finalizer (D-703). `File`'s finalizer closes the descriptor and ignores the error; there is never data to flush (D-994). The socket finalizers remove the socket from the poller and close it without `SO_LINGER`, so the close never waits. A `Connection` that carries TLS drops the TLS state without sending `close_notify`, since a finalizer cannot write and HTTP/1.1 frames its bodies. They print as `<File>`, `<Listener>` and `<Connection>`, `==` and hashing panic on them, W0011 warns about comparing them, and E0068 keeps them out of constants (D-233, D-518). The handle lists of §9.5, §12.5.3 and `docs/stdlib/README.md` name them. Why: the tier-2 handle machinery already gives the placeholder printing, the panicking `==` and finalization once, and D-703's contract fits these finalizers exactly. Rejected: user-style external types with C drop functions inside the runtime (needs plumbing at the level of `cheby.h` for std); one shared kind for all three (cannot tell them apart when printing, and needs a tag test in every kernel). Extends D-647.
- **D-983 Where tier-3 operations run**: `std::file`'s opening, closing, writing, directory functions and a read that empties the handle's buffer run on the blocking pool (D-683); a cancellation meanwhile waits for the work, then releases its result (D-670, D-816). `read_line` and `read_bytes` served from the handle's buffer run on the calling fiber's thread. Name lookup in `net::listen`, `net::connect`, `http::get` and `http::send` runs on the blocking pool and cannot be interrupted. `net::accept`, `connect`, `read`, `write` and every network wait of `std::http` wait on the poller (D-680, ADR-0059), and a cancellation interrupts them at once. `time::sleep` waits on the timer heap (D-682) and can be interrupted. `time::now`, `time::monotonic`, HTTP head parsing and TLS record work are ordinary kernels on the calling fiber's thread. `process::exit` is a termination (D-709). No tier-3 kernel is `@blocking`: the suspending ones are `@std_context` kernels that submit their own pool job or register their own wait (D-692, D-819). Without a scheduler a pool job runs inline, and a socket wait or a sleep ends the program with a fatal runtime error that names the operation, so on Windows until M6 `std::net`, `std::http` and `time::sleep` are fatal while `std::file` works (D-714). §10.9 says that name lookup runs on the blocking pool and cannot be interrupted. Why: D-042 and §10.9 already fix this split, and this states it for each function. Rejected: file IO on the poller through io_uring (D-680); sockets on the blocking pool (cannot be cancelled, and caps concurrency at 512 threads, D-683); `@blocking` kernels for files (lose the read-ahead buffer and D-816's check); a name resolver on the poller.
- **D-984 Sockets on the poller**: `mio` is built with the `net` feature. The poll driver keeps a `mio::Registry` clone outside the poller's lock, so a fiber can register a socket while a worker blocks in `poll`. Each socket is registered once, when it is created, for reading and writing, edge-triggered, and its finalizer deregisters it. Each socket has a reference-counted heap entry with its token, readiness bits per direction and one parked fiber per direction, and a token carries a generation, so a late event for a closed socket finds nothing; wait nodes never live on a fiber's stack (D-708). A kernel tries the system call first. On `WouldBlock` it arms its direction, which stores the fiber and adds one to its domain's pending-IO count, checks the readiness bits again and parks. The poll holder sets the bits and wakes the waiting fiber, and the count drops after that wake, as a timer's does (D-857); a cancellation that wins the wait disarms it and drops the count. On each tick the monitor polls with a zero timeout when no worker holds the poller, as D-789 made it fire timers. D-724's, D-857's and D-860's "IO registration" means a wait that a parked fiber armed, not a registered socket. D-858's wait operations stay as they are, so a fiber parked on a socket or in `time::sleep` never appears in a deadlock report. The runtime's Windows stubs get the same interface. Why: this is the seam D-681 promised, it reuses the timer heap's rules for frozen fibers, and it keeps D-858's report text. A socket that no fiber waits on cannot wake anyone, so counting registered sockets would hide a real deadlock in a program that holds an idle `Listener` while its fibers block on channels. Rejected: an IO thread per runtime (a hop on every wake, against ADR-0059); polling only from idle workers (starves sockets under CPU load, as D-789 found for timers); counting registered sockets; new wait operations for socket and sleep waits (a report can never print them). Refines D-724, D-857, D-860.
- **D-985 Tier 3's kernel boundary**: Every public tier-3 function is written in Cheby over private kernels named `sys_<name>` and bound as `@external(native, "cheby_<module>_sys_<name>")` (D-484, D-755, D-793). Kernels never call a Cheby closure: `with_open`'s body, `serve`'s handler and every other callback is called from Cheby (D-675). Kernels return `Result<T, (Int, String)>` with D-573's codes as D-981 extends them. The kernels are: for `std::file`, `sys_open(path, mode)`, `sys_close`, `sys_read_line`, `sys_read_bytes`, `sys_write`, `sys_write_bytes`, `sys_list_dir`, `sys_create_dir`, `sys_create_dir_all`, `sys_remove_file`, `sys_remove_dir`, `sys_rename` and `sys_info`; for `std::net`, `sys_listen`, `sys_local_port`, `sys_accept`, `sys_connect`, `sys_read`, `sys_write`, `sys_shutdown_write` and the package-visible `sys_connect_tls`; for `std::http`, `sys_parse_request_head` and `sys_parse_response_head`, which return the parsed head as Cheby values and never wait; for `std::time`, `sys_now`, `sys_monotonic` and `sys_sleep`; for `std::process`, `sys_exit`; and for `std::fiber`, the package-visible `cancel_members` (D-1012). Why: the pattern of tiers 1 and 2, and the smallest native surface. Rejected: a Rust HTTP client or server as kernels (D-1003); public kernels, which tie the runtime's ABI to the public API (D-755).
- **D-986 Tier 3's JS bindings are declared now**: Every tier-3 kernel declares its JS binding now, `@external(js, "./<module>.mjs", "sys_<name>")`, to a file M4 writes, so every function exists on both targets for the cross-target check. Kernels whose host API returns a `Promise` also carry `@async`. A kernel may carry both `@async` and `@std_context`, since `@async` affects only JS and `@std_context` only native; §4.8 says so. Every std `@async` kernel resolves with the `(kind, message)` error pair instead of rejecting, because a rejection panics the fiber (D-188) while an IO failure must be `Err`. Why: without the bindings, every package that keeps the default `targets` and imports a tier-3 module gets E0067, as D-450 and D-661 found for tiers 1 and 2. Rejected: declaring the bindings in M4; restricting tier 3 to `@target(native)`. Extends D-661.
- **D-987 The order in which tier 3 lands**: Tier 3 lands in this order, each step with its tests: `std::time`; the poller's socket seam (D-984) with `std::net`; `std::file`; `std::http`'s client, which unblocks 031; `std::http`'s server, which std's own client tests need; `std::process`. Each `std::io` predicate lands with the first function that can cause its kind. TLS comes last within the client, so 031 passes on plain HTTP first. Why: 031 is an exit example, and every step is testable on its own. Rejected: the plan's table order (`file`, `net`, `http`, `time`, `process`), which delays 031 behind directory functions it does not use.
- **D-988 No additions to `std::env`**: Tier 3 adds nothing to `std::env`: no `set`, no `remove` and no listing of every variable. Why: nothing needs them, `env::set` is `unsafe` in Rust 2024 while other threads run and changes state every fiber shares, and a later process spawner can give a child a changed environment. Rejected: `env::set`; `env::vars`.
- **D-989 No std module stays unshipped**: When tier 3 ships, the compiler's list of planned std modules is empty. D-480's E0038 for an unshipped std module stays in the compiler, and its probes, which use `std::http` since D-729, move to a unit test with a synthetic planned module. The UI test `unshipped_std_module`, the CLI's and driver's probes and `E0038.md`'s example are rewritten or removed. Why: a later tier will need the rule again, and a fake catalog entry is enough to test it. Rejected: keeping one tier-3 module unshipped as a probe, which ships an incomplete tier. Refines D-729.
- **D-990 Std's network tests**: Std's own tests of `std::net` and `std::http` run over the loopback address, with ports the operating system chooses, and servers in detached fibers that `cheby test` tears down when the test ends (D-723). A test that needs the internet or a particular resolver is an _Example_. `cargo xtask test-std` runs them on CI. Why: loopback works on every CI runner and in sandboxes without a network. Rejected: only _Example_ blocks (leave the server and the client untested); a fixed port (collides between tests run in parallel).
- **D-991 Tier 3 in constants**: No tier-3 kernel joins §4.5's list of kernels that constants may call. `std::time`'s functions other than `now`, `monotonic` and `sleep` are Cheby without kernels, so they run in constants, as `std::duration`'s do (D-650). `http::json`, `text`, `with_header`, `header`, `request_header` and `method_name` are Cheby over `std::json`, `std::list` and `std::string`, so they run in constants too, and a constant may hold a `Response`. Why: D-090 and D-537 already decide this, and the pages state it per function. Rejected: clock readings in constants (D-090).
- **D-992 `std::file`'s three openers**: `file::open(path)` opens for reading, `file::create(path)` creates or truncates and writes, and `file::append(path)` creates if needed and appends. Each has a scoped form, `with_open`, `with_create` and `with_append`. §5.10's example, which writes through `file::with_open`, uses `file::with_create`. Why: each name says what it does, reading is the safe default for the name `open`, and six names cover every common case with no flags. Rejected: a mode argument such as `with_open(path, file::Write)` (an exposed ADT that freezes at 1.0, and an argument between the path and the callback); `with_open` opening for reading and writing without truncating (matches §5.10 literally, but overwrites the start of an existing file and keeps its tail).
- **D-993 Scoped opening**: `with_open<T>(path: String, body: fn(File) -> Result<T, io::Error>) -> Result<T, io::Error>` opens the file, calls `body` with it and closes it when `body` returns; `with_create` and `with_append` are the same. When `body` unwinds, the handle closes at its last reference, as every handle does (D-101), since Cheby has no `finally` (ADR-0029). A failure to close is returned only when `body` returned `Ok`. A handle that `body` lets escape is closed all the same, and every later operation on it fails with `io::is_closed`. `file::close` closes a handle early. Why: the handle already closes at its last use (D-205), so what `with_open` adds is a close whose error is reported and a hard end to the handle's life, which is D-101's "explicit scoped closing". Rejected: a `wrap: fn(io::Error) -> E` parameter, as 030 assumes for `sql::with_connection` (§5.10 does not write it, and it puts an argument before the callback); a `body` of any `T` with `Result<T, io::Error>` outside (nests results under `use`, so §5.10's function would no longer check).
- **D-994 File writes are unbuffered**: Writes to a `File` are not buffered. Each `write`, `write_line` and `write_bytes` is one blocking-pool job that returns once the operating system has the data, so a write that returned `Ok` is in the file and the drop function never has anything to flush. Reads are buffered inside the handle (D-995). There is no `flush`. Why: a write that returned `Ok` is in the file, every target behaves the same, and the drop function stays a plain `close`. The cost is one pool round trip per call, which a program avoids by building its text first. Rejected: buffered writes flushed by the drop function (it would block and could fail, where D-171 and D-703 allow neither); buffered writes whose drop function hands the flush to the pool as a job nobody waits for (loses its error, and races a program that reads the file back right after the handle's last use); buffered writes with an explicit `flush` and silent loss at drop; a separate buffered-writer type now (more API than v1 needs). → [ADR-0065](../adr/0065-unbuffered-file-writes.md)
- **D-995 Read-ahead in file handles**: A handle opened for reading reads ahead in blocks of 64 KiB, and serves `read_line` and `read_bytes` from that buffer on the calling fiber's thread without the pool, as standard input's reader does (D-548, D-823). Only a read that empties the buffer is a pool job. The size is informative. Why: the shape of standard input, which M2 already measured. Rejected: one pool job per call (a thread handoff per line).
- **D-996 `std::file`'s functions and paths**: `std::file` has `open`, `create`, `append`, `with_open`, `with_create`, `with_append`, `close`, `read_line`, `read_bytes`, `write`, `write_line`, `write_bytes`, `list_dir`, `create_dir`, `create_dir_all`, `remove_file`, `remove_dir`, `rename` and `info`, and nothing else. `Info` is opaque, with the accessors `size`, `is_file`, `is_dir` and `modified`, whose parameter is named `entry` because `info` is a function of the module (D-051, E0044). Paths are `String`s, and `/` is accepted on Windows. Tier 3 has no path functions and no way to read or change the working directory. Why: the common operations, and every later addition breaks nothing. Rejected: an exposed `Info` record (its fields freeze at 1.0, D-075, while operating systems keep adding facts); `exists(path) -> Bool` (hides permission failures); a `std::path` module now; `file::set_current_dir` (changes state every fiber shares).
- **D-997 Directory listings**: `list_dir` returns entry names, not paths, sorted by their UTF-8 bytes, without `.` and `..`. A name that is not valid UTF-8 makes the call return `Err` for which `io::is_invalid_data` holds. Why: the same listing on every platform, which goldens need. Rejected: the operating system's order (differs between platforms and file systems, D-232); skipping or replacing names that are not UTF-8 (D-311 forbids it for functions that return a `Result`); full paths (callers can join them).
- **D-998 `std::net`'s scope and addresses**: `std::net` is public and has TCP only: `Listener`, `Connection`, `listen(host, port)`, `local_port`, `accept`, `connect(host, port)`, `read`, `write` and `shutdown_write`. A host is a `String`, an IP address such as `127.0.0.1` or `::1` or a name the operating system resolves. A port is an `Int` from 0 to 65535, and 0 asks the operating system for a free port, which `local_port` reports. A port outside that range is `Err` for which `io::is_invalid_input` holds, not a clamped value. Why: the smallest API that serves `std::http` and std's tests, and an out-of-range port is a wrong argument whose failure the caller must see (D-297). Adding UDP or other protocols later breaks nothing. Rejected: sockets private to `std::http` (the plan lists the module, and other protocols need it); UDP now; an `Address` type with a parser; clamping a port (D-298); `"host:port"` strings (they need IPv6 brackets and a second parser).
- **D-999 Reading and writing on a connection**: `read(connection, limit)` returns `Ok(Some(data))` with at least one and at most `limit` bytes as soon as any are available, and `Ok(None)` once the peer has closed its side, as `io::read_line` reports the end of input (D-305). `write` returns only when all of its data is handed to the operating system. Why: the two calls every stream protocol is written with, in the shape `std::io` already has. Rejected: a read that fills exactly `limit` bytes (blocks a protocol that does not know the length); an empty `Bytes` at the end (confusable with a read of zero bytes); a `write` that returns how much it wrote (pushes the retry loop onto every caller).
- **D-1000 Cancelling socket operations**: Name lookup runs on the blocking pool and cannot be interrupted (D-670). `accept`, `connect`, `read` and `write` wait on the poller and can be cancelled. A cancelled `accept` or `read` has taken nothing, and a cancelled `connect` leaves no connection. A cancelled `write` may have sent a prefix of its data, after which the connection is still usable. Why: D-670's "a cancelled operation takes and delivers nothing" holds for reads, and the one operation that cannot promise it says so. Rejected: writes that are all or nothing (the operating system cannot give them); waits that cannot be interrupted (`fiber::timeout` would be useless for network code).
- **D-1001 Several fibers on one connection**: Several fibers may use one `Connection`. Reads are serialized with each other and writes with each other, and a read and a write may proceed at once. Each `write` is atomic with respect to other writes on the connection, as each call on standard output is (D-684). Several fibers may `accept` on one `Listener`. Why: a handle is shared, not owned (D-647), and full-duplex use is common. Rejected: one fiber at a time per connection (blocks a reader behind a writer); no ordering promise (two writes could interleave inside each other).
- **D-1002 `TCP_NODELAY`**: Connections from `connect` and `accept` have Nagle's algorithm off. Why: the operating system's default delays the second small write of a request-response protocol by up to 40 ms, and HTTP sends small heads. Go does the same. Rejected: the operating system's default.
- **D-1003 `std::http` is written in Cheby over `std::net`**: On native, `std::http` is written in Cheby over `std::net` and `std::fiber`, with kernels only for parsing request and response heads, through the `httparse` crate, and for TLS. A handler is an ordinary Cheby call on a fiber, and every wait is a socket wait on the poller, which `fiber::timeout` can cancel. On JS, private kernels bind to `fetch` and the host's server API (D-207), in M4. Why: D-274 puts in Cheby what can be written over kernels, handlers become ordinary Cheby calls, and cancellation works through the poller. The cost is an HTTP/1.1 implementation of a few hundred lines of Cheby that M3 writes and tests. Rejected: a Rust client such as `ureq` on the blocking pool (cannot be cancelled, D-670, so 031's `fiber::timeout` would wait for every slow fetch, and concurrency is capped at the pool's 512 threads, D-683); `hyper` on a Tokio runtime inside the Cheby runtime (a second scheduler, which would call `serve`'s handler from a kernel, against D-484 and D-675); parsing heads in Cheby (a byte-level parser in std before the optimizer is measured). → [ADR-0066](../adr/0066-http-in-cheby-over-std-net.md)
- **D-1004 `Request` and `Response` are exposed records**: `Request` and `Response` are `pub exposed` records and `Method` a `pub exposed` ADT, plain data with structural `==`. `Request` has `method`, `path`, `segments`, `query`, `headers` and `body`. `path` is the request target's path as received, without the query. `segments` is `path` split at `/`, empty segments dropped and each percent-decoded, with `.` and `..` kept. `query` holds the query's name and value pairs in order, decoded as an HTML form decodes them, with `""` for a pair without `=`. `Response` has `status`, `headers` and `body`. Why: the examples read fields and match variants from another package (§4.3.4), and 030 compares `List<http::Method>` with `==`. Fields freeze at 1.0 (D-075), so the obvious ones go in now: `path` and `query` give a way to read a query string and to tell `/a%2Fb` from `/a/b`. Rejected: opaque types with accessors such as `http::status(response)` (would rewrite 030 and 031); `segments` only.
- **D-1005 HTTP methods**: `Method` has the variants `Get`, `Head`, `Post`, `Put`, `Patch`, `Delete`, `Options` and `Other(String)`. `Other` keeps any other token exactly as received, and a token that names another variant is always that variant. `method_name(method) -> String` gives the wire token, and `Method` does not satisfy `Show`. 030's module comment lists the eight variants. Why: RFC 9110's common methods plus an escape for extensions, since a server receives `HEAD`, `OPTIONS` and extension methods and an exposed ADT cannot grow after 1.0. A module has one `show` (D-045), which `Error` needs more, and the token is an encoding, which D-307 exempts. Rejected: exactly 030's five variants; a plain `String` (030 matches `http::Get`); `Connect` and `Trace`, which `Other` covers; `show` for `Method`.
- **D-1006 HTTP headers**: Both records hold `headers: List<(String, String)>`, with names lower-cased in everything `std::http` produces or receives, in wire order, with repeats kept. `header` and `request_header` match names without regard to ASCII case and return the first value. `with_header` removes every header of that name and appends the new one. Why: a list is plain data with `==`, keeps order and repeats such as `set-cookie`, and lower case matches `fetch` on JS; 030's test `http::header(response, "allow") == Some("POST")` holds. Rejected: `Map<String, String>` (loses order and repeats); an opaque `Headers` type (a module's worth of API); a `with_header` that appends (`with_header(with_header(r, "a", "1"), "a", "2")` would hold both).
- **D-1007 HTTP bodies are text**: `body` is a `String` in both records. A response body that is not valid UTF-8 is `Err` for which `is_invalid_response` holds, and a request body, path or query that does not decode to valid UTF-8 gets `400` from `serve` without calling the handler (D-311). Binary bodies come later through new functions and types. Why: the examples treat bodies as text, and the frozen records are extended by adding functions, not fields. Rejected: `body: Bytes` with an accessor for text (rewrites both examples); replacing invalid sequences (D-311 forbids it for functions that return a `Result`).
- **D-1008 What the HTTP client does**: Every status is `Ok`, so 031 sees its `404` (D-297). Redirects with status 301, 302, 303, 307 and 308 are followed, at most 10 in a row, as `fetch` does: 303, and 301 or 302 after a method other than `GET` or `HEAD`, continue as `GET` without a body, and `authorization` and `cookie` headers are dropped when a redirect leaves the origin. The client has no timeout of its own. Each request opens one connection and closes it afterwards. No content encoding is asked for, so bodies arrive uncompressed. A URL is absolute, `http` or `https`, with a host name, an IPv4 address or an IPv6 address in brackets, an optional port, path and query; a fragment is not sent, and characters outside ASCII are sent percent-encoded as UTF-8. Names outside ASCII are an invalid URL until the client converts them. Why: the smallest client that runs 031, with the same results on both targets wherever `fetch` allows it (D-232). Rejected: `Err` for statuses outside 2xx (hides the body that comes with them); not following redirects (`fetch` cannot reproduce that in browsers); a default timeout (duplicates `fiber::timeout` and needs its own error kind); reusing connections in v1 (an optimization that changes no result).
- **D-1009 The general client call `send`**: The client has `send(method, url, headers, body) -> Result<Response, Error>` beside `get(url)`, which is `send(Get, url, [], "")`. Why: a program that posts JSON needs it, `fetch` gives JS all of it at no extra cost, and the native client needs it for redirects anyway. It is not named `request`, because the module needs a parameter of that name (D-051, E0044). Rejected: `get` only; a `ClientRequest` record (another set of fields that would freeze).
- **D-1010 The spec's examples use `http::get`'s result**: §10.3's `fetch_both` works on `String` URLs and `Result<http::Response, http::Error>` results, for example with a local `type Fetched = Result<http::Response, http::Error>` and a result type `Result<(Fetched, Fetched), fiber::Panic>`. §10.8's arm `Ok(page) => render(page)` becomes `Ok(Ok(page))`, with an arm for `Ok(Err(_))`. Why: the examples' `Url` and `Page` types do not exist in `std::http`, and `get` returns `Result<Response, Error>`. Rejected: keeping types that `std::http` does not have.
- **D-1011 An HTTPS client**: The client speaks HTTPS in v1. On native it uses `rustls` with the `ring` provider, over `std::net` sockets on the poller, since `rustls` is sans-IO. Certificates are checked against the Mozilla roots of `webpki-roots`, built into the runtime, so every machine trusts the same roots. The TLS connection is package-visible inside std, not public, and there is no HTTPS server. `rustls`, `ring` and `webpki-roots` are dependencies of `cheby_runtime` only on targets other than Windows until M6; on Windows, `sys_connect_tls` ends the program with a fatal runtime error, as every socket kernel there does (D-983). The root store is built once per process in one function: the roots of `webpki-roots`, plus, only in a runtime built with debug assertions, a PEM certificate from the file that the hidden `CHEBY_RT_TEST_TLS_ROOT` names. A release runtime never reads the variable. Runtime unit tests pair the TLS core in memory with a `rustls` server. One end-to-end test in `cheby_cli`, ignored on Windows, runs a Cheby package against a `rustls` server on loopback, with and without the test root. The test CA and its leaf for `localhost` and `127.0.0.1` are ECDSA P-256 certificates valid until 9999-12-31 23:59:59 UTC, checked in as PEM with a README that gives the commands that made them. TLS lands last in the client, so 031 passes on plain HTTP first. If `ring` fails to build in the Linux containers, or the tier-3 work runs late, TLS moves after M3 with the user's consent. Why: 031 accepts `https://`, most of the web needs it, and `rustls` keeps the poller design. `ring` needs `clang` on Windows arm64, which CI's image may lack, and sockets do nothing on Windows before M6 anyway. Every TLS path is tested offline, and release builds keep exactly the built-in roots. The cost is a few hundred KiB in every executable that uses `std::http`. Rejected: HTTP only in v1 (`https://` would be an invalid URL); `native-tls` (links OpenSSL dynamically on Linux and differs per platform, D-214); `rustls-platform-verifier` (results differ between machines); `aws-lc-rs` (needs CMake); the TLS crates on Windows now; honoring `SSL_CERT_FILE` (results differ between machines); generating certificates at test time with `rcgen` (one more dependency); an HTTPS server in std for its tests.
- **D-1012 A scope per request in `http::serve`**: Each connection is a fiber in `serve_on`'s scope, and each request's handler runs inside a scope of its own on that fiber. A handler that panics gives its request `500` with the body `internal server error`, its panic report is printed to standard error as a detached fiber's is (D-669), the connection stays open, and nothing else is cancelled. A cancellation from outside `serve`, such as a `fiber::timeout` around it, still cancels every connection and handler. When accepting fails in a way that does not pass, `serve_on` cancels its own connections and handlers with a package-visible runtime operation, `fiber::cancel_members(owner)`, waits until they have unwound, and returns `Err`; it does not wait for idle connections to reach their limit. `cancel_members` cancels every fiber spawned into the scope so far, and every scope nested in them, with the scope as the target, and does not cancel its caller, which must be the scope's owner. Why: a server must survive a bad request, and a scope per request gives exactly that isolation while keeping every handler inside `serve`'s structure. It is the one place in std where a panic does not cancel its siblings. Without `cancel_members`, the scope's close would wait for every connection fiber, an idle one for up to 60 seconds. Rejected: handlers as plain members of `serve`'s scope (one panic ends the server); handlers in detached fibers (nothing could cancel them, §10.4, and their panics would still print); returning the panic to `serve`'s caller (also ends the server); a private panic marker to end the connections. → [ADR-0067](../adr/0067-a-scope-per-request-in-http-serve.md)
- **D-1013 The server's limits**: Request heads may be up to 64 KiB (`431` beyond) and bodies up to 10 MiB (`413` beyond). `chunked` is the only transfer coding (`501` for others), and a head that is not valid HTTP/1.1 gets `400`. A connection with no new request for 60 seconds is closed, and so is one whose request head is not complete 30 seconds after it began. A request with `expect: 100-continue` gets `100 Continue` before its body is read. The server sets `content-length` and `date` itself, replacing any `content-length`, `transfer-encoding`, `connection` or `date` in a response's headers, and a status outside 100 to 999 or a header that `send` would reject becomes `500`, so a handler can never split a response. A failure to accept that passes, such as too many open files, is retried after 100 ms. The numbers are informative. Why: the defaults of common servers, and changing them later breaks nothing. Rejected: no limits (one client could exhaust memory or hold connections forever); limits as parameters of `serve` (a configuration record before anyone needs one).
- **D-1014 Serving on a listener**: `serve_on(listener, handler)` sits beside `serve(port, handler)`. Why: tests that run in parallel need ports the operating system chooses, and std's tests and 030's fixture in M5 would otherwise guess a free port. Rejected: `serve` only.
- **D-1015 Where `serve` listens**: `serve(port, …)` listens on `::` and accepts IPv4 connections on the same socket, and listens on `0.0.0.0` where the host has no IPv6. Why: "every interface" as a reader expects it. Rejected: `0.0.0.0` only (misses `localhost` on hosts that resolve it to `::1` first); two sockets (two accept loops).
- **D-1016 `http::Error`**: `http::Error` is opaque with `@std_hooks(equal, hash, debug)`, the predicates `is_invalid_url`, `is_invalid_header`, `is_invalid_response`, `is_too_many_redirects` and `is_tls`, and `io_error(error) -> Option<io::Error>` for the IO failure underneath, whose text and predicates are `std::io`'s. `show` gives a fixed text per kind, such as `invalid URL` or `connection refused`, and debug printing gives the same text. Two errors are `==` when their kinds are equal and, for an IO failure, their `io::Error`s are. Why: the pattern of `io::Error` (D-345, D-368, ADR-0051), stable texts for goldens, and IO kinds named once. Rejected: returning `io::Error` (URL, header, protocol and redirect failures are not IO kinds); an exposed ADT (network failures follow the operating system and the host, D-304); a predicate for each IO kind in this module (duplicates `std::io`'s).
- **D-1017 Response builders**: `std::http` has `json(status, value)`, with a compact body and its content type, `text(status, body)` and `with_header(response, name, value)`. Why: 030 pins `json`'s body text and content type, and `text` is the other common case. Rejected: only `json`; builders per status, such as `not_found(body)`, which multiply names.
- **D-1018 `std::time`'s two clocks**: `std::time` has two opaque types, each a Cheby record that holds a `Duration`: `Timestamp` for the wall clock and `Instant` for the monotonic clock. `Timestamp` has `now`, `from_unix`, `to_unix`, `add`, `compare`, `show` and `parse`. `Instant` has `monotonic`, `elapsed` and `between`, and no `compare` or `show`. Why: the two clocks answer different questions, and records keep the API in Cheby with three small kernels. Rejected: one type for both clocks (a program could subtract a wall-clock reading from a monotonic one); plain `Int` milliseconds (loses resolution and units when `Duration` exists); `Instant` in a submodule of its own (a module for three functions).
- **D-1019 Time as text**: `time::show` writes RFC 3339 in UTC with `Z`, with a fraction of a second only when it is not zero, without trailing zeros. `time::parse(text) -> Result<Timestamp, Nil>` reads RFC 3339 with any offset. Tier 3 has no calendar fields, no other formats and no local time zones. Both are written in Cheby. Why: one unambiguous text that sorts as it reads and that every other system parses, the same on every target. Calendar fields and zones can be added later without breaking anything. Rejected: local time (depends on the host's zone database, D-214, D-232); a format language like `strftime` (a large API to freeze); calendar fields now.
- **D-1020 `time::sleep`**: `time::sleep(span)` waits on the runtime's timer heap and can be cancelled, following D-652 and D-891. 019 and 031 keep their own sleeps through selectors, which teach selectors. Why: the most common timing call should exist by name. Rejected: no sleep in std; `fiber::sleep` (reopens a tier-2 page); a sleep through a selector inside std (a selector per sleep).
- **D-1021 Clock sources**: The wall clock is the system's real-time clock. The monotonic clock is one process-wide origin, taken when the runtime first needs it and read through Rust's `Instant`, the clock the timer heap uses; it is not a runtime's own timer epoch. Why: runtimes in one process, such as the REPL's and a test's, have different epochs (D-690), and `Instant`s from them must be comparable. Rejected: the running runtime's timer epoch.
- **D-1022 `process::exit`**: `std::process` has `exit(status: Int) -> Never`. It ends the program as a termination does (D-709): the calling fiber stops where it is, every other fiber stops without unwinding, no drop function and no user code runs, standard output is flushed, and the status goes to the host. A status from 0 to 255 is used as given, and any other is 255. Why: the same end as every other termination, so the leak check, `cheby test` and the REPL already handle it (D-709, D-710, D-828), and a status that never wraps to 0. Rust's `process::exit` runs no destructors either. Rejected: cancelling every fiber and letting it unwind first (runs drop functions in order, but a stuck pool job or a long handler could hold the exit, and code would run after the program asked to end); libc's `exit` from the kernel (D-709 rules it out: it races running workers and kills hosts such as `cheby test`); clamping a negative status to 0 (turns a failure into success); passing statuses above 255 through (POSIX truncates them, so 256 would read as success).
- **D-1023 `process::exit` under the hosts**: Under `cheby test`, `exit` fails the calling test with `process::exit called with status N`, stops that test's fibers as when a test ends, and the run continues. In the REPL it ends the session with the status (D-828). An executable ends as under `cheby run`. Why: the host decides what a termination means (D-709), as D-724 already does for a deadlock inside a test. Rejected: ending the whole test run (hides other tests' results and depends on which test runs first, D-723); ignoring `exit` under `cheby test`.
- **D-1024 No process spawning in tier 3**: Tier 3 does not start processes, and `std::process` has only `exit`. Why: nothing needs spawning yet, browsers cannot provide it, it needs pipes on the poller or the pool, and adding it later breaks nothing. Rejected: a capture-only `run(program, args)` in M3; full spawning with pipes and signals (a large API to freeze).
- **D-1025 Every example also runs as an executable**: Every example that runs gets one more conformance trial, `<name>@aot`. It builds the example once with `-o` into a temporary directory and runs the executable at every thread variant (D-722) with the same goldens, comparing standard error as the build's followed by the executable's. Its milestone is M3 for examples of M3 or earlier. It runs in the serial group for `timing` examples (D-888), is never blessed and is ignored on Windows (D-935). `@rc=naive` and `::test` trials stay on the JIT. Why: the exit criterion names every example as an executable, not every variant, and one link per example keeps the suite's time. Rejected: one trial per variant (links each program three times); comparing the executable's standard error alone (022's warning comes from the build, not the program).
- **D-1026 UI tests of `cheby build`**: The UI runner gains `// @command: build`, which builds and runs the test's package and compares the same goldens as a run test. E0053 at build time, E0055's new names and errors of the `[native]` table get UI tests. Why: each code needs a UI test that pins it (D-420, D-476). Rejected: covering them only through examples.
- **D-1027 An HTTP fixture for examples**: `cheby_test_support` gets a module `http_fixture`: a small static HTTP/1.1 server in Rust on `std::net::TcpListener`, with a thread per connection and no new dependency. `http_fixture::url_for(site)` starts a server for a site directory the first time a process asks, on `127.0.0.1` with a port the operating system chooses, and returns `http://127.0.0.1:<port>`; later calls in the process share it, so every trial of a harness run uses one server. It serves the files under the example's `site/` directory, next to `cheby.toml` (D-415): a path maps to the file of the same name, `/` to `index.html`, and anything else is `404`. An `args` or `env` golden may hold `{http_fixture}`, which stands for the server's URL, and any other `{…}` word in them is an error. The substitution lives in `Example::run_args`, which every runner of examples calls: the conformance harness and `check-determinism`'s runs. 031's site has `index.html`, `about`, `team`, `blog` and `first-post`, linked so that `/old` is a `404` and a fragment link and an external link test `html.cheby`'s rules; no page is slow. 031's `args` holds `{http_fixture}`, its `test.toml` drops `skip-run`, `xtask`'s list of later-tier examples drops it, and its module comment points to `docs/stdlib/http.md`. Its output prints paths, never the port. `testing.md` §1 describes the placeholder. Why: deterministic output with no network, and 030 can reuse the mechanism in M5 with an `env` placeholder for `PORT`. Rejected: a fixed port (collides between parallel trials); a real website (neither offline nor stable); a fixture written in Cheby with `std::http` (tests the module with itself); reading the URL from an environment variable in 031 (changes the example for the harness's sake); a slow page (031 would need `timing`, which D-886 forbids with `skip-run`). Refines D-576.
- **D-1028 Where the reference evaluator lives**: The reference evaluator that D-719 moved to M3 lives in `cheby_test_support`, beside the program generator. `rc_differential` compares naive counting, Perceus and the evaluator on its 40 seeds in every CI run, and the nightly fuzz target does the same on random seeds. Why: D-809 already runs the generator on every change. Rejected: the evaluator only in `fuzz/` (a nightly toolchain, and only scheduled runs). Extends D-809.
- **D-1029 The M5 slices beside M3**: The formatter core and `cheby new`, which `m5-tooling.md` placed with M2 and which did not land, run beside M3 in a lane of their own. After CP2 one change formats `docs/examples/**/src` and `std/`, blesses the goldens whose locations moved after a review by eye, and adds `cheby fmt --check` to CI. Files written later are formatted by their authors. The LSP core lands with M4, starting no earlier than CP3, and LSP navigation and constant evaluation, planned with M3, move behind it; the protocol's open questions are settled in its own design step then. `m5-tooling.md`'s table and `m2-runtime.md`'s changes against the plan record the slip. Why: without a formatter, AGENTS.md's rule that code is in `cheby fmt`'s form is unchecked while M3 writes a lot of Cheby, and a reformat that moves lines is cheaper once, early, than in M5 after more goldens exist. The LSP core sits on the driver's front-end APIs, which M3's cache rewrites. Rejected: leaving the table as it is (it claims slices that never landed); all three slices now (the LSP would be rebuilt after CP3); a deferral of all three to M4 or M5 (the formatter's cost above). Refines D-099.
- **D-1030 The formatter's style**: `cheby fmt` is a pretty printer driven by one line width, with no options (D-079). The user chooses the width at CP2 from the formatter's measurement of how much of `docs/examples/` and `std/` changes at 80, 100 and 120 columns. Imports are sorted within their block, runs of blank lines collapse to one, comments and single blank lines are kept, files use LF and end with one newline, and string literals are never split. Why: one canonical form. Rejected: keeping the author's line breaks (not canonical, against D-079). Extends D-079.
- **D-1031 The `cheby fmt` command**: With no argument, `cheby fmt` formats every `.cheby` file under the package's `src/`; with paths, those files; `-` reads standard input and writes standard output. A file with a syntax error is reported and left alone, with status 1. `--check` lists the files that would change and exits 1 if any would. The formatter's tests check that every example and std source formats idempotently and that formatting keeps the token stream without trivia, and each example's output, unchanged. Why: the usual shape of formatters, and `--check` serves CI.
- **D-1032 `cheby new`**: `cheby new <name>` writes `cheby.toml` with the name and `version = "0.1.0"`, `src/main.cheby` with a `//!` comment and a hello-world `main` that is a `cheby fmt` fixed point, and a `.gitignore` with `build/`. It runs no `git init`. An invalid name or an existing path is a tool error. A test checks that the skeleton passes `cheby check`, `cheby run` and `cheby fmt --check`. Why: the smallest package that runs, in the form the formatter produces.
- **D-1033 M3 in five checkpoints**: M3 runs as five checkpoints, each with a gate, a verification package in a wave of its own, a report, and a stop for the user. CP1 is the first vertical slice: `001_hello_world` built by `cheby build --target native` and run by its `@aot` trial. CP2 runs every M1 and M2 example as an executable. CP3 delivers the cache, the loader and precompiled std, with the first OQ-P11 reading. CP4 delivers foreign code from packages and tier 3, so 020 and 031 pass. CP5 is M3's exit: fuzzing, the measurements and the strict run. Why: each checkpoint ends where a user decision or an exit criterion falls due: line tables, the OQ-P11 fallbacks and the formatter's width. Rejected: M2's single slice checkpoint followed by long waves (M3's lanes are more independent).
- **D-1034 Logging M3's design**: Agents draft and the user answers before anything is logged. This session logs every answer of the 25 questions and the drafts they accept. Drafts that come later, such as the backtrace's (D-939) and those packages propose at checkpoints, are logged in a short session after the user's review, before the first package that needs them, and that package waits for them. Slugs never appear in code once logged; code cites decisions. Why: M2's working rules, and a package never builds on an answer the log does not hold.
- **D-1035 The reference machine for M3's readings, and the bench job**: "The CI reference machine" of M3's exit criterion is GitHub's `ubuntu-24.04` x86-64 runner, for D-247's throughput (as D-644 and D-889 read it) and for OQ-P11's latency (D-974), with D-640's Apple M3 reported beside it. The readings run in a new `workflow_dispatch` workflow, `.github/workflows/bench.yml`: it builds both `cheby` binaries release with `--locked` after `.github/actions/c-compiler`, and runs three rounds of `cargo xtask bench-run --against` a base ref, M2's exit by default, then the OQ-P11 and D-247 readings. The user pushes `aot` and triggers it at CP1 (the run-time reading on x86-64), at CP3 (the first OQ-P11 reading) and at the exit. Agents never push. Why: one machine for both exit readings, and the x86-64 cost of D-921's fixed features is seen at CP1, not at the exit. Rejected: an emulated Linux x86-64 container (timing under Rosetta means nothing); latency read on the Apple M3 only; the job only at the exit.
- **D-1036 Dependencies M3 may add**: M3 may add `cranelift-object 0.135.5` and `object 0.39` for objects and the archive's symbol index, `socket2` for clearing `IPV6_V6ONLY` and `httparse` for HTTP heads, and `rustls`, `ring` and `webpki-roots` for TLS (D-1011), only on targets other than Windows until M6. `mio`'s `net` feature, `gimli` and `mimalloc` are already locked. Any other crate may land, and is reported at the next checkpoint, as M2's rule allowed. Every crate in `cheby_runtime` reaches every executable. Why: the list covers every crate the designs name, and M2's rule worked. Rejected: asking before any other crate lands; asking before each crate, this list included.
- **D-1037 No release profile in M3**: M3 has no `--release` flag and no full debug info. `cheby build` uses the debug profile, as `cheby run` does (D-259, D-912). Both come with M6. Why: the release profile's optimizer and budgets are M6's work (D-251, D-267). Rejected: a release flag that would build the same code. Confirms D-259.
- **D-1038 The backtrace's variable**: `CHEBY_BACKTRACE=1` turns the panic backtrace on. Unset, empty or `0` leaves it off. Any other value stops the program before `main` runs, with `error: CHEBY_BACKTRACE must be 0 or 1, not "<value>"` on standard error and status 1, as D-658 does for `CHEBY_THREADS`. The runtime reads it once per process, before the first entry runs: in `cheby run`, `cheby test`, the REPL (once per session) and executables, which honor it as they honor `CHEBY_THREADS` (D-927). Compiling never reads it, so `cheby check`, `cheby build` and constant evaluation ignore it. JS does not read it in M3, and M4 decides what JS prints. Why: the name follows `CHEBY_THREADS`, a setting that the spec names and a user sets, while `CHEBY_RT_*` names are hidden switches for the runtime's own tests (D-565, D-635, D-678). `RUST_BACKTRACE` is the model; its `full` value has no use, since every Cheby frame is shown already. Rejected: a `CHEBY_RT_` name (those are not user settings); accepting any non-empty value as on (a typo such as `yes` would pass unnoticed). Refines D-939. Applies D-658.
- **D-1039 What a backtrace shows**: A backtrace follows the report's two lines (D-449). It is the line `backtrace:`, then one line per frame, innermost first: `  in <function> at <path>:<line>:<column>`, in the words of the deadlock report (D-858). The first line's position is the report's location. Every other line's position is the call to the function on the line above, located as D-856 locates calls. A std function's line has no position, `  in std::list::fold`, since std's calls have no recorded site (D-870). A frame that a tail call replaced has no line (D-015). Code without a name is not shown: value shims, `dyn` forwarders and the other Cheby-ABI shims. A large function's outer frame (D-679) is not shown when its inner body is the next frame, and a continuation (D-780) is shown under its owner's name. A run of more than three equal lines prints three, then `  (repeated <n> more times)`. At most 100 frame lines are printed, counted after folding, and the rest is `  (<n> more frames)`. The walk reads at most 4096 frame records, D-856's cap, and stops with `  (more frames not walked)`. The walk's end gives the last line: none at an entry thunk of `main`, a test or a REPL entry; `  <called from outside Cheby code>` at a trampoline, a `@blocking` shim, other C-ABI code or an address outside generated code, as for a spawned fiber (D-526) or a callback from a kernel; and `  <no frames: the panic was raised in runtime code>` alone when no start frame is known (D-1040). Rust frames are never walked. Why: one golden holds on every platform and in both hosts, since x86-64 Linux Rust code keeps no frame records; folding and caps keep a deep recursion's report readable, as Go's traceback caps it. Rejected: walking Rust frames (differs by platform); printing addresses (not deterministic). Refines D-449, D-939.
- **D-1040 Where the backtrace's walk starts**: The walk starts at a frame record that generated code made, and generated code does not change for it, so no CLIF dump changes (D-551, D-869). A panic that generated code raises through `cheby_rt_panic`, and a stack overflow that the entry check finds through `cheby_rt_stack_slow` or `cheby_rt_stack_slow_frame`, starts at the caller's frame: these three entries become naked assembly stubs that pass the caller's frame pointer and return address to the Rust body and jump to it, keeping their exported names. A panic that a waiting kernel raises, `joined fiber failed` or `spawn into a scope that has ended`, starts at the frame published in the control block's wait-frame word (D-868), if it lies in the fiber's stack above the raising code's stack pointer. Its first line is then the user's call that reached the kernel, while the report keeps `<unknown location>` (D-732). Every other panic raised in Rust code, such as `out of memory` or equality reaching a function value inside a kernel, has no known start and prints the no-frames line. Windows x64 gets its stub in M6, and Miri builds keep plain functions. Why: the stubs cost two moves and a jump only on paths that already panic or take the slow path. Rejected: stubs on `cheby_rt_equal`, allocation and the kernels (a cost on every call, even with the variable off); walking Rust frames from the raise (x86-64 Linux keeps no records, unless the whole workspace pays for `-C force-frame-pointers=yes`). Refines D-856, D-868.
- **D-1041 Function names in the call-site map**: Each function's entry in a module record's call-site table gains the offset of its display name in the record's string table, and each range of the call-site map (D-867) points at that name, so registration copies nothing and the name lives as long as the code. A function is named by its module path and name joined by `::`, as source code names it: `demo::check`, `std::list::fold`. A closure is its owner's name followed by `::{fn#<n>}`, with `n` counted from 0 as in D-456's symbol. A test is named as `cheby test` prints it, `demo "adds"`. A REPL entry is `<repl:N>`, and a definition made in the REPL is named by its item path. Built-in code with no source is `<builtin>` (D-583). Value shims, `dyn` forwarders, trampolines, entry thunks and `@blocking` shims have no name and are never shown. A std function that a user unit instantiates is named by its std path. N7b reports the records' growth. Why: names that live in the read-only module record cost nothing at registration and are the same in both hosts. Rejected: demangling D-456's symbols at panic time (symbols are not kept in executables, and the escape is not a display form). Refines D-867.
- **D-1042 Where the backtrace prints**: A raise captures the backtrace when it records the panic in the fiber (D-699), while every frame is still on the stack, and keeps the resolved lines in the fiber's unwind record. The walk resolves frames under the map's read lock, a leaf lock (D-867). Nothing is captured with the variable off, for a cancellation or in constant evaluation. A stack overflow captures in the slow path's reserved room (D-679), iteratively and with one buffer. The backtrace is part of the report's text, printed right after the report in the same write (D-669): on standard error for the root of `main`, a REPL entry and a detached fiber; in the test's failure block (D-541, D-595); before `fatal runtime error: …` under `CHEBY_UNWIND_EDGES=off` (D-770); and before a debug toolchain's leak line (D-458). A panic that a scope or a timeout catches prints nothing and its backtrace is dropped, and a `fiber::Panic` carries none (D-473). `joined fiber failed` has its own backtrace from its `join`. A constant's panic never has one, so build output never depends on the environment (D-264). Deadlock reports (D-858) and terminations (D-709) do not change. Why: unwinding by return flags (D-733) pops frames before any catch point runs, so the capture cannot wait; printing with the report keeps the two together under D-669. Rejected: capturing at the catch point (the frames are gone). Refines D-449, D-541, D-595, D-669.
- **D-1043 Environment files for UI tests**: A UI test may have a `<name>.env` file beside it, `KEY=VALUE` lines in the format of an example's `env` file, which the harness applies to the child's environment. Every other UI test, every example trial and the REPL harness remove `CHEBY_BACKTRACE` from the child's environment, as D-747 removes `CHEBY_THREADS`. The backtrace's UI tests live in `tests/ui/11-errors-and-panics/backtrace/`, with one test without the variable and one through `cheby build`. A stack overflow's backtrace is checked only by runtime unit tests on synthetic stacks, since its repeat count depends on frame sizes. Why: a developer's own setting must never change a golden, and a test of the backtrace needs the variable set. Extends D-747.

Open:

- ~~The six drafts of the panic backtrace (D-939), from the M3 plan's backtrace design: the variable `CHEBY_BACKTRACE`, whose value `1` turns it on and any value other than unset, empty, `0` or `1` stops the program with status 1, as for `CHEBY_THREADS`; the output, one `  in <function> at <path>:<line>:<column>` line per frame after a `backtrace:` line, with folding of repeated lines and caps; the walk's start at naked assembly stubs of `cheby_rt_panic` and the stack check's slow paths, or at D-868's wait-frame word, with no frames for panics raised inside runtime code; function names in the module record's call-site table; where the backtrace prints; and the UI harness's `<name>.env` file. They refine D-449, D-541, D-595, D-669, D-747, D-856, D-867 and D-868, so the user reviews them before they are logged. N7b waits for them. Windows x64 needs its own stub in M6.~~ → D-1038 to D-1043
- OQ-P11 stays open until M3's exit (D-974). At CP3, K7's breakdown decides whether any of three fallbacks is built: lazy compilation of changed modules (D-923) if code generation dominates, our own loader (D-914) if declaring symbols dominates on `deep`, and a stat cache in the package index (D-963) if hashing dominates. Each gets its own entry if it lands.
- The formatter's line width, chosen by the user at CP2 from the formatter's measurement at 80, 100 and 120 columns (D-1030).
- Whether code records for `cheby run` keep source positions, decided by the user at CP2 from the measurement of their cost (D-938).
- The link experiment of D-913 left nothing open for the code model. On macOS, ld64's `LC_UUID` also depends on the output's basename, which D-933 and D-934 already treat as an input. `ring` builds in both Linux containers. It does not cross-compile for Windows from macOS without a `clang` that targets Windows, and whether CI's `windows-11-arm` image has one is unread; M6 settles both before it lifts D-1011's Windows gate.
- Duplicate descriptors: every record now has its own copies (D-918). No address comparison of descriptors was found, but the implementation must confirm it for `dyn` descriptors.
- `cheby clean` (D-961) and a size limit for the cache need decisions of their own.
- Symbol names hold `$`, `::`, `<`, `>`, spaces and parentheses (D-456). ELF and Mach-O accept them; Windows linkers may need an escape in M6.
- macOS executables carry no debug map until a `dsymutil` step or the bundled linker in M6 (D-938). Release archives must not embed bitcode, and the archive's debug info holds build paths until M6.
- The rule that std's facts from bodies may cross the package boundary, with its ADR, comes with the first of D-976's items that lands.
- Tier 3 on JS, for M4 (OQ-P14): hosts without a capability fail with a new `io::is_unsupported` kind rather than panic; `fetch` must match the native client's redirects, lower-cased headers and strict decoding, and a CORS failure is an IO failure; servers queue requests to a Cheby loop inside `serve`'s scope; drop functions close without waiting; `process::exit` on Node drains standard output first; file sizes beyond 2⁵³ panic.
- International domain names: the native client rejects them as an invalid URL until it converts them, while `fetch` accepts them. M4 makes the two agree (D-232).
- Whether a handler's panic report in `http::serve` should also name the request's method and path (D-1012); the report is the plain one for now.
- D-731's parked question, handing writes to standard output that would block to the pool, can be measured once `std::http` servers exist. 031 does not need it.
- Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.

## 2026-10-23: Scoped context values

Goal: log the user's choice of `std::context` for dependency injection, after a design discussion that compared it with explicit parameters, context parameters and effect handlers, and the answers that shape its keys, the std modules that read it and where it is implemented.

- **D-1044 `std::context`**: A new module `std::context` holds values that a program provides for the duration of a callback, and that every fiber spawned meanwhile inherits. `context::provide(key, value, body)` runs `body` on the calling fiber with `key` bound to `value`, and returns what `body` returns. `context::get(key)` returns the innermost value the calling fiber has for `key`, or the key's default. When `body` returns or unwinds, the fiber's context is again what it was before the call, so a `fiber::scope` or `fiber::timeout` that catches a panic continues with the context it was called with. Every spawn, scoped or detached, gives the new fiber the spawning fiber's context at the moment of the spawn, and the new fiber keeps it after `provide` returns. The fiber that calls `spawn` decides, not the scope's owner (D-666). The body of a scope, the function of a timeout and a selector's mapping function run on the calling fiber, so they see its context (D-665, D-675). `main`, each test and each REPL entry start with an empty context, in which every key has its default. Nothing is mutated: a provided value is an ordinary immutable value, and it becomes shared with the fibers that inherit it, as captures do (§9.4, D-072). Why: logging, clocks and random numbers are dependencies that deep code reads without every function in between passing them, and tests need to replace them. Each test is a root fiber of its own (D-723), so a value that one test provides never reaches another, unlike process-wide settings (D-835). Rejected: explicit parameters or a record of dependencies (verbose at every call); context parameters resolved by type, as in Scala 3 and Kotlin (a clause on every function along a call chain, and a default would silently bypass a test's value); effect handlers, as in Koka and OCaml 5 (effect rows in every signature, effect-polymorphic std signatures, and a large language change before 1.0). An effect whose operations all resume at once is a typed form of this module, so effects can take it over later. → [ADR-0068](../adr/0068-scoped-context-values.md)
- **D-1045 Context keys**: `context::key(default)` makes a `Key<T>` whose `get` returns `default` when no value is provided. `context::key_without_default()` makes a `Key<Option<T>>` whose default is `None`, so `get` returns an `Option` and a value is provided as `Some(v)`. A call of either must be the whole initializer of a `const`, and any other call is a compile error. A key is identified by its constant's path, `package::module::name`: two constants are two keys even when their defaults are equal, and redefining the constant in the REPL keeps its key.
