A key is as visible as its constant, so a `priv` key is read and provided only in its own module. Two keys are `==` when they are the same constant, and a key's debug text is `Key(<path>)`. `key` and `key_without_default` run in constants, and reaching `get` or `provide` while computing a constant is E0077 (D-533). Why: a key without a default shows in its type that a value may be missing, and an application asserts it with `let assert Some(pool) = context::get(pool_key)`, whose panic names the line. A path identifies a key stably across modules, builds and REPL redefinitions, and names it in debug output. Rejected: a default on every key, with `Key<Option<T>>` written by hand (being optional would be a convention, not part of the API); a `require` that panics when no value is provided (it duplicates `let assert` and sits badly with D-297); keys named by strings (packages could collide, and one name used with two types needs a check at run time); keys identified as objects (it relies on each constant being installed once, and a REPL redefinition would make a new key).
- **D-1046 What std reads from context**: Std reads three dependencies from context: `std::time`'s clock (D-1047), the draws of `std::random`'s host functions (D-1048), and `std::log`'s logger, once `std::log` is specified. No other std function reads context, so standard output, files, the environment and the scheduler's settings stay explicit. Why: time and randomness are what make a test's output change between runs, and a logger is the dependency that deep code needs most often. Rejected: only `std::log` (clocks and randomness would stay hard to replace in tests); no std module (`std::log` would need another way to find its logger).
- **D-1047 Clocks in context**: `std::time` has an opaque `Clock` and a key `time::clock: context::Key<Clock>` whose default is `system_clock()`. `fixed_clock(at)` is a clock whose `now` is always `at` and whose `monotonic` is always `instant_at(duration::zero)`. `custom_clock(now, monotonic)` is a clock that calls the two functions it is given. `instant_at(span)` is the `Instant` `span` after the monotonic clock's origin, so a custom clock can build readings. `time::now`, `time::monotonic` and `time::elapsed` read the clock in context. Timers never do: `time::sleep`, `fiber::timeout`, a selector's timers and the deadlock check use the runtime's monotonic clock whatever clock is provided. `system_clock`, `fixed_clock`, `custom_clock` and `instant_at` are Cheby without kernels and run in constants. Why: a fixed clock makes output with timestamps deterministic, and a custom clock lets a test move time forward. Faking timers would need a virtual timer heap that the scheduler advances, which is a far larger feature. Rejected: fixed clocks only (`elapsed` would always be zero under a fake). Refines D-1018, D-1021.
- **D-1048 A seeded random stream per fiber**: `random::with_generator(generator, body)` runs `body` with a seeded stream in context that starts at `generator`. While a stream is in context, the host functions draw from it instead of the host's source: `int_between` is `next_int_between` on the stream, `fraction` is `next_fraction`, and `from_host` takes one step and returns the generator whose state is that step's output. Each draw advances the calling fiber's copy of the stream, and no other fiber sees it. A spawn while a stream is in context leaves the spawning fiber's stream alone. The new fiber's stream starts from a state derived from the spawning fiber's current state `s` and the spawn's number `n`, counted from 1 among the fiber's spawns since its last draw: `s + n × 0xD1B54A32D192ED03` modulo 2⁶⁴, put through SplitMix64's output function, the mixing of a step. So a fiber's own draws never depend on its spawns, a child's depend on its parent's draws before the spawn and on its place among the spawns since then, and none depends on how the scheduler interleaves fibers. When `body` returns or unwinds, the fiber's stream is again what it was before the call. The stream's key is private to `std::random`, and outside a stream the host functions draw from the host as before. Why: a test or a replay can make code that uses the host functions reproducible, also under M:N scheduling. A `Generator` in an ordinary context key could not advance, since context values are immutable. Leaving the parent's stream alone keeps a test's exact values stable when code it calls starts one more fiber, for example inside a library, and the second constant keeps a child's states apart from its parent's steps, which add `0x9E3779B97F4A7C15`. Rejected: a spawn that takes one step of the parent's stream (simpler, but adding or removing a fiber anywhere shifts every later number of the parent); children that draw from the host (concurrent seeded code would not be reproducible); providing a function that returns 64 bits (a reproducible fake needs a fiber that serves numbers, and several fibers would draw in an order that the scheduler decides); leaving randomness out of context (D-653 would stand, but tests would keep passing generators by hand). Refines D-653, whose module now has per-fiber state while a stream is provided, and D-655.
- **D-1049 `std::context` lands in M4**: `std::context`, and the changes of D-1046 to D-1048 in `std::time`, `std::random` and `std::fiber`, are implemented in M4, native and JS together, as workstream 4.6 of `m4-js.md`. `std::context` starts tier 4 of the standard library: the modules M4 implements. Until then the pages are drafts with no code, and their new tests of shipped modules are pending for M4 (D-741). Why: per-fiber state is needed on JS too, and M4 already writes the `.mjs` files of `std::time` and `std::random`. Rejected: M6 (the native side would wait long); M3's side lane (it would take attention from M3's critical path).

Open:

- The compile error for a call of `context::key` or `context::key_without_default` that is not a constant's whole initializer (D-1045) gets its code, its explanation and its UI test when M4 implements it (D-420, D-476).
- `time.md` gains tests for clocks in context (D-1047). `std::time` ships in M3, and when it does, those tests go into `PENDING_SPEC_TESTS` for M4 (D-741).
- `std::log` is not specified. D-1046 settles only where its logger comes from. The discussion that led here also raised message templates whose holes become fields (`Template<T>`), caller locations (`@track_caller`), argument source text (`Source<T>`), build constants and embedded files. None of them is decided.
- Context follows spawns, not channel messages (D-1044), so an actor or a worker pool spawned earlier never sees its senders' context. A sender passes a value with the message today. Whether `std::context` should also capture a fiber's whole context as a value and provide it again elsewhere, for such servers, needs a decision of its own.
- How a fiber's context is stored and restored on unwinding, and what a spawn copies, are M4's implementation work. A persistent map per fiber makes a spawn O(1).
- Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.

## 2026-10-23: Standard-library additions, part 1

Goal: log the parts of the standard-library design session that M3's packages need before their waves, together with the naming rules that every addition follows.

The user answered the 31 questions of the session (UQ-1 to UQ-31, which belong to this session only). Every answer is the recommended option except UQ-29 and UQ-30, where the user chose (b): the standard-stream handles land in S3, and the stream interfaces and `process::until_interrupted` in S7. Part 2, the additions to tier-1 and tier-2 modules, is logged later (D-1054).

- **D-1050 Names for early exit, checked forms and operations that do not wait**: Three affixes have one meaning each across std. `try_x` is the form of `x` whose callback returns a `Result`, and which stops at the first `Err` and returns it, as `result::try` does. `checked_x` is the form of `x` that returns `Err` where `x` panics (D-309, D-356). A form of a waiting operation that never waits takes the suffix `_now`, as the planned `channel::send_now`, `channel::receive_now`, `selector::select_now` and `fiber::yield_now` do. A join that does not panic, if one is ever added, is not named `try_join`. `docs/stdlib/README.md`'s "Names" section states the three rules. Why: nothing named `try_send` exists yet, so one meaning per affix costs nothing now. `checked_` already has its meaning, `_now` matches `yield_now`, and `poll` would collide with the runtime's poller (D-984). Rejected: Rust's two meanings, `try_send` beside `try_fold`, where the prefix's meaning depends on the signature; Java's `offer` and `poll` (two unrelated names for one idea).
- **D-1051 Two meanings of `with_`**: A `with_` function whose last parameter is a callback scopes a resource for the duration of that callback, as `file::with_open` and `random::with_generator` do (D-993, D-1048). Any other `with_` function returns a copy of its first argument with one part replaced, as `http::with_header` does (D-1006). Every new `with_` name follows one of the two, and README "Names" states both. Why: both uses are established, 030 and D-1017 pin `with_header`, and the last parameter tells the two apart. Rejected: renaming `with_header` to `set_header` for one meaning per prefix (rewrites 030 and part of D-1017).
- **D-1052 `http::add_header`**: `add_header(response: Response, name: String, value: String) -> Response` appends a header, with `name` in lower case, and keeps every other header, including those of the same name. `with_header` stays as D-1006 has it. `add_header` is Cheby without kernels, so it runs in constants, and S4a writes it with the other builders in wave 6. Why: a builder must be able to send two `set-cookie` headers, which D-1006 keeps repeats for, and Fetch has the same `set` and `append` pair. Rejected: no change (no second `set-cookie` from a builder); an `add_header` on `Request` (handlers do not build requests). Extends D-1006, D-1017.
- **D-1053 Module names that are common local names**: Std's modules keep their natural names even where the name is a common local, such as `url`. D-062 bans a local or a parameter that has the name of an imported module, so std's own sources import such a module under a plural alias, as `std/http.cheby` writes `import std::json as jsons` and will write `import std::url as urls`, and the module's page says so in its introduction. A user who meets the clash gets D-062's error, which suggests a new name. A later `std::path` decides its name in its own tier, since `path` is the parameter of most functions of `std::file` and `std::io`. `hex`, `base64` and `instant` are rare as locals. Why: names that read well at every call, and std already writes plural aliases (`maps`, `lists`, `jsons`). Rejected: names that are rarely locals, such as `std::urlencode` and later `std::filepath` (longer, and `urlencode` would not fit a later `url::parse`); URL functions in `std::http` (URLs that are not HTTP would import `std::http`, and a later move breaks callers).
- **D-1054 How the standard-library additions land**: The additions of this session land in two batches. Part 1, this session, holds the naming rules and what M3's packages need: each item is implemented by the M3 package that owns its module (S1 `std::time`, S2 `std::net`, S3 `std::file`, S4a and S5 `std::http`, S7 `std::process` and `std::io`'s streams) or by a small package of its own (D-1055, and standard input's half of D-1065), and its page changes with its code. Part 2 holds the additions to tier-1 and tier-2 modules, with `std::base64` and `std::hex`. They are logged with their std code as one batch on the branch `design/stdlib`, each page in the same change as its `std/` code and tests, and merged into `aot` between waves, after CP1 and before wave 9, so that T1b reformats them with the rest of std. A page that lands before its code lists its tests in `PENDING_SPEC_TESTS` with their milestone (D-728, D-741). An addition to an existing module keeps that module's tier. A new module joins the tier of the milestone that ships it, so `std::url`, `std::base64` and `std::hex`, which ship in M3, are tier 3, although they are Cheby with no kernels. Why: the S-package items are due before their waves, the tier-1 batch then conflicts with no M3 package and gets one review, and D-276 defines a tier as the modules a milestone needs, as D-1049 does for tier 4. Rejected: folding each item into the nearest M3 package (packages on critical paths grow); everything after the M3 exit (the tier-1 batch would wait for months, and the S-package items are due earlier anyway); `std::base64` and `std::hex` in tier 1 because they are pure (a tier would then name a kind of module, not a milestone). Refines D-276.
- **D-1055 Interpolation appends to a leading `String`**: When the first part of an interpolated string literal is an interpolation `{path}` without `:?` whose value is a `String`, lowering uses that value as the buffer and appends the other parts to it, as `+` does. The buffer is appended to in place when nothing else refers to it, and copied otherwise (D-577, D-849), so `"{acc}{piece}"` costs what `acc + piece` costs. Every other interpolation starts from a fresh buffer, as before. The text and the left-to-right evaluation order do not change (§5.3.2). §5.3.2 gains a _Note_: when the first part is a `String` value that nothing else refers to, the rest is appended to it in place, so `"{acc}{piece}"` costs what `acc + piece` costs (D-577). `string.md`'s Note on in-place kernels gains the same sentence. No M3 package owns the change, so a small package of its own makes it in `crates/cheby_mir/src/lower/interp.rs` between waves 3 and 5, before N7a edits lowering. Why: interpolation is the idiomatic way to build text, and nothing warns about the trap: with 200,000 pieces, `"{acc}x{i},"` took 4.76 s against 0.03 s for `acc + "x{i},"`. The fix is local to one lowering function. Rejected: Notes only, steering accumulators to `+` (the idiomatic form stays quadratic); a lint that suggests `+` (a new W-code and flow analysis, and the slow path remains); a string builder type (D-316 keeps `Bytes` without one too); the same change after the M3 exit (it would be rebased onto N7a); starting from any first part, such as an `Int`'s text (no measured gain). Extends D-577.
- **D-1056 Floored division in `std::int`**: `std::int` has `floor_div(a: Int, b: Int) -> Int`, the quotient rounded toward negative infinity, and `modulo(a: Int, b: Int) -> Int`, the remainder with the sign of `b`, so `a == floor_div(a, b) * b + modulo(a, b)`. Both panic when `b` is 0, as `/` and `%` do (D-069), and `floor_div(min_value, -1)` panics on overflow, as `div` does. `checked_floor_div(a: Int, b: Int) -> Result<Int, Nil>` and `checked_modulo(a: Int, b: Int) -> Result<Int, Nil>` return `Err(Nil)` in those cases (D-356). The four exist only for `Int` (D-389). They are written in Cheby, so they run in constants and need no JS kernel. They land in `std/int.cheby` with their page and tests before S1 starts in wave 5, since `std/time.cheby` uses them for dates before 1970, and `std/duration.cheby` uses them too. D-297 gains a sentence: a function that does an operator's arithmetic panics where that operator would. Why: wrap-around indexing and calendar arithmetic need them, examples 006 and 028 and `std::duration` write them by hand, and Python, Gleam, Elixir and Haskell use floored semantics. Rejected: Rust's Euclidean forms, which differ only for a negative divisor (the less common rule); a `Result` for a zero divisor, as in Gleam (the main use, `modulo(i, length)`, has a divisor known not to be zero, and `let assert` would follow every call); the same functions on the signed sized types (against D-389). Extends D-389. Refines D-297.
- **D-1057 `std::time::instant`**: `Instant` moves to a public submodule `std::time::instant`, with its page at `docs/stdlib/time/instant.md` and its source at `std/time/instant.cheby` (D-351). The submodule holds the opaque `Instant` and its pure functions: `add(at: Instant, span: duration::Duration) -> Instant`, `compare(a: Instant, b: Instant) -> Order`, `between(earlier: Instant, later: Instant) -> duration::Duration`, and D-1047's `instant_at`, renamed `from_origin(span: duration::Duration) -> Instant`. `instant::compare` makes `Instant` satisfy `Compare`, so `<` and the other comparison operators work on instants (D-045). `Instant` does not satisfy `Show`, since its origin is hidden, and its debug text is the record's. `instant::add` panics where `Duration` arithmetic overflows, as `time::add` does (D-025, D-650). The submodule imports only `std::duration`, and none of its functions has a kernel or reads context, so all of them run in constants. The clock readers stay in `std::time`, which imports the submodule: `monotonic() -> instant::Instant`, `elapsed(start: instant::Instant) -> duration::Duration`, `Clock`, the key `time::clock` and `custom_clock(now: fn() -> Timestamp, monotonic: fn() -> instant::Instant) -> Clock` keep D-1047's names and rules, and `monotonic` builds its reading with `instant::from_origin`. `std::time` gains `until(deadline: instant::Instant) -> duration::Duration`, the span from the monotonic reading of the clock in context to `deadline`, negative once it has passed, and `sleep_until(deadline: instant::Instant)`, which sleeps until the runtime's monotonic clock reaches `deadline` and returns at once for a past deadline (D-652). As a timer, `sleep_until` never reads the clock in context (D-1047), so a deadline built from a provided clock's readings means nothing to it. `time::between` on instants becomes `instant::between`, and `std::time` has no `between` for timestamps, whose span is `time::to_unix(b) - time::to_unix(a)`. S1 writes both files in wave 5. `time::custom_clock`'s parameters are renamed `read_now` and `read_monotonic`, because D-1047's `now` and `monotonic` share names with functions of `std::time`, which D-062 forbids (E0044). Why: a module satisfies `Compare` for one type (D-045), and `time::compare` is `Timestamp`'s, so only a submodule gives `<` on instants, which can no longer move after 1.0 (D-075). A deadline computed once and checked with `<`, `until` and `sleep_until` bounds a loop and keeps its partial results, which a `fiber::timeout` releases by unwinding, and gives periodic loops without drift. `std/time.cheby` does not exist before S1, so the move breaks nothing. D-1018 rejected the submodule as a module for three functions; it now has four and the operators. Keeping the readers in `std::time` avoids a cycle (D-145), since `Clock`'s key lives there, and keeps D-1047's names. `from_origin` avoids both `instant::instant_at` and a third meaning of `at`. Rejected: moving `now`, `elapsed`, `until`, `Clock` and its key into the submodule (renames D-1047's names days after logging, and splits the clock from `time::now`); one module with `until` and `sleep_until` and no `<` on instants; leaving it as it is; a separate `Deadline` type (a second monotonic type with the same representation); `time::between` for timestamps (`to_unix` says the same, and `between` would name three things). Refines D-1018, D-1047. Extends D-1020.
- **D-1058 An endless sleep is a deadlock wait**: `time::sleep` with a span past the cap of D-891, and `time::sleep_until` with a deadline that far away, arm no timer and park with the wait operation `time::sleep`. D-858's list of operations gains `time::sleep`, so §10.4's report lists such a fiber as `  blocked in time::sleep at <location>`. A sleep up to the cap arms a timer, counts as pending work and never appears in a report, which is what D-984's sentence about `time::sleep` now covers. S1 implements it in `sys_sleep`, and a UI test pins the report. Why: a program whose fibers all wait, one of them in an endless sleep, would otherwise hang with no report, the worst outcome a deadlock rule can give (D-724), while `selector::select` on an endless timer arm is already reported. Rejected: counting an endless sleep as pending work forever, like Go's longest timer (hides a program that can never make progress); clamping spans past the cap to the cap (`sleep` would differ from `after` and `timeout`, D-891); reporting it as `selector::select` (names an operation the program did not call, D-858). Refines D-858, D-891, D-984.
- **D-1059 Tail calls to std waits keep their caller's frame**: D-861's rule covers every public std function that can wait on a channel, a fiber, a scope or a timer and calls no function value on the calling fiber. Calling an interface function through a type parameter's dictionary counts as calling a function value. Today that is D-861's three functions plus `time::sleep` and `time::sleep_until` (D-1057), and each later wait of that kind is covered without a new entry. `crates/cheby_mir/src/waits.rs` lists them, and its test checks that each calls no function value. A waiting function that calls a function value keeps its tail call, as `selector::select` does, such as `fiber::race` (D-1061), `process::until_interrupted` (D-1073) and `io::copy` (D-1072), and a report locates a fiber waiting in one at the nearest frame outside std that is still on the stack. For a function written in Cheby, the report names the operation its kernel waits in. Socket waits and blocking-pool jobs never appear in a report (D-857, D-984), so the rule leaves them out. Why: the location is the point of the report (D-856), and D-861's argument, at most one frame per waiting fiber and no loop that grows the stack, holds exactly for functions that call no function value. Rejected: extending D-861's list one function at a time (a missed entry shows only as `<unknown location>`); naming the public function as the operation (a `WaitOp` per helper). Extends D-861.
- **D-1060 `fiber::cancel`**: `cancel(owner: Scope)` cancels every fiber spawned into `owner` so far, and every scope nested in them, with `owner` as the target (D-667). It never cancels `owner`'s body and does not mark the scope as cancelled: fibers spawned afterwards run normally, and the scope returns `Ok` with the body's value once every fiber has ended. A panic of a fiber after `cancel` has reached it is not recorded, as for a timeout (D-671). Any fiber that holds the `Scope` may call it. When the caller is one of the fibers it cancels, `cancel` does not return, and the caller unwinds at once, as a cancelled blocking operation does (D-102). On a scope that has ended, failed or been cancelled, `cancel` does nothing. Cancellation stays uncatchable (D-102). Joining a fiber that `cancel` reached follows D-668: a joiner that is itself being cancelled unwinds, and any other joiner, the body included, panics with `joined fiber failed`. D-668's remark that a member of the scope "is always" being cancelled no longer holds, and §10.3 says so in a _Note_. §10.7 lists `fiber::cancel` as a cause of cancellation. `cancel` is Cheby over a private `@std_context` kernel `sys_cancel`, bound as `@external(native, "cheby_fiber_sys_cancel")` and `@external(js, "./fiber.mjs", "sys_cancel")`, which replaces D-1012's package-visible `cancel_members`. S5 builds it in wave 7 in place of `cancel_members`, and `http::serve_on` calls `fiber::cancel`. Why: cancellation is the one structured-concurrency tool a program cannot build itself, and "first answer wins", "stop at the first `Err`" and "stop the workers now" otherwise need a fake panic whose `Err(Panic)` means success. Making it public adds no machinery beyond D-1012's walk and the case where the caller cancels itself. Trio's cancel scopes, Kotlin's `cancelChildren`, tokio's `JoinSet::abort_all` and Java's `StructuredTaskScope` cover the same need. Rejected: keeping it package-visible and shipping only `race` and a parallel `try_map` over it (users could not write policies of their own, such as the first two of three); a `cancel` that also refuses later spawns, like Trio's cancel scope (breaks worker pools that cancel one round and start the next); cancelling the body too (`scope`'s `Result<T, Panic>` would need a new `Err` case); a join that returns `None` for a failed fiber (a second way to join, which can still come later under D-1050's names); deferring past M3 (S5 would build an owner-only kernel to be reworked). Refines D-667, D-668, D-985, D-1012. → [ADR-0069](../adr/0069-public-cancellation-of-a-scope.md)
- **D-1061 `fiber::race`**: `race<T>(contenders: List<fn() -> T>) -> Result<Option<T>, Panic>` calls each function on a fiber of its own, in a scope of its own, and returns `Ok(Some(value))` with the value of the first one to return. It cancels the others with `fiber::cancel` and returns once they have unwound. An empty list gives `Ok(None)` and spawns nothing. A contender that panics before the race is decided makes it return `Err` with that panic, as a panic in any scope does, and a panic after that is not recorded (D-1060). It is written in Cheby over `scope`, `spawn`, a channel and `cancel`, with no kernel, and S5 writes it beside `cancel`. Why: "first answer wins", as in hedged requests and fallbacks, is the most common use of cancellation, and it is subtle to write by hand. Rejected: `Result<T, Panic>` with a panic on an empty list (D-297); a "first `Ok` wins" variant, which users build with `cancel` when they need it.
- **D-1062 `net::read_bytes` and `net::write_bytes`**: `net::read` becomes `read_bytes(connection: Connection, limit: Int) -> Result<Option<bytes::Bytes>, io::Error>`, and `net::write` becomes `write_bytes(connection: Connection, data: bytes::Bytes) -> Result<Nil, io::Error>`, with the behavior D-999 gives them. Their kernels become `sys_read_bytes` and `sys_write_bytes`, bound as `cheby_net_sys_read_bytes` and `cheby_net_sys_write_bytes` and as `./net.mjs`'s `sys_read_bytes` and `sys_write_bytes`. The name `net::write` stays unused, so that a later text `write(connection, text)` can match `file::write`. `net.md` and `file.md` say that `limit` caps the result and never sizes an allocation: a read takes at most the kernel's own block. S2 writes the new names in wave 5. Why: one name per operation across std, as `file::read_bytes`, `file::write_bytes` and `io::read_file_bytes` have, and the condition for `File` and `Connection` to satisfy one interface, since satisfaction is by name (§8.2, D-1072). D-998 and D-999 chose the old names without comparing them with D-996's. Rejected: keeping `read` and `write` (the mismatch freezes at 1.0, and a stream interface would need wrapper types); renaming `file`'s functions to `read` and `write` (`file::write` takes a `String`, as §5.10's examples do); adding a text `net::write` now (no caller needs it, and `bytes::from_string` covers it). Refines D-985, D-998, D-999.
- **D-1063 Closing sockets**: `close(connection: Connection) -> Result<Nil, io::Error>` and `close_listener(listener: Listener) -> Result<Nil, io::Error>` close the handle now. A fiber parked in `accept`, `read_bytes` or `write_bytes` on it wakes and gets `Err` for which `io::is_closed` holds, and so does every later operation on the handle, another close included. Releasing a closed handle does nothing more, as for `file::close`. Closing a `Connection` sends the peer the end of the stream, or a reset where the operating system sends one because unread data is waiting. The handle keeps its descriptor until a system call that another worker is making on it returns (D-1001), so a descriptor number is never reused under a running call. Those calls never block, so `close` never parks. Kind 12 covers socket handles, with the text `closed`. The kernels are `sys_close` and `sys_close_listener`, over the poller's existing wake of parked fibers, and S2 writes them in wave 5. There are no `net::with_*` functions. Why: Go's way to stop a blocked accept or read from another fiber, with no new cancellation primitive. `close` takes the plain name because `Connection` has the shape of `file::close`, so one later interface can cover both, and a module has one function per name (D-045). A socket's close reports nothing useful, so a scoped form would add only a hard end to the handle's life, which `close` gives. Rejected: no `close` (a server stops only by cancellation, and a fiber blocked on a peer that never closes is stuck); one `close` for both types (no overloading, D-045); `shutdown_read` (a half-close does not wake a reader on every platform); `net::with_connect` and `net::with_listen` (no close error to report). Extends D-981. Refines D-985, D-998.
- **D-1064 A closed listener stops `serve_on`**: When `accept` in `http::serve_on` fails with `io::is_closed`, `serve_on` cancels its connections and handlers with `fiber::cancel(owner)` (D-1060), as for any other accept failure that does not pass, waits until they have unwound and returns `Ok(Nil)`. A handler that closes its own server's listener is cancelled with the rest, so its response may not be sent. Draining, which lets requests in flight finish, stays out of tier 3. S5 writes the rule in wave 7. Why: closing the listener is how a program, or one of its own handlers, stops a server on purpose, and `Ok` says that it stopped as asked. Rejected: returning the `Err` (a requested stop would look like a failure); draining in tier 3 (`serve_on` would need state about which connections are idle, which Cheby code cannot hold without a kernel); relying on cancellation alone (a handler holds no scope of `serve_on`'s, so it could not stop its own server). Refines D-1012.
- **D-1065 Cancellation abandons name lookups and read refills**: A cancellation that reaches a fiber waiting on a host-name lookup, or on a refill of standard input's reader (D-823) or of a `File`'s read-ahead buffer (D-995), is delivered at once. The fiber unwinds without waiting for the job, which keeps running on its pool thread and holds its own reference to the reader or the descriptor. A lookup's result is dropped when it returns. A refill's bytes land in the reader's buffer, which the reader owns rather than the fiber, so the next read gets them and no line is lost, which is D-983's goal. The next read on that reader waits for a refill still running. Every other pool job keeps waiting, as D-670 says: writes, opens, closes and directory changes change the outside world, and a caller must know whether they happened. D-983's "cannot be interrupted" now means only that a lookup itself runs to its end. §10.7 and §10.9 say so. S2 implements it for lookups in wave 5 and S3 for `File` refills in wave 6. Standard input's reader is M2 code that no M3 package owns, so the M3 plan gives its half a package of its own beside S3. Why: neither job has an effect that the cancelled fiber would miss, so D-670's "a cancelled operation takes and delivers nothing" still holds, and `fiber::timeout` becomes a real deadline for network code and standard input, as D-1000 itself wanted. Tokio drops a `lookup_host` future the same way. The cost is that abandoned lookups hold pool threads until the resolver returns, so a program that retries in a loop can fill the pool's 512 threads (D-683). Rejected: keeping the wait (a lookup can take 5 to 30 s, so `fiber::timeout(…, fn() { http::get(url) })` is no deadline); lookups only (a fiber that reads standard input still holds its scope open); an asynchronous resolver on the poller (D-983 rejected it, and it differs from the system's resolver); abandoning writes too (the caller could not know what reached the file). Refines D-670, D-1000.
- **D-1066 Scratch directories, exclusive creation and recursive removal**: `std::file` gains four functions. `with_temp_dir<T>(body: fn(String) -> Result<T, io::Error>) -> Result<T, io::Error>` creates a new, empty directory with a random name under the host's temporary directory, calls `body` with its path in the host's own form, and removes it and everything in it when `body` returns. As for `with_open`, a failure to remove is returned only when `body` returned `Ok` (D-993). When `body` panics or is cancelled, the directory stays, since Cheby has no `finally` (ADR-0029) and drop functions must not wait (D-703). `create_new(path: String) -> Result<File, io::Error>` opens for writing a file that must not exist yet, atomically (`O_CREAT | O_EXCL`), and fails with `io::is_already_exists` otherwise. `with_create_new<T>(path: String, body: fn(File) -> Result<T, io::Error>) -> Result<T, io::Error>` is its scoped form, with `with_open`'s rules. `remove_dir_all(path: String) -> Result<Nil, io::Error>` removes a directory and everything in it, and never follows a symbolic link: a link inside the tree is removed, not its target, and when `path` itself is a symbolic link, the link is removed and its target is left alone, as Rust's `remove_dir_all` and Go's `RemoveAll` do. When nothing exists at `path`, or `path` names a file, it fails with `io::is_not_found` or `io::is_not_a_directory`, as `remove_dir` and Rust's `remove_dir_all` do, rather than succeeding as Go's `RemoveAll` does, so a mistyped path is not silently ignored. Entries removed before a failure stay removed. The kernels are `sys_create_temp_dir` and `sys_remove_dir_all`, and `create_new` is a fourth mode of `sys_open(path, mode)`. S3 writes them in wave 6. `std::file` then has eight openers, still one name per behavior and no flags. Why: the smallest API that makes file code testable, after Go's `t.TempDir` and `O_EXCL`, Rust's `create_new` and ExUnit's `tmp_dir`. `create_new` also gives lock files, and a recursive delete in std can be safe where one written over `list_dir` and `info`, which follows links, is not (CVE-2022-21658). Rejected: an unscoped `create_temp_dir` now (leaves the cleanup to every caller, and can come later); temporary files (a temporary directory holds any number of them); a drop function that removes the directory (D-703); a flag argument to `create` (D-992 rejected flags). Refines D-985, D-992, D-996.
- **D-1067 A temporary root per `cheby test` run**: `cheby test` creates one directory per run under the host's temporary directory, gives it to the runtime, and removes it with everything in it when the run ends, after failed tests too. Every `file::with_temp_dir` of the run creates its directory under it, so a test that panics or is cancelled leaves nothing behind once the run ends. Paths under it are never part of a golden. §13.6 says so. The change is in `cheby_cli` or `cheby_driver`, with S3. Why: the cleanup matters most for a failing test. Rejected: no cleanup by `cheby test` (a failed test leaves its directory in the host's temporary directory). Extends D-1066.
- **D-1068 `file.md`'s examples become tests**: Every entry of `file.md`, and of `io.md`'s file functions and error predicates, whose _Example_ exists only because it writes or needs a file gets a `test` that works inside `file::with_temp_dir`. A test body may have type `Result<Nil, E>` (§13.6), so `use dir <- file::with_temp_dir()` works directly in a test. An entry keeps an _Example_ only where the environment still decides the result, such as permissions. `docs/stdlib/README.md`'s rule for environment-dependent examples says that a function that needs files is tested in a temporary directory. S3 writes the tests with `std/file.cheby`. Why: std's file functions get tests that run on every CI runner through `cargo xtask test-std`, and S3's done-when asks for tests in temporary directories. Rejected: keeping the _Examples_ (S3 would have no Cheby tests of its writes).
- **D-1069 `std::url`**: A new tier-3 module `std::url` (D-1054), Cheby with no kernels, so it runs in constants and on JS unchanged, has four functions. `encode_query(pairs: List<(String, String)>) -> String` writes name and value pairs as `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`, exactly as WHATWG's serializer and `URLSearchParams` do: ASCII letters, digits, `*`, `-`, `.` and `_` stay, a space becomes `+`, and every other byte of the UTF-8 text becomes `%XX` with upper-case hex digits. Pairs are joined with `&`, each is `name=value` also when the value is empty, and there is no leading `?`. `decode_query(text: String) -> Result<List<(String, String)>, Nil>` is the decoder that `serve` uses for `Request.query`, made public: it splits at `&`, skips empty pieces, splits each piece at its first `=` (a piece without one has the value `""`), reads `+` as a space and `%XX` as a byte, and keeps a `%` not followed by two hex digits as it is. It returns `Err(Nil)` when the decoded bytes are not valid UTF-8, where WHATWG would insert U+FFFD (D-311). It also decodes a form body, as `url::decode_query(request.body)`. `encode_segment(segment: String) -> String` percent-encodes every byte except RFC 3986's unreserved characters (letters, digits, `-`, `.`, `_` and `~`), so a `/` inside a segment becomes `%2F`. `decode_segment(segment: String) -> Result<String, Nil>` is the server's segment decoder: `%XX` is a byte, `+` stays, and invalid UTF-8 is `Err(Nil)`. `std::http` imports the module as `urls` (D-1053), and `serve` uses these decoders, so the two cannot drift. S4a writes the module in wave 6, with its entry in the std catalog of `crates/cheby_driver/src/std_lib.rs`. Why: building a query by hand is the common bug: under D-1008, `"{base}/search?q={term}"` with `term = "a&b=c"` sends two parameters. A module of its own fits URLs that are not HTTP, and a later `url::parse`. WHATWG's form set matches `URLSearchParams` byte for byte and is the inverse of D-1004's decoding, and RFC 3986's unreserved set is the strictest safe set for a segment. Rejected: the functions in `std::http` (URLs are not only HTTP, and a later move breaks callers); RFC 3986's set for queries too (`%20` for a space, unlike `URLSearchParams` and HTML forms); binding `URLSearchParams` on JS (a kernel where Cheby gives the same bytes everywhere); a public `url::parse` now (D-1008's parser stays private until a caller needs it); only the client fix of D-1070 (`"?q={term}"` would still inject). Extends D-1004.
- **D-1070 The client encodes URLs as WHATWG does**: The native client percent-encodes a URL's path with WHATWG's path percent-encode set and its query with WHATWG's query percent-encode set, as well as every character outside ASCII. It leaves an existing `%XX` as it is, and removes `.` and `..` segments, also when they are written as `%2e`, as the WHATWG parser does. So `http::get` and `http::send` send the same bytes as `fetch` on JS. S4a writes it in wave 6. Why: D-232, and D-1008's aim that `fetch` can match the client. D-1008 says nothing about a space, `"`, `<`, `>` or dot segments, which `fetch` encodes or removes. Rejected: encoding only characters outside ASCII (the two targets differ for `"a b"`); rejecting such URLs (`fetch` accepts them). Refines D-1008.
- **D-1071 Standard streams as `File` handles**: `file::stdin() -> File`, `file::stdout() -> File` and `file::stderr() -> File` each return a new handle for that stream. Reads from `stdin()` come from the runtime's process-wide reader (D-823), not from a buffer of the handle, and standard output is flushed first (D-346), so `file::read_line(file::stdin())` behaves as `io::read_line()`, the two may be mixed in order, and `file::read_bytes(file::stdin(), n)` gives binary input. A write to `stdout()` goes through standard output's buffer and then flushes it, as `io::write_stdout` does (D-365, D-574), so output from `io::print` and from the handle never reorders. A write to `stderr()` is written at once. Writing to `stdin()`, or reading from `stdout()` or `stderr()`, fails with `io::is_invalid_input`, as for a file opened in the other mode. `close` and the drop function mark only that handle closed, and never close descriptor 0, 1 or 2. A stream closed at start stays a null device (D-474). §10.10's rules for the standard streams gain a bullet saying that the handles follow them. The kernel is `sys_std_stream(which)`, and S3 writes it in wave 6, with a flag on `File`'s runtime kind. Why: Go's `os.Stdin` is an `*os.File`, and one handle type makes every `file` function, and the stream interfaces of D-1072, work on the standard streams, so that a binary filter over standard input can be written. `std::io` cannot return a `file::File`, since `std::file` imports `std::io` (D-145). Rejected: only `io::read_stdin_bytes(limit)` and `io::write_stdout_bytes(data)` (binary IO, but no "a file or standard input" parameter); `io::Stdin` and `io::Stdout` types (two more handle kinds, whose functions would need names other than `read_bytes` inside `std::io`); one shared handle per stream (a `close` by one part of a program would close the stream for every other part); the next tier (binary filters over standard input would wait past M3). Extends D-823, D-996.
- **D-1072 Byte streams in `std::io`**: `std::io` declares `pub interface Source { fn read_bytes(Self, Int) -> Result<Option<bytes::Bytes>, Error> }` and `pub interface Sink { fn write_bytes(Self, bytes::Bytes) -> Result<Nil, Error> }`, and two functions. `copy<S: Source, K: Sink>(source: S, sink: K) -> Result<Int, Error>` reads blocks of at most 64 KiB until the end of `source`, writes each one to `sink`, and returns the number of bytes copied. It stops at the first `Err` of either side, and what was written before stays written. `other(message: String) -> Error` builds an `Error` of kind 0 whose text is `message`, so that a user's `Source` or `Sink` can fail, and no predicate holds for it. `file::File`, `net::Connection` and the standard-stream handles satisfy both interfaces through their modules' `read_bytes` and `write_bytes`, with no new code (§8.2, D-1062, D-1071), and `std::io` imports neither `std::file` nor `std::net` (D-145). Both interfaces are `dyn`-safe (§8.6). Values are immutable (D-017), so a user's type satisfies `Source` only by wrapping something with state, such as a handle or a channel. It is all Cheby with no kernels, and S7 writes it in wave 10, with tests of `copy` between two files in `file::with_temp_dir` and between a file and a loopback `Connection`. Why: D-046 made interfaces runtime values because of Go's `io.Reader`, but std had no stream interface, so no function could take "a file or a socket". After D-1062, two interfaces and two functions give that, with `std::file` and `std::net` unchanged. Rejected: channel-based streams as the core abstraction (a fiber per stream and a second cancellation path; a `pump` helper over `Source` can come later); a text `LineSource` now (it waits for `net::read_line`); a `Source` with a buffer or seek functions (`Connection` has neither); `Source` and `Sink` without `other` (a user's `Sink` over a channel could only panic or claim success); a later tier (S7 stays smaller, but code generic over a file or a socket waits past M3). Extends D-046, D-981. → [ADR-0070](../adr/0070-byte-stream-interfaces-in-std-io.md)
- **D-1073 `process::until_interrupted`**: `until_interrupted<T>(body: fn() -> T) -> Result<T, Interrupt>` calls `body` on the calling fiber in a new scope, as `fiber::timeout` does, and the first SIGINT or SIGTERM that the process receives while the scope is open cancels it, with the scope as the target. It returns `Ok` with `body`'s value if `body` returns before a cancellation reaches it, `Err(Panicked(p))` if `body` panics first, and otherwise `Err(Interrupted)` once everything inside has unwound, so handles close, `with_*` scopes end and the program's own error path runs. `pub exposed type Interrupt { Interrupted, Panicked(fiber::Panic) }` has the shape of `fiber::Timeout`. A signal cancels every `until_interrupted` scope open in the program, and a signal while none is open takes the default action, as before. After the first signal the default action is restored, so a second Ctrl-C ends the process at once while a shutdown is stuck. An open scope is not pending work for deadlock detection, so a program whose fibers all block on channels inside one is still reported, as with an idle `Listener` (D-984). Under `cheby test` and in the REPL no signal reaches the scope: Ctrl-C keeps the host's behavior, and `body` runs as in any scope. `main` that returns after `Err(Interrupted)` exits with the status of its result, and a program that wants the shell's convention calls `process::exit(130)`. In-flight HTTP requests are cancelled, not drained (D-1064). It is written in Cheby over package-visible runtime operations of `std::fiber` that open and close a scope with a signal trigger, as `timeout` opens one with a timer (D-675, D-790), so no kernel calls `body`. The runtime turns a signal into an event on its poller through a self-pipe, and the poll holder runs the cancellation walk, as for an expired timer. Windows gets `SetConsoleCtrlHandler` in M6, and until then no signal reaches the scope there. JS hosts that have signals get their listeners in M4, and a browser never interrupts the scope. S7 writes it in wave 10, with a `cheby_cli` test that sends SIGINT to a running executable and checks the flushed output and the status, carrying the Windows `ignore` (D-545, D-641). A crate such as `signal-hook`, if one is added, is reported at the next checkpoint (D-1036). D-1024 still stands for starting processes and for every other signal. Why: Ctrl-C and a container's stop otherwise kill the process, with buffered output lost and no handle closed, and `http::serve` stops only by being killed. This is Go's `signal.NotifyContext` in Cheby's structured form, with no new kind of cancellation and no signal ADT to freeze. SIGINT and SIGTERM both mean "please stop", and every host with signals has them. Rejected: a channel of signals, `process::interrupts() -> channel::Receiver<Nil>` (with D-1060 it could stop a scope, but every program that wants a graceful stop would write the same fiber, `select` and `cancel`, could get their order wrong, and would need a fiber of its own); an exposed `Signal` ADT with SIGHUP and SIGUSR1 (it freezes at 1.0, and Windows and browsers lack them); `Result<T, Nil>` (loses `body`'s panic, which a scope must report); counting an open scope as pending work (hides real deadlocks behind a signal that may never come); a later tier (030-style servers would die on SIGTERM until then). Refines D-1024. Extends D-103. → [ADR-0071](../adr/0071-a-scope-that-signals-cancel.md)
- **D-1074 The client's head limit**: The HTTP client reads a response head of at most 64 KiB, the server's own limit (D-1013), and a longer head is `Err` for which `http::is_invalid_response` holds. Bodies have no limit in v1, and `http.md` says so: a body larger than memory ends the program with `out of memory` (D-642). The number is informative. Node's own head limit is 16 KiB, so on JS a head between 16 and 64 KiB may fail there first. S4a writes it in wave 6. Why: the head limit is the cheap half and needs no new error kind, while a body cap needs a kind that `fetch` can match, which belongs with streaming bodies. Rejected: a body cap with an `is_too_large` kind now (a kind that freezes before streaming bodies exist, and legitimate large downloads would fail); no limit at all (one hostile header line ends the program). Refines D-1008.
- **D-1075 `with_open` with another error type**: `std::file` keeps D-993's `with_open`, and `file.md`'s "Scoped opening" shows how a function with its own error type keeps the IO inside the `with_*` call and maps the error outside it: `let read = file::with_open(path, fn(source) { file::read_line(source) })`, then `use found <- result::try(result::map_error(read, ReadFailed))`. A `with_open_as(path, wrap, body)` can be added later without breaking anything. The same holds for `with_temp_dir` and `with_create_new` (D-1066). Why: D-993's reasons stand, and most code reads first and then parses. Rejected: `with_open_as` and its siblings now (four or six more names before anyone asks); a `with_open` generic over the error type (D-993 rejected it); building an `io::Error` with `io::other` in the body (loses the caller's typed error).
- **D-1076 Deferred file and socket functions**: Tier 3 has no `net::read_line`, no directory walk and no `file::symlink_info`, and the "Not in tier 3" lists of `net.md` and `file.md` name them. When `net::read_line` comes, it reads through a read-ahead buffer in the `Connection`, as D-995 gives `File`, and `read_bytes` serves that buffer first, so the two may be mixed. S2 keeps a connection's runtime state such that the buffer can be added without an API change. When a walk comes, `symlink_info(path: String) -> Result<Info, io::Error>` reads a path without following a final link, `is_symlink(entry: Info) -> Bool` reads the result, and `walk(root)` returns sorted paths relative to `root` and never follows links. Why: no tier-3 caller needs them, `std::http` parses heads with kernels, and `remove_dir_all` (D-1066), the one case that could not wait, never follows links. Rejected: `net::read_line` in S2 (a buffer and serialized line reads in the busiest tier-3 package); `symlink_info` and `is_symlink` in S3 (more S3 work, only for walkers written by hand); the name `link_info` (it could mean hard links).
- **D-1077 `std::path`'s `join` stays under its base**: When a later tier adds `std::path`, it works on `String`s, with no `Path` type, and `join("a", "/b")` gives `a/b`, as Go's `path.Join` does, not `/b` as in Rust and Python. Its name and its other functions are decided in that tier (D-1053). Why: Go's rule keeps `join(base, user_input)` inside `base`, while Rust's lets an absolute input replace it, which causes path-traversal bugs, and fixing the rule now keeps the other one from creeping in meanwhile. A `Path` type buys little when D-311 already turns names that are not UTF-8 into errors. Rejected: Rust's rule; a `Path` type (doubles every signature); leaving the rule to that tier.
- **D-1078 Constraints on a later process API**: Tier 3 does not start processes (D-1024), and `process.md`'s "Not in tier 3" records the direction for the tier that does: an opaque `Command` builder, never an exposed record (D-1009's reason), whose replacing builders are named `with_`, such as `with_env` (D-1051); the program and its arguments as a list, never a shell string; a cancelled wait that kills and reaps the child; and pipes that satisfy `io::Source` and `io::Sink` (D-1072). No signature is logged. Why: the constraints are cheap to state now, and the API is not. Rejected: logging a full shape now (it freezes nothing yet and invites drift from the decision that finally lands); recording nothing (the constraints may be forgotten).
- **D-1079 A zero timer is not a default arm**: `selector.md`'s `after` gains a _Note_ now: a timer arm of zero or less is ready at once and competes uniformly with every other ready arm (D-673, D-791), so it does not act as Go's `default`, and a `select` over a ready receive arm and a zero timer arm takes either one at random. Operations that do not wait come as `channel::send_now`, `channel::receive_now` and `selector::select_now` (D-1050), logged in part 2 and implemented after M3. Why: the pitfall exists today, with or without the new operations.

Open:

- Virtual time in tests (UQ-23), an opt-in mode in which a test's clock stands still and jumps to the next timer when the test is idle, is designed for M5 after D-1047's clocks exist in M4. The design must say what `time::now` returns in such a test when a clock is also provided, and whether `fixed_clock` and `custom_clock` still matter then. D-1047 keeps timers off the clock in context until then.
- Part 2 of this session: the tier-1 and tier-2 additions answered under UQ-5 to UQ-9, UQ-12 to UQ-16, UQ-20, UQ-21 and the rest of UQ-22 (`.stdlib-work/answers.md`). They are logged with their std code on `design/stdlib`, and merged into `aot` before wave 9 (D-1054). They include the `try_fold` protocol with its ADR, `random::split` over D-1048's derivation, `duration::scale` with a floored carry, `duration::divide` only once its exact algorithm is written, `fiber::map` and `fiber::try_map` after S5's `cancel`, and `fiber::yield_now` in M4.
- ~~D-1057 renames D-1047's `time::instant_at` to `instant::from_origin`, to avoid `instant::instant_at` and a third meaning of `at`. The user confirms the name at the CP1 review, before S1 writes it.~~ Done: the user confirmed `instant::from_origin`, and that `time::sleep_until` reads the runtime's clock (D-1057).
- The M3 plan (`.m3-work/plan.md` and [`m3-aot-and-cache.md`](../plan/m3-aot-and-cache.md)) takes in the scope changes: S1 (D-1056, D-1057, D-1058, D-1059), S2 (D-1062, D-1063, D-1065, D-1076), S3 (D-1065's file refills, D-1066 to D-1068, D-1071), S4a (D-1052, D-1069, D-1070, D-1074), S5 (D-1060, D-1061, D-1064), S7 (D-1072, D-1073), and two new small packages, for D-1055 and for standard input's half of D-1065. D-1071 grows S3, and D-1072 and D-1073 grow S7, which sits on CP4's path with one wave of slack.
- `crates/cheby_driver/src/std_lib.rs`: `time::instant` and `url` join the std catalog when S1 and S4a land, and `base64` and `hex` with part 2. The S packages edit the same lists.
- ~~What `file::remove_dir_all` does when `path` itself is a symbolic link, removing the link as Rust's `remove_dir_all` does or failing, is settled before S3's code relies on it.~~ → D-1066: the link is removed, not its target.
- Tier 3 on JS (OQ-P14, M4) gains `fiber::cancel`'s kernel; `net::close` and `close_listener` (Node's `socket.destroy()` and `server.close()`, Deno's `close()`, with a rejected pending accept mapped to `is_closed`); `with_temp_dir`, `create_new` and `remove_dir_all` (`fs.mkdtemp`, `open(path, "wx")` and `fs.rm`); the standard-stream handles (`process.stdin` and `process.stdout`); and `until_interrupted`'s listeners on Node, Deno and Bun.
- 030 may adopt `process::until_interrupted` and `net::close_listener`, but it does not have to: it is an M5 example, and its goldens do not depend on signals.
- No new diagnostic code. Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.

## 2026-10-23: Standard-library additions, part 2

Goal: log the additions to tier-1 and tier-2 modules, with `std::base64` and `std::hex`, that part 1 of this session left for a second batch (D-1054).

The answers are those of UQ-5 to UQ-9, UQ-12 to UQ-16, UQ-20, UQ-21 and the rest of UQ-22 (`.stdlib-work/answers.md`). The user chose the recommended option each time, with the defaults of the sub-questions: `decode::lazy` keeps its name, D-386 is not reopened, and 018 keeps its assert. Every name follows D-1050's affixes, and each addition lands as D-1054 says: its page, its `std/` code and its tests in one change on `design/stdlib`, in the tier of the module it extends. Functions that need runtime work after M3 are documented now, and their tests are pending for M4 (D-741).

- **D-1080 Early exit is `try_fold` over `Result`**: Every std module that has `fold` also has `try_fold`. It takes `fold`'s parameters in `fold`'s order, `try_fold(c, initial, f)`, and its callback returns `Result<A, E>`, taking the accumulator first and then the module's element shape: `T`, `K, V`, `K, List<V>` or `U8`. It calls `f` in the module's order, as `fold` does (D-354), stops at the first `Err` and returns it, and otherwise returns `Ok` of the last accumulator, which is `Ok(initial)` for an empty collection. A search stops with its answer as `Err(answer)`, and `find`, `any`, `all` and `find_map` hide that for the common cases. There is no lazy sequence type, no `fold_until` and no control-flow type of its own. Code that is generic over collections takes a walk function, such as `fn(initial, step) { set::try_fold(s, initial, step) }`, which works as a push iterator; a capture such as `set::try_fold(_, _, _)` is an error, since an argument list holds at most one `_` (D-202). The modules with `fold` are `list` (D-1081), the nine map and set modules (D-1082), `bytes`, and `channel`, which gains `fold` (D-1100). `bytes::try_fold<A, E>(data: Bytes, initial: A, f: fn(A, U8) -> Result<A, E>) -> Result<A, E>` is Cheby over `bytes::get`, as `bytes::fold` is (D-484). `docs/stdlib/README.md`'s conventions state the rule, and its sentence on visiting order names `try_fold` beside `any`, `all` and `find`. Why: without loops (D-002) and without interfaces over type constructors, early exit must come from each module, and `Result` already is a two-way control type that `use` and `result::try` chain inside the callback (§5.10). One shape in every module keeps D-317's vocabulary small. Rejected: Gleam's `fold_until` with a `Continue` and `Stop` type (a second protocol, which stops with the accumulator's type, so a search for another type still needs `Result`, and failing callbacks need `Result` anyway); both protocols (twelve modules with two shapes); a `ControlFlow` type in a module of its own (`Result` under another name, imported by every collection); a lazy `std::seq` (adapters per collection without such interfaces, a closure per element against Perceus reuse, D-894, and Gleam moved its iterator out of its standard library); an `Option` callback (loses the reason, D-297). Extends D-354. → [ADR-0072](../adr/0072-early-exit-is-try-fold-over-result.md)
- **D-1081 Early exit in `std::list`**: `std::list` has `try_map<T, U, E>(xs: List<T>, f: fn(T) -> Result<U, E>) -> Result<List<U>, E>`, `try_fold<T, A, E>(xs: List<T>, initial: A, f: fn(A, T) -> Result<A, E>) -> Result<A, E>`, `try_each<T, E>(xs: List<T>, f: fn(T) -> Result<Nil, E>) -> Result<Nil, E>` and `find_map<T, U>(xs: List<T>, f: fn(T) -> Option<U>) -> Option<U>`. The three `try_` functions call `f` front to back, stop at the first `Err` and return it. Otherwise `try_map` returns `Ok` of every result in order, `try_fold` follows D-1080, and `try_each` returns `Ok(Nil)`. `find_map` returns the first `Some` that `f` returns and stops calling `f` there, and its callback returns `Option`, as `filter_map`'s does (D-379). Each entry says where it stops calling `f`, as `any`'s does (D-354). They are Cheby over list patterns and `push`, with no kernel (D-484), and they match `Ok` and `Err` with `case`, because `std::result` imports `std::list` (D-145). Every recursive call is a tail call. The costs are O(n) plus the calls, informative (D-300). `result.md`'s `all` gains a sentence: `list::try_map` stops calling its function at the first error, while `all` collects results already computed. Why: `result::all(list::map(xs, f))` keeps calling `f` after the first `Err`, and D-354 makes those calls observable, so a program that writes files or sends on channels keeps doing it after a failure. 029 and 024 write the loops by hand, and `list::first(list::filter_map(xs, f))` calls `f` on every element. Gleam, Rust, Elixir and Base have the family. Rejected: the functions in `std::result` (the subject is the list, D-296, and the import would be a cycle); `try_filter_map` (rare, and `try_fold` covers it); `Option` forms of the `try_` functions (`option::all` collects, and an `Option` loses the reason, D-297); the `try_` functions without `find_map` (a search would build a list or go through `Err`).
- **D-1082 Early exit in the map and set modules**: `std::map`, `set`, `multimap`, `sorted_map`, `sorted_set`, `sorted_multimap`, `ordered_map`, `ordered_set` and `ordered_multimap` each gain `try_fold`, `any`, `all` and `find`, with the callback shape of the module's `fold`: `fn(K, V)` for the maps, `fn(T)` for the sets and `fn(K, List<V>)` for the multimaps. In `std::map` they are `try_fold<K, V, A, E>(m: Map<K, V>, initial: A, f: fn(A, K, V) -> Result<A, E>) -> Result<A, E>`, `any<K, V>(m: Map<K, V>, predicate: fn(K, V) -> Bool) -> Bool`, `all<K, V>(m: Map<K, V>, predicate: fn(K, V) -> Bool) -> Bool` and `find<K, V>(m: Map<K, V>, predicate: fn(K, V) -> Bool) -> Option<(K, V)>`. The other modules follow over their own types: the sets' `find` returns `Option<T>`, the multimaps' `Option<(K, List<V>)>`, and the sorted modules carry `K: Compare` or `T: Compare` (D-385). Each stops calling its callback where its entry says, as `list`'s do (D-354). `any` of an empty collection is `False`, and `all` is `True`. In `map`, `set` and `multimap` the order is the per-process order (D-093), so when several entries match, which one `find` returns can change between runs, and the entry says so, as `keys` and `fold` already do. The walks are Cheby, and D-484 is unchanged. The hashed modules walk `to_list`, as their `fold` does, so the list is built before the first call, and a stop saves calls but not that allocation. The sorted modules walk the tree and stop when the callback does, through a package-visible `sorted_map::try_fold_entries` with no bound, as D-845's `fold_entries` has none. The ordered modules walk their order tree and look each value up (D-848). The costs are O(n) plus the calls, informative (D-300), and in the sorted and ordered modules a walk that stops after k entries costs O(log n + k), also informative. Why: one primitive and three short forms cover the survey's uses, and they repeat `list`'s names, so code moves between collections by changing the module. Rejected: `try_fold` alone (users would write `any` and `find` through `Err` again and again); a walk kernel that calls closures for the hashed modules (extends D-484 with re-entry, unwinding through Rust and a generator on JS, for a saving nobody has measured; a private kernel that hands out entries in chunks can come later without an API change); no `find` on the hashed modules (the names would differ across modules, while `fold` already exposes the order); a predicate `count` (clashes with the multimaps' `count`, D-363); `try_each`, `find_map` and `position` on the maps (`try_fold` covers them, and a hashed map has no positions). Extends D-354.
- **D-1083 Splitting and positions in the sorted modules**: `sorted_map::split<K: Compare, V>(m: SortedMap<K, V>, key: K) -> (SortedMap<K, V>, Option<V>, SortedMap<K, V>)` returns the map of the keys below `key`, the value stored at `key` if there is one, and the map of the keys above it. Like `get`, it gives the stored value and not the stored key (D-341). `sorted_set::split<T: Compare>(s: SortedSet<T>, item: T) -> (SortedSet<T>, Bool, SortedSet<T>)` has a `Bool` in the middle. `at` returns the entry at a position in key order, counting from 0, and `None` outside 0 to `size - 1`, negative indexes included (D-318): `sorted_map::at<K: Compare, V>(m: SortedMap<K, V>, index: Int) -> Option<(K, V)>`, `sorted_set::at<T: Compare>(s: SortedSet<T>, index: Int) -> Option<T>` and `sorted_multimap::at<K: Compare, V>(m: SortedMultimap<K, V>, index: Int) -> Option<(K, List<V>)>`, whose positions count keys, as its `size` does (D-363). `split` and `at` cost O(log n), required (D-300), and `split` calls `compare` O(log n) times. The tree already stores the size of every subtree and has `link`, so both are short Cheby. A required O(log n) `at` means that a tree moved into a kernel under D-336 must keep subtree sizes, and the paragraph of each sorted page on how it is written says so. `sorted_multimap` gets no `split`: the value counts of its parts would cost O(log n + m) to recount, while its `count` is O(1), required. D-386 stands for the insertion-ordered modules. Why: with `first`, `last` and `size`, `split` gives floor and ceiling and their strict forms, half-open ranges in both directions, the rank of a key and the size of a range, and `at` gives selection, for medians, percentiles and pages of a ranking, on the tree that already exists. OCaml and Base have `split`, Haskell `splitLookup`, and Base `nth`. `decode::at` takes a path (D-1088), but in another module and over other argument types. Rejected: `floor`, `ceiling`, `range_from` and `range_to` (four or more names per module and still no rank; each can be added later); the name `nth` (less familiar); an informative cost for `at` (users could not rely on O(log n) selection, which is its reason to exist); `rank` and `sorted_multimap::split` now (two more names, one with only an informative cost; both can come later); a record type for `split`'s result (a 3-tuple with no tuple inside raises no warning, D-243). Refines D-336.
- **D-1084 `take_while`, `drop_while` and `split_while`**: `std::list` has `take_while<T>(xs: List<T>, keep: fn(T) -> Bool) -> List<T>`, the longest prefix whose elements all satisfy `keep`, `drop_while<T>(xs: List<T>, keep: fn(T) -> Bool) -> List<T>`, the rest, and `split_while<T>(xs: List<T>, keep: fn(T) -> Bool) -> (List<T>, List<T>)`, which equals `(take_while(xs, keep), drop_while(xs, keep))`. Each calls `keep` front to back and stops at the first element for which it returns `False`, as its entry says (D-354). They are Cheby: `position` finds the cut, and the kernels `take`, `drop` and `split_at` make it, so on a list at its last use `take` and `drop` cut in place (D-894). The cost is O(p + log n), where p is the length of the prefix, informative. `int::fold_range` is deferred. Why: lexers, parsers and log scanners need them, 015 writes `split_while` by hand with this signature, and the names extend `take`, `drop` and `split_at`, with the parameter name of `filter` and `partition`. Rejected: Haskell's `span` and `break` (they do not match `split_at`); Rust's `skip_while` (`std::list` says `drop`); `split_while` alone (`take_while` is the common call); a public `prefix_length`.
- **D-1085 `float::to_fixed`**: `to_fixed(x: Float, digits: Int) -> String` writes `x` in decimal with exactly `digits` digits after the point, with no point when `digits` is 0, and never with an exponent. The digits are the exact binary value of `x` rounded to `digits` decimals, and a tie, which only a value exactly halfway such as 0.125 can be, is rounded away from zero, as `float::round` rounds (D-314). The text starts with `-` whenever the sign bit of `x` is set, so `-0.0` and `-0.004` both give `-0.00`, as `show` writes `-0.0` (D-219). NaN and the infinities are written as `show` writes them: `NaN`, `inf` and `-inf`. `digits` is a count clamped to 0 to 100 (D-298), declared with `@std_clamped(digits)`. So `to_fixed(0.125, 2)` is `"0.13"`, `to_fixed(1.005, 2)` is `"1.00"`, `to_fixed(2.5, 0)` is `"3"`, and `to_fixed(1.0e21, 1)` is `"1000000000000000000000.0"`. It is a native kernel, bound as `@external(native, "cheby_float_to_fixed")` and `@external(js, "./float.mjs", "to_fixed")` (D-484). Rust's formatting rounds ties to even, so the kernel rounds the exact decimal expansion itself. Constants can run it (D-537). The JS kernel, which M4 writes, uses `toFixed` below 1e21, which is exact and rounds ties away from zero, adds the `-` of a negative zero and `show`'s spellings of the special values, and writes a larger value from `BigInt(x)` followed by zeros. `std::f32` has no `to_fixed` (D-397): `float::from_f32` is exact, so `float::to_fixed(float::from_f32(x), digits)` writes an `F32`'s exact value, and `f32.md` says so. Why: fixed decimals are the usual text of money, percentages and timings, `show` cannot give them (D-203), interpolation has no format specifiers (D-115), and Rust and JS disagree on ties, on `-0.0` and from 1e21 up, so the rule must be pinned (D-232). Text in another form is a different operation from `show`, as for `int::to_base` (D-390). Rejected: ties to even, the rule of Rust, C, Go and Python (`to_fixed(2.5, 0)` would be `"2"` while `float::round(2.5)` is 3); no `-` on an all-zero result, Python's `z` option (differs from `show`'s `-0.0`); JS's `"0.00"` for `-0.0`, which disagrees with its own `"-0.00"` for `-0.004`; Gleam's `to_precision`, which returns a `Float` (it loses trailing zeros and goes through `show`'s switch to an exponent); digits up to 1074, every digit of the exact value (100 is JS's limit and enough for output). Refines D-307.
- **D-1086 `decode::lazy`**: `lazy<T>(placeholder: T, make: fn() -> Decoder<T>) -> Decoder<T>` is a decoder that calls `make` each time it decodes a value and decodes the value with the decoder that `make` returns. Where a placeholder is needed without a value, which is for a missing field and for a value that is not an object, a `lazy` decoder gives `placeholder` and does not call `make`. So a recursive decoder is built in O(1), and computing its placeholders never recurses. `decode.md` gets the entry under "Combining decoders", with an example of a recursive decoder, and "How errors are reported" names `lazy`'s placeholder. It is Cheby: a variant of the opaque `Decoder` that `apply` runs through `make()` and whose placeholder `missing_placeholder` returns, so constants can run it and M4 has nothing to write. Why: decoders are built eagerly, so whether a recursive decoder terminates depends on the order of its fields, and the survey's probe overflows the stack with `children` first. D-401's placeholder rule also makes a recursion that is reached on `null` loop forever, as the probe's `expr()` does on `{"op": "neg"}`. `decode::failure` already takes a placeholder, and the name follows Elm and `option::lazy_unwrap_or` (D-317). Rejected: `lazy(make)`, as Elm and Gleam have it (fixes only the first trap); Gleam's name `recursive`; documenting `then(success(Nil), fn(_) { make() })` (obscure, and it keeps the second trap). Refines D-401. Extends D-320.
- **D-1087 `json::value()`**: `std::json` has `value() -> decode::Decoder<Value>`, a decoder that accepts every JSON value and gives it as a `json::Value`, with `Null` as its placeholder. It costs O(size of the value), through the rebuild of `std::json::tree` (D-483). It lives in `std::json` because `std::json::decode` cannot name `Value` (D-145, D-361), and `std::json` builds it from the package-visible parts of `Decoder`, as it already calls `decode::run`. A binding may not have the name of a top-level function of its module (D-062), so every parameter and local named `value` in `std/json.cheby` is renamed, and so is every `let value` in `json.md`'s tests, which check unchanged in `std/` (D-662). The signatures on `json.md` keep their parameter names. `json.md`'s sentence that no decoder returns a raw `Value` goes, `decode.md`'s introduction points to `json::value`, and ADR-0053's status line becomes `accepted; refined by D-1087`. Why: generic parts of a document, such as `metadata` and `extensions`, can then pass through, and it is the escape hatch for shapes the combinators cannot express, as Elm's `value`, Gleam's `decode.dynamic` and Go's `json.RawMessage` are. ADR-0053's alternatives, moving `Value` or moving the decoders, stay rejected. Rejected: `json::raw()` (no renames, but "raw" suggests unparsed text, as Go's `RawMessage` and serde's `RawValue` mean); keeping ADR-0053's position (such fields stay impossible to decode). Refines D-361.
- **D-1088 `decode::one_of` and `decode::at`**: `one_of<T>(first: Decoder<T>, others: List<Decoder<T>>) -> Decoder<T>` runs `first` and then each decoder of `others` in order, on the same value, and gives the result of the first one that records no errors. When all of them fail, it gives `first`'s result and errors, and its placeholder is `first`'s. A missing field follows D-401 as for any decoder: `one_of` decodes `null`, so a later decoder that accepts `null`, such as an `optional`, gives the field's value. `at<T>(path: List<String>, decoder: Decoder<T>) -> Decoder<T>` decodes the member reached through the object keys of `path` with `decoder`, with the errors and paths of the nested `field` calls it stands for, and `at([], decoder)` is `decoder`. `one_of` runs up to `1 + length(others)` decoders on the value, and `at` costs what its `field` calls cost, both informative. D-403's sentence under `pairs` changes to point to them. Why: an untagged union, such as `"id": 1` beside `"id": "a1"`, cannot be decoded without `one_of`, and `at` shortens the nested reads that `decode.md`'s own test writes. Elm and Gleam have both, and D-403 left them out without rejecting them. Rejected: reporting the errors of every alternative (noise, and Gleam reports only the first's); array indexes in `at` (D-400's string steps already make `"1"` ambiguous); `one_of` alone (nested reads stay long). Extends D-403.
- **D-1089 `std::base64` and `std::hex`**: Two new modules encode `Bytes` as text and decode it. `std::base64` has `encode(data: bytes::Bytes) -> String`, with the standard alphabet and `=` padding (RFC 4648 §4), `url_encode(data: bytes::Bytes) -> String`, with the URL-safe alphabet and no padding (§5), as JWTs use, and `decode(text: String) -> Result<bytes::Bytes, Nil>` and `url_decode(text: String) -> Result<bytes::Bytes, Nil>`. `std::hex` has `encode(data: bytes::Bytes) -> String`, two lowercase digits per byte as debug printing writes them (D-388), and `decode(text: String) -> Result<bytes::Bytes, Nil>`. Decoding is strict. `base64::decode` requires the padding, and `url_decode` accepts the complete padding or none, so a partial padding such as `"Zm9vYg="` is `Err(Nil)`. Both reject a character outside the alphabet, whitespace, a length that no encoding gives, and unused bits that are not zero, so a text has at most one decoding: `"aGVsbG9="` is `Err(Nil)`. `hex::decode` accepts either case, needs an even length, and rejects a prefix and whitespace. Every function costs O(n), required (D-300). Both modules are Cheby over `std::bytes` and `std::string`, so constants can run them (D-360) and JS needs no kernel of theirs. They are in tier 3, the tier of the milestone that ships them (D-1054), get pages `base64.md` and `hex.md` and entries in the std catalog of `crates/cheby_driver/src/std_lib.rs`, and `bytes.md`'s introduction points to them. A package of the user's that is named `base64`, as 021 is, binds the same name as `import std::base64`, so a module that imports both renames one with `as`. Why: HTTP Basic authentication, JWTs, data URLs, binary fields in JSON and checksums need them, D-388 expects a program to pick hex or Base64 itself but only UTF-8 exists, 021 writes 244 lines of Base64, and every compared library ships them. As modules of their own they need no exception to the placement rule of `string.md`, under which a conversion lives in the module of its target type (D-308), and they read `encode` and `decode` as `std::json` does. Rejected: functions in `std::bytes`, as Gleam's `bit_array` has them (`std::bytes` would return `String`, the opposite of `string::from_bytes`); one `std::encoding` module (names such as `base64_encode` repeat what a module path says); lenient decoding of whitespace and line breaks, as MIME has it (both targets would have to agree on a larger rule); kernels (Cheby is fast enough for tokens, headers and keys, and a kernel can follow a measurement); tier 1 because they are pure (D-1054). Extends D-388.
- **D-1090 `to_base` on every integer type**: Each sized integer module has `to_base(n: T, base: Int) -> Result<String, Nil>`, with `int::to_base`'s meaning (D-390): the digits `0` to `9` and then lowercase `a` to `z`, `-` for a negative value, no prefix and no leading zeros, and `Err(Nil)` for a base outside 2 to 36. So `u64::to_base(18446744073709551615, 16)` is `Ok("ffffffffffffffff")` and `i8::to_base(-128, 2)` is `Ok("-10000000")`. They are Cheby with no new kernels: a type whose every value is an exact `Int` on every target, `I8`, `I16`, `I32`, `U8`, `U16` and `U32`, widens to `Int` and calls `int::to_base`, and `I64` and `U64` write their digits with their own arithmetic, since their values do not all fit `Int`. Constants can run them (D-537), and M4 owes nothing for them. The README's integer families list `to_base` beside `parse_base`, and `int.md`'s sentence on the functions only `Int` has drops it. `clamp`, `pow`, `checked_pow` and the floored division of D-1056 stay in `std::int` only. The two's-complement hex of a negative value is `u64::to_base(u64::wrapping_from_i64(n), 16)` (D-1091). Why: a type that reads a base should write it, a `U64` above 2⁶³ cannot be written in hex at all today, and on JS neither can an `I64` beyond 2⁵³ (D-037). D-389's own reason, that the sized types are for binary data (D-105), argues for the function, since hex is the usual text of binary data. Rejected: one kernel per type, as drafted (eight native kernels and eight JS bindings where Cheby suffices; D-484 names their symbols but does not require kernels); a total `to_hex(n) -> String` (a second way to write `to_base(n, 16)`); keeping D-389 (fails above 2⁶³, and beyond 2⁵³ on JS). Refines D-389.
- **D-1091 Wrapping conversions**: For every pair of integer types whose `from_` conversion is narrowing and returns `Result` (D-308), 47 pairs with `Int` counted as a 64-bit type, the target module has `wrapping_from_<source>(n: S) -> T`. It keeps the low bits of `n`'s two's-complement value that fit `T` and reads them as `T`. So `u8::wrapping_from_u32(0x1234)` is `0x34`, `i8::wrapping_from_int(200)` is `-56`, `u64::wrapping_from_int(-1)` is `u64::max_value`, and `int::wrapping_from_u64(u64::max_value)` is `-1`. On JS, an `Int` result outside ±(2⁵³−1) panics, as every `Int` result does (D-037), so `int::wrapping_from_i64` and `int::wrapping_from_u64` panic there where the 64-bit result is out of that range. They are Cheby over `&` and the existing conversions, with no new kernels, so constants can run them (D-537) and M4 owes nothing for them. The README's "Conversions" states the family. Why: "modulo 2ⁿ" is how D-025 defines the `wrapping_` family, and D-308 makes every pair exist so that nobody has to guess. Hashes, CRCs and binary codecs need these conversions, `std/random.cheby` writes two of them by hand with `let assert` and a complement trick, and `u8.md` masks and still unwraps. Rejected: kernels for each pair, as drafted (47 native kernels and their JS bindings); only the same-width sign changes and `Int` with `U64`, about 10 (`U32` to `U8` would still mask and unwrap, and "every narrowing pair" is easier to remember); none. Extends D-025, D-308.
- **D-1092 `string::lines` and `string::words`**: `lines(text: String) -> List<String>` splits `text` at each `\n`, removes one `\r` right before each `\n`, and gives no part after a final `\n`, so `lines("")` is `[]`, `lines("\n")` is `[""]` and `lines("a\r\n")` is `["a"]`. No other code point ends a line: a lone `\r`, U+0085, U+2028 and U+2029 stay in the text. `words(text: String) -> List<String>` splits `text` at every run of code points with Unicode's `White_Space` property, the set that `trim` removes (D-310), and gives no empty parts, so `words("")` is `[]`, U+0085 separates words and U+FEFF does not. Both cost O(n), required (D-300). `lines` is Cheby over `split` and `remove_suffix`. `words` is a native kernel that shares `trim`'s `White_Space` test, bound as `@external(native, "cheby_string_words")` and `@external(js, "./string.mjs", "words")` (D-484). Constants can run both (D-360). The JS kernel, which M4 writes, uses an explicit table of the `White_Space` code points that `trim`'s JS kernel shares, never `\s`, and tests with U+0085 and U+FEFF pin the difference. Why: splitting into lines and words is the first step of most text input, and the hand-written versions in 023, 029 and 032 get CRLF and tabs wrong. The line endings are the ones `io::read_line` removes (D-305), as in Rust's `lines`. JS's `\s` and `trim` use ECMAScript's sets, which include U+FEFF and exclude U+0085, so they would differ from `string::trim` (D-232). Rejected: Unicode's full set of line terminators, as Python's `splitlines` has it (disagrees with `io::read_line`, and a stray `\r` inside a field would split it); `words` only (CRLF handling stays hand-written). Extends D-305, D-310.
- **D-1093 `string::from_bytes_lossy`**: `from_bytes_lossy(data: bytes::Bytes) -> String` decodes `data` as UTF-8 and replaces each invalid sequence with U+FFFD, by the maximal-subpart practice that D-905 fixes for both targets.
