A leading U+FEFF is kept (D-366). It costs O(n), required (D-300). It is Cheby over D-905's package-visible kernel `from_byte_range`, so it adds no kernel, and its JS side is the kernel D-905 already asks M4 for. Why: text from sockets, logs and other programs must often be shown even when it is invalid, D-311 already chose replacement where no error can be returned, and the kernel exists; D-905 kept it private only until a page settled the lossy decode for every user, which this entry does. Rejected: the range form `(data, start, end)`, the kernel's own shape, which saves the copy of a `slice` (every caller with a whole `Bytes` would pass 0 and a length; it can come later if a measurement asks); both forms. Extends D-311, D-905.
- **D-1094 `bytes::index_of`**: `index_of(data: Bytes, part: Bytes, from: Int) -> Option<Int>` returns the position of the first occurrence of `part` in `data` that starts at or after `from`, or `None`. `from` is a bound, clamped to 0 to `length(data)` as `slice` clamps its bounds (D-298, D-334), and declared with `@std_clamped(from)`. An empty `part` is found at the clamped `from`. It costs O(n + m), required (D-300), where m is the length of `part`, on every target, so neither kernel may be a naive nested loop. It is a native kernel, bound as `@external(native, "cheby_bytes_index_of")` and `@external(js, "./bytes.mjs", "index_of")` (D-484), a linear search: on native the `memchr` crate's `memmem` finder, a new runtime dependency. It allocates its `Some`, so it runs under a guard and stays off the never-raise table (D-767), and constants can run it (D-360). M4 writes the JS kernel. Why: delimiter search is the first step of every byte protocol over `net::read_bytes` (D-1062) and of fast lexers, and std's own parser shows what a loop over each byte costs in Cheby (D-900, D-904). D-371's reason against `string::index_of`, that a position in code points needs a second scan to use, does not hold for `Bytes`, whose `get` is O(1). `from` is clamped because it is where a search starts, a bound like `slice`'s, not the offset of a value to read: `read_u16_be` returns `None` past the end because there is no value to give, and a list index names an element (D-318), while a search that starts past the end has an answer. Rejected: `None` for a `from` outside 0 to the length, as the offset reads do (it would differ from `slice`'s bounds, which a starting position is); a separate one-byte `index_of_byte`, as Go has `IndexByte` (a second way, and a one-byte `part` takes the kernel's fast path). Extends D-334. Extends D-1036.
- **D-1095 `result::try_with` and `result::replace_error`**: `try_with<T, E, F, U>(r: Result<T, E>, wrap: fn(E) -> F, next: fn(T) -> Result<U, F>) -> Result<U, F>` is `try(map_error(r, wrap), next)`, so `use config <- result::try_with(json::parse(text, decoder), InvalidConfig)` wraps a lower error in the caller's error type in one step. `replace_error<T, E, F>(r: Result<T, E>, error: F) -> Result<T, F>` replaces any error of `r` with `error`, for the `Result<T, Nil>` that `int::parse` and the conversions give. Both are Cheby. Why: `use x <- result::try(result::map_error(r, Wrap))` is the most common line of the larger examples, 21 times, and these functions stand in for Rust's `?` with `From`, which D-018 rules out. D-993 rejected a `wrap` parameter for `with_open` partly because it puts an argument before the callback, but there the parameter would have added a second error type to every opener; here `wrap` is exactly `map_error`'s argument, and the callback stays last, so `use` reads as with `result::try`. D-372's "one way to do each thing" rejected `string::concat` because it saved nothing over `join(parts, "")`, while `try_with` removes a nested call from the dominant line. Rejected: `replace_error` alone (the nested lines stay); neither (one way, but the nesting stays). Extends D-317.
- **D-1096 Splitting a seeded generator, and three seeded draws**: `std::random` gains four seeded functions, each fixed bit for bit from 1.0 on, as D-655 fixes the generator. `split(generator: Generator) -> (Generator, Generator)` returns a new generator and the given one advanced. With `generator`'s state `s`, the new generator's state is the state D-1048 gives the first fiber spawned from state `s`: `s + 0xD1B54A32D192ED03` modulo 2⁶⁴, put through the output mix of a step. The advanced generator is the one a step leaves, with state `s + 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15`. `Generator` stays one `U64`, and its equality and debug text do not change. `next_bool(generator: Generator) -> (Bool, Generator)` takes one step and returns `True` when the output's highest bit is 1. `shuffle<T>(generator: Generator, xs: List<T>) -> (List<T>, Generator)` is Fisher–Yates: for `i` from `length - 1` down to 1, it draws `j` with `next_int_between(generator, 0, i)` and swaps the elements at `i` and `j`, and a list of fewer than two elements draws nothing. `choose<T>(generator: Generator, xs: List<T>) -> (Option<T>, Generator)` returns `(None, generator)` for an empty list and draws nothing, and otherwise the element at `next_int_between(generator, 0, length - 1)`. The generator comes first and the result is `(value, generator)`, as in every seeded function of the module: the generator is the module's subject, the value that every call threads through (D-296, D-653). There are no host forms, since a caller passes `random::from_host()`. All four are Cheby, so constants can run them and M4 owes nothing for them. `random.md` gives fixed test vectors for each, computed by the implementation and checked against a SplitMix64 written outside Cheby. `split`, `next_bool` and `choose` cost O(1) plus `choose`'s read of the list, and `shuffle` O(n log n), informative. Why: two fibers given the same generator draw the same numbers (D-653), so seeded parallel code needs a way to derive generators, and `next_bool`, `shuffle` and `choose` are the draws every seeded program writes by hand, which std fixes so that they stay reproducible. Reusing D-1048's derivation gives the module one rule for deriving a stream and keeps `Generator` as D-655 has it. The cost is that streams are offsets in one 2⁶⁴ cycle, the overlap risk D-1048 already accepted. Rejected: a gamma per generator, as JDK 8's `SplittableRandom` has it (better-separated streams, but `Generator` grows to two `U64`s, its debug text changes, and D-1048 would be restated days after it was logged); no `split` (seeded parallel code derives seeds by hand); host forms of each function (double the module); the list first, under D-296 (breaks the module's own order). Extends D-653, D-655, D-1048.
- **D-1097 `fiber::raise`**: `raise(failure: Panic) -> Never` panics on the calling fiber with `failure`'s message and location, so a scope that catches it gets a `Panic` that is `==` to `failure`, and an uncaught one prints the original `panicked at <location>:` line and message. The backtrace, when it is on, starts with the line `  in fiber::raise`, with no location, and goes on with the calling fiber's stack at the call of `raise`, since the original stack has unwound, while the report's two lines keep the original location (D-1039). It is a panic that the caller asks for, like `panic as`, so D-297's rule that std panics only where a language rule does gains this one deliberate case. There is still no accessor for the location (D-648). `raise` is Cheby over a private kernel `sys_raise`, bound as `@external(native, "cheby_fiber_sys_raise")` and `@external(js, "./fiber.mjs", "sys_raise")`, which panics with a stored message and location. §11.6 says that a raised panic is reported at its original location, and how its backtrace starts. It is implemented after M3, with D-1098, and its tests are pending for M4 (D-741). Why: re-raising a caught panic with `panic as fiber::message(p)`, as 031 does, loses the location, and `fiber::map` must panic where `list::map` would. Rust's `resume_unwind` keeps the first panic's report the same way. Rejected: a package-visible `raise` for `fiber::map` only (031's workaround stays in user code); a location accessor (D-648 rejected it); a `fiber::map` that returns a `Result` instead (D-1098). Refines D-297, D-648.
- **D-1098 `fiber::map` and `fiber::try_map`**: `map<T, U>(xs: List<T>, limit: Int, f: fn(T) -> U) -> List<U>` calls `f` once per element, on at most `limit` fibers at a time, in a scope of its own, and returns the results in the order of `xs`, as `list::map` does. When calls of `f` panic, `map` cancels the remaining calls with `fiber::cancel` (D-1060), waits until they have unwound, and raises again with `fiber::raise` the panic that its scope records, so it panics where `list::map` would. Which panic that is can depend on timing when several calls panic (D-232). `try_map<T, U, E>(xs: List<T>, limit: Int, f: fn(T) -> Result<U, E>) -> Result<List<U>, E>` returns `Ok` with every result, or the `Err` of the element with the lowest index whose call returned `Err`. That is what `list::try_map(xs, f)` returns when `f` has no effects, whatever the scheduling. Both start the calls of `f` in the order of `xs`, and the lowest-index `Err` wins whatever order the calls end in. `f` is called exactly once for each element before that one and at most once for each element after it: once the answer is known, `try_map` hands out no more elements and cancels the calls in flight. `limit` is clamped to at least 1 (D-298), declared with `@std_clamped(limit)`, and a `limit` above the length runs every call at once. An empty list gives `[]` or `Ok([])` and spawns nothing. The elements, `f`'s captures and the results cross fibers, so they become shared (§9.4). Both are Cheby over `scope`, `spawn`, a channel, `cancel` and `raise`, with no kernel, and they call function values, so they keep their tail calls (D-1059). The costs are O(n) plus the calls, informative. `fiber.md` gets a section on parallel work. They are implemented after M3, once S5 has built `fiber::cancel`, and their tests are pending for M4 (D-741). 018 keeps its own pool, which teaches channels, and its `assert workers > 0`, and its module comment may point to `fiber::map`. Why: a bounded parallel map is the most common use of a worker pool, as Elixir's `Task.async_stream` with `max_concurrency`, Go's `errgroup.SetLimit` and Rust's `buffered(n)` show, and 018 writes about 100 lines for one. As drop-ins for `list::map` and `list::try_map` they need no new result type, and the lowest-index `Err` makes the outcome independent of timing. Rejected: `Result<List<U>, Panic>`, as 018 returns (nests into `Result<Result<List<U>, E>, Panic>` for `try_map`, and is no drop-in); the first `Err` in completion order (depends on timing); the name `parallel_map` (longer, and it repeats the module); a `fiber::all(tasks)` (it is `fiber::map(tasks, list::length(tasks), fn(task) { task() })`); `map` alone (stopping at the first `Err` stays hand-written).
- **D-1099 Channel operations that do not wait**: `channel::send_now<T>(sender: Sender<T>, value: T) -> Result<Nil, Refused<T>>` enqueues `value` when the buffer has room, or hands it to a receiver or a selector that is waiting now. Otherwise it returns `Err(Full(value))`, or `Err(Closed(value))` when every `Receiver` is gone, which is checked first. On capacity 0 it succeeds only when it wins a waiting receiver or selector, as `send` claims one (D-206), and it never waits for a selector's choice. `pub exposed type Refused<T> { Full(T), Closed(T) }` gives the value back either way (D-179). `channel::receive_now<T>(receiver: Receiver<T>) -> Option<Result<T, Nil>>` is `Some` of what `receive` would return when `receive` would not wait, so `Some(Err(Nil))` means closed and empty, and `None` otherwise. `selector::select_now<E>(selector: Selector<E>) -> Option<E>` takes a value from a ready arm, choosing among ready arms uniformly at random as `select` does (D-673), calls that arm's mapping function and returns `Some` of its result, or returns `None` when no arm is ready. A timer arm is ready in `select_now` only when its span is zero or less. None of the three waits or is a suspension point, so a loop that only polls keeps its thread, and on JS it starves the other fibers (D-185); §10.5 and §10.6 say so. The names follow D-1050. They are Cheby over private kernels, `sys_send_now` and `sys_receive_now` in `std::channel` and `sys_select_now` in `std::selector`, bound as D-484 binds every kernel, over the runtime's existing checks of ready arms and claims of waiting fibers. They are implemented after M3, and their tests are pending for M4 (D-741). Why: a timer arm of zero is no substitute (D-1079), and load shedding, draining what is there before shutdown and lossy tickers need these operations, which every channel library has. `select_now` covers several channels with no new type, and `receive_now` reuses `receive`'s result. Rejected: a default arm on selectors (gives `select` two behaviors); an error type of its own for `receive_now`, with `Empty` and `Closed` (a second way to say what `Option<Result<T, Nil>>` says); no `receive_now` (the common case of one channel would build a selector per call); `try_send`, `try_receive` and `poll` (D-1050). Refines D-041, D-054, D-673.
- **D-1100 Draining and filling channels**: `channel::fold<T, A>(receiver: Receiver<T>, initial: A, f: fn(A, T) -> A) -> A` receives until the channel is closed and empty and folds each value into the accumulator in the order received, as `list::fold` does. `channel::try_fold<T, A, E>(receiver: Receiver<T>, initial: A, f: fn(A, T) -> Result<A, E>) -> Result<A, E>` follows D-1080: it stops at the first `Err` and returns it, and the values still in the channel stay there. `channel::to_list<T>(receiver: Receiver<T>) -> List<T>` returns every value received until the channel is closed and empty. `channel::send_all<T>(sender: Sender<T>, xs: List<T>) -> Result<Nil, List<T>>` sends the elements in order and returns `Ok(Nil)`, or, once every `Receiver` is gone, `Err` with the elements it could not send, starting with the refused one (D-179). A cancellation while one of them waits unwinds as usual, and what it has gathered is lost. All four are Cheby over `receive` and `send`. `to_list` and `send_all` call no function value, so a tail call to them keeps its caller's frame (D-1059) and `crates/cheby_mir/src/waits.rs` lists them; `fold` and `try_fold` call `f` and keep their tail calls. Why: 005, 009, 018 and 019 write "receive until closed" by hand, and 018 also writes "send all, then drop". `send_all`'s unsent tail follows D-179's rule of giving values back. Rejected: a lazy iterator over a channel (std's only iteration protocol is `try_fold`, D-1080); none (the loop keeps being written). Extends D-054.
- **D-1101 `duration::scale`**: `scale(d: Duration, factor: Int) -> Duration` returns `d` times `factor`, exactly. It panics only where the result does not fit, as `Duration` arithmetic does (D-650), and the message is `integer overflow` on every target: on JS, where the seconds must stay within ±(2⁵³−1) (D-037), the JS side checks that range and reports the same message, which M4 writes. `duration.md` states the algorithm that keeps every intermediate value within the result's range. Write `d` as `a` seconds plus `b` nanoseconds such that `a` and `b` never have opposite signs, which is D-650's representation when `d` is not negative and `(seconds + 1, nanoseconds - 10⁹)` when it is negative and has nanoseconds, and write `factor` as `q × 10⁹ + r`, with `/` and `%`, which truncate (D-069, D-092). Then the result is `a × factor + b × q + (b × r) / 10⁹` seconds plus `(b × r) % 10⁹` nanoseconds, carried with floor division into D-650's representation, as `normalized` carries. `b × r` is below 10¹⁸ in size and is computed in `I64`, which is exact on JS (ADR-0030). Every partial product has the sign of the result or is 0, so no step overflows unless the result does. It is Cheby, so constants can run it. `duration::divide` waits until its exact algorithm is written. Why: backoff and rate limits multiply spans, 019 keeps a `backoff_ms: Int` for want of `scale`, and D-651's reason against it, that it "overflows `Int` on JS for spans whose result fits", does not hold for this algorithm. A negative factor or a negative span gives a negative nanosecond part before the carry, so the carry must be floored. Rejected: `Mul` (needs `Self` on both sides, §8.7); a `Float` factor (not exact); `divide` now (not yet shown to be exact on every target). Refines D-651.
- **D-1102 `fiber::yield_now`**: `yield_now()` suspends the calling fiber once and puts it behind the fibers that can run, and a pending cancellation is delivered there. On JS it is a real suspension point, so the functions that call it are compiled as suspending (D-014). §10.2 lists it among the suspension points, and §10.8's advice for bounding CPU work on JS names it. It is Cheby over a private `@std_context` kernel `sys_yield_now`, bound as D-484 binds every kernel. It waits and calls no function value, so `waits.rs` lists it when it lands (D-1059). It is implemented in M4, with the JS scheduler, and its tests are pending for M4 (D-741). Why: it is the one portable way to bound a CPU loop's latency on JS, where a CPU-bound fiber can starve the others (D-185), while `time::sleep(zero)` returns at once (D-652) and a zero timer arm is ready at once (D-673), so neither is sure to suspend. Go's `runtime.Gosched` and tokio's `yield_now` fill the same role. Rejected: making `sleep(zero)` suspend (breaks D-652's "already elapsed"); the name `yield` (a reserved word, §2.4); documenting a `select` on a 1 ns timer (a timer per yield, and an obscure idiom). Extends D-185.

Open:

- `int::fold_range(start, end, initial, f)`, a counting fold that builds no list (UQ-9), is deferred until a program needs it. The examples count with local recursion, and `list::range` stays the counting idiom.
- `duration::divide` is logged once its exact algorithm is written: truncation toward zero to whole nanoseconds, a panic for a divisor of 0 (D-069), and exactness on JS, where `seconds × 10⁹ + nanoseconds` passes ±(2⁵³ − 1) after about 104 days.
- D-386 stands. The order tree of `ordered_map` is a size-annotated `SortedMap<Int, K>`, so once `sorted_map::at` exists, positions in the insertion-ordered modules would cost O(log n) without new nodes. A later session may reopen D-386. `sorted_multimap::split` and `rank` can come later too (D-1083).

- Examples may adopt the additions, but none has to: 029's `read_sales` could use `list::try_map` and its CSV reader `string::lines`, 023 and 032 `string::words`, 015 `list::split_while`, 023 and 029 `float::to_fixed`, 005, 009, 018 and 019 `channel::fold`, `to_list` or `send_all`, 019 `duration::scale`, 021 `std::base64` and the wrapping conversions, 031 `fiber::raise`, and 008, 010, 014, 015, 021, 029 and 030 `result::try_with`. 015, 018, 021, 024 and 028 teach their hand-written versions and keep them (D-230's precedent).
- No new diagnostic code. Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.
